Theorem 1.II.T2 — Exact full-turn fiber of the ordinary unit-circle phase [R]


For x,y∈ℝ,


(cos x,sin x)=(cos y,sin y)


if and only if


x−y∈2πℤ.


Proof.


If x−y=2πn, ordinary trigonometric periodicity gives equality of both coordinates.


Conversely, equality of both sine and cosine values means that the two angles determine the same point of the ordinary unit circle. Equality of the full ordered pair fixes the angle modulo one complete revolution, hence x≡y mod 2π. Therefore x−y∈2πℤ. ∎


Corollary 1.II.C1 — Full-turn quotient


The ordinary unit-circle phase is represented by the quotient


𝕋₂π=ℝ/(2πℤ).


The quotient class of x is written [x]₂π.


Thus


[x]₂π=[y]₂π


if and only if


x−y∈2πℤ.


Clarification 1.II.CL2 — Quotient equality is not literal equality


The statement


[x]₂π=[x+2π]₂π


does not assert x=x+2π as real numbers. It asserts equality of two equivalence classes after the quotient has intentionally forgotten the integer full-turn translation.


1.II.B — The half-period quotient


The later reciprocal-trigonometric calculus will contain objects whose natural static period is π rather than 2π. Book 1 therefore defines the corresponding quotient now, but it does not yet claim that any particular R-operator descends to it. That descent will be proved only after the unnamed reciprocal factors have been established and their period has been derived in 1.V.


Definition 1.II.D4 — Half-turn equivalence


Two real phases x and y are half-turn equivalent when


x−y∈πℤ.


Definition 1.II.D5 — Half-period quotient


Define


𝕋π=ℝ/(πℤ).


The quotient class of x is written [x]π.


Thus


[x]π=[y]π


if and only if


x−y∈πℤ.


No physical state-space interpretation is attached to 𝕋π by this definition.


Definition 1.II.D6 — Natural folding map


Define


κ:𝕋₂π→𝕋π


by


κ([x]₂π)=[x]π.


Theorem 1.II.T3 — Well-defined two-to-one folding [R]


The map κ is well defined and surjective. Every point of 𝕋π has exactly two preimages in 𝕋₂π:


κ^{-1}([x]π)={ [x]₂π, [x+π]₂π }.


Proof.


Suppose [x]₂π=[y]₂π. Then x−y∈2πℤ⊂πℤ, so [x]π=[y]π. Hence κ is well defined.


Surjectivity is immediate because every [x]π is the image of [x]₂π.


If κ([y]₂π)=[x]π, then y−x∈πℤ. Modulo 2π there are exactly two parity classes of integer multiples of π: even multiples give [y]₂π=[x]₂π and odd multiples give [y]₂π=[x+π]₂π. These two classes are distinct on 𝕋₂π because π∉2πℤ. ∎


Corollary 1.II.C2 — Half-turn invisibility after folding


On 𝕋π,


[x]π=[x+π]π.


On 𝕋₂π, however,


[x]₂π≠[x+π]₂π.


Thus the passage from 𝕋₂π to 𝕋π removes one binary half-turn distinction.


Negative Theorem 1.II.N1 — No recovery of the original full-turn class from the π quotient alone


There is no deterministic map


F:𝕋π→𝕋₂π


such that


F([x]π)=[x]₂π


for every real x.


Proof.


The same input satisfies


[x]π=[x+π]π,


but the demanded outputs would be distinct:


[x]₂π≠[x+π]₂π.


A deterministic function cannot assign two different outputs to one input. ∎


Corollary 1.II.C3 — A binary half-turn datum is sufficient pointwise


Because every κ-fiber contains exactly two points, one additional binary choice is sufficient to select a member of the fiber. The quotient class itself does not canonically supply that choice.


Clarification 1.II.CL3 — Selection is not reconstruction from the quotient


One may impose a convention that chooses one representative from each two-point fiber. Such a convention is additional data. It does not show that the discarded half-turn distinction was secretly present in [x]π.


1.II.C — Quotient maps and information hierarchy


Definition 1.II.D7 — Canonical quotient maps


Define


q₂π:ℝ→𝕋₂π,

q₂π(x)=[x]₂π,


and


qπ:ℝ→𝕋π,

qπ(x)=[x]π.


Theorem 1.II.T4 — Quotient factorization [R]


The maps satisfy


qπ=κ∘q₂π.


Proof.


For every x∈ℝ,


(κ∘q₂π)(x)=κ([x]₂π)=[x]π=qπ(x). ∎


Theorem 1.II.T5 — Exact fiber hierarchy [R]


For every x∈ℝ,


q₂π^{-1}([x]₂π)=x+2πℤ,


qπ^{-1}([x]π)=x+πℤ,


and


κ^{-1}([x]π)={ [x]₂π,[x+π]₂π }.


Proof.


Each statement is the corresponding equivalence relation written as a fiber. The third is Theorem 1.II.T3. ∎


Corollary 1.II.C4 — Three static levels of phase information


The three spaces retain different amounts of static translation information:


1. a lifted value x∈ℝ distinguishes all translated real representatives;

2. [x]₂π forgets the integer number of complete 2π translations but retains the distinction between x and x+π;

3. [x]π additionally identifies the two half-turn representatives.


No statement here implies that any of these three packages is physically fundamental.


Negative Theorem 1.II.N2 — No recovery of an original lift from a periodic class alone


No deterministic function of [x]₂π alone can recover the original real number x for every x∈ℝ. Likewise, no deterministic function of [x]π alone can recover the original real number x for every x∈ℝ.


Proof.


For the 2π quotient, x and x+2π have the same class but are distinct real inputs. For the π quotient, x and x+π have the same class but are distinct real inputs. A function of the quotient class must return the same output for all members of one fiber, so it cannot reproduce every original member. ∎


Clarification 1.II.CL4 — Principal representatives are conventions


Choosing a principal interval such as [0,2π) or [0,π) produces a useful representative of a quotient class. It does not recover the original lift. A principal representative answers “which conventional representative shall we use?” rather than “which translated real phase generated this class?”


Clarification 1.II.CL5 — Static lift and history are different questions


Even a real lifted value at one instant is not a complete history. Winding history requires an ordered path or an independently retained lift ledger. The historical T0 carrier analysis makes this distinction explicit: a continuous path plus an initial lift can determine a continuous lifted history, whereas isolated periodic states cannot determine how many intervening windings occurred. Book 1 records that distinction without assigning a physical meaning to the path parameter.


1.II.D — Descent of the phase transformations


The translations and scalar involutions introduced in the Book 1 legend act naturally on the quotient spaces because they respect the corresponding equivalence relations.


Theorem 1.II.T6 — Translation descent [R]


For every a∈ℝ, the translation τ_a descends to well-defined maps on both quotients:


τ_a([x]₂π)=[x+a]₂π,


τ_a([x]π)=[x+a]π.


Proof.


If x−y belongs to 2πℤ, then


(x+a)−(y+a)=x−y∈2πℤ.


The same argument holds with πℤ. Therefore the result is independent of the chosen representative. ∎


Corollary 1.II.C5 — Full-turn identities on the quotients


On 𝕋₂π,


τ_{2π}=id,


while τ_π is a nontrivial involution.


On 𝕋π,


τ_π=id.


Thus the same real translation can be visible on one quotient and invisible on another.


Theorem 1.II.T7 — Quarter-turn order changes under the quotient [R]


The translation τ_{π/2} has order four on 𝕋₂π and order two on 𝕋π.


Proof.


On 𝕋₂π,


τ_{π/2}^2=τ_π≠id,


but


τ_{π/2}^4=τ_{2π}=id.


On 𝕋π,


τ_{π/2}^2=τ_π=id,


while τ_{π/2} itself is nontrivial. ∎


Corollary 1.II.C6 — Quotienting can reduce the visible order of a symmetry


The order of an induced transformation on a quotient need not equal the order of its lifted transformation. Quotienting can identify intermediate states and thereby reduce the order of the visible action.


This fact will later explain why an induced permutation or operator action may have lower order than the phase transformation from which it descends. It is a quotient fact, not evidence of extra dynamics.


Theorem 1.II.T8 — Reflection descent [R]


The involutions


ι(x)=−x,


ι_d(x)=π/2−x


descend to both 𝕋₂π and 𝕋π.


Proof.


If x−y is an integer multiple of the relevant period L∈{π,2π}, then


ι(x)−ι(y)=−(x−y)


is also an integer multiple of L. Likewise,


ι_d(x)−ι_d(y)=−(x−y).


Therefore both maps preserve each equivalence relation. ∎


Theorem 1.II.T9 — Basic reflection relations [R]


On the lifted line and on either quotient where the expressions are interpreted modulo the appropriate period,


ι²=id,


ι_d²=id,


ι_d=τ_{π/2}∘ι,


ι∘τ_a∘ι=τ_{−a},


ι_d∘τ_a∘ι_d=τ_{−a}.


Proof.


Each identity follows by direct substitution. For example,


ι(τ_a(ι(x)))=ι(−x+a)=x−a=τ_{−a}(x).


The remaining formulas are equally immediate. ∎


Clarification 1.II.CL6 — Symmetry is not physical reversibility


A mathematical involution of phase space is not by itself a time-reversal law, parity law, charge conjugation, mirror universe, or physical reversal process. Such meanings require later structures and operational contracts.


1.II.E — The quotient-information firewall


The purpose of introducing both circles is not to choose between them. It is to keep explicit which distinctions survive a representation.


Theorem 1.II.T10 — Quotient information cannot be restored by downstream relabeling [R]


Let G be any deterministic function defined on 𝕋π. Then G takes the same value on the two full-turn classes [x]₂π and [x+π]₂π after they have been folded by κ.


Proof.


Both classes map to the same quotient point:


κ([x]₂π)=κ([x+π]₂π)=[x]π.


Therefore any function evaluated only after κ receives identical input in the two cases. ∎


Corollary 1.II.C7 — More output coordinates do not restore a discarded branch


The conclusion remains true even when G has many output components. Increasing display dimension downstream does not reconstruct information removed by κ.


Corollary 1.II.C8 — Information preservation is task-relative


A π-periodic representation may be complete for a task whose observables are themselves π-periodic, while being incomplete for a task that asks for phase modulo 2π or for a lifted representative. “Complete” therefore always means complete for a declared reconstruction task.


Clarification 1.II.CL7 — The π quotient is admitted, not preferred


The existence and simplicity of 𝕋π do not establish that nature identifies half-turn-separated phases. Book 1 introduces 𝕋π because later inherited mathematics contains exactly π-periodic structures. Whether a physical application should identify those states is a separate question.


1.II.F — Prospective connection to the reciprocal calculus


The historical T0 corpus proves two facts that motivate the present separation without being used here as hidden premises.


First, the later primitive reciprocal family is invariant under x↦x+π, so its complete static values cannot distinguish x from x+π. Second, the seam-safe harmonic carrier has exact fibers x+πℤ and therefore provides a coordinate model of ℝ/(πℤ). Those source results justify why 𝕋π will matter downstream, but Book 1 does not import the named operators or carrier into this section. Their relevant periodicity and reconstruction statements are re-established at the proper dependency depth later.


The source corpus also proves that static phase modulo 2π requires one additional half-turn branch relative to the π-periodic internal state, and that lifted winding history requires still more historical information. Those are examples of the general quotient-information principle proved above: algebraic simplification can remove redundant display, but it cannot erase genuine branch or history data and then recover them for free.


1.II.G — Non-scope


Nothing in this section establishes:


• that x is time or an evolution parameter;

• that increasing x is a physical forward direction;

• that τ_a is a dynamical flow generated by a Hamiltonian or action;

• that 2π periodicity means a physical system repeats after one revolution;

• that π periodicity means antipodal physical states are identical;

• that 𝕋₂π or 𝕋π is the ontological state space of nature;

• that a quotient is a measurement process;

• that a lost branch corresponds to hidden matter, a mirror sector, spin, charge, parity, or any other physical label;

• or that path lifting supplies a physical law of motion.


These questions are deferred until the required mathematical and physical structures exist.


Book 1, Section 1.II — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: ℝ, ℤ, radian phase, ordinary sine/cosine periodicity, quotient sets, equivalence relations, elementary maps, and ordinary function composition.


Normalized Book 1 notation used: τ_a, ι, ι_d, 𝕋₂π, 𝕋π, [x]₂π, [x]π, q₂π, qπ, and κ.


Source provenance: T0.I supplies the lifted phase line and the 2π phase circle; T0.II supplies the historical half-turn invariance and half-turn information-loss motivation; T0.III supplies the later branch-versus-lift distinction; T0.IV supplies the exact π-periodic carrier fibers and the path-lifting/history distinction. The present section rederives only the underlying quotient mathematics and imports none of the later operator names or physical interpretations.


New R-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical state-space declarations: 0.


Proved in 1.II: exact 2π fibers of the ordinary unit-circle phase; the two-to-one folding 𝕋₂π→𝕋π; exact quotient fibers; impossibility of recovering discarded half-turn or lift information from the quotient class alone; descent of translations and reflections; reduced visible symmetry order under quotienting; and the quotient-information firewall.


Certified handoff: 1.III restricts the lifted phase line to the reciprocal-trigonometric domain D, decomposes its analytic seam set into sine-zero and cosine-zero classes, and establishes the quadrant and octant atlas without yet assigning R-operator names.


1.III — RECIPROCAL-TRIGONOMETRIC DOMAIN D, QUADRANTS, OCTANTS, AND THE ANALYTIC SEAM SET


1.III.A — Why a restricted domain is required


The ordinary trigonometric functions used later do not share one natural domain. The functions tan x and sec x fail where cos x=0, while cot x and csc x fail where sin x=0. Because the reciprocal-conjugate calculus will use all four simultaneously, Book 1 must first isolate the common domain on which every constituent is defined.


This restriction is analytic. It says where particular formulas are defined as ordinary real functions. It does not identify a singular point with a physical event, boundary, collision, horizon, transition, or discontinuity in nature.


Definition 1.III.D1 — Sine-zero seam set


Define


Σ_s={kπ:k∈ℤ}.


Equivalently, Σ_s is the zero set of sin x.


Definition 1.III.D2 — Cosine-zero seam set


Define


Σ_c={π/2+kπ:k∈ℤ}.


Equivalently, Σ_c is the zero set of cos x.


Definition 1.III.D3 — Analytic seam set


Define


Σ=Σ_s∪Σ_c.


Theorem 1.III.T1 — Uniform seam formula [R]


The analytic seam set is exactly


Σ={kπ/2:k∈ℤ}.


Proof.


Every even integer k=2n gives


kπ/2=nπ∈Σ_s.


Every odd integer k=2n+1 gives


kπ/2=π/2+nπ∈Σ_c.


Conversely, every element of Σ_s has the form 2n·π/2 and every element of Σ_c has the form (2n+1)π/2. Therefore their union is precisely {kπ/2:k∈ℤ}. ∎


Definition 1.III.D4 — Indexed seam points


For k∈ℤ, write


b_k=kπ/2.


Thus


Σ={b_k:k∈ℤ}.


Theorem 1.III.T2 — Alternating seam decomposition [R]


The two seam families are disjoint and alternate along the lifted phase line:


Σ_s={b_{2n}:n∈ℤ},


Σ_c={b_{2n+1}:n∈ℤ},


Σ_s∩Σ_c=∅.


Proof.


The first two identities follow from the even/odd decomposition in Theorem 1.III.T1. If a point belonged to both Σ_s and Σ_c, sine and cosine would vanish simultaneously there. But sin²x+cos²x=1 forbids this. Hence the two sets are disjoint. ∎


Corollary 1.III.C1 — Exactly one trigonometric channel fails at each seam


At every b_k∈Σ, exactly one of sin b_k and cos b_k is zero.


If k is even, sin b_k=0 and cos b_k=±1.


If k is odd, cos b_k=0 and sin b_k=±1.


Therefore the sine-reciprocal pair {csc,cot} and the cosine-reciprocal pair {sec,tan} have alternating pole locations rather than a common pole at each seam.


1.III.B — The common reciprocal-trigonometric domain


Definition 1.III.D5 — Reciprocal-trigonometric domain


Define


D=ℝ\Σ.


Equivalently,


D={x∈ℝ:sin x≠0 and cos x≠0}.


Theorem 1.III.T3 — Common-domain characterization [D]


D is exactly the largest subset of ℝ on which tan x, cot x, sec x, and csc x are all simultaneously defined as ordinary finite real functions.


Proof.


The pair tan x,sec x requires cos x≠0. The pair cot x,csc x requires sin x≠0. Therefore all four are defined precisely when both sine and cosine are nonzero, which is exactly ℝ\(Σ_s∪Σ_c)=D. No larger subset can satisfy this condition because every point of Σ makes one reciprocal pair undefined. ∎


Corollary 1.III.C2 — D is invariant under π/2 translation


Because


τ_{π/2}(Σ)=Σ,


we have


τ_{π/2}(D)=D.


The same is therefore true for every integer multiple τ_{nπ/2}.


Theorem 1.III.T4 — Topological form of D [D]


D is open in ℝ and disconnected. Its connected components are the open intervals between consecutive seam points.


Proof.


Σ is a discrete closed subset of ℝ, so its complement D is open. Removing every b_k divides the line into the intervals (b_k,b_{k+1}), which are mutually disjoint, connected, and maximal connected subsets of D. ∎


Definition 1.III.D6 — Open quadrants


For each k∈ℤ, define


Q_k=(kπ/2,(k+1)π/2)=(b_k,b_{k+1}).


Theorem 1.III.T5 — Quadrant decomposition [D]


D is the disjoint union


D=⨆_{k∈ℤ}Q_k.


Each Q_k is one connected component of D.


Proof.


This is the component decomposition established in Theorem 1.III.T4, written with the indexed seam notation. ∎


Corollary 1.III.C3 — Every admissible lifted phase has a unique quadrant index


For every x∈D, there exists a unique k∈ℤ such that x∈Q_k.


Thus any chart-local statement may carry its quadrant index explicitly rather than relying on an unstated sign convention.


1.III.C — Closed quadrant copies and boundary bookkeeping


Definition 1.III.D7 — Closed quadrant copy


For each k∈ℤ, define


Q̄_k=[kπ/2,(k+1)π/2].


This is an ordinary compact interval when considered by itself. Later, in the split-boundary construction, different copies of a shared numerical endpoint are kept distinct until a gluing rule is explicitly declared.


Clarification 1.III.CL1 — Closed copy does not assign primitive seam values


The notation Q̄_k includes its endpoints as points of a boundary chart. It does not assert that tan, cot, sec, csc, or any later reciprocal factor acquires a finite value there. Boundary inclusion and function extension are separate questions.


Clarification 1.III.CL2 — Shared numerical endpoint versus identified boundary point


The right endpoint of Q̄_{k−1} and the left endpoint of Q̄_k have the same real coordinate b_k. In a split-boundary space they may nevertheless be represented by distinct directional copies. Equality of the numerical coordinate is not, by itself, a gluing instruction.


1.III.D — Octant subdivision


Quadrants are the natural connected components of the common reciprocal domain, but several later comparisons change character at the midpoint of a quadrant. Book 1 therefore introduces octants purely as a finer geometric subdivision. No operator-dominance meaning is attached to them here.


Definition 1.III.D8 — Open octants


For j∈ℤ, define


O_j=(jπ/4,(j+1)π/4).


Theorem 1.III.T6 — Octant subdivision of a quadrant [Q]


For each k∈ℤ,


Q_k=O_{2k} ∪ {(2k+1)π/4} ∪ O_{2k+1},


and this union is disjoint.


Proof.


The point (2k+1)π/4 is the midpoint of the interval


(kπ/2,(k+1)π/2).


The two adjacent open half-intervals are exactly O_{2k} and O_{2k+1}. ∎


Corollary 1.III.C4 — Octant boundaries are not all analytic seams


The points jπ/4 with odd j lie strictly inside D. Therefore the π/4 subdivision introduces chart boundaries that need not be singularities of the reciprocal-trigonometric functions.


This distinction is essential: an analytic seam is a point removed from D because one reciprocal trigonometric channel is undefined, whereas an octant boundary may be introduced solely to organize a finer comparison on an otherwise regular interval.


Negative Theorem 1.III.N1 — No singularity follows from an octant label


Membership in the boundary of an octant does not, by itself, imply singular behavior of any trigonometric quantity.


Proof.


Take x=π/4. It is an octant boundary, yet sin x and cos x are both nonzero, so tan x,cot x,sec x,csc x are all finite. Therefore octant-boundary status alone is insufficient to imply singularity. ∎


1.III.E — Action of the normalized phase symmetries on the atlas


The normalized transformations introduced in 1.I act exactly on the seam, quadrant, and octant indexing.


Theorem 1.III.T7 — Quarter-turn transport [R]


For every k,j∈ℤ,


τ_{π/2}(b_k)=b_{k+1},


τ_{π/2}(Q_k)=Q_{k+1},


τ_{π/2}(O_j)=O_{j+2}.


Proof.


Add π/2 to each interval endpoint. For example,


τ_{π/2}(Q_k)

=(kπ/2+π/2,(k+1)π/2+π/2)

=((k+1)π/2,(k+2)π/2)

=Q_{k+1}.


The seam and octant formulas are identical index shifts. ∎


Corollary 1.III.C5 — Half-turn transport [R]


τ_π(b_k)=b_{k+2},


τ_π(Q_k)=Q_{k+2},


τ_π(O_j)=O_{j+4}.


Theorem 1.III.T8 — Phase-reflection transport [R]


For ι(x)=−x,


ι(b_k)=b_{−k},


ι(Q_k)=Q_{−k−1},


ι(O_j)=O_{−j−1}.


Proof.


Negation reverses each interval. For example,


ι(Q_k)

=(-(k+1)π/2,−kπ/2)

=Q_{−k−1}.


The seam and octant statements follow similarly. ∎


Theorem 1.III.T9 — Complementary-reflection transport [R]


For ι_d(x)=π/2−x,


ι_d(b_k)=b_{1−k},


ι_d(Q_k)=Q_{−k},


ι_d(O_j)=O_{1−j}.


Proof.


For Q_k,


ι_d(Q_k)

=(π/2−(k+1)π/2, π/2−kπ/2)

=(−kπ/2,(1−k)π/2)

=Q_{−k}.


The other formulas follow by the same endpoint calculation. ∎


Corollary 1.III.C6 — The atlas is symmetry-stable


The maps τ_{π/2}, τ_π, ι, and ι_d preserve D and permute its quadrant and octant charts. Hence these transformations can be analyzed without leaving the common reciprocal-trigonometric domain except at the seam boundaries already removed.


1.III.F — The seam structure on the quotient circles


The quotient spaces of 1.II compress the repeated seam pattern in different ways.


Theorem 1.III.T10 — Seam classes on 𝕋₂π [R]


Modulo 2π, Σ has four distinct seam classes:


[0]₂π,

[π/2]₂π,

[π]₂π,

[3π/2]₂π.


The sine-zero classes are [0]₂π and [π]₂π; the cosine-zero classes are [π/2]₂π and [3π/2]₂π.


Proof.


The seam sequence advances in steps of π/2. Exactly four such steps make a 2π period. Even-indexed seams are sine zeros and odd-indexed seams are cosine zeros by Theorem 1.III.T2. ∎


Theorem 1.III.T11 — Seam classes on 𝕋π [R]


Modulo π, Σ has two distinct seam classes:


[0]π,

[π/2]π.


All sine-zero seams project to [0]π, and all cosine-zero seams project to [π/2]π.


Proof.


Every sine-zero point is kπ and therefore belongs to [0]π. Every cosine-zero point is π/2+kπ and therefore belongs to [π/2]π. These two classes are distinct because π/2∉πℤ. ∎


Corollary 1.III.C7 — The π quotient preserves seam type but loses half-turn location


Passing from 𝕋₂π to 𝕋π identifies the two sine-zero classes with each other and the two cosine-zero classes with each other. It therefore forgets which half-turn representative occurred while retaining whether the seam is of sine-zero or cosine-zero type.


This is another example of task-relative information loss: the quotient preserves one discrete distinction while discarding another.


1.III.G — Singularities, seams, and physical noninterpretation


The word seam is retained because later sections need a compact term for the alternating analytic boundaries of the common reciprocal domain. Its meaning in Book 1 is nevertheless exact and limited.


Definition 1.III.D9 — Analytic seam


An analytic seam is a point b_k removed from D because at least one constituent reciprocal trigonometric function required by the later common calculus is undefined there.


Negative Theorem 1.III.N2 — Analytic seam does not imply physical discontinuity


No physical discontinuity follows from Definition 1.III.D9.


Proof.


The definition refers only to the domain of ordinary real functions of x. It contains no physical observable, law of motion, source, boundary condition, time parameter, metric, or projection contract. Therefore no statement about a physical process can be deduced from seam membership alone. ∎


Corollary 1.III.C8 — Smooth downstream carriers do not retroactively regularize the primitive domain


If a later composite quantity admits a continuous or smooth extension through b_k, that extension is a theorem about the composite quantity. It does not assign finite values to tan,cot,sec,csc at a point where their defining denominators vanish.


The converse also fails: a pole in a primitive constituent does not prove that every later invariant or composite built from the calculus must be singular there.


1.III.H — Domain labels for the remainder of Book 1


From this point forward, the bracketed tags introduced in 1.I will be used with the following discipline.


A statement tagged [D] may use the global absolute-value forms and every ordinary trigonometric constituent defined on D.


A statement tagged [Q] may additionally fix the signs of sin x and cos x because Q_k is connected and contains no zero of either function; the sign theorem itself is proved in 1.IV.


A statement tagged [O] may use a fixed octant where a finer inequality or ordering has been proved. No such ordering is assumed merely from the octant label.


A statement tagged [B] concerns one-sided limits toward b_k from a specified adjacent chart.


A statement tagged [S] concerns an explicitly constructed extension or split-boundary object and must state what has, and has not, been identified.


Constitutional Rule 1.III.R1 — Seam-aware citation


Whenever a proof divides by sin x, cos x, tan x, cot x, or a later expression known to vanish at specified points, its domain must be reduced accordingly before the theorem is stated. Cancellation performed algebraically on D does not automatically define the canceled expression at Σ.


Constitutional Rule 1.III.R2 — No chart boundary by implication


A change in formula, sign convention, or preferred local coordinate at a quadrant or octant boundary does not imply a change in the underlying mathematical object unless a theorem proves such a change.


Book 1, Section 1.III — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: zero sets of sine and cosine; natural domains of tan,cot,sec,csc; elementary topology of ℝ; intervals; quotient classes from 1.II; and the normalized translations/reflections of 1.I–1.II.


Normalized notation fixed: Σ_s, Σ_c, Σ, b_k, D, Q_k, Q̄_k, O_j.


New R-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical seam laws: 0.


Proved in 1.III: the exact alternating decomposition Σ=Σ_s⨆Σ_c; the common reciprocal-trigonometric domain D; the disjoint quadrant decomposition D=⨆Q_k; the geometric octant subdivision; the exact action of τ_{π/2}, τ_π, ι, and ι_d on seams, quadrants, and octants; the four seam classes on 𝕋₂π and two seam classes on 𝕋π; and the distinction between analytic seam, chart boundary, and physical interpretation.


Certified handoff: 1.IV defines the branch-sign functions α=sgn(sin x), β=sgn(cos x), and ε=αβ on D, proves their chartwise constancy, and uses them to convert the global absolute-value envelopes into branch-explicit rational forms without ever discarding the absolute-value information.


1.IV — ABSOLUTE-VALUE ENVELOPES, SIGN CHARTS, AND BRANCH-EXPLICIT RATIONAL FORMS


1.IV.A — Why the absolute values are structural


The common domain D removes the zeros of sin x and cos x, but it does not make either function globally positive. Their signs change from quadrant to quadrant. Any formula that uses reciprocal sine or reciprocal cosine while requiring a branch-independent positive envelope must therefore retain the absolute values unless the relevant signs have first been recorded.


This point is constitutional rather than cosmetic. Replacing |csc x| by csc x or |sec x| by sec x changes the function wherever the corresponding denominator is negative. The lawful alternative is not to suppress the branch information, but to expose it explicitly.


Definition 1.IV.D1 — Sine branch sign [D]


For x∈D, define


α(x)=sgn(sin x).


Because sin x≠0 on D,


α(x)∈{−1,+1}.


Definition 1.IV.D2 — Cosine branch sign [D]


For x∈D, define


β(x)=sgn(cos x),


so that


β(x)∈{−1,+1}.


Definition 1.IV.D3 — Product sign [D]


Define


ε(x)=α(x)β(x).


Since α and β each take only the values ±1,


ε(x)∈{−1,+1}.


Theorem 1.IV.T1 — Exact sign reconstruction [D]


For every x∈D,


sin x=α|sin x|,


cos x=β|cos x|,


|sin x|=α sin x,


|cos x|=β cos x.


Proof.


For any nonzero real u,


u=sgn(u)|u|


and


|u|=sgn(u)u.


Apply this first to u=sin x and then to u=cos x. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C1 — Reciprocal envelopes [D]


For every x∈D,


|csc x|=1/|sin x|=α/sin x,


|sec x|=1/|cos x|=β/cos x.


Proof.


Because α²=β²=1,


1/|sin x|=1/(α sin x)=α/sin x,


and similarly for cosine. ∎


Theorem 1.IV.T2 — Tangent and cotangent sign [D]


For every x∈D,


sgn(tan x)=sgn(cot x)=ε(x),


and therefore


|tan x|=ε tan x,


|cot x|=ε cot x.


Proof.


Since


tan x=sin x/cos x,


its sign is α/β. Because β∈{−1,+1}, β^{-1}=β, so α/β=αβ=ε. The reciprocal cotangent has the same sign. The absolute-value identities then follow from the ordinary sign rule. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C2 — Double-angle sign [D]


ε(x)=sgn(sin 2x).


Proof.


sin 2x=2 sin x cos x.


The factor 2 is positive, so the sign of sin 2x is the product of the signs of sin x and cos x, namely αβ=ε. ∎


1.IV.B — Sign constancy on the quadrant atlas


The branch signs are not continuously variable degrees of freedom. They are locally constant records determined by which connected component of D contains x.


Theorem 1.IV.T3 — Quadrant sign constancy [Q]


On every fixed quadrant Q_k, the functions α, β, and ε are constant.


Proof.


Neither sin x nor cos x vanishes on Q_k. A continuous real function cannot change sign on a connected interval without passing through zero. Hence the signs of sine and cosine are constant on Q_k, and so is their product. ∎


Theorem 1.IV.T4 — Four-quadrant sign cycle [Q]


Modulo four in the quadrant index, the ordered sign pair (α,β) and the product sign ε are:


Q_{4m}:      α=+1, β=+1, ε=+1,

Q_{4m+1}:    α=+1, β=−1, ε=−1,

Q_{4m+2}:    α=−1, β=−1, ε=+1,

Q_{4m+3}:    α=−1, β=+1, ε=−1,


for every m∈ℤ.


Proof.


This is the standard sine/cosine sign pattern on the four quadrants, repeated with period 2π. The product ε is obtained by multiplying the first two signs in each row. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C3 — ε records quadrant parity but not the full quadrant branch


The sign ε distinguishes even-indexed quadrants from odd-indexed quadrants:


ε=+1 on Q_{2m},


ε=−1 on Q_{2m+1}.


But ε does not distinguish Q_{4m} from Q_{4m+2}, nor Q_{4m+1} from Q_{4m+3}.


Therefore the single sign ε contains less branch information than the ordered pair (α,β).


Negative Theorem 1.IV.N1 — ε alone cannot reconstruct both α and β [D]


There is no single-valued rule depending only on ε that recovers the ordered pair (α,β) throughout D.


Proof.


On Q_0 the pair is (+1,+1), while on Q_2 the pair is (−1,−1). Both have ε=+1. Hence the same ε value corresponds to two distinct ordered pairs. The same failure occurs for ε=−1 on Q_1 and Q_3. ∎


This is a useful model of partial information compression: a derived sign can preserve one distinction while losing another.


1.IV.C — Exact transport of the branch signs


The normalized phase symmetries of 1.II–1.III transport the sign records by exact algebraic rules.


Theorem 1.IV.T5 — Quarter-turn sign transport [D]


For every x∈D,


α(x+π/2)=β(x),


β(x+π/2)=−α(x),


ε(x+π/2)=−ε(x).


Proof.


Use


sin(x+π/2)=cos x,


cos(x+π/2)=−sin x.


Taking signs gives the first two identities. Their product gives the third. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C4 — Half-turn sign transport [D]


For every x∈D,


α(x+π)=−α(x),


β(x+π)=−β(x),


ε(x+π)=ε(x).


Thus α and β are half-turn odd, while ε is π-periodic.


Corollary 1.IV.C5 — Full-turn periodicity [D]


α(x+2π)=α(x),


β(x+2π)=β(x),


ε(x+2π)=ε(x).


Theorem 1.IV.T6 — Reflection transport [D]


For ι(x)=−x,


α(−x)=−α(x),


β(−x)=β(x),


ε(−x)=−ε(x).


Proof.


Sine is odd and cosine is even. ∎


Theorem 1.IV.T7 — Complementary-reflection transport [D]


For ι_d(x)=π/2−x,


α(π/2−x)=β(x),


β(π/2−x)=α(x),


ε(π/2−x)=ε(x).


Proof.


Use


sin(π/2−x)=cos x,


cos(π/2−x)=sin x.


Taking signs gives the result. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C6 — Different symmetries preserve different branch information


The half-turn reverses α and β but preserves ε. The complementary reflection exchanges α and β while preserving ε. Ordinary phase reflection reverses α and ε while preserving β. Thus no one of the three signs should be treated as a universal synonym for “orientation.” Each records a specific algebraic distinction.


1.IV.D — Absolute envelopes and chartwise rationalization


The absolute-valued reciprocal functions admit exact branch-explicit forms once α and β have been declared.


Theorem 1.IV.T8 — Branch-explicit reciprocal envelopes [D]


For every x∈D,


|csc x|=α/sin x,


|sec x|=β/cos x.


Equivalently, on a fixed quadrant Q_k where α and β are constants, the absolute-valued reciprocal envelopes are ordinary rational trigonometric expressions with those fixed signs.


This theorem is global on D because the sign functions remain part of the formula. If α or β is replaced silently by +1, the theorem becomes only a chart statement on the corresponding positive-sign region.


Theorem 1.IV.T9 — Branch-explicit sine-channel pair [D]


For either choice of sign ±,


|csc x| ± cot x

= (α ± cos x)/sin x

= (1 ± ε|cos x|)/|sin x|.


Proof.


Using Corollary 1.IV.C1,


|csc x| ± cot x

= α/sin x ± cos x/sin x

= (α ± cos x)/sin x.


Now substitute sin x=α|sin x| and cos x=β|cos x|:


(α ± β|cos x|)/(α|sin x|)

= (1 ± αβ|cos x|)/|sin x|

= (1 ± ε|cos x|)/|sin x|. ∎


Theorem 1.IV.T10 — Branch-explicit cosine-channel pair [D]


For either choice of sign ±,


|sec x| ± tan x

= (β ± sin x)/cos x

= (1 ± ε|sin x|)/|cos x|.


Proof.


The proof is identical after exchanging sine and cosine:


|sec x| ± tan x

= β/cos x ± sin x/cos x

= (β ± sin x)/cos x,


then substitute cos x=β|cos x| and sin x=α|sin x| to obtain


(1 ± αβ|sin x|)/|cos x|

= (1 ± ε|sin x|)/|cos x|. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C7 — Two lawful branch descriptions


The same global absolute-valued expression may be written in either of two exact ways:


1. a raw-trigonometric form carrying α or β explicitly; or

2. a magnitude form carrying ε explicitly.


Neither form authorizes removal of the sign data. They merely package the same branch information differently.


1.IV.E — Why the absolute values cannot be dropped globally


Theorem 1.IV.T11 — Exact conditions for removing one absolute value [D]


On D,


|csc x|=csc x


if and only if α=+1, and


|csc x|=−csc x


if and only if α=−1.


Likewise,


|sec x|=sec x


if and only if β=+1, and


|sec x|=−sec x


if and only if β=−1.


Proof.


The sign of csc x is the sign of sin x, namely α. Therefore csc x is positive exactly when α=+1. The cosine statement is identical. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C8 — Simultaneous signless simplification is quadrant-local


The simultaneous replacements


|csc x|→csc x,


|sec x|→sec x


are valid only where α=β=+1, namely on quadrants Q_{4m}.


They are not valid on all of D and not even on every positive-ε quadrant.


Negative Theorem 1.IV.N2 — Dropping the absolute values changes the global function


The expressions


|csc x|±cot x


and


csc x±cot x


are not the same functions on D. Likewise,


|sec x|±tan x


and


sec x±tan x


are not the same functions on D.


Proof.


Choose any x with sin x<0. Then |csc x|=−csc x≠csc x, so the sine-channel expressions differ. Choose any x with cos x<0 for the cosine-channel case. Such points occur in D, for example x=5π/4 for negative sine and x=3π/4 for negative cosine. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C9 — A chart simplification must carry its chart


Whenever a derivation replaces |csc x| or |sec x| by an unsigned reciprocal function, the theorem must record the sign condition or quadrant on which the replacement is valid. Omitting that condition is not a shorter proof of the global theorem; it is a change of domain.


Constitutional Rule 1.IV.R1 — Absolute-value preservation


The canonical global expressions retain |csc x| and |sec x|. A chartwise derivation may simplify them only after α and β, or an equivalent sign declaration, have been fixed. The global statement is recovered only by restoring the branch data or by supplying an independent branch-invariant proof.


1.IV.F — Smoothness and sign changes


The absolute-value envelopes are smooth on D even though the corresponding global absolute-value functions develop nonsmooth points at the zeros that have been removed.


Theorem 1.IV.T12 — Componentwise smoothness [D]


The functions


|sin x|,

|cos x|,

|csc x|,

|sec x|,

α,

β,

ε


are smooth on each connected component Q_k of D. The discrete sign functions α,β,ε have derivative zero on every Q_k.


Proof.


On Q_k, α and β are constant by Theorem 1.IV.T3. Hence


|sin x|=α sin x,

|cos x|=β cos x,

|csc x|=α csc x,

|sec x|=β sec x,


with fixed coefficients ±1. Each trigonometric function is smooth on Q_k, and constant sign functions have zero derivative there. ∎


Clarification 1.IV.CL1 — Zero derivative does not mean absent information


Although α,β,ε are locally constant and have zero derivative within a quadrant, they distinguish disconnected branches of D. Differential data inside one connected chart therefore need not contain all global discrete branch information.


This distinction will matter later whenever a local differential equation is used to reconstruct a global state: continuous generator data and discrete chart data must not be conflated.


Clarification 1.IV.CL2 — The sign functions are not extended through seams here


Book 1 defines α and β only where their underlying sine and cosine values are nonzero. Assigning sgn(0)=0 would be a conventional extension of the sign function, but it would not make reciprocal trigonometric expressions finite at the seam. No such extension is needed for the present calculus.


1.IV.G — Branch information under the π quotient


The half-turn behavior of α and β gives a precise relation to the quotient hierarchy of 1.II.


Theorem 1.IV.T13 — Half-turn branch loss [D]


The ordered pair (α,β) is not π-periodic:


(α(x+π),β(x+π))=(−α(x),−β(x)).


By contrast,


ε(x+π)=ε(x).


Therefore the full ordered sign pair does not descend unchanged through the half-period identification x∼x+π, while ε does.


Proof.


This is Corollary 1.IV.C4. ∎


Corollary 1.IV.C10 — The π quotient preserves product sign but erases simultaneous sign reversal


The half-period quotient can retain the distinction ε=+1 versus ε=−1 while identifying the two branch pairs


(+1,+1)∼(−1,−1)


and


(+1,−1)∼(−1,+1).


Thus the passage to π-periodic data is not loss of all sign information. It is specifically loss of the simultaneous half-turn sign reversal.


Negative Theorem 1.IV.N3 — A π-periodic scalar cannot recover the full ordered sign pair without extra branch data


Suppose f:D→Y satisfies f(x+π)=f(x) whenever both points lie in D. Then no deterministic function of f(x) alone can distinguish the ordered pair (α,β) at x from the ordered pair at x+π in every case.


Proof.


The two inputs have the same f-value by π-periodicity, while their ordered sign pairs are opposite. A deterministic downstream function of the common f-value must return the same output for both and therefore cannot reproduce both branch pairs. ∎


This theorem is purely mathematical. It does not identify the missing binary distinction with any physical hidden variable.


1.IV.H — Non-scope


Nothing in the sign chart establishes that α, β, or ε is a physical orientation, charge, parity, spin, arrow of time, matter/antimatter label, or mirror-sector index. The sign functions are exact branch records for ordinary trigonometric functions on the disconnected domain D.


Likewise, the fact that ε is π-periodic does not make it a preferred physical observable. It merely shows that ε survives one quotient that erases the simultaneous reversal of α and β.


Book 1, Section 1.IV — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: sign and absolute value; ordinary sine, cosine, tangent, cotangent, secant, and cosecant; double-angle identity; continuity on connected intervals; and the domain/quadrant/symmetry structure proved in 1.III.


Book 1 symbols used: α, β, ε, D, Q_k, τ_{π/2}, τ_π, ι, and ι_d. No new operator names are introduced.


New R-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical sign interpretations: 0.


Proved in 1.IV: exact sign reconstruction; reciprocal absolute-value envelopes; tangent/cotangent sign ε; ε=sgn(sin 2x); quadrant sign constancy and the four-quadrant sign cycle; the fact that ε contains less branch information than (α,β); exact sign transport under quarter-turn, half-turn, and the two reflections; branch-explicit rational forms for the four unnamed reciprocal-conjugate expressions; precise conditions under which absolute values may be simplified; componentwise smoothness; and the half-turn branch information lost by π-periodic representations.


Certified handoff: 1.V treats the four still-unnamed expressions |csc x|±cot x and |sec x|±tan x as a mathematical reciprocal-conjugate family, proves positivity, exact reciprocal pairing, reflection exchange, π-periodicity, and descent to the half-period quotient, and only then hands the structure to Book 2 for canonical R-operator naming.


1.V — RECIPROCAL-CONJUGATE IDENTITIES BEFORE OPERATOR NAMING


1.V.A — Neutral notation for the four factors


The previous section established the exact global absolute-value envelopes and the sign data required to rationalize them chartwise. We may now study the four reciprocal-conjugate factors as ordinary mathematical functions on D without yet assigning their later canonical R names.


Definition 1.V.D1 — Sine-channel factors [D]


Define


F_s^+(x)=|csc x|+cot x,


F_s^−(x)=|csc x|−cot x.


Definition 1.V.D2 — Cosine-channel factors [D]


Define


F_c^+(x)=|sec x|+tan x,


F_c^−(x)=|sec x|−tan x.


The symbols F_s^± and F_c^± are neutral Book 1 placeholders. They exist only to state the pre-operator mathematics compactly. Book 2 will assign the canonical R-operator names after this section closes.


1.V.B — Positivity and reciprocal conjugacy


Lemma 1.V.L1 — Strict envelope inequality [D]


For every x∈D,


|csc x|>|cot x|


and


|sec x|>|tan x|.


Proof.


From the ordinary Pythagorean reciprocal identities,


csc²x−cot²x=1,


sec²x−tan²x=1.


Therefore


|csc x|²−|cot x|²=1>0,


|sec x|²−|tan x|²=1>0.


Both leading magnitudes are positive, so the first magnitude in each pair is strictly larger than the second. ∎


Theorem 1.V.T1 — Positivity of the four factors [D]


For every x∈D,


F_s^+(x)>0,

F_s^−(x)>0,

F_c^+(x)>0,

F_c^−(x)>0.


Proof.


For either sign,


|csc x|±cot x ≥ |csc x|−|cot x|>0


by Lemma 1.V.L1. The cosine-channel proof is identical. ∎


Theorem 1.V.T2 — Exact reciprocal conjugacy [D]


For every x∈D,


F_s^+(x)F_s^−(x)=1,


F_c^+(x)F_c^−(x)=1.


Proof.


Using difference of squares,


F_s^+F_s^−

=(|csc x|+cot x)(|csc x|−cot x)

=|csc x|²−cot²x

=csc²x−cot²x

=1.


Likewise,


F_c^+F_c^−

=(|sec x|+tan x)(|sec x|−tan x)

=sec²x−tan²x

=1. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C1 — Mutual inversion [D]


Because all four factors are positive and nonzero,


F_s^−=1/F_s^+,

F_s^+=1/F_s^−,

F_c^−=1/F_c^+,

F_c^+=1/F_c^−.


Thus each channel consists of one positive scalar and its exact reciprocal.


Corollary 1.V.C2 — No zero and no sign ambiguity on D


None of the four factors vanishes or becomes negative anywhere on D. Any later sign change assigned to a difference, quotient, composite, or transition function therefore cannot be attributed to a sign change of these four factors themselves.


1.V.C — Sum, difference, and threshold identities


Theorem 1.V.T3 — Channel reconstruction by sum and difference [D]


For every x∈D,


F_s^+ + F_s^− = 2|csc x|,


F_s^+ − F_s^− = 2cot x,


F_c^+ + F_c^− = 2|sec x|,


F_c^+ − F_c^− = 2tan x.


Proof.


Add and subtract the defining expressions. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C3 — Each conjugate pair determines its envelope and signed tangent variable [D]


The sine pair determines


|csc x|=(F_s^+ + F_s^−)/2,


cot x=(F_s^+ − F_s^−)/2,


while the cosine pair determines


|sec x|=(F_c^+ + F_c^−)/2,


tan x=(F_c^+ − F_c^−)/2.


Theorem 1.V.T4 — Unit-threshold classification [D]


For the sine channel,


F_s^+>1  ⇔ cot x>0,

F_s^+=1  ⇔ cot x=0,

F_s^+<1  ⇔ cot x<0.


Likewise,


F_c^+>1  ⇔ tan x>0,

F_c^+=1  ⇔ tan x=0,

F_c^+<1  ⇔ tan x<0.


On D, the equalities F_s^+=1 and F_c^+=1 never occur.


Proof.


Because F_s^+F_s^−=1 with both factors positive,


F_s^+>1 ⇔ F_s^+>F_s^−.


By Theorem 1.V.T3,


F_s^+−F_s^−=2cot x,


so the sign of F_s^+−1 agrees with the sign of cot x. The equality case would require cot x=0, which occurs when cos x=0, excluded from D. The cosine-channel statement is identical, with tan x=0 requiring sin x=0, also excluded from D. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C4 — Common threshold side is ε [D]


Because


sgn(tan x)=sgn(cot x)=ε,


we have


ε=+1  ⇔ F_s^+>1 and F_c^+>1,


ε=−1  ⇔ F_s^+<1 and F_c^+<1.


Equivalently, when ε=+1 the minus factors lie in (0,1), and when ε=−1 the minus factors lie in (1,∞).


Thus the product sign ε may be read from which side of the unit threshold either plus factor occupies. This is a mathematical branch statement, not a physical binary observable.


1.V.D — Reflection and complementary exchange


The reciprocal structure is not merely algebraic; the normalized phase involutions exchange the conjugate factors exactly.


Theorem 1.V.T5 — Phase-reflection conjugation [D]


Under ι(x)=−x,


F_s^+(−x)=F_s^−(x),


F_s^−(−x)=F_s^+(x),


F_c^+(−x)=F_c^−(x),


F_c^−(−x)=F_c^+(x).


Proof.


Absolute secant and cosecant are even, while tangent and cotangent are odd:


|csc(−x)|=|csc x|,

cot(−x)=−cot x,


|sec(−x)|=|sec x|,

tan(−x)=−tan x.


Substitution into the four definitions gives the result. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C5 — Reflection is reciprocal inversion [D]


Combining Theorem 1.V.T5 with reciprocal conjugacy,


F_s^+(−x)=1/F_s^+(x),


F_c^+(−x)=1/F_c^+(x),


and likewise for the minus factors.


Thus phase reflection acts as inversion within each positive conjugate channel.


Theorem 1.V.T6 — Complementary-reflection channel exchange [D]


Under ι_d(x)=π/2−x,


F_s^+(ι_d x)=F_c^+(x),


F_s^−(ι_d x)=F_c^−(x),


F_c^+(ι_d x)=F_s^+(x),


F_c^−(ι_d x)=F_s^−(x).


Proof.


Use


sin(π/2−x)=cos x,

cos(π/2−x)=sin x,


so


|csc(π/2−x)|=|sec x|,


cot(π/2−x)=tan x,


and similarly in the opposite channel. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C6 — Complementarity preserves conjugate sign


The complementary reflection exchanges sine and cosine channels without exchanging plus and minus. Phase reflection exchanges plus and minus without exchanging channels. These are distinct symmetries and must not be conflated.


1.V.E — Quarter-turn and half-turn transport


Theorem 1.V.T7 — Quarter-turn channel-conjugate exchange [D]


For every x∈D,


F_s^+(x+π/2)=F_c^−(x),


F_s^−(x+π/2)=F_c^+(x),


F_c^+(x+π/2)=F_s^−(x),


F_c^−(x+π/2)=F_s^+(x).


Proof.


Use


sin(x+π/2)=cos x,

cos(x+π/2)=−sin x,


which imply


|csc(x+π/2)|=|sec x|,


cot(x+π/2)=−tan x,


|sec(x+π/2)|=|csc x|,


tan(x+π/2)=−cot x.


Substitution gives all four relations. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C7 — Two quarter-turns return each factor [D]


Applying Theorem 1.V.T7 twice yields


F_s^±(x+π)=F_s^±(x),


F_c^±(x+π)=F_c^±(x).


Thus the four-factor family is π-periodic.


Theorem 1.V.T8 — Direct half-turn periodicity [D]


For every x∈D,


F_s^±(x+π)=F_s^±(x),


F_c^±(x+π)=F_c^±(x).


Proof.


Under x↦x+π, sine and cosine both change sign. Their absolute reciprocals are unchanged, while


tan(x+π)=tan x,


cot(x+π)=cot x.


Therefore each defining expression is unchanged. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C8 — The family cannot distinguish the half-turn branch


The complete ordered quadruple


(F_s^+,F_s^−,F_c^+,F_c^−)


has the same value at x and x+π. Consequently the quadruple, despite containing four displayed components, does not recover phase modulo 2π from static values alone.


This is the first pre-operator instance of the display-dimension firewall: four displayed positive functions can still encode only π-periodic state information.


1.V.F — Descent to the half-period quotient


Theorem 1.V.T9 — Descent theorem [D]


Each of the four functions F_s^± and F_c^± descends uniquely to a function on the admissible part of the half-period quotient 𝕋π obtained by removing the two seam classes [0]π and [π/2]π.


Equivalently, if x,y∈D and


[x]π=[y]π,


then


F_s^±(x)=F_s^±(y),


F_c^±(x)=F_c^±(y).


Proof.


Equality [x]π=[y]π means y=x+nπ for some n∈ℤ. By Theorem 1.V.T8, all four factors are π-periodic, so their values agree. The two seam classes are removed because the original reciprocal-trigonometric factors are undefined there. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C9 — Natural static state quotient


For the four-factor family alone, the quotient 𝕋π is sufficient to represent static phase dependence on its admissible domain. The larger circle 𝕋₂π contains a half-turn distinction that the family does not see.


Clarification 1.V.CL1 — Sufficient representation is not physical ontology


The statement that 𝕋π is sufficient for this family means only that the four functions factor through the quotient. It does not assert that physical states separated by π are identical, nor that 𝕋π is nature’s state space.


Negative Theorem 1.V.N1 — No static recovery of the 2π branch from the four-factor family [D]


There is no deterministic function of the static quadruple


(F_s^+,F_s^−,F_c^+,F_c^−)


that returns [x]₂π for every x∈D.


Proof.


The quadruple has the same value at x and x+π by Corollary 1.V.C8, while


[x]₂π≠[x+π]₂π.


A deterministic function of identical input cannot return two distinct outputs. ∎


1.V.G — Internal redundancy of the four-factor display


The reciprocal products show immediately that the four displayed entries are not four independent positive variables.


Theorem 1.V.T10 — Pairwise algebraic dependence [D]


Within each channel, either factor uniquely determines its conjugate by reciprocal inversion.


Therefore the four-factor display contains at most two independent positive scalar values before cross-channel relations are considered.


Proof.


This is Corollary 1.V.C1 applied separately to the two channels. ∎


Theorem 1.V.T11 — Trigonometric common-source constraint [D]


The two positive plus factors are not freely specifiable as arbitrary independent positive numbers. They arise from one common phase x and therefore obey the compatibility conditions inherited from


sin²x+cos²x=1,


tan x·cot x=1,


and the sign chart of 1.IV.


Consequently the four-factor family is the image of a one-real-dimensional domain D under a four-component map. Its displayed dimension is four, but its source dimension is one.


Proof.


Each factor is explicitly a deterministic function of the single real input x on D. Hence the image of the map


x↦(F_s^+,F_s^−,F_c^+,F_c^−)


is parameterized by one real variable on each connected component. The reciprocal identities further constrain the image algebraically. ∎


Clarification 1.V.CL2 — One-dimensional source does not yet prove a globally injective generator


Theorem 1.V.T11 establishes a one-parameter source, not yet a global one-scalar coordinate theorem. Injectivity, chart reconstruction, ranges, and half-angle parameterizations require additional analysis. Those are developed in 1.VI and, with the canonical operator names, in Book 2.


1.V.H — Neutral handoff to Book 2


The mathematics established so far is entirely independent of the historical names assigned to the four factors. We have proved that the family consists of four positive, π-periodic, mutually paired reciprocal-conjugate functions on D with exact reflection, complementary, and quarter-turn exchange laws.


Nothing in these proofs depends on the words Sininverx, Sinexpon, Cosinexpon, or Cosinverx. Those names may therefore be introduced in Book 2 as canonical labels for already-defined mathematical objects rather than as additional axioms.


Theorem 1.V.T12 — Naming neutrality [D]


Assigning distinct labels to F_s^+, F_s^−, F_c^+, and F_c^− does not alter their positivity, reciprocal products, domains, periods, ranges, or transformation laws.


Proof.


The properties proved in 1.V.T1–1.V.T11 depend only on the defining expressions and inherited trigonometric identities. A one-to-one relabeling changes notation, not the objects denoted. ∎


Corollary 1.V.C10 — Exact Book 1 / Book 2 boundary


Book 1 establishes the reciprocal-conjugate mathematics. Book 2 begins by assigning the canonical R-operator names to these four factors and then develops the named operator calculus. No new mathematical axiom is required at the naming step.


1.V.I — Non-scope


The positivity of the four factors does not make them probabilities, amplitudes, energies, populations, concentrations, weights, or physical rates.


Their reciprocal pairing does not by itself imply creation/annihilation, expansion/contraction, supply/demand, forward/backward time, particle/antiparticle duality, or any dynamical reciprocity.


Their π-periodicity does not prove that nature identifies half-turn-separated states.


Their quarter-turn permutation does not prove a physical four-state cycle.


These interpretations remain outside Book 1 unless and until a later projection contract or physical law supplies them.


Book 1, Section 1.V — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: reciprocal Pythagorean identities; absolute value; sign; the domain D and symmetry maps of 1.III; and the branch-sign results of 1.IV.


Neutral notation introduced: F_s^+, F_s^−, F_c^+, F_c^−. These are temporary mathematical labels, not canonical R-operator names.


New R-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Canonical R-operator names introduced: 0.


Proved in 1.V: strict positivity of all four factors; exact reciprocal conjugacy; sum/difference reconstruction; unit-threshold classification; threshold encoding of ε; inversion under phase reflection; channel exchange under complementary reflection; channel-and-conjugate exchange under quarter-turn; π-periodicity; descent to the admissible domain of 𝕋π; impossibility of static recovery of the 2π half-turn branch; pairwise algebraic redundancy; one-dimensional common-source status; and naming neutrality.


Certified handoff: 1.VI derives the half-angle forms on declared charts, proves their ranges and monotonicity, and gives explicit transport rules that preserve the absolute-value and branch information established in 1.IV–1.V.


1.VI — HALF-ANGLE FORMS, CHART TRANSPORT, AND RANGE STATEMENTS


1.VI.A — Principal positive chart


The historical source gives the familiar half-angle identities only after declaring the principal positive chart


Q_0=(0,π/2).


On this chart,


α=+1,

β=+1,

ε=+1,


so the absolute-value envelopes reduce lawfully to


|csc x|=csc x,

|sec x|=sec x.


The reduction is valid because the chart has already fixed the signs. It is not a global deletion of the absolute values.


Theorem 1.VI.T1 — Principal sine-channel half-angle forms [Q]


For 0<x<π/2,


F_s^+(x)=cot(x/2),


F_s^−(x)=tan(x/2).


Proof.


On Q_0,


F_s^+=csc x+cot x

=(1+cos x)/sin x

=cot(x/2),


and


F_s^−=csc x−cot x

=(1−cos x)/sin x

=tan(x/2).


These are the ordinary half-angle identities. ∎


Theorem 1.VI.T2 — Principal cosine-channel half-angle forms [Q]


For 0<x<π/2,


F_c^+(x)=tan(π/4+x/2),


F_c^−(x)=tan(π/4−x/2).


Proof.


On Q_0,


F_c^+=sec x+tan x

=(1+sin x)/cos x,


F_c^−=sec x−tan x

=(1−sin x)/cos x.


The standard tangent addition and subtraction identities give the displayed forms. ∎


Corollary 1.VI.C1 — Principal reciprocal pairing is manifest [Q]


The two half-angle pairs satisfy


cot(x/2)tan(x/2)=1,


and


tan(π/4+x/2)tan(π/4−x/2)=1.


Thus the reciprocal-conjugate theorem of 1.V appears directly in half-angle coordinates on the principal chart.


1.VI.B — Principal ranges and monotonicity


Theorem 1.VI.T3 — Principal-chart ranges [Q]


As x increases through Q_0=(0,π/2),


F_s^+ decreases strictly from +∞ toward 1,


F_s^− increases strictly from 0 toward 1,


F_c^+ increases strictly from 1 toward +∞,


F_c^− decreases strictly from 1 toward 0.


The endpoint values are one-sided limits and are not attained inside D.


Proof.


On Q_0, x/2 ranges through (0,π/4). Hence cot(x/2) decreases strictly from +∞ to 1, while tan(x/2) increases strictly from 0 to 1.


Also,


π/4+x/2∈(π/4,π/2),


so its tangent increases strictly from 1 to +∞, while


π/4−x/2∈(0,π/4),


and its tangent decreases strictly from 1 to 0. ∎


Corollary 1.VI.C2 — Principal open ranges [Q]


On Q_0,


F_s^+(Q_0)=F_c^+(Q_0)=(1,+∞),


F_s^−(Q_0)=F_c^−(Q_0)=(0,1).


Clarification 1.VI.CL1 — Monotonicity is coordinate variation, not dynamics


The words increases and decreases in this section refer only to the ordering of function values as the coordinate x varies across a declared chart. They do not imply temporal evolution, growth, decay, energy transfer, causation, or an arrow of time.


1.VI.C — Local quadrant coordinate and transported half-angle charts


A principal-chart identity can be moved to every other quadrant only after the chart transformation is stated explicitly.


Definition 1.VI.D1 — Reduced quadrant coordinate [Q]


For x∈Q_k, define


ξ_k(x)=x−kπ/2.


Then


0<ξ_k<π/2.


The quantity ξ_k is a local coordinate on Q_k. It is determined by x and the quadrant index k and is not an additional degree of freedom.


Theorem 1.VI.T4 — Even-quadrant transport [Q]


If k is even and x∈Q_k, then


F_s^+(x)=cot(ξ_k/2),


F_s^−(x)=tan(ξ_k/2),


F_c^+(x)=tan(π/4+ξ_k/2),


F_c^−(x)=tan(π/4−ξ_k/2).


Proof.


Write k=2m. Then


x=ξ_k+mπ.


All four factors are π-periodic by Theorem 1.V.T8. Hence their values at x equal their values at ξ_k∈Q_0, where Theorems 1.VI.T1–T2 apply. ∎


Theorem 1.VI.T5 — Odd-quadrant transport [Q]


If k is odd and x∈Q_k, then


F_s^+(x)=tan(π/4−ξ_k/2),


F_s^−(x)=tan(π/4+ξ_k/2),


F_c^+(x)=tan(ξ_k/2),


F_c^−(x)=cot(ξ_k/2).


Proof.


Write k=2m+1. Then


x=ξ_k+π/2+mπ.


By π-periodicity, it is enough to transport the principal chart through one quarter-turn. Theorem 1.V.T7 gives


F_s^+(ξ_k+π/2)=F_c^−(ξ_k),


F_s^−(ξ_k+π/2)=F_c^+(ξ_k),


F_c^+(ξ_k+π/2)=F_s^−(ξ_k),


F_c^−(ξ_k+π/2)=F_s^+(ξ_k).


Substituting the principal half-angle forms gives the stated formulas. ∎


Corollary 1.VI.C3 — Only two half-angle chart types occur [Q]


Because the family is π-periodic, the transported half-angle form depends only on the parity of k. Even quadrants share one chart type; odd quadrants share the quarter-turn-exchanged chart type.


Since ε=+1 on even quadrants and ε=−1 on odd quadrants, the two chart types may equivalently be indexed by ε.


1.VI.D — Global quadrant ranges


Theorem 1.VI.T6 — Exact ranges by branch sign [Q]


On any quadrant with ε=+1,


F_s^+,F_c^+ ∈(1,+∞),


F_s^−,F_c^− ∈(0,1).


On any quadrant with ε=−1,


F_s^+,F_c^+ ∈(0,1),


F_s^−,F_c^− ∈(1,+∞).


Each displayed interval is the exact image of the corresponding function on that quadrant.


Proof.


If ε=+1, the quadrant index k is even, so Theorem 1.VI.T4 reduces the problem to the principal ranges of Theorem 1.VI.T3.


If ε=−1, k is odd. Then Theorem 1.VI.T5 shows that the plus factors are tan(π/4−ξ_k/2) and tan(ξ_k/2), both ranging through (0,1), while the minus factors are tan(π/4+ξ_k/2) and cot(ξ_k/2), both ranging through (1,+∞). ∎


Corollary 1.VI.C4 — Range recovers ε [D]


For either plus factor,


ε=sgn(F_s^+−1)=sgn(F_c^+−1).


For either minus factor,


ε=−sgn(F_s^−−1)=−sgn(F_c^−−1).


Thus the unit threshold encodes the product-sign branch throughout D.


Theorem 1.VI.T7 — Uniform quadrant monotonicity [Q]


Across every open quadrant Q_k, as x increases from its left seam toward its right seam,


F_s^+ decreases strictly,


F_s^− increases strictly,


F_c^+ increases strictly,


F_c^− decreases strictly.


Proof.


The reduced coordinate ξ_k increases strictly with x. In the even-chart formulas of Theorem 1.VI.T4, cot(ξ_k/2) and tan(π/4−ξ_k/2) decrease, while tan(ξ_k/2) and tan(π/4+ξ_k/2) increase.


The odd-chart formulas of Theorem 1.VI.T5 assign those same monotone functions to the corresponding factors in exactly the same directional pattern. ∎


Corollary 1.VI.C5 — One-sided range table [B]


For even k, as x runs left-to-right across Q_k,


F_s^+: +∞→1,

F_s^−: 0→1,

F_c^+: 1→+∞,

F_c^−: 1→0.


For odd k,


F_s^+: 1→0,

F_s^−: 1→+∞,

F_c^+: 0→1,

F_c^−: +∞→1.


Every arrow denotes one-sided limiting behavior. No endpoint value is assigned to the original reciprocal-trigonometric function at the excluded seam.


Corollary 1.VI.C6 — Each factor is a quadrant coordinate [Q]


On every fixed Q_k, each of F_s^+, F_s^−, F_c^+, and F_c^− is continuous and strictly monotone, hence injective onto its exact open range.


Therefore any one of the four factors can be used as a local scalar coordinate on a declared quadrant. This is a local mathematical statement; it does not yet establish a preferred global generator or physical coordinate.


1.VI.E — Midpoint values and why octants matter


The octant subdivision introduced in 1.III becomes mathematically useful because the two plus factors cross exactly once inside each quadrant, at the quadrant midpoint.


Definition 1.VI.D2 — Quadrant midpoint [Q]


For each k∈ℤ, define


m_k=(2k+1)π/4.


Then m_k is the common boundary between O_{2k} and O_{2k+1} and satisfies


ξ_k(m_k)=π/4.


Theorem 1.VI.T8 — Exact midpoint values [Q]


Let ε be the constant branch sign on Q_k. At x=m_k,


F_s^+(m_k)=F_c^+(m_k)=√2+ε,


F_s^−(m_k)=F_c^−(m_k)=√2−ε.


Proof.


If k is even, ε=+1. The even-chart formulas at ξ_k=π/4 give


cot(π/8)=tan(3π/8)=√2+1,


and


tan(π/8)=tan(π/8)=√2−1.


If k is odd, ε=−1. The odd-chart formulas exchange the two values, giving plus factors √2−1 and minus factors √2+1. These two cases are exactly √2±ε. ∎


Corollary 1.VI.C7 — Reciprocal midpoint pair [Q]


At every quadrant midpoint,


(√2+ε)(√2−ε)=1,


so the common plus value and common minus value remain reciprocal conjugates.


Theorem 1.VI.T9 — Octant comparison ordering [O]


For x∈O_{2k}, the first octant of Q_k,


F_s^+(x)>F_c^+(x),


F_s^−(x)<F_c^−(x).


For x∈O_{2k+1}, the second octant of Q_k,


F_s^+(x)<F_c^+(x),


F_s^−(x)>F_c^−(x).


At their common boundary m_k, the corresponding factors are equal.


Proof.


By Theorem 1.VI.T7, F_s^+ decreases strictly across Q_k while F_c^+ increases strictly. Theorem 1.VI.T8 gives their unique crossing at m_k. Hence the stated ordering holds on the two sides.


The minus-factor inequalities follow by reciprocal inversion of positive quantities. ∎


Clarification 1.VI.CL2 — Comparison ordering is not yet operator dominance


The inequalities in Theorem 1.VI.T9 are exact mathematical comparisons. Book 1 does not attach a physical meaning such as dominant channel, active mode, growth sector, energy preference, or causal direction to the larger value. Any such interpretation is downstream.


1.VI.F — Principal formulas are not global formulas


Negative Theorem 1.VI.N1 — No unqualified global promotion of the principal half-angle package


The four formulas of Theorems 1.VI.T1–T2, written directly in terms of the unreduced x, are not a valid single global chart on all of D.


Proof.


It is enough to exhibit one failure. At x=5π/4,


F_s^+(5π/4)=|csc(5π/4)|+cot(5π/4)=√2+1.


But


cot((5π/4)/2)=cot(5π/8)=1−√2,


which is negative and therefore cannot equal the positive factor F_s^+.


Thus the principal formula F_s^+=cot(x/2) cannot be promoted from Q_0 to all of D without chart transport. ∎


Corollary 1.VI.C8 — Chart transport, not absolute-value deletion, globalizes the half-angle description


The lawful global statement is the atlas formed by Theorems 1.VI.T4–T5 together with the quadrant index or equivalent branch data. The principal formulas themselves remain [Q] statements.


Constitutional Rule 1.VI.R1 — Half-angle chart discipline


Whenever a half-angle identity is used downstream, its chart must be recoverable from the theorem statement or from already-declared branch data. A principal-chart identity may not be cited as a global identity merely because the underlying absolute-valued function is globally defined on D.


1.VI.G — Relation to the π quotient


Because only the parity of k affects the transported half-angle form and the four-factor family is π-periodic, the admissible part of 𝕋π requires exactly two open quadrant-chart types separated by the two seam classes [0]π and [π/2]π.


The even-chart type carries ε=+1 and the odd-chart type carries ε=−1. The quotient therefore preserves the distinction required to choose the correct local half-angle chart even though it has discarded the additional half-turn branch encoded by the simultaneous reversal of α and β.


This is consistent with the information hierarchy established in 1.II and 1.IV: the π quotient loses some branch information, but not the product-sign information needed for the reciprocal-factor ranges.


1.VI.H — Non-scope


Nothing in the half-angle representation establishes that the tangent coordinate is a physical amplitude, rapidity, probability odds, scale factor, or time variable.


Nothing in the monotonicity theorem establishes a physical flow.


Nothing in the octant ordering establishes a preferred physical channel.


Nothing in the exact midpoint values √2±1 establishes a fundamental physical constant or calibration.


The half-angle forms are coordinate identities in ordinary trigonometry. Their value here is structural: they make positivity, reciprocity, ranges, local invertibility, and chart transport transparent.


Book 1, Section 1.VI — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: standard half-angle identities; tangent addition/subtraction identities; monotonicity of tan and cot on their ordinary intervals; π-periodicity; and the reciprocal-factor symmetry proved in 1.V.


Source provenance: the principal positive chart, the four principal half-angle identities, the one-sided ranges, and the explicit warning that coordinate monotonicity is not dynamics are inherited from T0.II. The quadrant transport, parity atlas, exact midpoint theorem, and octant comparison ordering are re-derived here from those inherited results plus the already-proved Book 1 quarter-turn and half-turn symmetries.


New Book 1 notation: ξ_k for the reduced quadrant coordinate and m_k for the quadrant midpoint.


New R-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Canonical R-operator names introduced: 0.

Physical dominance or dynamics claims: 0.


Proved in 1.VI: the principal half-angle forms; principal and global quadrant ranges; exact chart transport to every Q_k; the two parity-indexed half-angle chart types; uniform monotonicity on every quadrant; local injectivity of each reciprocal factor; exact midpoint values √2±ε; the octant comparison ordering; failure of unqualified global promotion of the principal chart; and compatibility of the two chart types with the π quotient.


Certified handoff: 1.VII separates interior identities from boundary behavior. It formalizes one-sided limits, removable versus nonremovable singularities, continuous and smooth extension, gluing, and the split-boundary domain D̂ without inventing a physical seam-crossing law.


1.VII — BOUNDARY LIMITS, CONTINUOUS EXTENSION, GLUING, AND SPLIT-BOUNDARY GRAMMAR


1.VII.A — Common domain versus channel-natural domains


The common domain D was chosen because all four reciprocal-trigonometric constituents must coexist there. That common restriction must not be mistaken for the natural domain of each individual channel.


Definition 1.VII.D1 — Sine-channel natural domain


Define


D_s=ℝ\Σ_s.


On D_s, the functions csc x and cot x are finite, so the two sine-channel factors


|csc x|±cot x


are ordinary finite real functions.


Definition 1.VII.D2 — Cosine-channel natural domain


Define


D_c=ℝ\Σ_c.


On D_c, the functions sec x and tan x are finite, so the two cosine-channel factors


|sec x|±tan x


are ordinary finite real functions.


Theorem 1.VII.T1 — Common-domain intersection [D]


The four-factor common domain is


D=D_s∩D_c.


Proof.


The sine channel requires sin x≠0 and the cosine channel requires cos x≠0. Their simultaneous domain is therefore the set on which both conditions hold, namely ℝ\(Σ_s∪Σ_c)=D. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C1 — Not every exclusion from D is a singularity of every channel


At a cosine-zero seam b∈Σ_c, the sine-channel factors are regular because sin b=±1. At a sine-zero seam b∈Σ_s, the cosine-channel factors are regular because cos b=±1.


Thus “excluded from the common domain D” and “singular for this particular function” are different statements.


Theorem 1.VII.T2 — Opposite-seam regular values [R]


At every cosine-zero seam b∈Σ_c,


|csc b|+cot b=1,


|csc b|−cot b=1.


At every sine-zero seam b∈Σ_s,


|sec b|+tan b=1,


|sec b|−tan b=1.


Proof.


If b∈Σ_c, then cos b=0 and sin b=±1. Hence |csc b|=1 and cot b=0. The sine-channel identities follow.


If b∈Σ_s, then sin b=0 and cos b=±1. Hence |sec b|=1 and tan b=0. The cosine-channel identities follow. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C2 — Channelwise extension from D to its natural domain


The sine-channel factors defined in 1.V on D extend uniquely and smoothly to D_s by the same formulas |csc x|±cot x.


The cosine-channel factors extend uniquely and smoothly to D_c by the same formulas |sec x|±tan x.


At the newly admitted opposite-seam points, the value is 1.


This is not a regularization by convention. The formulas were already ordinary smooth functions there; only the four-factor common-domain restriction had temporarily excluded those points.


1.VII.B — One-sided boundary language


Definition 1.VII.D3 — Left and right seam limits


For a real or extended-real-valued function f defined on D, the symbols


lim_{x→b_k^-} f(x)


and


lim_{x→b_k^+} f(x)


denote approach to b_k from Q_{k−1} and Q_k respectively.


The superscripts − and + record direction of approach on the lifted real line. They do not denote negative and positive function values.


Theorem 1.VII.T3 — Sine-zero boundary profile [B]


Let b_{2n}=nπ∈Σ_s. Then


lim_{x→b_{2n}^-}F_s^+(x)=0,

lim_{x→b_{2n}^-}F_s^−(x)=+∞,


lim_{x→b_{2n}^+}F_s^+(x)=+∞,

lim_{x→b_{2n}^+}F_s^−(x)=0.


For the cosine channel,


lim_{x→b_{2n}^-}F_c^+(x)

=lim_{x→b_{2n}^+}F_c^+(x)=1,


lim_{x→b_{2n}^-}F_c^−(x)

=lim_{x→b_{2n}^+}F_c^−(x)=1.


Proof.


The left quadrant Q_{2n−1} is odd and the right quadrant Q_{2n} is even. Apply the one-sided range table of Corollary 1.VI.C5 to the right endpoint of the odd quadrant and the left endpoint of the even quadrant. ∎


Theorem 1.VII.T4 — Cosine-zero boundary profile [B]


Let b_{2n+1}=π/2+nπ∈Σ_c. Then


lim_{x→b_{2n+1}^-}F_c^+(x)=+∞,

lim_{x→b_{2n+1}^-}F_c^−(x)=0,


lim_{x→b_{2n+1}^+}F_c^+(x)=0,

lim_{x→b_{2n+1}^+}F_c^−(x)=+∞.


For the sine channel,


lim_{x→b_{2n+1}^-}F_s^+(x)

=lim_{x→b_{2n+1}^+}F_s^+(x)=1,


lim_{x→b_{2n+1}^-}F_s^−(x)

=lim_{x→b_{2n+1}^+}F_s^−(x)=1.


Proof.


The left quadrant Q_{2n} is even and the right quadrant Q_{2n+1} is odd. Apply Corollary 1.VI.C5. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C3 — Own-channel zero–pole exchange [B]


At a sine-zero seam, the sine reciprocal-conjugate pair exchanges the directional roles 0 and +∞.


At a cosine-zero seam, the cosine reciprocal-conjugate pair exchanges the directional roles +∞ and 0.


The reciprocal product remains exactly 1 at every interior point of D, but the seam boundary profile is not an ordinary real product 0·∞. No value of that indeterminate expression is assigned at the seam.


Corollary 1.VII.C4 — No finite own-channel continuous extension


Neither F_s^+ nor F_s^− admits a finite continuous extension across a sine-zero seam.


Neither F_c^+ nor F_c^− admits a finite continuous extension across a cosine-zero seam.


Proof.


For each own-channel factor, one one-sided limit is finite zero and the other is +∞. Hence no common finite two-sided limit exists. ∎


1.VII.C — Removable omission versus nonremovable singular behavior


Definition 1.VII.D4 — Removable omission relative to a restricted domain


Let A⊂X and let f:A→ℝ. A point b∈X\A is a removable omission for f relative to A if there exists a finite L such that every approach to b through A has limit L. Defining f(b)=L then gives the unique continuous one-point extension at b.


The word omission is used here because the point may have been excluded only by a common-domain convention rather than by a singularity of the formula itself.


Definition 1.VII.D5 — Nonremovable real singularity relative to a domain


A missing point b is nonremovable as a real-valued point when no finite common limit exists through the declared domain.


Theorem 1.VII.T5 — Channelwise classification of the seams [B]


Relative to the four-factor common domain D:


• every cosine-zero seam is a removable omission for each sine-channel factor, with unique extension value 1;


• every sine-zero seam is a removable omission for each cosine-channel factor, with unique extension value 1;


• every sine-zero seam is nonremovable as a finite real point for each sine-channel factor;


• every cosine-zero seam is nonremovable as a finite real point for each cosine-channel factor.


Proof.


The removable cases follow from the equal finite two-sided limits in Theorems 1.VII.T3–T4 and agree with the direct values of Theorem 1.VII.T2. The nonremovable cases follow from the directional zero–pole exchange. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C5 — Seam type is function-relative


A point can be a seam of the common four-factor atlas while simultaneously being an ordinary smooth point of one channel and a nonremovable boundary of the other.


Therefore the phrase “the function is singular at the seam” is incomplete unless the function and domain are named.


1.VII.D — Continuous and smooth extension are separate theorems


Definition 1.VII.D6 — Continuous extension


Let A⊂X and f:A→Y. A continuous extension of f to X is a continuous map F:X→Y satisfying F|_A=f.


Definition 1.VII.D7 — Smooth extension


When A lies in a smooth ambient manifold and f is smooth on A, a smooth extension is a smooth map F on the larger declared domain satisfying F|_A=f.


A continuous extension need not automatically be smooth; smoothness requires its own proof or an explicit smooth formula.


Theorem 1.VII.T6 — Uniqueness on a dense source


Let A be dense in X and Y be Hausdorff. If two continuous maps F,G:X→Y agree on A, then F=G on all of X.


Proof.


For any x∈X choose a net or, in the present metric setting, a sequence from A converging to x. Continuity forces both F(x) and G(x) to be the limit of the same values on A. Uniqueness of limits in a Hausdorff space gives F(x)=G(x). ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C6 — Seam values of a genuine continuous extension are not free parameters


If a real-valued function on D has a continuous extension to a larger domain containing a seam, that seam value is fixed uniquely by the common limit. It cannot be chosen independently without destroying continuity.


Example 1.VII.EX1 — Smooth restriction and recovery


The ordinary function


g(x)=sin 2x


is smooth on ℝ. If one temporarily restricts g to D, every seam becomes a removable omission of that restricted presentation, and the unique smooth extension is simply the original function g on ℝ, with g(b_k)=0.


This example illustrates extension grammar only. It does not introduce the later harmonic carrier or assign a physical meaning to sin 2x.


Negative Theorem 1.VII.N1 — Extension does not commute with inversion through a zero


A finite continuous extension of a reciprocal combination does not imply a finite extension of the quantity that was inverted.


Proof.


On ℝ\{0}, let a(x)=1/x. Then 1/a(x)=x extends smoothly through x=0 with value 0, while a itself has no finite extension there. Therefore extending a simplified reciprocal expression and then attempting to invert the seam value is not a valid operation in general. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C7 — A seam-safe composite does not regularize its singular constituents


If a later composite of reciprocal factors simplifies on D to a function with a smooth extension across Σ, the extended composite value belongs to that composite. It does not assign finite seam values to the raw factors whose formulas remain undefined or divergent there.


This is the exact boundary rule later required by the carrier construction.


1.VII.E — The split-boundary domain


The common domain D omits the seam points entirely. A different mathematical task is to retain the directional endpoint data of every quadrant without forcing the left and right copies of a seam to coincide.


Definition 1.VII.D8 — Split-boundary domain [S]


Define


D̂=⨆_{k∈ℤ} Q̄_k,


where each


Q̄_k=[kπ/2,(k+1)π/2]


is treated as a component disjoint from every other component, even when two components contain endpoints with the same numerical real coordinate.


Definition 1.VII.D9 — Directional seam copies [S]


For each seam coordinate b_k, write


b̂_k^- 


for the copy of b_k occurring as the right endpoint of Q̄_{k−1}, and


b̂_k^+


for the copy of b_k occurring as the left endpoint of Q̄_k.


These are distinct points of D̂.


Definition 1.VII.D10 — Coordinate projection [S]


Define


p:D̂→ℝ


by sending every point in every closed-quadrant copy to its ordinary real coordinate.


In particular,


p(b̂_k^-)=p(b̂_k^+)=b_k.


Theorem 1.VII.T7 — Projection fiber structure [S]


The coordinate projection p is surjective. It is one-to-one over ℝ\Σ and exactly two-to-one over every seam b_k∈Σ:


p^{-1}(b_k)={b̂_k^-,b̂_k^+}.


Proof.


Every nonseam real number lies in exactly one open quadrant and therefore has one copy in D̂. Every seam is the right endpoint of one closed-quadrant copy and the left endpoint of the next, giving exactly two copies. ∎


Theorem 1.VII.T8 — Component compactness does not compactify the lifted line [S]


Each component Q̄_k of D̂ is compact. The full split-boundary space D̂ is not compact.


Proof.


Each Q̄_k is a closed bounded interval and is therefore compact. The family {Q̄_k:k∈ℤ} is an open cover of the disjoint-union topology with no finite subcover, because each component is disjoint and nonempty. Hence D̂ is not compact. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C8 — Split boundary is bookkeeping, not a global compactification


Adding directional copies of every seam closes each quadrant separately. It does not turn the infinite lifted phase line into one compact object.


1.VII.F — Boundary traces on the split domain


To record the zero–pole exchange without pretending that +∞ is an ordinary real value, it is useful to separate a boundary trace from a function value.


Definition 1.VII.D11 — Extended nonnegative boundary codomain


Write


ℝ̄_+=[0,+∞]


for the nonnegative extended real line used only to record finite nonnegative limits together with the symbol +∞.


Definition 1.VII.D12 — Split boundary trace [S]


For any positive function f on D having one-sided limits in ℝ̄_+ at every seam, define its split boundary trace by assigning to b̂_k^- the left limit at b_k and to b̂_k^+ the right limit at b_k.
