A boundary trace equal to +∞ is not a real-valued extension of f.


Theorem 1.VII.T9 — Exact four-factor split traces [S]


At a sine-zero seam b_{2n}, the ordered boundary traces


(F_s^+,F_s^−,F_c^+,F_c^−)


are


at b̂_{2n}^-:  (0,+∞,1,1),


at b̂_{2n}^+:  (+∞,0,1,1).


At a cosine-zero seam b_{2n+1}, they are


at b̂_{2n+1}^-:  (1,1,+∞,0),


at b̂_{2n+1}^+:  (1,1,0,+∞).


Proof.


This is Theorems 1.VII.T3–T4 rewritten on the split-boundary copies. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C9 — The split boundary preserves directional role information


The numerical seam coordinate b_k alone does not distinguish which member of the singular reciprocal pair tends to zero and which tends to +∞. The split copy b̂_k^- versus b̂_k^+ does.


Thus split-boundary bookkeeping retains information that is lost when the two copies are identified.


1.VII.G — Gluing as a quotient operation


Definition 1.VII.D13 — Seam-gluing equivalence [S]


On D̂ define an equivalence relation ∼_g by identifying exactly the two copies of each numerical seam:


b̂_k^-∼_g b̂_k^+


for every k∈ℤ,


and making no further identifications.


Theorem 1.VII.T10 — Gluing quotient recovers the lifted line [S]


The quotient space


D̂/∼_g


is naturally homeomorphic to ℝ, with the induced map supplied by p.


Proof.


The projection p is continuous on each disjoint closed interval and is constant exactly on the pairs declared equivalent by ∼_g. It therefore factors through a bijection from D̂/∼_g to ℝ. Locally away from seams this map is the ordinary interval coordinate; at a glued seam it joins the adjacent closed half-neighborhoods into the usual real neighborhood. The induced bijection and its inverse are continuous, giving the natural homeomorphism. ∎


Definition 1.VII.D14 — Continuous gluing criterion for scalar boundary data [S]


A real-valued function given separately on adjacent closed-quadrant components descends continuously through the glued seam only if the two boundary values assigned to b̂_k^- and b̂_k^+ are equal. For a function originally defined only on the interiors, this requires equal finite one-sided limits.


Theorem 1.VII.T11 — Channel gluing criterion [S]


At a sine-zero seam, the cosine-channel factors can be glued continuously with common value 1, while the sine-channel factors cannot be glued as finite real-valued functions.


At a cosine-zero seam, the sine-channel factors can be glued continuously with common value 1, while the cosine-channel factors cannot be glued as finite real-valued functions.


Proof.


Read the two split traces in Theorem 1.VII.T9. The regular channel has the same finite value 1 on both copies; the singular channel has mismatched traces 0 and +∞. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C10 — Gluing can discard directional information


When b̂_k^- and b̂_k^+ are identified, any distinction carried solely by which split copy was occupied is lost unless it is stored in an additional label.


This loss may be intentional. It is nevertheless a quotient operation and must be recorded as such.


1.VII.H — Split boundary and smooth gluing solve different problems


Theorem 1.VII.T12 — Separation–gluing distinction [S]


The split-boundary construction and a smooth glued continuation are not competing descriptions of the same mathematical task.


The split boundary is designed to retain distinct directional endpoint data.


A glued continuation is designed to identify compatible directional copies so that a selected quantity becomes continuous through one common point.


Therefore:


1. split-boundary separation can preserve a zero-versus-pole role exchange that gluing would erase;


2. gluing can produce a globally continuous representation for a seam-safe quantity whose two directional limits agree;


3. a seam-safe glued composite need not induce a gluing of its singular constituents;


4. neither construction supplies a physical rule mapping an incoming raw state to an outgoing raw state.


Proof.


Items 1–3 follow from the definitions and Theorems 1.VII.T9–T11. Item 4 follows because no physical dynamics, transition map, action, or projection contract has been introduced. ∎


Negative Theorem 1.VII.N2 — No canonical raw crossing map from the boundary algebra


The one-sided limits of the reciprocal factors do not uniquely determine a map from the exiting split state b̂_k^- to the entering split state b̂_k^+.


Proof.


The boundary data specify the two endpoint traces but contain no rule declaring how one endpoint is transformed into the other. Identification, reflection, reset, relabeling, continuation through another coordinate, termination, or any other crossing prescription would be additional structure. The mathematics of the traces alone selects none of them. ∎


Corollary 1.VII.C11 — A reset can be representational without being physical


If a later scalar chart represents one split copy by an endpoint near 1 and the next by an endpoint near 0, the resulting jump may be a consequence of the chosen chart and gluing convention. It is not automatically an event in the represented system.


1.VII.I — Boundary taxonomy for the reconstructed theory


The preceding results justify a precise vocabulary.


Definition 1.VII.D15 — Common-domain seam


A common-domain seam is any b_k∈Σ excluded from D because the complete four-factor family is not simultaneously finite there.


Definition 1.VII.D16 — Channel-regular seam


A seam is channel-regular for a selected factor when that factor extends smoothly through the seam on its own natural channel domain. In the present system, opposite-channel seams are channel-regular with value 1.


Definition 1.VII.D17 — Channel-singular seam


A seam is channel-singular for a selected factor when no finite continuous extension exists there. In the present reciprocal pairs, an own-channel seam exhibits a directional zero–pole exchange.


Definition 1.VII.D18 — Chart boundary


A chart boundary is a point introduced to delimit a chosen coordinate region. It need not be a singularity. The octant midpoints m_k are examples.


Definition 1.VII.D19 — Split boundary


A split boundary retains distinct copies of a common numerical boundary coordinate so that directional endpoint data remain separately represented.


Definition 1.VII.D20 — Glued boundary


A glued boundary identifies selected boundary copies by an explicit equivalence relation. Continuous descent of a selected scalar quantity additionally requires compatible finite boundary values.


Corollary 1.VII.C12 — Boundary words are not interchangeable


A common-domain seam, a singularity of one channel, a removable omission of another channel, an octant chart boundary, a split boundary copy, and a glued boundary point can refer to different mathematical structures even when they share the same numerical coordinate.


Constitutional Rule 1.VII.R1 — State the object before stating the seam behavior


Every later claim about “what happens at the seam” must identify:


• which mathematical object is being discussed;

• its declared domain;

• whether the statement concerns a function value or a one-sided limit;

• whether the boundary copies are split or glued;

• whether any extension is continuous, smooth, or merely conventional;

• and whether a physical crossing law has been separately introduced.


Without those declarations, the phrase “at the seam” is under-specified.


1.VII.J — Non-scope


Nothing in this section derives passage, reflection, reset, collapse, rebirth, termination, finite transition width, hidden content, causal direction, or a physical seam duration.


Nothing in a value +∞ in a boundary trace establishes infinite physical energy or any other physical divergence.


Nothing in a zero boundary trace establishes annihilation, absence, vacuum, or disappearance.


Nothing in gluing establishes that two physically distinct states are identical.


Nothing in split-boundary separation establishes that two physical worlds or sectors exist.


These are mathematical boundary constructions only.


Book 1, Section 1.VII — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: natural domains of reciprocal trigonometric functions; one-sided limits; continuity and smoothness; dense-set uniqueness of continuous extension; disjoint unions; quotient topology; compactness of closed intervals; and the half-angle boundary ranges proved in 1.VI.


Source provenance: T0.IV supplies the central distinction between seam-safe continuation and seam-unsafe raw data, the warning that extension does not commute with inversion at a zero, the split-boundary construction, and the theorem that gluing and split-boundary separation solve different tasks. Book 1 strips those statements back to pre-operator reciprocal-trigonometric mathematics and re-derives the exact four-factor boundary traces before the later carrier is introduced.


New Book 1 notation: D_s, D_c, b̂_k^−, b̂_k^+, p, ℝ̄_+, and ∼_g.


New R-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical seam laws: 0.

Canonical R-operator names introduced: 0.


Proved in 1.VII: the channel-natural domains and their intersection D; opposite-seam regular values; exact directional zero–pole traces; removable versus nonremovable seam classification by function; uniqueness grammar for continuous extension; failure of inversion through an extended zero; the structure and noncompactness of the split-boundary domain; exact four-factor split traces; the gluing quotient D̂/∼_g≅ℝ; the channelwise gluing criterion; the distinction between split preservation and smooth gluing; and the absence of any canonical raw seam-crossing map.


Certified handoff: 1.VIII separates display minimality, generator minimality, axiomatic minimality, and interpretive minimality, then proves the representation-invariance rules needed to prevent coordinate changes, redundant displays, or quotient reductions from being mistaken for new mathematical or physical degrees of freedom.


1.VIII — FOUR MINIMALITIES, REPRESENTATION DIMENSION, COORDINATE GAUGE, AND INVARIANT CONTENT


1.VIII.A — Four questions hidden inside the word minimal


The word minimal is dangerous unless the object being minimized is stated. A short formula, a one-parameter state space, a small axiom set, and a theory requiring few external assumptions are different achievements. None implies the others.


Definition 1.VIII.D1 — Display minimality


Display minimality concerns the number of named quantities or coordinate slots shown in a formula, theorem, diagram, table, or representation for a declared task.


A display is minimal only relative to that task. A representation that is minimal for numerical evaluation need not be minimal for exposing symmetry, branch structure, reciprocal pairing, or boundary roles.


Definition 1.VIII.D2 — Generator minimality


Generator minimality concerns the smallest number of nonconstant mathematical quantities from which a declared family can be reconstructed using a declared class of operations on a declared domain.


The generator count therefore depends on at least:


• the domain;

• the allowed operations;

• the coefficient field or constant data;

• whether local inverse functions are admitted;

• and which branch, chart, or lift records are supplied separately.


A generator count stated without these conditions is incomplete.


Definition 1.VIII.D3 — Axiomatic minimality


Axiomatic minimality concerns the number of independent propositions admitted without proof inside the current mathematical layer.


Inherited mathematics, definitions, notation choices, and proved consequences are not new Projection-specific axioms merely because they are used repeatedly.


Definition 1.VIII.D4 — Interpretive minimality


Interpretive minimality concerns the number of independent external meaning assignments, projection contracts, physics imports, dimensional scales, calibrations, or empirical assumptions required for a declared application.


No application has yet been instantiated in Book 1. Therefore Book 1 has zero external interpretive commitments, but it does not yet claim to be an interpretively minimal physical theory. There is no physical theory here to minimize.


Theorem 1.VIII.T1 — Non-equivalence of the four minimalities [D]


Display minimality, generator minimality, axiomatic minimality, and interpretive minimality are logically distinct.


Proof.


The four-factor family of 1.V is displayed by four positive scalar functions. Yet every member is a deterministic function of one phase coordinate x, and on each fixed quadrant every one of the four factors is itself a continuous injective local coordinate by Corollary 1.VI.C6. Thus display count is not intrinsic state dimension and need not equal a coordinate-generator count.


At the same time, the entire Book 1 construction through 1.VII has introduced zero R-specific mathematical axioms. Hence a nonconstant state family may have nonzero generator requirements while adding no new axiom.


Finally, exact internal mathematics through 1.VII has acquired no physical meaning because no projection contract or physics import has been supplied. Therefore axiomatic closure does not imply interpretive closure. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C1 — Shortest notation is not a theorem of minimality


A shorter expression does not, by itself, establish fewer intrinsic degrees of freedom, fewer generators, fewer axioms, greater global completeness, or greater physical adequacy.


Corollary 1.VIII.C2 — Redundant display can be mathematically useful


A representation may intentionally display more quantities than are intrinsically independent in order to make symmetry, reciprocal pairing, sign, boundary behavior, or reconstruction rules visible.


Redundancy of display is not automatically redundancy of purpose.


1.VIII.B — Abstract state and representation language


Definition 1.VIII.D5 — Abstract state set


Let 𝒮 denote a declared mathematical state set for a particular internal task.


The word state is used here in the mathematical sense of an element of the set being represented. It carries no quantum, dynamical, statistical, or physical meaning by itself.


Definition 1.VIII.D6 — Representation map


A representation of 𝒮 is a map


E:𝒮→Z,


where Z is a declared representation space or codomain.


The actual represented image is E(𝒮)⊆Z.


Definition 1.VIII.D7 — Complete representation for static reconstruction


A representation E is statically complete for 𝒮 when E is injective.


Then every represented value in E(𝒮) corresponds to exactly one state in 𝒮, and the inverse


E^{-1}:E(𝒮)→𝒮


is well defined as a set map.


Definition 1.VIII.D8 — Information-equivalent representations


Two representations


E:𝒮→Z,


E':𝒮→Z'


are information-equivalent when there exists a bijection


T:E(𝒮)→E'(𝒮)


such that


E'=T∘E.


The map T is then an invertible change of representation on the realized images.


Theorem 1.VIII.T2 — Reconstruction invariance under bijective reparameterization [D]


If E and E' are information-equivalent, then they retain exactly the same static reconstruction information about 𝒮.


Proof.


Because T is bijective,


E=T^{-1}∘E'.


Therefore E determines E' and E' determines E. Any distinction between two states that is visible in one representation is visible in the other, because equality of one represented value is equivalent to equality of the other. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C3 — Invertible relabeling creates no new mathematical distinction


An invertible coordinate change may alter numerical values, formula shape, apparent linearity, coefficient values, sparsity, or the number of ambient coordinate slots used by a chosen embedding. It does not create a state distinction absent from the original representation, nor erase one that was present.


Clarification 1.VIII.CL1 — Information equivalence is weaker than geometric equivalence


A set-theoretic bijection is sufficient for exact reconstruction equivalence. Preservation of continuity, smoothness, metric structure, orientation, symplectic structure, or another geometric invariant requires T and T^{-1} to preserve the corresponding structure.


Therefore “invertible” must not be used as a synonym for “isometric,” “smooth,” “canonical,” or “physically equivalent” without additional hypotheses.


1.VIII.C — Coordinate gauge


Definition 1.VIII.D9 — Coordinate-gauge equivalence


Within Book 1, two representations belong to the same coordinate-gauge class when they are related on their realized images by an invertible change of representation preserving every mathematical structure declared relevant to the task.


If only static reconstruction matters, bijectivity is enough.


If topology matters, the coordinate change must be a homeomorphism.


If smooth differential structure matters, it must be a diffeomorphism.


The word gauge in this definition means representational freedom only. It is not a Yang–Mills gauge symmetry, does not introduce a gauge field, and does not imply local physical redundancy.


Theorem 1.VIII.T3 — Coordinate-gauge invariance of intrinsic manifold dimension [Q]


Let E(𝒮) and E'(𝒮) be smooth manifolds related by a diffeomorphism T. Then they have the same intrinsic manifold dimension.


Proof.


A diffeomorphism provides mutually inverse smooth local coordinate maps between neighborhoods. Its derivative is an invertible linear map between tangent spaces at corresponding points. Invertible linear maps preserve vector-space dimension, so corresponding tangent spaces have equal dimension. Therefore the manifolds have equal intrinsic dimension. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C4 — Ambient component count is not intrinsic dimension


A one-dimensional state manifold may be embedded in ℝ², ℝ³, ℝ⁴, or a higher-dimensional display space without acquiring additional intrinsic degrees of freedom.


Conversely, reducing the number of displayed components by an invertible reparameterization does not reduce intrinsic dimension.


1.VIII.D — The four-factor curve as the first internal example


Definition 1.VIII.D10 — Four-factor representation on a quadrant [Q]


Fix one open quadrant Q_k and define


E_k:Q_k→ℝ⁴


by


E_k(x)=

(F_s^+(x),F_s^−(x),F_c^+(x),F_c^−(x)).


Theorem 1.VIII.T4 — Four displayed components, one intrinsic local dimension [Q]


The image E_k(Q_k) is a one-dimensional smooth curve. The four displayed components do not represent four independent continuous state coordinates.


Proof.


By Corollary 1.VI.C6, F_s^+ is continuous and strictly monotone on Q_k and is therefore a valid local scalar coordinate there. Hence x can be reconstructed continuously from F_s^+ on that quadrant. Once x is known, all four displayed factors are determined by their definitions.


Thus E_k is injective and its image is parameterized by one scalar. The half-angle formulas show smoothness and nonvanishing local derivative of the chosen scalar coordinate. Therefore the image is a one-dimensional smooth curve in ℝ⁴. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C5 — Constraint count is representation-dependent, state dimension is not


One representation may expose the one-dimensional family through one local scalar. Another may display four components together with reciprocal and cross-channel constraints. These presentations differ in visible constraint count and ambient dimension while representing the same one-dimensional local state family.


Clarification 1.VIII.CL2 — One source parameter is not automatically one algebraic generator


Theorem 1.VIII.T4 proves one intrinsic local dimension and one local coordinate. It does not yet prove that all four factors are rational functions of one selected factor under a specified algebraic operation class.


That stronger generator-minimality question belongs to the named operator calculus of Book 2. Book 1 does not smuggle it backward from historical T0 results.


1.VIII.E — Local scalar coordinates and global topology


The existence of one local coordinate on every quadrant does not imply the existence of one globally valid Euclidean scalar coordinate for every complete phase space.


Theorem 1.VIII.T5 — No continuous injective real scalar on a complete phase circle [C]


There is no continuous injective map


f:𝕋₂π→ℝ.


The same statement holds for 𝕋π.


Proof.


Each quotient circle is compact, connected, and homeomorphic to S¹. Suppose a continuous injective f into ℝ existed. A continuous injection from a compact space into the Hausdorff line is a homeomorphism onto its image. The image would be a compact connected subset of ℝ and therefore a closed interval [a,b].


Choose an interior point c∈(a,b) and let p be its unique preimage. Removing p from a circle leaves a connected space homeomorphic to an open interval, while removing c from [a,b] disconnects the interval into two nonempty pieces. A homeomorphism cannot change this connectedness property. Contradiction. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C6 — One intrinsic dimension does not guarantee one global Euclidean scalar chart


A complete circle has one continuous intrinsic dimension but requires either a cut, an omitted point, a periodic coordinate, multiple scalar charts, or a higher-dimensional constrained embedding if one demands global continuity and uniqueness.


Corollary 1.VIII.C7 — Local generator and global representation questions are distinct


A single scalar may be sufficient on every local chart while no single globally continuous injective real scalar exists on the complete space.


There is no contradiction because the domains and continuity requirements differ.


1.VIII.F — Lossy compression and task-relative sufficiency


Definition 1.VIII.D11 — Compression map


Let Z be a representation image and let


χ:Z→W


be any map. The map χ is a compression when the downstream representation uses χ(z) instead of the full z.


The compression is lossless on Z when χ is injective and lossy when χ is not injective.


Definition 1.VIII.D12 — Mathematical task map


A task map is a declared function


u:Z→Y


whose value is the mathematical output the representation is required to preserve for a particular task.


Theorem 1.VIII.T6 — Exact task-factorization criterion [D]


A compression χ:Z→W preserves the task u exactly if and only if u is constant on every fiber of χ.


Equivalently, exact preservation holds if and only if there exists a map


ū:χ(Z)→Y


such that


u=ū∘χ


on Z.


Proof.


If u=ū∘χ and χ(z₁)=χ(z₂), then


u(z₁)=ū(χ(z₁))=ū(χ(z₂))=u(z₂),


so u is constant on each fiber.


Conversely, suppose u is constant on every fiber. For w∈χ(Z), choose any z with χ(z)=w and define


ū(w)=u(z).


Fiber constancy makes this definition independent of the chosen z. Hence u=ū∘χ. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C8 — Lossy can be sufficient without being complete


A noninjective compression may preserve one task exactly while destroying information required by another task.


Therefore “lossy” does not mean “useless,” and “sufficient for this task” does not mean “complete representation of the original state.”


Corollary 1.VIII.C9 — Quotient sufficiency is task-relative


The folding


κ:𝕋₂π→𝕋π


is lossy relative to recovery of phase modulo 2π because it identifies [x]₂π and [x+π]₂π. Yet it is sufficient for every π-periodic task, including evaluation of the four-factor family proved in 1.V.


This is a direct example of Theorem 1.VIII.T6.


Negative Theorem 1.VIII.N1 — More outputs cannot undo a prior noninjective compression


Suppose χ identifies z₁≠z₂. Then no deterministic downstream function


G:W→Y


can produce different outputs for z₁ and z₂ from χ(z) alone.


Proof.


Both states supply the same downstream input χ(z₁)=χ(z₂). A deterministic function takes one value on one input. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C10 — Display expansion is not information recovery


A downstream map may turn one compressed scalar into ten, one hundred, or infinitely many displayed quantities. If those quantities are all deterministic functions of the same compressed value, distinctions already collapsed by the compression remain collapsed.


1.VIII.G — Invariant content


Definition 1.VIII.D13 — Reconstruction invariant


A reconstruction invariant of an information-equivalence class is any property of the represented state distinctions that is preserved under every admitted invertible change of representation.


Examples include injectivity or noninjectivity relative to a fixed state set, fiber cardinalities under a transported map, intrinsic smooth dimension under diffeomorphic coordinate change, and whether a declared state distinction is reconstructible.


By contrast, raw coordinate values, coefficient patterns, plotting axes, apparent linearity, and the number of ambient display components need not be invariant.


Theorem 1.VIII.T7 — Fiber structure under invertible output coordinate change [D]


Let E:𝒮→Z and let T:E(𝒮)→Z' be bijective. Define E'=T∘E. Then for every s₁,s₂∈𝒮,


E(s₁)=E(s₂)


if and only if


E'(s₁)=E'(s₂).


Hence E and E' induce exactly the same partition of 𝒮 into reconstruction fibers.


Proof.


If E(s₁)=E(s₂), applying T gives equality of E'. Conversely, equality of E' and injectivity of T imply equality of E. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C11 — Information loss is invariant under later invertible coordinates


Once a representation has identified two states, any later invertible reparameterization of that representation preserves the identification. An invertible change of output coordinates cannot restore the missing distinction.


1.VIII.H — Coordinate change and empirical novelty


Book 1 can prove an internal invariance theorem but cannot yet prove an empirical one, because no external readout or observation protocol has entered.


Theorem 1.VIII.T8 — Internal coordinate-change theorem [D]


Let E and E'=T∘E be information-equivalent internal representations with T invertible on E(𝒮). Then replacing E by E' changes only the internal coordinate description; it creates no new internal state distinction and removes none.


Proof.


This is Theorems 1.VIII.T2 and 1.VIII.T7. ∎


Clarification 1.VIII.CL3 — The empirical corollary is downstream


To conclude that two coordinate descriptions make the same external prediction, one must also transform the external readout consistently. That theorem requires the projection/readout layer and is not a premise of Book 1.


Historical T1 later proves exactly this stronger statement: a structural coordinate isomorphism leaves predictions unchanged when the readout is composed with the inverse coordinate transformation. Book 1 records that result only as provenance for the boundary, not as an imported physical theorem.


Negative Theorem 1.VIII.N2 — Coordinate novelty is not empirical novelty


A new plot shape, linearization, logarithmic form, half-angle form, Möbius form, higher-dimensional embedding, or lower-dimensional coordinate description does not by itself establish a new empirical prediction.


Proof.


Book 1 contains no empirical output map at all. Moreover, when the new form is invertibly related to the old internal representation, Theorem 1.VIII.T8 shows that no new internal state distinction has been created. Any empirical novelty claim therefore requires additional external structure and a non-equivalent prediction after that structure is declared. ∎


1.VIII.I — Axiomatic and interpretive audits


Theorem 1.VIII.T9 — Axiom-free does not mean assumption-free [R]


The absence of new Projection-specific mathematical axioms in Book 1 does not mean that Book 1 is derived from nothing.


Proof.


Every section uses inherited real arithmetic, trigonometry, analysis, topology, and the frozen source declarations established in Book 0. These inherited foundations are assumptions of the mathematical setting even though they are not new Projection-specific axioms. ∎


Theorem 1.VIII.T10 — Mathematical closure does not imply physical closure [E]


Exact identities, coordinate reconstructions, boundary theorems, quotient theorems, and representation invariants do not by themselves establish a physical ontology or empirical model.


Proof.


A physical application requires at minimum a declared connection from external instances to internal states and from internal structure to external observables, together with units or calibrations when dimensional quantities are claimed. No such external record has entered Book 1. Therefore physical closure does not follow from mathematical closure. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C12 — Zero interpretation is a boundary condition, not a physical accomplishment


Book 1’s zero physics imports and zero projection contracts certify that its mathematical claims are not being supported by hidden physical assumptions. They do not establish that the mathematics describes nature.


1.VIII.J — Minimality is always relative to a reconstruction task


Theorem 1.VIII.T11 — No universally minimal representation [D]


There need not exist one representation that is simultaneously minimal in display size, globally continuous, globally injective, branch-complete, seam-safe, algebraically simple, numerically well conditioned, and optimal for every task.


Proof.


These requirements can conflict. The circle obstruction of Theorem 1.VIII.T5 shows that one globally continuous injective real scalar is impossible for a complete phase circle even though local scalar charts exist. The quotient results of 1.II show that a smaller π-periodic representation loses the half-turn branch. The split-boundary results of 1.VII preserve directional information by duplicating boundary points, while gluing reduces that display by intentionally identifying them. Thus minimizing one representational burden can increase another or discard information relevant to another task. ∎


Corollary 1.VIII.C13 — Minimal representation package must name the task


Any later statement that a coordinate, generator, pair, quotient, or atlas is “minimal” must state what must be reconstructed and which properties—continuity, injectivity, branch resolution, seam resolution, history, or external readout—are required.


Constitutional Rule 1.VIII.R1 — Minimality qualifier


The reconstructed theory shall not use the unqualified phrase “the minimal representation” when different tasks admit different minimal packages.


Constitutional Rule 1.VIII.R2 — Coordinate-gauge firewall


Invertibly related internal coordinates are to be treated as alternative representations of the same internal mathematical distinctions unless an additional structure makes the coordinate choice itself meaningful.


Constitutional Rule 1.VIII.R3 — Compression ledger


Every noninjective quotient, normalization, projection, or compression must state which distinctions its fibers identify and which declared tasks still factor through the compressed representation.


1.VIII.K — Non-scope


Nothing in coordinate-gauge equivalence establishes gauge theory.


Nothing in one-dimensional intrinsic state structure establishes one-dimensional physical space, one physical field, one quantum degree of freedom, or one dynamical variable.


Nothing in a four-component display establishes four spacetime dimensions or four independent physical channels.


Nothing in a constrained two-component display establishes a qubit.


Nothing in local one-generator reconstruction establishes a globally single-chart topology.


Nothing in invertible coordinate equivalence establishes empirical novelty.


These are precisely the category errors the four-minimality audit is designed to prevent.


Book 1, Section 1.VIII — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: sets and maps; injectivity and bijectivity; quotient fibers; continuity, homeomorphism, and diffeomorphism; tangent-space dimension; compactness and connectedness of the circle; and the Book 1 domain, quotient, local-coordinate, and split-boundary results already proved.


Source provenance: T0.VI supplies the canonical distinction among display, generator, axiomatic, and interpretive minimality and the warning that one local generator does not imply one global seam-safe scalar. T0.IV supplies the historical scalar-obstruction example. T1.I later supplies the stronger external theorem that bijective structural coordinate changes preserve predictions when the readout is transformed consistently. Book 1 re-derives only the internal mathematical invariance and task-factorization results; no T1 projection contract, empirical readout, calibration, or physical conclusion is imported.


New Book 1 symbols: 𝒮 for an abstract state set; E and E' for representation maps; Z and Z' for representation spaces or images; T for an invertible representation change; χ for a compression map; u for a mathematical task map; and ū for its factored downstream form.


New R-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Empirical prediction theorems imported from T1: 0.


Proved in 1.VIII: non-equivalence of the four minimalities; exact reconstruction invariance under bijective reparameterization; coordinate-gauge preservation of intrinsic smooth dimension; one-dimensionality of the four-factor quadrant image despite four displayed components; the complete-circle obstruction to one global continuous injective real scalar; the exact task-factorization criterion for lossy compression; invariance of reconstruction fibers under invertible coordinate changes; the distinction between internal coordinate novelty and empirical novelty; the assumptions hidden beneath the phrase axiom-free; and the theorem that no representation is universally minimal across incompatible reconstruction tasks.


Certified handoff: 1.IX freezes the complete Book 1 dependency graph, certifies the no-smuggling and no-domain-widening firewall, records every introduced symbol and status, and defines the exact airlock through which Book 2 may finally assign the canonical R-operator names to the already-proved reciprocal-conjugate factors.


1.IX — DEPENDENCY GRAPH, NO-SMUGGLING RULE, AND BOOK 1 CERTIFICATION


1.IX.A — Purpose of the terminal audit


Book 1 has not attempted to construct the full R calculus. Its task has been narrower and prior: to determine exactly what mathematical structure is available before the canonical operator names, derived operator layers, physical interpretations, projection contracts, or empirical calibrations enter.


The terminal section therefore does not add a new mathematical object. It audits the dependency chain already proved, freezes the domain and status rules that later books must inherit, and states the exact condition under which Book 2 may begin naming and extending the reciprocal-conjugate family.


The audit has four questions:


1. Does every theorem depend only on earlier admitted mathematics?

2. Has any local, quotient, boundary, or split-boundary statement been silently promoted to a broader domain?

3. Has any physical meaning, dimensional scale, empirical claim, or later-source result been used to support the Book 1 mathematics?

4. Is the handoff to Book 2 definitional rather than axiomatic?


The answers will be proved rather than assumed.


1.IX.B — The Book 1 dependency chain


Theorem 1.IX.T1 — Book 1 dependency-order theorem [R]


The logical dependency chain of Book 1 is:


inherited real arithmetic, analysis, topology, and ordinary trigonometry


→ lifted phase and quotient spaces


→ analytic seam set and common reciprocal-trigonometric domain


→ quadrant and octant atlas


→ branch-sign records and absolute-value envelopes


→ positive reciprocal-conjugate factors


→ half-angle charts, ranges, and chart transport


→ one-sided boundary traces and channel-natural domains


→ split-boundary and gluing grammar


→ representation, compression, and minimality theorems


→ terminal status and dependency audit.


Every arrow is dependency-bearing. No later stage is required to prove an earlier stage.


Proof.


Section 1.I establishes the status grammar and domain discipline before any substantive construction.


Section 1.II uses only the inherited real line, ordinary trigonometric periodicity, quotient sets, and normalized phase transformations.


Section 1.III uses 1.II together with the natural domains and zero sets of the ordinary reciprocal trigonometric functions to define Σ, D, Q_k, and O_j.


Section 1.IV uses 1.III and inherited sign/absolute-value mathematics to define α, β, and ε and to derive branch-explicit forms.


Section 1.V uses 1.IV together with the inherited reciprocal Pythagorean identities to prove positivity, reciprocal conjugacy, symmetry transport, and π-periodicity of the unnamed factors.


Section 1.VI uses 1.V plus standard half-angle identities to derive lawful local charts, ranges, monotonicity, midpoint values, and octant comparison ordering.


Section 1.VII uses 1.VI plus inherited limit, continuity, topology, and quotient constructions to classify boundaries and define split and glued domains.


Section 1.VIII uses the already-proved quotient, local-coordinate, boundary, and representation results together with standard topology and smooth-manifold facts to distinguish the four minimalities and prove coordinate/compression invariance results.


No proof in this chain cites a later physical interpretation, calibration, or Book 2 definition. ∎


Corollary 1.IX.C1 — No circular support


No theorem of Book 1 is supported by a result whose validity depends on that theorem.


In particular, no later operator name, derived operator identity, carrier construction, physical interpretation, or empirical application is needed to establish the pre-operator reciprocal mathematics.


1.IX.C — Domain hierarchy and the anti-promotion firewall


The Book 1 domain tags form a discipline, not a decorative annotation.


Theorem 1.IX.T2 — Domain inheritance theorem [R]


A result proved on a domain X may be restricted to a smaller subset Y⊆X without a new proof, but it may not be promoted to a larger domain Z⊃X without an independent extension or reproof.


Proof.


Every hypothesis valid on X remains valid on Y⊆X. The converse fails in general: points of Z\X may violate a denominator condition, sign declaration, continuity condition, quotient compatibility, branch assumption, or boundary rule used in the proof on X. Therefore restriction is automatic while enlargement is not. ∎


Corollary 1.IX.C2 — Book 1 domain nonpromotion rules


The following promotions are forbidden unless separately proved:


[O] to [Q];


[Q] to [D];


[D] to [R];


[B] to a two-sided or interior theorem;


[S] to an unsplit or glued domain;


and any internal domain tag to [E].


Corollary 1.IX.C3 — Absolute-value chart rule


A formula obtained by replacing |csc x| or |sec x| with a signless reciprocal on a fixed chart remains a [Q] statement unless the missing branch signs are restored or a global proof is supplied.


Corollary 1.IX.C4 — Boundary cancellation rule


An identity proved on D after cancellation of a nonzero denominator does not thereby define the uncancelled expression at Σ. A seam value must arise from an independent extension theorem for the specific derived quantity.


Corollary 1.IX.C5 — Split/glued nonpromotion


A theorem on D̂ that distinguishes b̂_k^- from b̂_k^+ may not be quoted after gluing as though the distinction survived. Conversely, a theorem on the glued line cannot be used to infer equality of split directional traces unless the trace compatibility has been proved.


1.IX.D — The no-smuggling theorem


The historical constitution prohibited “trigonometric smuggling.” The reconstructed Book 1 can state the prohibition more precisely because the failure modes are now explicit.


Definition 1.IX.D1 — Smuggling


Smuggling is the use of a conclusion at a stronger logical, domain, interpretive, dimensional, or informational status than has been proved, without declaring the additional hypothesis or construction that performs the promotion.


Theorem 1.IX.T3 — No-smuggling theorem [R]


The following eight promotions are invalid unless their missing bridge is explicitly supplied:


1. Domain smuggling: using a [Q], [O], [B], or [S] result as though it were valid on a broader domain.


2. Branch smuggling: deleting α, β, ε, a quadrant index, a split-boundary side, or another branch record and then using a formula as though the discarded distinction remained recoverable.


3. Quotient smuggling: identifying states under a quotient and later claiming recovery of a distinction that the quotient fibers erased.


4. Boundary smuggling: extending a seam-safe composite and then assigning the extension value to singular constituents from which it was derived.


5. Coordinate smuggling: treating an invertible reparameterization or higher-dimensional display as a new intrinsic degree of freedom or new internal state distinction.


6. Dimensional smuggling: assigning physical units or a dimensional constant to a dimensionless internal coordinate without an external scale or readout declaration.


7. Interpretive smuggling: treating mathematical positivity, normalization, reciprocity, symmetry, monotonicity, orientation, or topology as a physical probability, energy, time flow, charge, spin, spacetime dimension, field, or dynamics without a projection contract or physics import.


8. Source smuggling: using a result from a later book, historical T4+, an S essay, loose paper, calibration exercise, or exploratory conversation as a premise of Book 1 without independently re-admitting it under the revised source rule.


Proof.


Cases 1–4 contradict the exact domain, quotient, branch, and extension theorems proved in 1.II–1.VII.


Case 5 contradicts the representation and fiber-invariance theorems of 1.VIII.


Case 6 adds dimensional structure absent from the internal dimensionless mathematics.


Case 7 adds an external meaning assignment absent from Book 1’s declarations.


Case 8 violates the dependency-first and source-boundary rules fixed in 1.I and Book 0.


Each case therefore changes the hypothesis set while concealing the change. ∎


Corollary 1.IX.C6 — Familiar form is not promotion evidence


A formula does not acquire external status because it resembles a known physical law, standard distribution, rapidity relation, conservation equation, wave equation, or field-theory expression.


Resemblance may motivate a later test. It does not supply the missing bridge.


Corollary 1.IX.C7 — Repeated use does not change status


A convention, ansatz, interpretation, quotient choice, or projection contract does not become a theorem by repetition. Status changes only when a proof establishes the stronger claim under a declared candidate class and hypotheses.


1.IX.E — Exact source boundary of Book 1


Theorem 1.IX.T4 — Source-provenance theorem [R]


The mathematical content of Book 1 is supported by:


• the revised inherited framework certified in Book 0;


• ordinary real arithmetic, trigonometry, analysis, topology, and the limited smooth-manifold facts explicitly invoked;


• and claim-level re-derivation of the pre-operator mathematical content historically present in T0–T3.


No post-T3 physics import, later T-series result, S-series result, loose-paper conjecture, empirical calibration, spreadsheet pattern, or conversational exploration is required as a premise of Book 1.


Proof.


The source provenance of each section has been recorded in its closure ledger. Sections 1.I–1.VII draw their historical mathematical questions primarily from T0 but re-establish the relevant statements in the revised dependency order. Section 1.VIII uses T0’s minimality distinctions and standard mathematical representation results, while explicitly deferring T1’s empirical-coordinate invariance theorem. No theorem in Book 1 requires later physical or empirical material. ∎


Corollary 1.IX.C8 — Historical agreement is not dependency


A later historical source may agree with a Book 1 theorem or contain an earlier version of it. That agreement does not make the later source a premise of the reconstructed proof.


Corollary 1.IX.C9 — Historical disagreement is auditable


If a later historical document uses a different sign convention, domain, notation, seam rule, or physical interpretation, the disagreement must be handled downstream as a source-reconciliation question. It does not silently alter the certified Book 1 mathematics.


1.IX.F — Certification of the symbol and collision policy


Theorem 1.IX.T5 — Book 1 symbol-certification theorem [R]


Every symbol materially used in Book 1 is either:


1. inherited standard mathematical notation;

2. defined in the Book 1 Legend of Symbols and formally introduced in the body before theorem-level use;

3. or explicitly marked as reserved for a later book.


The collision policy fixed in 1.I remains in force.


In particular:


• H remains reserved for the later harmonic carrier and has not been used for a phase translation;


• τ_a denotes phase translation;


• ι and ι_d denote the two scalar reflections;


• Σ, Σ_s, and Σ_c denote seam sets;


• O_j denotes geometric octants while [O] is a domain tag;


• ρ and δ remain reserved for later roles rather than being reused casually;


• and the unnamed reciprocal-conjugate factors retain the neutral Book 1 notation F_s^± and F_c^± until Book 2.


Proof.


This follows from the Legend of Symbols, the collision rules of 1.I, and the definitions introduced section by section. ∎


Corollary 1.IX.C10 — Historical aliases are not parallel current notation


Retired historical symbols may appear in provenance notes, but they do not create alternate current definitions. The reconstructed manuscript has one active notation at each dependency layer.


1.IX.G — What Book 1 has established


Theorem 1.IX.T6 — Book 1 mathematical-constitution theorem [R]


Relative to the inherited mathematical background and the revised source boundary, Book 1 establishes all of the following without a Projection-specific mathematical axiom:


1. a lifted real phase coordinate with distinct 2π and π quotient spaces;

Proof.


Items 1–18 summarize the proved results of Sections 1.II–1.IX. Each item is supported by the theorem and closure ledgers already established. ∎


Corollary 1.IX.C11 — Book 1 is mathematically substantive but physically uncommitted


The book establishes an exact reciprocal-trigonometric representation theory, but it does not establish what, if anything, that mathematics represents in nature.

Book 1 may close mathematically while the physical-debt ledger remains almost entirely open.
