1.IX.J — Exact airlock into Book 2


The first Projection-specific act of Book 2 is permitted to be definitional because the underlying mathematical factors already exist and their core properties have already been proved.


Theorem 1.IX.T9 — Pre-naming sufficiency theorem [D]


Before Book 2 begins, the four neutral functions F_s^+, F_s^−, F_c^+, and F_c^− already possess, as theorems rather than axioms:


• explicit formulas;

• domain declarations;

• strict positivity;

• exact reciprocal products;

• exact reciprocal inversion;

• branch-explicit forms;

• reflection and complementary exchange laws;

• quarter-turn transport;

• π-periodicity;

• half-angle chart formulas;

• range and monotonicity theorems;

• boundary traces;

• and exact representation limits.


Therefore assigning canonical names to these four existing functions adds notation but adds no new mathematical proposition.


Proof.


Each listed property was proved in Sections 1.IV–1.VII before the naming step. A one-to-one assignment of names to already-defined functions changes the vocabulary used to reference them, not their formulas or truth conditions. ∎


Corollary 1.IX.C14 — Reciprocal pairing may not be reintroduced as an axiom


Once Book 2 names the four functions, their reciprocal relations remain inherited theorems from Book 1. They are not independent declarations to be assumed again.


Corollary 1.IX.C15 — Absolute values survive the airlock


The canonical Book 2 definitions must inherit the global absolute-valued formulas proved here. Any signless form remains chart-local unless its branch conditions are explicitly carried.


Corollary 1.IX.C16 — The π quotient survives the airlock as a proved representation fact


Because the four factors are already proved π-periodic, Book 2 may use the half-period quotient as their natural static quotient representation. It may not infer from that fact alone that physical states separated by π are identical.


Corollary 1.IX.C17 — The sign convention of later derived differences is not fixed by the present reciprocal pairs


Book 1 has proved the four primitive reciprocal factors and their transformations. It has not yet defined later differences, sums, saw coordinates, FlatWave, carrier quantities, or derived operator signs. Those definitions must enter Book 2 in dependency order and be audited independently.


This prevents historical sign errors in derived layers from being smuggled backward into the primitive mathematics.


1.IX.K — Book 1 closure theorem


Theorem 1.IX.T10 — Foundational closure of Book 1 [R]


Book 1 is closed.


Its mathematical constitution is sufficient for the named reciprocal-operator calculus to begin, and no unresolved Book 1 question prevents the naming step.


The closure is limited to the mathematical tasks actually undertaken. It does not close the later questions of global named-operator generation, derived operator sign conventions, bounded transfer, seam-safe carrier construction, physical interpretation, spacetime, quantum mechanics, field theory, calibration, or empirical adequacy.


Proof.


The dependency graph is acyclic by Theorem 1.IX.T1. Domain promotion is controlled by Theorem 1.IX.T2. The no-smuggling theorem closes the principal category errors. Source provenance is certified by Theorem 1.IX.T4. Symbol usage is certified by Theorem 1.IX.T5. The positive mathematical content is collected in Theorem 1.IX.T6, while the physical nonclosure is explicit in Negative Theorem 1.IX.N1. The axiom and import counts are certified by Theorems 1.IX.T7–T8. Finally, Theorem 1.IX.T9 proves that the next act may be purely definitional. Therefore the Book 1 task is complete. ∎


Book 1 — Final certification ledger


Dependency ledger. Closed and acyclic through 1.IX.


Definition ledger. Every active Book 1 symbol is defined in the Legend of Symbols and in the body before theorem-level use, or explicitly marked reserved.


Domain ledger. [R], [D], [Q], [O], [B], [S], and [E] remain noninterchangeable. No narrower result is silently widened.


Branch ledger. α, β, ε, quadrant indices, quotient fibers, and split-boundary side labels are retained whenever required by the reconstruction task.


Absolute-value ledger. Global reciprocal formulas retain |csc x| and |sec x|. Signless simplifications remain chart-qualified.


Quotient ledger. 𝕋₂π and 𝕋π are mathematically distinct. The π quotient discards the half-turn branch but retains the product-sign distinction needed by the four-factor family.


Boundary ledger. Common-domain seams, channel-regular seams, channel-singular seams, chart boundaries, split copies, and glued points remain distinct categories.


Representation ledger. Display dimension, generator count, intrinsic dimension, global chart count, and task-relative sufficiency are not conflated.


Source ledger. Revised Book 1 depends on the admitted Book 0 framework, standard background mathematics, and claim-level pre-operator re-derivation. No post-T3 source is a premise.


Axiom ledger. Zero new Projection-specific mathematical axioms.


Ansatz ledger. Zero application ansätze.


Projection-contract ledger. None instantiated.


Physics-import ledger. None.


Calibration ledger. None.


Empirical-prediction ledger. None.


Negative-result ledger. The book cannot recover information discarded by quotient or compression; cannot globalize chart formulas without branch transport; cannot regularize singular constituents through a smooth composite; cannot derive a physical seam law; cannot infer intrinsic dimension from displayed component count; cannot obtain one global continuous injective real coordinate on a complete circle; and cannot cross from mathematical structure into physics by resemblance.


Promotion ledger. Positivity, reciprocal pairing, π-periodicity, chart transport, boundary classification, split/gluing grammar, and representation minimality are theorem-level results available before canonical operator naming.


Book 1 status: CLOSED.


Transition to Book 2 — Canonical R-Operator Calculus


Book 2 begins at the exact point where Book 1 stops. It may now assign the canonical R names to the four proved reciprocal-conjugate factors. The naming act will be recorded as a definition, not an axiom. Immediately afterward, Book 2 must restate the inherited global formulas, domains, reciprocal pairing, and absolute-value requirements before constructing any derived differences, partitions, transition functions, or carriers.


The first Book 2 audit question is therefore not whether the four factors exist. That is closed. The question is what follows once those four existing factors are given their canonical Projection names and allowed to generate the named operator calculus.


End of Book 1


BOOK 2 — CANONICAL R-OPERATOR CALCULUS


Purpose


Book 1 established the reciprocal-trigonometric mathematical constitution before any canonical R-operator name was allowed to carry mathematical weight. Book 2 begins at that airlock. Its first act is definitional: it assigns the canonical names srx, sxp, cxp, and crx to four positive reciprocal-conjugate functions whose absolute-value structure, branch behavior, reciprocal products, chart transport, and boundary grammar have already been proved.


The book is organized around three theorem-level climaxes.


First climax — Double-angle closure. The half-angle primitive coordinates are recombined through the UNA differences until the apparently asymmetric reciprocal system collapses to the exact double-angle identity


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x.


Second climax — FlatWave. The reciprocal UNA partition then collapses further to a binary mathematical character,


FlatWave=sgn(sin 2x)∈{−1,+1}.


Third climax — Möbius reduction. The FlatWave sign becomes the discrete branch datum in the same-phase cross-family relation, yielding a Möbius involution between the sine and cosine primary coordinates and, ultimately, an exact one-generator theorem.


The narrative order is therefore deliberate:


absolute reciprocal forms

→ reciprocal-conjugate primitive quartet

→ half-angle atlas

→ UNA differences

→ double-angle closure

→ FlatWave binary character

→ Möbius reduction

→ one-generator calculus.


No physical interpretation is introduced by this order. The words operator, wave, expon, inverx, carrier, branch, and character remain mathematical labels unless a later book supplies a projection contract or physics import.


BOOK 2 LEGEND OF SYMBOLS


ℝ, ℤ, x, π — inherited real phase notation. x is a dimensionless radian coordinate and is not identified with physical time.


Σ_s={kπ:k∈ℤ} — sine-zero seam set.


Σ_c={π/2+kπ:k∈ℤ} — cosine-zero seam set.


Σ=Σ_s∪Σ_c — common reciprocal-trigonometric seam set.


D_s=ℝ\Σ_s — maximal ordinary real domain of the sine reciprocal-conjugate pair.


D_c=ℝ\Σ_c — maximal ordinary real domain of the cosine reciprocal-conjugate pair.


D=D_s∩D_c=ℝ\Σ — common domain on which all four primitive operators coexist.


Q_k=(kπ/2,(k+1)π/2) — inherited common-domain quadrants.


α=sgn(sin x), β=sgn(cos x), ε=αβ=sgn(sin 2x) — inherited branch signs on the domains where the relevant quantities are nonzero.


τ_a(x)=x+a — inherited phase translation.


ι(x)=−x — inherited phase reflection.


ι_d(x)=π/2−x — inherited complementary reflection.


𝕋_π=ℝ/(πℤ) — inherited half-period quotient. It is a mathematical quotient, not a declaration of physical state identity.


srx — canonical sine positive-tangential primitive, introduced in 2.I:


srx(x)=|csc x|+cot x.


sxp — canonical sine negative-tangential reciprocal-conjugate primitive, introduced in 2.I:


sxp(x)=|csc x|−cot x.


cxp — canonical cosine positive-tangential primitive, introduced in 2.I:


cxp(x)=|sec x|+tan x.


crx — canonical cosine negative-tangential reciprocal-conjugate primitive, introduced in 2.I:


crx(x)=|sec x|−tan x.


𝒫 — canonical primitive quartet, introduced after the four names are active:


𝒫={srx,sxp,cxp,crx}.


urx — canonical UNA difference activated in 2.IV: urx=srx−crx. Type: derived real function on D; not a primitive generator.


uxp — canonical UNA difference activated in 2.IV: uxp=cxp−sxp. Type: derived real function on D; not a primitive generator. The historical reversed expression sxp−cxp equals −uxp and is retired, not an alternate current convention.


FlatWave — reserved derived quantity for the binary-character section. Its capitalization is canonical in the revised manuscript. The name does not imply a physical wave.


H_D — reciprocal UNA-sum kernel on D, activated in 2.VIII: H_D=1/(urx+uxp)=sin 2x/4. Type: derived real function on D. The subscript records the pre-extension domain.


H — unique smooth extension of H_D to ℝ, activated in 2.VIII: H(x)=sin 2x/4. Type: derived real harmonic coordinate. H is not a phase-translation symbol and has no physical carrier interpretation by name alone.


V — derivative harmonic coordinate activated in 2.VIII: V=H'=cos 2x/2 on ℝ. Type: derived real function.


U=4H, W=2V — normalized harmonic coordinates activated in 2.VIII. They satisfy U=sin 2x, W=cos 2x and U²+W²=1.


C_car — carrier ellipse {(h,v)∈ℝ² : v²+4h²=1/4}. Type: one-dimensional smooth closed curve, linearly equivalent to the unit circle. Status: mathematical carrier representation only.


F — carrier map activated in 2.VIII: F(x)=(H(x),V(x)) from ℝ to C_car. Type: smooth π-periodic map with exact fibers modulo π.


[R], [D], [Q], [O], [B], [S], [E] — inherited domain/status tags. The nonpromotion rules of Book 1 remain binding.


The Legend is an index, not a substitute for definition. A reserved symbol becomes active only when formally introduced in the body.


θ_s — reduced sine-channel phase: the unique representative of [x]_π in (0,π) for x∈D_s. Type: local/global quotient coordinate on the punctured half-period circle. Status: Book 2 chart notation; dimensionless.

θ_c — reduced cosine-channel phase: the unique representative of [x]_π in (−π/2,π/2) for x∈D_c. Type: quotient chart coordinate. Status: Book 2 chart notation; dimensionless.

ξ_k=x−kπ/2 for x∈Q_k — reduced common-domain quadrant coordinate in (0,π/2). Type: local chart coordinate inherited from Book 1 and activated in Book 2.

z=srx — primary positive half-angle coordinate used for rational recovery and later one-generator reduction. Type: positive real scalar. Natural range on D_s: (0,+∞); on common D, z≠1.

w=cxp — cosine primary positive coordinate used later in the same-phase Möbius relation. Type: positive real scalar. Natural range on D_c: (0,+∞); on common D, w≠1.

m_k=(2k+1)π/4 — midpoint of Q_k and common boundary of its two octants. Type: regular point of D. Status: inherited Book 1 notation used in the half-angle atlas.

V₄ — Klein four group describing the induced permutation symmetry of the primitive quartet after half-turn invariance is taken into account. Type: finite group with four elements, each nonidentity element of order two. Status: activated in 2.III as a mathematical symmetry of primitive labels; not a physical gauge or internal-symmetry group.

M_ε — branch Möbius map activated in 2.VII: M_ε(z)=(z+ε)/(εz−1), with ε∈{−1,+1}. Type: real fractional-linear involution on the admissible interval selected by ε. Status: same-phase cross-family coordinate map; not a physical transformation by itself.

I_+=(1,+∞), I_-=(0,1) — admissible positive generator intervals for the ε=+1 and ε=−1 Möbius branches respectively. Type: open subsets of ℝ_{>0}. Status: Book 2 range notation.

saw_r — canonical reciprocal UNA response activated in 2.VI: saw_r=1/urx. Type: derived real function on D. Historical numbered Saw aliases are noncanonical unless explicitly mapped to this formula.

saw_x — canonical reciprocal UNA response activated in 2.VI: saw_x=1/uxp. Type: derived real function on D. Historical numbered Saw aliases are noncanonical unless explicitly mapped to this formula.

FlatWave — canonical derived binary character activated in 2.VI: FlatWave=saw_r+saw_x=(srx+cxp)/(srx·cxp−1)=sgn(sin 2x) on D. Type: derived {−1,+1}-valued function; undefined at Σ in the present domain. The historical spelling Flatwave is a source alias only.

χ_F — nontrivial one-dimensional real sign character of the induced primitive V₄ symmetry carried by FlatWave. Values: +1 on identity and complementary reflection; −1 on phase reflection and quarter-turn. Type: group homomorphism V₄→{−1,+1}. Status: mathematical symmetry character, not a physical charge or chirality.

arctan — principal inverse tangent with values in (−π/2,π/2); applied in Book 2 primarily to positive primitive values, hence taking values in (0,π/2). Status: standard calculus function.

ln — natural logarithm. In Book 2 it is applied only to strictly positive arguments such as 1+srx². Status: standard calculus function.

f~g as h→0^+ — asymptotic equivalence: f/g→1 in the stated one-sided limit. Status: standard asymptotic notation.

Prime notation f' — derivative df/dx with respect to the inherited dimensionless phase coordinate x. It carries no physical-time meaning by itself.

i — imaginary unit, i²=−1. Status: standard complex-number notation; no physical interpretation by itself.

Ψ_U — optional UNA complex package activated in 2.XI: Ψ_U=urx+i·uxp on D. Type: complex-valued derived representation of the real UNA pair; seam-singular and information-equivalent to (urx,uxp).

Z_car — canonical optional complex carrier package activated in 2.XI: Z_car=2V+i·4H=cos 2x+i sin 2x=e^{2ix}. Type: unit-modulus complex representation of the smooth carrier; defined on ℝ; exact fibers modulo π.

conj(z) — complex conjugation. Re z and Im z — real and imaginary parts. |z| — complex modulus when the argument is complex. Status: standard complex analysis notation.

2.I — THE CANONICAL PRIMITIVE QUARTET: POSITIVE AND NEGATIVE TANGENTIAL FORMS


2.I.A — The first Projection-specific naming act


Book 1 deliberately withheld the names srx, sxp, cxp, and crx until the underlying reciprocal-conjugate mathematics had been established without them. We may now name the four functions without changing their content.


The sine channel and cosine channel each possess the same architecture: an absolute reciprocal envelope combined once with the positive tangential term and once with the negative tangential term. The two forms are not independent constructions. The negative-tangential member is exactly the reciprocal of the positive-tangential member, and conversely.


This equivalence is the structural fact that should be visible at the moment the quartet is introduced.


Definition 2.I.D1 — Sine reciprocal-conjugate pair


On D_s, define


srx(x)=|csc x|+cot x,


sxp(x)=|csc x|−cot x.


Definition 2.I.D2 — Cosine reciprocal-conjugate pair


On D_c, define


cxp(x)=|sec x|+tan x,


crx(x)=|sec x|−tan x.


Definition 2.I.D3 — Common primitive quartet [D]


On the common domain D, define the ordered primitive quartet


𝒫(x)=(srx(x),sxp(x),cxp(x),crx(x)).


The word primitive means that these operators are named before the later UNA, Saw, FlatWave, harmonic, and generator-reduction layers. It does not mean that the four displayed functions are algebraically independent.


Clarification 2.I.CL1 — Maximal natural domains


Historical presentations often introduced all four functions on D because the quartet was being treated simultaneously. Book 1 proved a sharper domain statement.


The sine pair requires only sin x≠0, so its maximal ordinary real domain is D_s. At cosine-zero points, cot x=0 and |csc x|=1, hence


srx=sxp=1.


The cosine pair requires only cos x≠0, so its maximal ordinary real domain is D_c. At sine-zero points, tan x=0 and |sec x|=1, hence


cxp=crx=1.


Whenever an identity mixes sine and cosine channels, the domain restricts to their intersection D unless a larger domain is proved for that specific composite.


2.I.B — Reciprocal form is the negative-tangential conjugate


Theorem 2.I.T1 — Sine reciprocal–negative-tangential equivalence [D_s]


For every x∈D_s,


1/(|csc x|+cot x)=|csc x|−cot x,


and


1/(|csc x|−cot x)=|csc x|+cot x.


Equivalently,


sxp=1/srx,


srx=1/sxp.


Proof.


The reciprocal Pythagorean identity gives


csc²x−cot²x=1.


Because |csc x|²=csc²x,


(|csc x|+cot x)(|csc x|−cot x)

=|csc x|²−cot²x

=csc²x−cot²x

=1.


Both factors are nonzero. Therefore each is the exact reciprocal of the other. More explicitly, rationalizing the positive-tangential form gives


1/(|csc x|+cot x)

=(|csc x|−cot x)/(|csc x|²−cot²x)

=|csc x|−cot x.


The converse follows identically. ∎


Theorem 2.I.T2 — Cosine reciprocal–negative-tangential equivalence [D_c]


For every x∈D_c,


1/(|sec x|+tan x)=|sec x|−tan x,


and


1/(|sec x|−tan x)=|sec x|+tan x.


Equivalently,


crx=1/cxp,


cxp=1/crx.


Proof.


Use


sec²x−tan²x=1


and |sec x|²=sec²x. Then


(|sec x|+tan x)(|sec x|−tan x)=1,


so rationalization yields


1/(|sec x|+tan x)

=|sec x|−tan x.


The converse is identical. ∎


Corollary 2.I.C1 — Reciprocal-conjugate tautology of form


Within each channel, the following two descriptions denote the same second member:


negative-tangential conjugate


and


reciprocal of the positive-tangential member.


Thus


sxp=|csc x|−cot x=1/(|csc x|+cot x)=1/srx,


crx=|sec x|−tan x=1/(|sec x|+tan x)=1/cxp.


The word tautology here is used in the algebraic sense of exact equivalence after the inherited reciprocal Pythagorean identity is applied. It is not an additional axiom and not a mere naming convention.


Corollary 2.I.C2 — Positive and negative tangential forms are conjugate presentations of one positive coordinate [D_s,D_c]


Once srx is known, sxp contributes no independent continuous value; it is fixed by inversion. Once cxp is known, crx is fixed by inversion.


Therefore the quartet begins as two positive reciprocal coordinates displayed in conjugate form, not as four freely independent primitives.


2.I.C — Positivity


Theorem 2.I.T3 — Primitive positivity on maximal natural domains


For every x∈D_s,


srx(x)>0,


sxp(x)>0.


For every x∈D_c,


cxp(x)>0,


crx(x)>0.


Proof.


Since


csc²x−cot²x=1,


we have


|csc x|>|cot x|


on D_s. Hence both |csc x|+cot x and |csc x|−cot x are strictly positive.


Likewise,


|sec x|>|tan x|


on D_c, so both cosine-channel factors are strictly positive. ∎


Corollary 2.I.C3 — The primitive codomain is positive


The sine pair maps D_s into ℝ_{>0}² and the cosine pair maps D_c into ℝ_{>0}². On D, the quartet maps into ℝ_{>0}⁴.


No primitive sign change occurs. Later signs appearing in UNA differences, FlatWave, or other derived quantities must arise from relations among positive primitives rather than from a primitive crossing through zero.


2.I.D — Pairwise reconstruction of ordinary trigonometric data


Theorem 2.I.T4 — Conjugate sum and difference recovery


On D_s,


srx+sxp=2|csc x|,


srx−sxp=2cot x.


On D_c,


cxp+crx=2|sec x|,


cxp−crx=2tan x.


Proof.


Add and subtract each reciprocal-conjugate defining pair. ∎


Corollary 2.I.C4 — Envelope and tangential recovery


On D_s,


|csc x|=(srx+sxp)/2,


cot x=(srx−sxp)/2.


On D_c,


|sec x|=(cxp+crx)/2,


tan x=(cxp−crx)/2.


Thus the named primitive pairs reorganize ordinary trigonometric data; they do not erase it.


Corollary 2.I.C5 — Reciprocal form and sign of the tangential coordinate


Because each pair is positive and has product one,


srx>1 ⇔ cot x>0,


srx<1 ⇔ cot x<0,


cxp>1 ⇔ tan x>0,


cxp<1 ⇔ tan x<0,


where equality with 1 occurs only at the opposite-channel regular seams admitted by the maximal natural domain but excluded from D.


On the common domain D, the value 1 is therefore not attained by any primitive whose associated tangential coordinate is nonzero.


2.I.E — Absolute-value preservation


Theorem 2.I.T5 — Canonical global form versus chart form


The formulas


srx=|csc x|+cot x,


sxp=|csc x|−cot x,


cxp=|sec x|+tan x,


crx=|sec x|−tan x


are the canonical maximal-domain definitions.


On D, with


α=sgn(sin x),


β=sgn(cos x),


they may equivalently be written


srx=(α+cos x)/sin x,


sxp=(α−cos x)/sin x,


cxp=(β+sin x)/cos x,


crx=(β−sin x)/cos x.


Proof.


Book 1 proved


|csc x|=α/sin x,


|sec x|=β/cos x


on D. Substitute into the canonical definitions. ∎


Corollary 2.I.C6 — Absolute values are branch data, not decoration


Removing |csc x| or |sec x| without retaining α or β changes the global function. Signless formulas are valid only after a chart or sign condition has been declared.


This rule survives every later reduction in Book 2.


2.I.F — Naming status


Theorem 2.I.T6 — Definitional quartet, theorem-level reciprocity


Relative to Book 1:


1. the symbols srx, sxp, cxp, and crx are canonical definitions;


2. positivity is inherited and re-established here on the sharper maximal natural domains;


3. reciprocal pairing is a theorem;


4. the identity of reciprocal form with negative-tangential conjugate form is a theorem;


5. the quartet introduces no Projection-specific mathematical axiom.


Proof.


The four names are assigned to already-admitted trigonometric expressions. The reciprocal results follow from the inherited Pythagorean reciprocal identities. Positivity follows from their strict difference-of-squares inequalities. No independent proposition has been assumed. ∎


Negative Theorem 2.I.N1 — Four names do not establish four degrees of freedom


The primitive quartet does not represent four independent continuous mathematical degrees of freedom.


Proof.


Theorem 2.I.T1 gives sxp=1/srx and Theorem 2.I.T2 gives crx=1/cxp. Therefore at most two continuous positive values remain before cross-family relations are considered. Later sections will test whether even those two are independent at the same phase. ∎


Negative Theorem 2.I.N2 — Historical names do not establish physical exponentiation


Nothing in the abbreviations srx, sxp, cxp, or crx, nor in the historical verbal names from which they arose, establishes temporal exponential growth, decay, energy flow, probability, quantum amplitude, a spacetime scale factor, causal direction, or any other physical interpretation.


The operators are presently positive real trigonometric functions of a dimensionless phase coordinate.


2.I.G — Handoff to the half-angle atlas


The primitive definitions conceal a simpler coordinate geometry. On the principal positive chart 0<x<π/2, the absolute values simplify lawfully and the four functions become


srx=cot(x/2),


sxp=tan(x/2),


cxp=tan(π/4+x/2),


crx=tan(π/4−x/2).


The next section will not treat these as incidental identities. It will make the half-angle structure explicit, transport it through every quadrant without dropping branch information, derive exact ranges and inverse-coordinate formulas, and prepare the rational half-angle generator from which the later double-angle closure emerges.


Book 2, Section 2.I — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: Book 1 absolute-value envelopes, branch signs, natural channel domains, reciprocal-conjugate products, positivity, and ordinary reciprocal Pythagorean identities.


Source provenance: historical T0.II introduced the four canonical names and proved the reciprocal products on the common domain D. The revised section preserves those formulas while incorporating Book 1’s sharper maximal-domain distinction D_s and D_c and foregrounding the exact equivalence between the reciprocal form and the negative-tangential conjugate form.


New Projection-specific names activated: srx, sxp, cxp, crx, 𝒫.


New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical interpretations: 0.


Proved in 2.I: canonical naming of the quartet; maximal natural domains; exact reciprocal–negative-tangential equivalence in each channel; strict positivity; pairwise recovery of the reciprocal envelope and ordinary tangential coordinate; branch-explicit rational forms; preservation of the absolute-value requirement; and the reduction from four displayed primitive names to at most two independent continuous positive values.


Certified handoff: 2.II makes the half-angle structure a principal feature of the calculus rather than a side identity. It will derive the principal formulas, transported quadrant atlas, exact ranges, inverse phase coordinate modulo π, stereographic recovery formulas, and the rational bridge from the half-angle generator to sin 2x and cos 2x.


2.II — THE HALF-ANGLE ATLAS: FROM RECIPROCAL PRIMITIVES TO A SINGLE POSITIVE PHASE COORDINATE


2.II.A — Why half-angle form is structural


The four canonical primitives were introduced in absolute reciprocal form because those formulas preserve the branch data globally on their maximal natural domains. Their half-angle forms reveal a different aspect of the same objects: they exhibit the primitives as projective coordinates on the phase quotient.


The half-angle identities are therefore not alternate definitions and not local approximations. They are exact chart representations of the canonical absolute-valued functions. The absolute-value forms control global branch correctness; the half-angle forms expose coordinate geometry, range, invertibility, and the path toward the later double-angle closure.


2.II.B — Principal positive chart


Declaration 2.II.B1 — Principal positive chart [Q]


For this subsection only, restrict to


0<x<π/2.


Then sin x>0 and cos x>0, so


|csc x|=csc x,


|sec x|=sec x.


Theorem 2.II.T1 — Principal sine half-angle pair [Q]


On 0<x<π/2,


srx(x)=cot(x/2),


sxp(x)=tan(x/2).


Proof.


Using the canonical definitions,


srx

=csc x+cot x

=(1+cos x)/sin x

=cot(x/2),


while


sxp

=csc x−cot x

=(1−cos x)/sin x

=tan(x/2).


The reciprocal relation is therefore also visible directly as


cot(x/2)tan(x/2)=1. ∎


Theorem 2.II.T2 — Principal cosine half-angle pair [Q]


On 0<x<π/2,


cxp(x)=tan(π/4+x/2),


crx(x)=tan(π/4−x/2).


Proof.


On the principal chart,


cxp

=sec x+tan x

=(1+sin x)/cos x,


crx

=sec x−tan x

=(1−sin x)/cos x.


It remains to derive the two half-angle identities explicitly. Write


a=x/2.


Then


tan(π/4+a)

=[cos a+sin a]/[cos a−sin a].


Multiplying numerator and denominator by cos a+sin a gives


tan(π/4+a)

=[(cos a+sin a)²]/[cos²a−sin²a]

=[1+2sin a cos a]/cos 2a

=(1+sin x)/cos x.


Likewise,


tan(π/4−a)

=[cos a−sin a]/[cos a+sin a]

=[(cos a−sin a)²]/[cos²a−sin²a]

=[1−2sin a cos a]/cos 2a

=(1−sin x)/cos x.


Therefore


cxp=tan(π/4+x/2),


crx=tan(π/4−x/2). ∎


Corollary 2.II.C1 — The primitive quartet is a reciprocal half-angle atlas [Q]


On the principal chart, the four canonical functions are


srx=cot(x/2),


sxp=tan(x/2),


cxp=tan(π/4+x/2),


crx=tan(π/4−x/2).


Thus each primitive is a tangent or cotangent of a half-angle coordinate, and each reciprocal partner is obtained by replacing a positive half-angle tangent by its cotangent complement.


2.II.C — Maximal sine half-angle coordinate


The principal sine formula extends naturally beyond the first quadrant because srx and sxp are π-periodic and remain finite at cosine-zero points.


Definition 2.II.D1 — Reduced sine-channel phase


For x∈D_s, let θ_s(x) be the unique representative of [x]_π lying in


(0,π).


Equivalently, θ_s is obtained from x by subtracting an integer multiple of π until the result lies strictly between 0 and π.


Theorem 2.II.T3 — Global sine-channel half-angle form on D_s


For every x∈D_s,


srx(x)=cot(θ_s/2),


sxp(x)=tan(θ_s/2).


Proof.


Write


x=nπ+θ_s


with n∈ℤ and 0<θ_s<π. Because the sine pair is π-periodic,


srx(x)=srx(θ_s),


sxp(x)=sxp(θ_s).


On 0<θ_s<π, sin θ_s>0, so the absolute cosecant reduces lawfully to csc θ_s. The ordinary half-angle identities then give


srx(θ_s)=cot(θ_s/2),


sxp(θ_s)=tan(θ_s/2). ∎


Theorem 2.II.T4 — Exact sine-channel range and bijection [D_s]


The map


srx:D_s/πℤ→ℝ_{>0}


is bijective when D_s/πℤ is represented by θ_s∈(0,π).


Equivalently, as θ_s increases from 0 to π,


srx=cot(θ_s/2)


decreases strictly from +∞ to 0, while


sxp=tan(θ_s/2)


increases strictly from 0 to +∞.


The value 1 occurs exactly at


θ_s=π/2,


which is a cosine-zero point regular for the sine channel.


Proof.


The half-angle θ_s/2 ranges over (0,π/2). Cotangent is strictly decreasing from +∞ to 0 there, and tangent is its reciprocal, strictly increasing from 0 to +∞. Both equal 1 only at θ_s/2=π/4. ∎


Corollary 2.II.C2 — Exact inverse sine-channel phase coordinate


Let


z=srx(x)>0.


Then the phase modulo π is reconstructed uniquely by


θ_s=2 arctan(1/z).


Equivalently, since sxp=1/z,


θ_s=2 arctan(sxp).


Thus srx is a complete positive scalar coordinate for the punctured half-period phase circle 𝕋_π\{[0]_π}.


Clarification 2.II.CL1 — Complete modulo π is not complete modulo 2π


The coordinate z=srx reconstructs [x]_π, not [x]_{2π}. The two lifted phases x and x+π have the same z. Recovering the signed unit-circle point requires an additional half-turn branch label such as α=sgn(sin x), exactly as established in Book 1.


2.II.D — Maximal cosine half-angle coordinate


The cosine pair has an analogous maximal chart centered on the cosine-positive interval.


Definition 2.II.D2 — Reduced cosine-channel phase


For x∈D_c, let θ_c(x) be the unique representative of [x]_π lying in


(−π/2,π/2).


Theorem 2.II.T5 — Global cosine-channel half-angle form on D_c


For every x∈D_c,


cxp(x)=tan(π/4+θ_c/2),


crx(x)=tan(π/4−θ_c/2).


Proof.


Write x=nπ+θ_c with −π/2<θ_c<π/2. The cosine pair is π-periodic, and cos θ_c>0 throughout this interval. Therefore |sec θ_c|=sec θ_c, and the principal cosine half-angle identities apply on the entire reduced cosine chart. ∎


Theorem 2.II.T6 — Exact cosine-channel range and inverse


As θ_c increases through (−π/2,π/2),


cxp=tan(π/4+θ_c/2)


increases strictly from 0 to +∞, while


crx=tan(π/4−θ_c/2)


decreases strictly from +∞ to 0.


If


w=cxp(x)>0,


then


θ_c=2 arctan(w)−π/2.


Equivalently,


θ_c=π/2−2 arctan(crx).


The value w=1 occurs exactly at θ_c=0, a sine-zero point regular for the cosine channel.


2.II.E — Common-domain quadrant atlas


The maximal channel charts overlap on D but use different natural cuts. For identities involving all four primitives at the same phase, the common-domain quadrant atlas remains the appropriate local language.


Definition 2.II.D3 — Reduced quadrant coordinate [Q]


For x∈Q_k, define


ξ_k=x−kπ/2,


so that


0<ξ_k<π/2.


Theorem 2.II.T7 — Named half-angle atlas on even quadrants [Q]


If k is even and x∈Q_k, then


srx=cot(ξ_k/2),


sxp=tan(ξ_k/2),


cxp=tan(π/4+ξ_k/2),


crx=tan(π/4−ξ_k/2).


Proof.


Every even quadrant is a half-turn translate of Q_0, and all four primitives are π-periodic. Apply Theorems 2.II.T1–T2 to the reduced coordinate ξ_k. ∎


Theorem 2.II.T8 — Named half-angle atlas on odd quadrants [Q]


If k is odd and x∈Q_k, then


srx=tan(π/4−ξ_k/2),


sxp=tan(π/4+ξ_k/2),


cxp=tan(ξ_k/2),


crx=cot(ξ_k/2).


Proof.


An odd quadrant is obtained from an even quadrant by a quarter-turn. The Book 1 transport theorem sends the sine plus factor to the cosine minus factor, the sine minus factor to the cosine plus factor, the cosine plus factor to the sine minus factor, and the cosine minus factor to the sine plus factor. Substitution of the principal formulas gives the stated atlas. ∎


Corollary 2.II.C3 — Two chart types, selected by ε [Q]


The even-quadrant chart occurs exactly where


ε=+1,


and the odd-quadrant chart occurs exactly where


ε=−1.


Thus the product-sign branch ε selects which of the two transported half-angle packages applies on the common domain.


2.II.F — Exact ranges on the common domain


Theorem 2.II.T9 — Primitive range classification by ε [Q]


On any quadrant with ε=+1,


srx,cxp∈(1,+∞),


sxp,crx∈(0,1).


On any quadrant with ε=−1,


srx,cxp∈(0,1),


sxp,crx∈(1,+∞).


Proof.


Apply the monotonic ranges of the tangent and cotangent functions in Theorems 2.II.T7–T8. ∎


Corollary 2.II.C4 — Branch sign from the primary range [D]


For z=srx on D,


ε=sgn(z−1).


Likewise, for w=cxp on D,


ε=sgn(w−1).


Proof.


Theorem 2.II.T9 gives z>1 and w>1 exactly on ε=+1 quadrants, while 0<z<1 and 0<w<1 exactly on ε=−1 quadrants. ∎


Clarification 2.II.CL2 — This is not yet the FlatWave theorem


The equality


ε=sgn(srx−1)


is a range theorem for the primitive half-angle coordinate. FlatWave has not yet been defined. A later section must independently derive FlatWave from the UNA and Saw layers before it may be identified with ε.


2.II.G — Midpoint structure and octant ordering


Definition 2.II.D4 — Quadrant midpoint


Let


m_k=(2k+1)π/4.


Then m_k separates the two open octants inside Q_k.


Theorem 2.II.T10 — Exact midpoint values [Q]


At x=m_k,


srx=cxp=√2+ε,


sxp=crx=√2−ε.


Proof.


At the midpoint, ξ_k=π/4. For ε=+1, the relevant values are


cot(π/8)=tan(3π/8)=√2+1


and


tan(π/8)=√2−1.


For ε=−1 the quarter-turn chart exchanges the two reciprocal values. Both cases are represented by √2±ε. ∎


Theorem 2.II.T11 — Octant crossing order [O]


On the first octant O_{2k} of Q_k,


srx>cxp,


sxp<crx.


On the second octant O_{2k+1},


srx<cxp,


sxp>crx.


At m_k the corresponding pairs are equal.


Proof.


Across every quadrant, srx decreases while cxp increases. Their unique crossing is the midpoint value given by Theorem 2.II.T10. Reciprocal inversion reverses the inequalities for sxp and crx. ∎


Clarification 2.II.CL3 — Ordering is mathematical, not physical dominance


The inequalities describe which positive primitive is numerically larger on each octant. No physical preference, active channel, energy dominance, growth mode, chirality, or causal direction is assigned.


2.II.H — Stereographic recovery from the sine half-angle generator


The half-angle coordinate z=srx allows the ordinary trigonometric data modulo the half-turn branch to be written rationally.


Theorem 2.II.T12 — Sine-channel stereographic recovery [D_s]


Let


z=srx>0.


Then


|sin x|=2z/(z²+1),


|csc x|=(z²+1)/(2z),


cot x=(z²−1)/(2z).


Proof.


Because sxp=1/z,


|csc x|=(srx+sxp)/2

=(z+1/z)/2

=(z²+1)/(2z),


and hence


|sin x|=2z/(z²+1).


Similarly,


cot x=(srx−sxp)/2

=(z−1/z)/2

=(z²−1)/(2z). ∎


Corollary 2.II.C5 — Common-domain tangent and cosine magnitude from z [D]


On D, where cot x≠0,


tan x=2z/(z²−1),


and


|cos x|=|z²−1|/(z²+1).


Proof.


Reciprocate the cotangent formula. Then use


|cos x|=|sin x|·|cot x|. ∎


Corollary 2.II.C6 — Signed unit-circle recovery with one half-turn label


If α=sgn(sin x) is supplied in addition to z, then


sin x=2αz/(z²+1),


cos x=α(z²−1)/(z²+1).


Thus z supplies the complete continuous half-period coordinate, while α supplies the discrete half-turn branch needed to reconstruct the signed unit-circle point.


2.II.I — Rational bridge to the double angle


The double angle is π-periodic. Consequently, unlike the signed unit-circle point, it can be reconstructed from z without the extra half-turn label α.


Theorem 2.II.T13 — Double-angle sine from the half-angle generator [D_s]


For z=srx,


sin 2x=4z(z²−1)/(z²+1)².


Proof.


From Corollary 2.II.C6,


sin x=2αz/(z²+1),


cos x=α(z²−1)/(z²+1).


Therefore


sin 2x

=2 sin x cos x

=2[2αz/(z²+1)][α(z²−1)/(z²+1)]

=4z(z²−1)/(z²+1)²,


since α²=1. The final expression is independent of the half-turn label. ∎


Theorem 2.II.T14 — Double-angle cosine from the half-angle generator [D_s]


For z=srx,


cos 2x=(z⁴−6z²+1)/(z²+1)².


Proof.


Using the signed recovery formulas,


cos 2x

=cos²x−sin²x

=[(z²−1)²−4z²]/(z²+1)²

=(z⁴−6z²+1)/(z²+1)². ∎


Corollary 2.II.C7 — Rational unit-circle closure


The two rational functions


Y(z)=4z(z²−1)/(z²+1)²,


X(z)=(z⁴−6z²+1)/(z²+1)²


satisfy


X(z)²+Y(z)²=1


for every z>0.


They are precisely


X(z)=cos 2x,


Y(z)=sin 2x


under the half-angle coordinate z=srx(x).


Proof.


Direct algebra gives


(z⁴−6z²+1)²

=z⁸−12z⁶+38z⁴−12z²+1,


and


16z²(z²−1)²

=16z⁶−32z⁴+16z².


Adding,


(z⁴−6z²+1)²+16z²(z²−1)²

=z⁸+4z⁶+6z⁴+4z²+1

=(z²+1)⁴.


Dividing by (z²+1)⁴ gives


X(z)²+Y(z)²=1. ∎


Corollary 2.II.C8 — Double-angle branch sign is encoded by the unit threshold


On D,


sgn(sin 2x)=sgn(z²−1)=sgn(z−1)=ε,


because z>0 and z≠1 on D.


This is the exact half-angle-to-double-angle branch bridge that the UNA and FlatWave constructions must later recover independently from the named operator algebra.


2.II.J — What the half-angle atlas has and has not done


The half-angle atlas has produced a strong mathematical reduction:


• srx is a bijective positive coordinate for phase modulo π on its natural sine domain;


• sxp is its reciprocal;


• the full common-domain quartet admits two transported quadrant charts;


• the branch sign ε is encoded by whether srx lies above or below 1;


• ordinary sine/cosine magnitudes are rational in z=srx;


• and the double-angle pair sin 2x, cos 2x is completely rational in z without the lost half-turn branch.


It has not yet proved the first climax of Book 2. In particular, urx and uxp have not yet been defined, so the identity


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x


has not yet entered the revised derivation. The present section only prepares the target double-angle object from the primitive half-angle side.


Negative Theorem 2.II.N1 — Half-angle coordinate closure does not create physical dynamics


The monotonicity of srx with phase, the rational form of sin 2x, and the existence of a positive phase coordinate do not establish temporal evolution, an energy law, probability flow, cosmological expansion, or any other dynamics.


Negative Theorem 2.II.N2 — One half-period coordinate does not recover the lifted phase


No function of z=srx alone can distinguish x from x+π. Therefore z cannot reconstruct the full 2π phase branch without an additional discrete label.


Book 2, Section 2.II — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: Book 1 half-angle chart transport, branch signs, quotient hierarchy, range theorems, reciprocal pairing, and standard trigonometric half-angle identities.


Source provenance: historical T0.II supplied the principal-chart half-angle forms and later stereographic recovery formulas. The revised section promotes the half-angle structure to a central atlas, extends the named functions over their maximal channel domains proved in Book 1, explicitly constructs inverse coordinates modulo π, and derives the double-angle rational bridge before any UNA definition is admitted.


New symbols activated: θ_s, θ_c, ξ_k, z=srx, w=cxp, and m_k.


New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical dynamics claims: 0.


Proved in 2.II: the principal and maximal-domain half-angle forms; exact positive ranges; inverse phase coordinates modulo π; the two common-domain quadrant chart types; branch-sign recovery from the unit threshold; midpoint and octant ordering; stereographic recovery of ordinary trigonometric data from z; and the exact rational formulas for sin 2x and cos 2x.


Certified handoff: 2.III develops the named primitive symmetry orbit under phase reflection, quarter-turn, half-turn, and complementary reflection. That symmetry layer permits the UNA differences to be introduced with their sign convention fixed before the first double-angle climax.


2.III — THE PRIMITIVE SYMMETRY ORBIT: REFLECTION, QUARTER-TURN, HALF-TURN, AND COMPLEMENT


2.III.A — Why symmetry precedes derived differences


The primitive quartet is now named, and its half-angle atlas has been made explicit. Before any UNA difference is introduced, the transformations of the primitive layer must be frozen independently. This prevents a later sign convention in a derived quantity from being used backward to decide how the primitives transform.


The relevant phase transformations were normalized in Book 1:


ι(x)=−x,


τ_{π/2}(x)=x+π/2,


τ_π(x)=x+π,


ι_d(x)=π/2−x.


On the common domain D, each of these maps sends admissible phase to admissible phase. Reflection preserves the sine and cosine channel domains separately; quarter-turn and complementary reflection exchange the sine and cosine channel domains; the half-turn preserves every primitive domain.


2.III.B — Phase reflection is reciprocal inversion


Theorem 2.III.T1 — Primitive reflection exchange [D]


For every x∈D,


srx(−x)=sxp(x),


sxp(−x)=srx(x),


cxp(−x)=crx(x),


crx(−x)=cxp(x).


Proof.


Absolute cosecant and secant are even, while cotangent and tangent are odd. Therefore


srx(−x)

=|csc(−x)|+cot(−x)

=|csc x|−cot x

=sxp(x).


The other three identities follow identically. ∎


Corollary 2.III.C1 — Reflection acts as reciprocal inversion [D]


Because sxp=1/srx and crx=1/cxp,


srx(−x)=1/srx(x),


cxp(−x)=1/cxp(x),


and likewise


sxp(−x)=1/sxp(x),


crx(−x)=1/crx(x).


Thus, within either primitive channel, phase reflection is represented by positive reciprocal inversion.


Corollary 2.III.C2 — Unit threshold is the reflection fixed value


A positive primitive can be fixed under reciprocal inversion only at value 1.


For the sine pair this occurs at the cosine-zero points admitted by D_s but excluded from the common domain D.


For the cosine pair it occurs at the sine-zero points admitted by D_c but excluded from D.


Hence no primitive value on D is fixed by the reflection-induced reciprocal exchange.


2.III.C — Quarter-turn exchange of channel and conjugate role


Theorem 2.III.T2 — Complete quarter-turn action [D]


For every x∈D,


srx(x+π/2)=crx(x),


sxp(x+π/2)=cxp(x),


cxp(x+π/2)=sxp(x),


crx(x+π/2)=srx(x).


Proof.


The ordinary quarter-turn identities give


sin(x+π/2)=cos x,


cos(x+π/2)=−sin x,


cot(x+π/2)=−tan x,


tan(x+π/2)=−cot x.


Therefore


|csc(x+π/2)|=|sec x|,


|sec(x+π/2)|=|csc x|.


Substitution yields


srx(x+π/2)

=|sec x|−tan x

=crx(x),


and similarly for the remaining three identities. ∎


Corollary 2.III.C3 — Quarter-turn swaps both channel and tangential sign


Under τ_{π/2},


srx↔crx,


sxp↔cxp.


Thus a quarter-turn does two things simultaneously at the primitive-label level:


• it exchanges sine and cosine channels;


• and it exchanges positive-tangential with negative-tangential conjugate role.


This is why the quarter-turn is not the same primitive permutation as complementary reflection.


Corollary 2.III.C4 — Quarter-turn preserves reciprocal pairing


The image of the sine reciprocal pair under τ_{π/2} is the cosine reciprocal pair in reversed displayed order:


(srx,sxp)↦(crx,cxp).


Since crx·cxp=1, reciprocal conjugacy is invariant under the quarter-turn.


2.III.D — Half-turn invariance and the π quotient


Theorem 2.III.T3 — Primitive half-turn invariance [D]


For every primitive f∈𝒫 and every x in its natural domain,


f(x+π)=f(x).


In particular, on D,


𝒫(x+π)=𝒫(x).


Proof.


Tangent and cotangent are π-periodic. Under x↦x+π, secant and cosecant reverse sign, but their absolute values do not. Therefore each canonical primitive definition is unchanged. ∎


Corollary 2.III.C5 — Primitive descent to 𝕋_π


Every primitive factors through the half-period quotient. The full quartet therefore defines a well-defined map on the admissible part of 𝕋_π.


Corollary 2.III.C6 — No primitive recovery of the half-turn branch


The quartet cannot distinguish x from x+π. Hence no deterministic function of


srx,sxp,cxp,crx


alone can recover [x]_{2π} for all x∈D.


This remains true regardless of how many derived quantities are later computed deterministically from the quartet.


Theorem 2.III.T4 — Induced quarter-turn has order two on the primitive representation [D]


Although τ_{π/2} has order four on the 2π phase circle, its action on the primitive quartet has order two:


𝒫(x+π)=𝒫(x).


Equivalently, applying the quarter-turn permutation twice restores every primitive label.


Proof.


Two quarter-turns equal the half-turn τ_π, and Theorem 2.III.T3 shows that τ_π acts trivially on every primitive. ∎


Clarification 2.III.CL1 — Phase-transform order and representation order are different


The phase transformation τ_{π/2} is not itself an involution on 𝕋_{2π}. Only its induced action after the primitive system has forgotten the half-turn branch is involutive.


This is an exact example of Book 1’s representation firewall: a representation may have a smaller effective symmetry order because it has already quotiented away information.


2.III.E — Complementary reflection exchanges channels without conjugation


Theorem 2.III.T5 — Complementary-reflection action [D]


For every x∈D,


srx(π/2−x)=cxp(x),


sxp(π/2−x)=crx(x),


cxp(π/2−x)=srx(x),


crx(π/2−x)=sxp(x).


Proof.


The complementary identities are


sin(π/2−x)=cos x,


cos(π/2−x)=sin x,


cot(π/2−x)=tan x,


tan(π/2−x)=cot x.


Therefore the absolute reciprocal envelopes exchange without changing the tangential sign. Substitution gives the four identities. ∎


Corollary 2.III.C7 — Complementary reflection preserves tangential sign class


Under ι_d,


srx↔cxp,


sxp↔crx.


Thus complementary reflection exchanges sine and cosine channels while preserving positive-tangential versus negative-tangential role.


This distinguishes ι_d sharply from τ_{π/2}, which exchanges both channel and conjugate role.


2.III.F — The quartet is one orbit of a single primitive function


The preceding theorems allow the entire primitive quartet to be generated from srx by evaluating that one function at symmetry-related phases.


Theorem 2.III.T6 — Single-function orbit representation [D]


For every x∈D,


srx(x)=srx(x),


sxp(x)=srx(−x),


cxp(x)=srx(π/2−x),


crx(x)=srx(x+π/2).


Equivalently,


𝒫(x)

=

(srx(x),

srx(−x),

srx(π/2−x),

srx(x+π/2)).


Proof.


The second identity is Theorem 2.III.T1, the third is Theorem 2.III.T5, and the fourth is Theorem 2.III.T2. ∎


Corollary 2.III.C8 — Primitive quartet is a symmetry atlas of one named master function


Every primitive name can be reached from srx by a normalized phase symmetry. Thus the four named primitives are not four unrelated functional species. They are four symmetry-related presentations of one reciprocal half-angle family.


Negative Theorem 2.III.N1 — Orbit equivalence is not same-phase algebraic reduction


The symmetry-orbit theorem does not yet prove that cxp(x) is an algebraic function of srx(x) at the same phase x.


Proof.


The orbit identity gives


cxp(x)=srx(π/2−x),


which relates values at different arguments. A same-phase reduction would require an explicit formula of the form


cxp(x)=F(srx(x), branch data)


with both sides evaluated at x. No such cross-family algebraic formula has yet been derived in the revised Book 2 dependency order. That result is reserved for the Möbius climax. ∎


Corollary 2.III.C9 — Symmetry orbit and generator closure are distinct achievements


The present section proves that one function under transformed arguments generates the primitive labels as an orbit.


The later Möbius section must prove something stronger: one scalar value at one phase determines the other primitive values at that same phase.


2.III.G — Induced permutation group


The three nontrivial primitive permutations established above are


reflection:

(srx sxp)(cxp crx),


quarter-turn:

(srx crx)(sxp cxp),


complementary reflection:

(srx cxp)(sxp crx).


Theorem 2.III.T7 — Klein-four primitive symmetry group [D]


Together with the identity permutation, these three transformations form a four-element group isomorphic to the Klein four group V₄.


Proof.


Each of the three nonidentity permutations is a product of two disjoint transpositions and therefore has order two.


Composing reflection with quarter-turn gives complementary reflection. For example,


srx

→ under reflection sxp

→ under quarter-turn cxp,


which is exactly the complementary-reflection image of srx. The same check holds for the remaining primitive labels.


The three nonidentity permutations commute, and the product of any two distinct nonidentity elements is the third. These are the defining multiplication properties of V₄. ∎


Corollary 2.III.C10 — The V₄ action is transitive on the primitive quartet


Starting from srx, the four group elements reach


srx,

sxp,

cxp,

crx


exactly once. Hence the action on the four primitive labels is transitive and, at the label level, regular.


Corollary 2.III.C11 — The primitive representation factors through half-period symmetry


The V₄ action is the natural finite symmetry seen after half-turn invariance has collapsed the order-four quarter-turn of the 2π circle to an order-two action on the primitive representation.


The primitive symmetry group therefore records the symmetries visible to this representation, not the full transformation group of the lifted phase line.


2.III.H — Branch-sign transport under the same symmetries


The inherited product sign


ε=sgn(sin 2x)


has not been defined from any derived R operator. It may therefore be transported independently at the primitive layer.


Theorem 2.III.T8 — ε transformation laws [D]


For every x∈D,


ε(−x)=−ε(x),


ε(x+π/2)=−ε(x),


ε(x+π)=ε(x),


ε(π/2−x)=ε(x).


Proof.


Use ε=sgn(sin 2x). Then


sin(−2x)=−sin 2x,


sin(2x+π)=−sin 2x,


sin(2x+2π)=sin 2x,


sin(π−2x)=sin 2x.


Taking signs on D gives the result. ∎


Corollary 2.III.C12 — Two primitive symmetries reverse the branch sector and two preserve it


Reflection and quarter-turn reverse ε.


Half-turn and complementary reflection preserve ε.


This is a theorem about the inherited sign of sin 2x. It does not yet establish anything about FlatWave, which remains undefined.


2.III.I — Symmetry and the half-angle primary coordinate


Let


z=srx(x),


w=cxp(x)


on D.


The primitive orbit gives a compact transformed-argument picture:


srx(x)=z,


srx(−x)=1/z,


srx(π/2−x)=w,


srx(x+π/2)=1/w.


Theorem 2.III.T9 — Reciprocal rectangle of transformed srx values [D]


The four symmetry-related values of the single master function srx are


{z,1/z,w,1/w},


with reflection pairing z with 1/z, complementary reflection pairing z with w, and quarter-turn pairing z with 1/w.


Proof.


Combine Theorem 2.III.T6 with the reciprocal identities sxp=1/srx and crx=1/cxp. ∎


Clarification 2.III.CL2 — w is not yet eliminated


The notation


{z,1/z,w,1/w}


makes the reciprocal and symmetry architecture transparent, but it still contains two same-phase positive coordinates z and w. Nothing in the present section has yet shown that w is determined algebraically by z at fixed x.


The later FlatWave relation will supply the missing same-phase constraint, and the Möbius theorem will solve it.


2.III.J — Primitive symmetry table


For reference, the exact named action on D is:


identity:

(srx,sxp,cxp,crx; ε)

→

(srx,sxp,cxp,crx; ε),


phase reflection ι:

(srx,sxp,cxp,crx; ε)

→

(sxp,srx,crx,cxp; −ε),


quarter-turn τ_{π/2}:

(srx,sxp,cxp,crx; ε)

→

(crx,cxp,sxp,srx; −ε),


half-turn τ_π:

(srx,sxp,cxp,crx; ε)

→

(srx,sxp,cxp,crx; ε),


complementary reflection ι_d:

(srx,sxp,cxp,crx; ε)

→

(cxp,crx,srx,sxp; ε).


This table is descriptive shorthand for Theorems 2.III.T1–T8. It introduces no new transformation law.


2.III.K — Non-scope


The primitive V₄ symmetry is a mathematical symmetry of named trigonometric functions. It is not yet a gauge group, internal particle symmetry, parity-violation law, charge-conjugation law, spacetime isometry group, or physical four-state system.


The fact that reflection acts as reciprocal inversion does not by itself identify the reciprocal partner with a physical antiparticle, reverse-time state, decay mode, or mirror universe.


The fact that ε reverses under reflection does not establish a preferred physical chirality. Book 0 already proved that a preferred orientation cannot be extracted canonically from orientation-symmetric data alone.


The fact that the quartet is one symmetry orbit does not prove the one-generator theorem. The latter is a same-phase algebraic statement and remains downstream.


Book 2, Section 2.III — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: canonical primitive definitions from 2.I; half-angle and quotient structure from 2.II; Book 1 phase transformations, π-periodicity, and branch-sign definitions; and standard trigonometric parity and quarter-turn identities.


Source provenance: historical T0.II proved the reflection, quarter-turn, half-turn, and diagonal-reflection primitive actions and observed that the quartet is one symmetry orbit. The revised section replaces historical scalar symbols J_x, R, H, and K with the collision-safe Book 1 notation ι, τ_{π/2}, τ_π, and ι_d; separates transformed-argument orbit equivalence from later same-phase Möbius reduction; and identifies the induced primitive permutation group explicitly as V₄.


New Book 2 group notation: V₄ for the Klein four group describing the induced primitive-label symmetry.


New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Derived UNA operators introduced: 0.

FlatWave introduced: no.


Proved in 2.III: reflection exchange and reciprocal inversion; complete quarter-turn exchange; half-turn invariance; complementary-reflection exchange; the single-function orbit representation of the primitive quartet; the induced V₄ permutation group; branch-sign transport; and the reciprocal rectangle {z,1/z,w,1/w} of symmetry-related srx values.


2.IV — UNA DIFFERENCES AND THE CANONICAL SIGN LOCK


2.IV.A — Derived differences enter only after the primitive layer is frozen


The primitive quartet has now been defined, expressed in half-angle coordinates, and assigned its complete symmetry orbit independently of any derived sign convention. We may therefore form the first named differences without allowing those differences to redefine the primitive structure from which they are built.


The UNA layer contains two differences. Their order matters. In particular, the cosine-primary difference is canonically cxp−sxp, not its negative.


Definition 2.IV.D1 — Unainverex difference [D]


Define


urx(x)=srx(x)−crx(x).


Definition 2.IV.D2 — Unaexpon difference and canonical sign lock [D]


Define


uxp(x)=cxp(x)−sxp(x).


This order is canonical.


The reversed historical transcription


sxp(x)−cxp(x)


is exactly −uxp(x). It is retired and is not an alternate convention.


Constitutional Rule 2.IV.R1 — UNA sign lock


Every later theorem, table, graph, symbolic manipulation, spreadsheet, code expression, and historical reconciliation in the revised theory shall use


uxp=cxp−sxp.


If a source displays sxp−cxp under the label uxp, the source is to be marked as carrying the retired sign error unless its expanded formula independently shows the canonical cxp−sxp order.


2.IV.B — Why the two difference orders are paired


The primitive symmetry orbit already suggests the canonical arrangement. Under complementary reflection, positive-tangential sine and cosine primitives exchange, and their reciprocal partners exchange. The two UNA differences should therefore exchange with each other rather than exchange with one another’s negatives.


Theorem 2.IV.T1 — Complementary exchange fixes the paired UNA order [D]


Under ι_d(x)=π/2−x,


urx(ι_d x)=uxp(x),


uxp(ι_d x)=urx(x).


Proof.


From Theorem 2.III.T5,


srx(ι_d x)=cxp(x),


crx(ι_d x)=sxp(x).


Therefore


urx(ι_d x)

=srx(ι_d x)−crx(ι_d x)

=cxp(x)−sxp(x)

=uxp(x).


Likewise,


uxp(ι_d x)

=cxp(ι_d x)−sxp(ι_d x)

=srx(x)−crx(x)

=urx(x). ∎


Corollary 2.IV.C1 — The retired reversed difference breaks complementary exchange


If one instead defines the second displayed difference by sxp−cxp, then complementary reflection sends urx to the negative of that quantity rather than to the quantity itself.


Thus the reversed sign destroys the direct channel-exchange symmetry of the UNA pair.


2.IV.C — Exact absolute-magnitude branch forms


The primitive signed-rational formulas can be combined into branch-explicit UNA forms without introducing an auxiliary coordinate.


Theorem 2.IV.T2 — Exact magnitude form of urx [D]


For every x∈D,


urx

=

[ε+|cos x|−|sin x|]

/

[|sin x||cos x|].


Proof.


Book 1 gives the magnitude forms


srx=(1+ε|cos x|)/|sin x|,


crx=(1−ε|sin x|)/|cos x|.


Therefore


urx

=(1+ε|cos x|)/|sin x|

−(1−ε|sin x|)/|cos x|.


Combining over the positive denominator |sin x||cos x| gives numerator


|cos x|+ε|cos x|²−|sin x|+ε|sin x|².


Since


|sin x|²+|cos x|²=1,


the numerator reduces to


ε+|cos x|−|sin x|. ∎


Theorem 2.IV.T3 — Exact magnitude form of uxp [D]


For every x∈D,


uxp

=

[ε+|sin x|−|cos x|]

/

[|sin x||cos x|].


Proof.


Use


cxp=(1+ε|sin x|)/|cos x|,


sxp=(1−ε|cos x|)/|sin x|.


Then


uxp

=(1+ε|sin x|)/|cos x|

−(1−ε|cos x|)/|sin x|.


Combining terms and using


|sin x|²+|cos x|²=1


gives the displayed numerator. ∎


Corollary 2.IV.C2 — UNA numerators are complementary deviations around ε


The two numerators are


ε+(|cos x|−|sin x|)


and


ε−(|cos x|−|sin x|).


Thus the same internal magnitude imbalance enters the two UNA functions with opposite sign, while the common branch sign ε enters both with the same sign.


This complementary structure is exactly what will allow the imbalance to cancel when the two functions are later combined.


The cancellation itself is reserved for the double-angle section.


2.IV.D — Common sign and nonvanishing


Lemma 2.IV.L1 — Strict magnitude-difference bound [D]


For every x∈D,


−1<|cos x|−|sin x|<1.


Proof.


On D, both |sin x| and |cos x| lie strictly between 0 and 1. Hence the difference of the two positive magnitudes lies strictly between −1 and 1. ∎


Theorem 2.IV.T4 — Common UNA sign [D]


For every x∈D,


sgn(urx)=sgn(uxp)=ε=sgn(sin 2x).


Proof.


The denominator |sin x||cos x| in Theorems 2.IV.T2–T3 is strictly positive.


Write conceptually the magnitude imbalance as a number d with −1<d<1. The two numerators are ε+d and ε−d.


If ε=+1, both 1+d and 1−d are strictly positive.


If ε=−1, both −1+d and −1−d are strictly negative.


Therefore both UNA functions have sign ε. ∎


Corollary 2.IV.C3 — UNA nonvanishing [D]


Neither urx nor uxp vanishes anywhere on D.


Consequently their reciprocals may later be defined on the full common domain D without further point deletion.


Corollary 2.IV.C4 — UNA sign alternates by quadrant


On Q_k,


sgn(urx)=sgn(uxp)=(-1)^k.


Both are positive on even quadrants and negative on odd quadrants.


Clarification 2.IV.CL1 — Common sign is not yet FlatWave


The common UNA sign equals the already-inherited branch function ε. FlatWave has still not been defined. A later theorem must derive FlatWave from the reciprocal UNA structure before FlatWave may be identified with this sign.


2.IV.E — Same-phase rational form in the two primary coordinates


Let


z=srx(x),


w=cxp(x)


on D. Reciprocal conjugacy gives


sxp=1/z,


crx=1/w.


Theorem 2.IV.T5 — Rational UNA forms [D]


For every x∈D,


urx=z−1/w=(zw−1)/w,


uxp=w−1/z=(zw−1)/z.


Proof.


Substitute the reciprocal primitive identities directly into Definitions 2.IV.D1–D2. ∎


Corollary 2.IV.C5 — Common algebraic numerator [D]


The two UNA differences share the same numerator


zw−1.


Because z>0 and w>0, Theorem 2.IV.T4 implies


sgn(zw−1)=ε.


In particular,


zw−1≠0


throughout D.


Corollary 2.IV.C6 — UNA ratio theorem [D]


For every x∈D,


urx/uxp=z/w=srx/cxp.


Proof.


The common nonzero factor zw−1 cancels from the rational forms of Theorem 2.IV.T5. ∎


Clarification 2.IV.CL2 — Common numerator is not yet the Möbius relation


The equations


urx=(zw−1)/w,


uxp=(zw−1)/z


show that both derived differences share one same-phase algebraic numerator. They do not yet determine w from z. The missing cross-family equation will emerge only after FlatWave is derived and then solved in the Möbius climax.


2.IV.F — Symmetry action on the UNA pair


Theorem 2.IV.T6 — Phase-reflection action [D]


Under ι(x)=−x,


urx(−x)=−uxp(x),


uxp(−x)=−urx(x).


Proof.


Using Theorem 2.III.T1,


urx(−x)

=srx(−x)−crx(−x)

=sxp(x)−cxp(x)

=−uxp(x).


The second identity is identical. ∎


Theorem 2.IV.T7 — Quarter-turn action [D]


Under τ_{π/2},


urx(x+π/2)=−urx(x),


uxp(x+π/2)=−uxp(x).


Proof.


By Theorem 2.III.T2,


urx(x+π/2)

=crx(x)−srx(x)

=−urx(x),


and


uxp(x+π/2)

=sxp(x)−cxp(x)

=−uxp(x). ∎


Theorem 2.IV.T8 — Half-turn and complementary actions [D]


Under τ_π,


urx(x+π)=urx(x),


uxp(x+π)=uxp(x).


Under ι_d,


urx(π/2−x)=uxp(x),


uxp(π/2−x)=urx(x).


Proof.


Half-turn invariance follows immediately from primitive half-turn invariance. Complementary exchange is Theorem 2.IV.T1. ∎


Corollary 2.IV.C7 — UNA pair symmetry summary


Reflection swaps the pair and reverses both signs.


Quarter-turn preserves the labels and reverses both signs.


Half-turn preserves both labels and signs.


Complementary reflection swaps the pair without reversing sign.


This symmetry pattern matches the transformation laws of ε established before the UNA layer was introduced.


2.IV.G — Permanent diagnostic for the retired sign


The historical transcription error can be detected algebraically without referring to notation history.


Negative Theorem 2.IV.N1 — Reversed-uxp diagnostic [D]


Let the retired reversed expression be


sxp−cxp.


Then it equals −uxp, has sign −ε, and when paired with urx gives


urx+(sxp−cxp)

=2|csc x|−2|sec x|.


Proof.


The first statement follows directly from Definition 2.IV.D2.


Since sgn(uxp)=ε, its negative has sign −ε.


For the sum,


urx+(sxp−cxp)

=(srx−crx)+(sxp−cxp)

=(srx+sxp)−(cxp+crx).


By primitive conjugate-sum recovery,


srx+sxp=2|csc x|,


cxp+crx=2|sec x|.


Therefore


urx+(sxp−cxp)

=2|csc x|−2|sec x|. ∎


Corollary 2.IV.C8 — The reversed sign destroys the common-sign UNA architecture


The canonical pair urx,uxp always has common sign ε. Replacing uxp by sxp−cxp forces the two displayed UNA quantities to have opposite signs everywhere on D.


Therefore the reversed expression cannot be substituted into any later theorem that depends on the canonical common-sign pair.


Corollary 2.IV.C9 — π/4 checksum [Q]


At x=π/4,


srx=cxp=√2+1,


sxp=crx=√2−1.


Hence


urx=2,


uxp=2,


while the retired reversed expression equals −2.


The correct and retired conventions therefore differ maximally even at the simplest symmetric interior point.


Clarification 2.IV.CL3 — The double-angle checksum remains downstream


The present section has already excluded the reversed sign by definition, symmetry, and common-sign structure. Section 2.V will provide a still stronger checksum: only the canonical pair produces the intended exact double-angle closure when the two UNA functions are added.


2.IV.H — Status of the UNA layer


Theorem 2.IV.T9 — Derived status of urx and uxp [D]


The UNA functions are derived from the primitive quartet and add no new continuous generator.


Proof.


Both urx and uxp are finite arithmetic combinations of the four primitive values at the same phase. Therefore they are deterministic readouts of the primitive representation. ∎


Corollary 2.IV.C10 — UNA does not enlarge primitive information


Any distinction invisible to the primitive quartet remains invisible to urx and uxp. In particular, because the primitives are π-periodic,


urx(x+π)=urx(x),


uxp(x+π)=uxp(x),


and neither derived function can recover the lost half-turn branch.


2.IV.I — Non-scope


The prefixes and historical verbal names attached to urx and uxp do not establish physical input/output, expansion/contraction, creation/annihilation, forward/backward time, supply/demand, probability flow, or energy transfer.


Their common sign is a mathematical consequence of the reciprocal-trigonometric branch structure. It is not yet a physical orientation or preferred chirality.


Their nonvanishing does not imply a physical prohibition against zero states. It means only that these two particular mathematical functions do not attain zero on D.


Book 2, Section 2.IV — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: primitive definitions and reciprocal identities from 2.I; half-angle range and ε threshold structure from 2.II; primitive symmetry orbit from 2.III; Book 1 absolute-magnitude branch forms; and ordinary Pythagorean trigonometry.


Source provenance: historical T0.II defines urx=srx−crx and the canonical uxp=cxp−sxp, then proves exact branch forms and common sign using an auxiliary branch coordinate. The revised derivation removes the unnecessary auxiliary coordinate by writing the UNA numerators directly in terms of |sin x|, |cos x|, and ε. The historical reversed transcription sxp−cxp is retained only as a retired diagnostic and is not an alternate convention.


New Projection-specific names activated: urx, uxp.


New persistent mathematical symbols introduced: 0.


New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

FlatWave introduced: no.

Saw reciprocals introduced: no.

Double-angle UNA sum proved: not yet.


Proved in 2.IV: the canonical UNA definitions; complementary exchange; exact absolute-magnitude branch forms; common sign ε; nonvanishing; the rational forms with common numerator zw−1; the UNA ratio; the full primitive-induced symmetry action on the UNA pair; and a permanent algebraic diagnostic excluding the retired reversed uxp sign.


Certified handoff: 2.V is the first major climax of Book 2. It will add the two canonically signed UNA functions, show the exact cancellation of their internal magnitude imbalance, and derive


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x.


The same section will derive the corresponding unsigned product identity, so that the signed and unsigned double-angle structures stand side by side in preparation for the FlatWave theorem.


2.V — FIRST CLIMAX: EXACT DOUBLE-ANGLE CLOSURE OF THE UNA PAIR


2.V.A — The target


The UNA functions were deliberately introduced one section before they were added. Their individual formulas retain an internal imbalance between |sin x| and |cos x|. The present section asks whether that imbalance survives when the two canonically signed differences are combined.


It does not.


The cancellation is exact, and the resulting object is not another half-angle expression. It is the reciprocal of the ordinary double-angle sine.


Theorem 2.V.T1 — UNA double-angle closure [D]


For every x∈D,


urx(x)+uxp(x)=4/sin 2x.


Proof I — Conjugate-difference collapse.


By definition,


urx+uxp

=(srx−crx)+(cxp−sxp).


Regroup by reciprocal-conjugate channel:


urx+uxp

=(srx−sxp)+(cxp−crx).


From Theorem 2.I.T4,


srx−sxp=2cot x,


cxp−crx=2tan x.


Therefore


urx+uxp

=2cot x+2tan x

=2(cos x/sin x+sin x/cos x)

=2(cos²x+sin²x)/(sin x cos x)

=2/(sin x cos x)

=4/sin 2x.


∎


Proof II — Branch-magnitude cancellation.


Theorems 2.IV.T2–T3 give


urx=

[ε+|cos x|−|sin x|]

/

[|sin x||cos x|],


uxp=

[ε+|sin x|−|cos x|]

/

[|sin x||cos x|].


Adding cancels the magnitude imbalance exactly:


urx+uxp

=2ε/[|sin x||cos x|].


Because


|sin 2x|=2|sin x||cos x|


and


ε=sgn(sin 2x),


we have


ε/|sin 2x|=1/sin 2x.


Hence


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x.


∎


Corollary 2.V.C1 — Canonical sign checksum


The double-angle theorem depends on the canonical definition


uxp=cxp−sxp.


If the retired reversed expression sxp−cxp is substituted instead, the conjugate-difference regrouping is destroyed and the sum becomes


2|csc x|−2|sec x|,


not 4/sin 2x.


Thus the double-angle identity is a permanent checksum for the UNA sign convention.


2.V.B — Why this is a genuine half-angle-to-double-angle closure


The primitive quartet was exposed in 2.II as a half-angle atlas. Theorem 2.V.T1 shows that a specific cross-channel recombination of those half-angle primitives closes exactly onto the double-angle harmonic denominator.


The structure can therefore be summarized without interpretation as


reciprocal half-angle primitives

→ UNA differences

→ exact double-angle reciprocal.


No approximation, fit, series truncation, or external calibration is involved.


Theorem 2.V.T2 — Half-angle-generator form of the UNA sum [D]


Let


z=srx(x)>0.


Then


urx+uxp

=

(z²+1)²/[z(z²−1)].


Proof.


Section 2.II proved


sin 2x=

4z(z²−1)/(z²+1)².


Substitute this into Theorem 2.V.T1:


urx+uxp

=4/[4z(z²−1)/(z²+1)²]

=(z²+1)²/[z(z²−1)]. ∎


Corollary 2.V.C2 — The double-angle sum is already one-generator data


Although urx and uxp individually still involve both same-phase primary values z=srx and w=cxp in the present dependency order, their sum is determined by z alone.


This does not yet prove the one-generator theorem for the full primitive or UNA system. It proves only that this particular derived combination has already collapsed to the half-angle generator.


2.V.C — The unsigned companion: UNA product closure


The sum carries the sign of the double-angle sine. The product carries the corresponding unsigned magnitude.


Theorem 2.V.T3 — UNA product theorem [D]


For every x∈D,


urx(x)uxp(x)=4/|sin 2x|.


Proof.


Let


s=|sin x|,


c=|cos x|.


Then s,c>0 and s²+c²=1. From Theorems 2.IV.T2–T3,


urx=(ε+c−s)/(sc),


uxp=(ε+s−c)/(sc).


Their product is


urx·uxp

=[(ε+c−s)(ε+s−c)]/(s²c²).


Writing d=c−s, the numerator is


(ε+d)(ε−d)

=ε²−d²

=1−(c−s)².


Since


(c−s)²=c²+s²−2sc=1−2sc,


we obtain


1−(c−s)²=2sc.


Therefore


urx·uxp

=2sc/(s²c²)

=2/(sc).


Finally,


|sin 2x|=2sc,


so


2/(sc)=4/|sin 2x|. ∎


Corollary 2.V.C3 — UNA product is strictly positive [D]


For every x∈D,


urx·uxp>0.


This agrees with the independently proved common-sign theorem: the two UNA factors always have the same sign.


Corollary 2.V.C4 — Half-angle-generator form of the UNA product [D]


For z=srx,


urx·uxp

=

(z²+1)²/[z|z²−1|].


Proof.


From 2.II,


|sin 2x|

=4z|z²−1|/(z²+1)²


because z>0. Substitute into Theorem 2.V.T3. ∎


2.V.D — Signed versus unsigned double-angle closure


The sum and product now stand side by side:


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x,


urx·uxp=4/|sin 2x|.


The first retains the quadrant sign.


The second discards that sign while retaining the same magnitude.


Theorem 2.V.T4 — Exact sign–magnitude relation [D]


For every x∈D,


urx+uxp

=ε·urx·uxp.


Equivalently,


|urx+uxp|=urx·uxp.


Proof.


From Theorems 2.V.T1 and 2.V.T3,


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x,


urx·uxp=4/|sin 2x|.


Since


1/sin 2x

=ε/|sin 2x|,


the signed relation follows. Taking absolute values gives the second identity. ∎


Corollary 2.V.C5 — UNA closure separates magnitude from binary branch sign


The product determines the positive magnitude


4/|sin 2x|,


while the sum is obtained from that magnitude by multiplication by ε∈{−1,+1}.


Thus the entire sign difference between the two closures is one binary branch character already present in the primitive phase atlas.


Clarification 2.V.CL1 — FlatWave is still not defined


Theorem 2.V.T4 makes the forthcoming binary collapse mathematically unavoidable, but the name FlatWave has not yet been assigned to any expression in the revised dependency chain.


The next section must first introduce the reciprocal UNA partition from the nonvanishing theorem and identify the historical FlatWave expression with that partition. Only then may the resulting quotient be named and proved equal to ε.


2.V.E — Reciprocal double-angle forms


Because the UNA sum and product never vanish on D, their reciprocals are defined there.


Corollary 2.V.C6 — Signed reciprocal closure [D]


For every x∈D,


1/(urx+uxp)=sin 2x/4.


Corollary 2.V.C7 — Unsigned reciprocal closure [D]


For every x∈D,


1/(urx·uxp)=|sin 2x|/4.


Clarification 2.V.CL2 — Algebraic reciprocal versus seam extension


The preceding formulas are identities on D. The right-hand sides have meaningful limiting and extension behavior at Σ, but this section does not yet use those smooth or continuous extensions to assign seam values to the left-hand reciprocal expressions.


That distinction remains governed by the Book 1 rule that a seam-safe composite does not retroactively regularize singular constituents.


The later harmonic section will study the unique global extension of the signed reciprocal closure as its own derived object.


2.V.F — Symmetry of the double-angle closures


Theorem 2.V.T5 — Symmetry of the UNA sum [D]


Let


S_U(x)=urx(x)+uxp(x)


as temporary descriptive notation within this proof only. Then


S_U(−x)=−S_U(x),


S_U(x+π/2)=−S_U(x),


S_U(x+π)=S_U(x),


S_U(π/2−x)=S_U(x).


Proof.


By Theorem 2.V.T1,


S_U=4/sin 2x.


Apply the double-angle transformation laws


sin(−2x)=−sin 2x,


sin(2x+π)=−sin 2x,


sin(2x+2π)=sin 2x,


sin(π−2x)=sin 2x. ∎


Theorem 2.V.T6 — Symmetry of the UNA product [D]


The product


urx·uxp


is invariant under phase reflection, quarter-turn, half-turn, and complementary reflection.


Proof.


By Theorem 2.V.T3 it equals 4/|sin 2x|. Every listed transformation changes sin 2x by at most a sign, which disappears under absolute value. ∎


Corollary 2.V.C8 — Signed sum and unsigned product transform differently


The sum carries the nontrivial sign response of ε under reflection and quarter-turn.


The product is invariant under the full primitive V₄ symmetry visible on D.


This signed-versus-invariant contrast is the exact algebraic setting from which the FlatWave binary character will emerge.


2.V.G — Boundary growth on the common domain


Theorem 2.V.T7 — Divergence toward every common seam [B]


As x approaches any b_k∈Σ through either adjacent quadrant,


|urx+uxp|→+∞,


urx·uxp→+∞.


The sign of the sum on each side is the corresponding ε sector.


Proof.


At every b_k,


sin 2b_k=0.


Approaching through D gives |sin 2x|→0^+, so both 4/|sin 2x| quantities diverge in magnitude. The signed sum additionally carries sgn(sin 2x)=ε. ∎


Corollary 2.V.C9 — Reciprocal closures vanish at the seam in their extended right-hand forms [B]


As x→b_k through D,


sin 2x/4→0,


|sin 2x|/4→0.


This is a limit statement. A global extension theorem is deferred.


2.V.H — First-climax statement


Theorem 2.V.T8 — Half-angle/double-angle closure theorem [D]


The named R calculus has now established an exact chain


canonical absolute-valued reciprocal primitives

→ reciprocal half-angle atlas

→ canonically signed UNA differences

→ signed and unsigned double-angle closure,


with


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x


and


urx·uxp=4/|sin 2x|.


No additional axiom, imported law, fitted parameter, dimensional calibration, or physical interpretation is required for this chain.


Proof.


The primitive identities are proved in 2.I, the half-angle atlas and rational double-angle formulas in 2.II, the primitive symmetry in 2.III, the canonical UNA definitions and signs in 2.IV, and the two closure identities in the present section. ∎


Negative Theorem 2.V.N1 — Double-angle closure is not a physical field equation


The identities of this section do not establish an equation of motion, wave equation, conservation law, force law, probability law, physical frequency doubling, or harmonic-generation mechanism.


They prove that the named reciprocal-trigonometric combinations are algebraically equivalent to ordinary double-angle trigonometric functions on the declared domain.


2.V.I — Handoff to the second climax


The first climax has produced two quantities with exactly the same magnitude and only one difference: the binary sign ε.


The following dependency step exploits UNA nonvanishing by taking the two individual reciprocals


1/urx,


1/uxp.


Their sum is the historical FlatWave construction. The central question of 2.VI is whether that apparently nontrivial rational expression contains a continuous amplitude or whether it collapses completely to the binary sign already isolated above.

The accompanying product identity urx·uxp=4/|sin 2x| is elevated in the revised manuscript because it exposes the unsigned companion required to understand FlatWave as a sign/magnitude quotient.
