Saw names introduced: no.


Proved in 2.V: the exact UNA double-angle sum; its independent branch-cancellation proof; the half-angle-generator form of the sum; the unsigned UNA product theorem; the exact relation |urx+uxp|=urx·uxp; signed and unsigned reciprocal double-angle forms; symmetry and boundary behavior of both closures; and the complete first-climax chain from the canonical primitive quartet to double-angle structure.


Certified handoff: 2.VI introduces the reciprocal UNA partition, resolves its historical Saw naming only after the formulas are fixed, and then defines FlatWave as their sum. The second climax will prove whether that expression carries more information than the branch sign ε.


2.VI — SECOND CLIMAX: THE RECIPROCAL UNA PARTITION AND THE FLATWAVE BINARY CHARACTER


2.VI.A — Formula first, label second


The UNA functions are nonzero everywhere on D, so their reciprocals exist on the full common domain. Historical materials used more than one numbering convention for the corresponding Saw expressions. The revised manuscript therefore fixes the formulas before permitting any historical alias to influence the notation.


The source T0.II is explicit:


saw_r=1/urx,


saw_x=1/uxp.


These are the canonical Saw labels in the revised theory. Historical labels Saw1 and Saw2 are noncanonical aliases unless a source explicitly states which reciprocal it means.


Definition 2.VI.D1 — Canonical Saw responses [D]


Define


saw_r(x)=1/urx(x),


saw_x(x)=1/uxp(x).


The subscripts r and x are fixed mathematical labels. They carry no physical direction, channel, or measurement meaning.


Definition 2.VI.D2 — Canonical FlatWave [D]


Define


FlatWave(x)=saw_r(x)+saw_x(x).


The capitalization FlatWave is canonical in the revised manuscript. The historical spelling Flatwave is retained only as a source alias.


The name wave does not imply that the function has a continuous amplitude, satisfies a wave equation, propagates, or represents a physical field.


2.VI.B — Rational Saw forms


Theorem 2.VI.T1 — Rational Saw formulas in the primary coordinates [D]


Let


z=srx(x),


w=cxp(x).


Then


saw_r=w/(zw−1),


saw_x=z/(zw−1).


Proof.


Section 2.IV proved


urx=(zw−1)/w,


uxp=(zw−1)/z,


with zw−1≠0 throughout D. Reciprocation gives the displayed formulas. ∎


Corollary 2.VI.C1 — Primitive rational form of FlatWave [D]


For every x∈D,


FlatWave

=(z+w)/(zw−1).


Equivalently,


FlatWave(x)

=

[srx(x)+cxp(x)]/[srx(x)cxp(x)−1].


Proof.


Add the two rational Saw expressions over their common denominator. ∎


This is the historical rational form whose apparently nontrivial amplitude will now be tested.


2.VI.C — The exact binary collapse


Theorem 2.VI.T2 — FlatWave Binary Character Theorem [D]


For every x∈D,


FlatWave(x)=ε(x)=sgn(sin 2x).


Hence


FlatWave(x)∈{−1,+1}.


Proof.


By Definition 2.VI.D2,


FlatWave

=1/urx+1/uxp

=(urx+uxp)/(urx·uxp).


The double-angle closure theorems of 2.V give


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x,


urx·uxp=4/|sin 2x|.


Therefore


FlatWave

=[4/sin 2x]/[4/|sin 2x|]

=|sin 2x|/sin 2x

=sgn(sin 2x)

=ε.


∎


Corollary 2.VI.C2 — Exact ±1 character


For every x∈D,


FlatWave²=1,


|FlatWave|=1,


1/FlatWave=FlatWave.


Thus FlatWave is exactly self-reciprocal and has no values between −1 and +1 on its domain.


Corollary 2.VI.C3 — Quadrant character


On every open quadrant Q_k,


FlatWave(x)=(-1)^k.


Therefore the quadrant sequence is


+1, −1, +1, −1, …


with period π.


Corollary 2.VI.C4 — Flatness on each quadrant [Q]


On every Q_k,


d(FlatWave)/dx=0.


Proof.


FlatWave is constant on each connected quadrant by Corollary 2.VI.C3. ∎


Clarification 2.VI.CL1 — The name FlatWave is mathematically descriptive only in a restricted sense


The function is flat on each quadrant because it is constant there, and its sign alternates from quadrant to quadrant. It is not a smooth oscillatory waveform. Its entire interior value set is the two-point set {−1,+1}.


2.VI.D — FlatWave as the quotient of signed and unsigned double-angle closure


Theorem 2.VI.T3 — Sign/magnitude quotient form [D]


FlatWave is exactly the quotient


signed UNA closure

/

unsigned UNA closure:


FlatWave

=

(urx+uxp)/(urx·uxp)

=

[4/sin 2x]/[4/|sin 2x|].


Thus FlatWave retains the sign sector and discards the common magnitude.


Corollary 2.VI.C5 — FlatWave contains no continuous double-angle magnitude


If two points x,y∈D satisfy


sgn(sin 2x)=sgn(sin 2y),


then


FlatWave(x)=FlatWave(y)


regardless of the values of |sin 2x| and |sin 2y|.


Therefore FlatWave alone cannot reconstruct the continuous double-angle amplitude.


Corollary 2.VI.C6 — FlatWave adds no branch information beyond ε


FlatWave is not a new independent binary generator. It is exactly the already-inherited product-sign branch ε expressed as a derived rational function of the named R operators.


Using Section 2.II,


FlatWave=sgn(srx−1)=sgn(cxp−1)


on D.


This equality is now a theorem about the derived FlatWave, not merely a range statement about ε.


2.VI.E — The signed Saw partition


Theorem 2.VI.T4 — Signed Saw partition of unity [D]


For every x∈D,


saw_r+saw_x=FlatWave=ε.


Because saw_r and saw_x have the same sign ε,


|saw_r|+|saw_x|=1.


Proof.


The signed identity is the definition of FlatWave combined with Theorem 2.VI.T2.


Since urx and uxp have sign ε, their reciprocals saw_r and saw_x also have sign ε. Hence


|saw_r|=ε saw_r,


|saw_x|=ε saw_x.


Adding gives


|saw_r|+|saw_x|

=ε(saw_r+saw_x)

=ε²

=1. ∎


Corollary 2.VI.C7 — Exact boundedness of each Saw response [D]


For every x∈D,


0<|saw_r|<1,


0<|saw_x|<1.


Proof.


Neither Saw response vanishes because neither UNA denominator diverges to an actual infinite value at an interior point. Their positive absolute values sum to 1, so each is strictly between 0 and 1. ∎


Theorem 2.VI.T5 — Saw product [D]


For every x∈D,


saw_r·saw_x=|sin 2x|/4.


Proof.


Reciprocate the UNA product identity


urx·uxp=4/|sin 2x|. ∎


Corollary 2.VI.C8 — Signed sum and positive product determine the unordered Saw pair


The Saw responses are the two real roots of


t²−εt+|sin 2x|/4=0.


Thus the pair has signed sum ε and positive product |sin 2x|/4.


Proof.


For any two numbers a,b,


(t−a)(t−b)=t²−(a+b)t+ab.


Set


a=saw_r,


b=saw_x.


Using


saw_r+saw_x=ε


and


saw_r·saw_x=|sin 2x|/4,


we obtain


(t−saw_r)(t−saw_x)

=t²−εt+|sin 2x|/4.


Hence saw_r and saw_x are exactly the two real roots. ∎


This is an algebraic statement only. No probability or conservation interpretation is assigned.


2.VI.F — Exact symmetry character


Theorem 2.VI.T6 — FlatWave transformation laws [D]


For every x∈D,


FlatWave(−x)=−FlatWave(x),


FlatWave(x+π/2)=−FlatWave(x),


FlatWave(x+π)=FlatWave(x),


FlatWave(π/2−x)=FlatWave(x).


Proof.


Use


FlatWave=sgn(sin 2x)


and the double-angle transformation laws already established for ε. ∎


Theorem 2.VI.T7 — Nontrivial one-dimensional sign character of the primitive V₄ symmetry [D]


The multiplier attached to the induced primitive symmetries is


identity → +1,


phase reflection ι → −1,


quarter-turn τ_{π/2} → −1,


complementary reflection ι_d → +1.


This multiplier map is a group homomorphism


V₄→{−1,+1}


under multiplication. FlatWave transforms by this nontrivial one-dimensional real character:


FlatWave(gx)=χ_F(g)FlatWave(x).


Proof.


Each nonidentity primitive symmetry has order two. The complementary reflection is the product of phase reflection and quarter-turn at the induced V₄ level. Their multipliers satisfy


(−1)(−1)=+1,


which is the multiplier of ι_d. Multiplication by the identity leaves the multiplier unchanged, and squaring any multiplier gives +1. Therefore the assignment is a homomorphism. The covariance equation is exactly Theorem 2.VI.T6 written in group form. ∎


Corollary 2.VI.C9 — The word character is now exact


FlatWave is not itself a group homomorphism from phase space. Rather, its transformation law carries the nontrivial sign character χ_F of the induced primitive V₄ symmetry.


This is the precise mathematical sense in which FlatWave has a ±1 character.


2.VI.G — Seam behavior: sign reversal without a seam value


Theorem 2.VI.T8 — Opposite one-sided FlatWave limits at every common seam [B]


For every seam b_k∈Σ,


lim_{x→b_k^-}FlatWave(x)

=−lim_{x→b_k^+}FlatWave(x),


and each one-sided limit belongs to {−1,+1}.


More explicitly,


lim_{x→b_k^-}FlatWave(x)=(-1)^{k-1},


lim_{x→b_k^+}FlatWave(x)=(-1)^k.


Proof.


The left side lies in Q_{k-1} and the right side in Q_k. Apply the quadrant character. ∎


Negative Theorem 2.VI.N1 — No continuous real extension of FlatWave through a seam


FlatWave has no continuous real-valued extension to any b_k∈Σ.


Proof.


The two one-sided limits exist but are opposite. Therefore no single seam value can equal both limits. ∎


Clarification 2.VI.CL2 — Defining sgn(0)=0 would be a convention, not a continuous extension


One may define the standard signum function at zero by assigning 0, but that assignment is not continuous at the seam and is not forced by the present FlatWave domain. The revised operator remains defined on D unless a later construction explicitly chooses another codomain or boundary convention.


2.VI.H — Saw symmetry


Theorem 2.VI.T9 — Saw transformation laws [D]


Under phase reflection,


saw_r(−x)=−saw_x(x),


saw_x(−x)=−saw_r(x).


Under quarter-turn,


saw_r(x+π/2)=−saw_r(x),


saw_x(x+π/2)=−saw_x(x).


Under half-turn,


saw_r(x+π)=saw_r(x),


saw_x(x+π)=saw_x(x).


Under complementary reflection,


saw_r(π/2−x)=saw_x(x),


saw_x(π/2−x)=saw_r(x).


Proof.


Reciprocate the UNA transformation laws of Section 2.IV. ∎


Corollary 2.VI.C10 — FlatWave symmetry follows from the Saw partition as well as from sin 2x


Adding the transformed Saw responses reproduces Theorem 2.VI.T6 directly from the named R calculus, independently of substituting the final trigonometric form.


2.VI.I — The same-phase cross-family constraint


The FlatWave theorem now converts the primitive rational expression into an exact algebraic relation between the two positive same-phase primary coordinates.


Theorem 2.VI.T10 — FlatWave cross-family equation [D]


For


z=srx(x),


w=cxp(x),


we have


(z+w)/(zw−1)=ε.


Equivalently,


z+w=ε(zw−1).


Proof.


Corollary 2.VI.C1 gives


FlatWave=(z+w)/(zw−1),


while Theorem 2.VI.T2 gives


FlatWave=ε. ∎


Corollary 2.VI.C11 — This is a same-phase relation


Unlike the symmetry-orbit identity


cxp(x)=srx(π/2−x),


the FlatWave equation relates z=srx(x) and w=cxp(x) evaluated at the same phase x.


It is therefore the first equation in the revised dependency chain capable of eliminating one primary value algebraically in favor of the other.


Clarification 2.VI.CL3 — The Möbius solution is deliberately deferred


The present section establishes the exact equation


z+w=ε(zw−1)


but does not solve it for w. That algebraic solution is the third climax of Book 2 and deserves its own section because it converts the FlatWave binary character into the branch datum of a Möbius involution.


2.VI.J — What FlatWave proves and what it does not


The second climax proves something simultaneously strong and limited.


Strong:


• the historical rational FlatWave expression collapses exactly;


• its only values on D are −1 and +1;


• it is constant on every quadrant;


• its sign alternates at every seam;


• it transforms by a nontrivial sign character of the induced V₄ symmetry;


• and it supplies the same-phase algebraic constraint needed for Möbius reduction.


Limited:


• FlatWave does not encode position within a quadrant;


• it does not recover |sin 2x|;


• it does not recover the half-turn branch;


• it does not define a seam-crossing rule;


• and it is not an independent binary datum once z=srx is known, because FlatWave=sgn(z−1).


Negative Theorem 2.VI.N2 — Binary character does not imply binary physical state


The theorem


FlatWave∈{−1,+1}


does not establish a qubit, spin-1/2 state, particle/antiparticle pair, parity-violating sector, physical chirality, Boolean ontology, or two-world interpretation.


Those would require additional mathematical structures and projection contracts not present in Book 2.


Book 2, Section 2.VI — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: UNA nonvanishing and symmetry from 2.IV; signed and unsigned double-angle closure from 2.V; primitive rational coordinates z,w from 2.II–2.IV; and the induced V₄ symmetry from 2.III.


Source provenance: T0.II fixes the historical formulas saw_r=1/urx and saw_x=1/uxp, defines Flatwave as their sum, proves Flatwave=ε, and derives the rational form (z+w)/(zw−1). The revised section preserves those supported formulas, adopts FlatWave as the canonical capitalization, explicitly rejects ambiguous Saw1/Saw2 numbering as canonical notation, and expands the result into the exact binary, flatness, symmetry-character, boundary, information-loss, and same-phase-constraint theorems justified by the already-proved revised framework.


New Projection-specific names activated: saw_r, saw_x, FlatWave.


New group symbol activated: χ_F for the nontrivial one-dimensional sign character carried by FlatWave under the induced V₄ action.


New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical wave claims: 0.


Proved in 2.VI: the canonical Saw formulas; the primitive rational FlatWave form; FlatWave=ε=sgn(sin 2x); exact ±1 range, self-reciprocity, and quadrant flatness; the signed and unsigned Saw partitions; the Saw product; the complete FlatWave and Saw symmetry laws; the nontrivial V₄ sign character; impossibility of continuous real seam extension; and the exact same-phase cross-family equation


(z+w)/(zw−1)=ε.


Certified handoff: 2.VII is the third climax. It will solve the FlatWave cross-family equation, prove


w=(z+ε)/(εz−1),


show that the branch map is a Möbius involution, and then determine whether the apparent two-primary description really reduces to one continuous generator at a fixed phase.


2.VII — THIRD CLIMAX: FLATWAVE-CONTROLLED MÖBIUS REDUCTION AND ONE-GENERATOR CLOSURE


2.VII.A — From binary character to same-phase reduction


The first climax exposed the double angle. The second showed that FlatWave retains only its binary sign sector. The third now uses that sign as the branch datum in the exact same-phase relation


z+w=ε(zw−1),


where


z=srx(x)>0,


w=cxp(x)>0,


ε=FlatWave(x)∈{−1,+1}.


Unlike the earlier symmetry-orbit identity cxp(x)=srx(π/2−x), this equation contains both primary values at the same phase. It can therefore be solved algebraically to determine whether the apparent two-primary representation is genuinely two-dimensional or reducible.


2.VII.B — Möbius denominator and branch ranges


Lemma 2.VII.L1 — Möbius denominator nonvanishing [D]


For every x∈D,


εz−1≠0.


Proof.


Section 2.II proved that


ε=+1 ⇔ z∈(1,+∞),


ε=−1 ⇔ z∈(0,1).


If ε=+1, then εz−1=z−1>0.


If ε=−1, then εz−1=−z−1<0.


Thus the denominator cannot vanish on either admissible branch. ∎


Corollary 2.VII.C1 — Companion denominator nonvanishing [D]


For every x∈D,


z+ε≠0.


Proof.


If ε=+1, z+1>0. If ε=−1, z−1<0 because 0<z<1. ∎


2.VII.C — The Möbius cross-family theorem


Definition 2.VII.D1 — Branch Möbius map


For fixed ε∈{−1,+1}, define


M_ε(z)=(z+ε)/(εz−1)


on the corresponding admissible interval


I_+=(1,+∞),


I_-=(0,1).


Theorem 2.VII.T1 — Exact same-phase Möbius reduction [D]


For every x∈D,


w=M_ε(z),


that is,


cxp(x)=

[srx(x)+FlatWave(x)]

/

[FlatWave(x)srx(x)−1].


Equivalently, using ε,


cxp=(srx+ε)/(ε·srx−1).


Proof.


Start from the FlatWave cross-family equation


z+w=ε(zw−1).


Expand:


z+w=εzw−ε.


Collect the terms containing w:


w(εz−1)=z+ε.


Division is valid by Lemma 2.VII.L1, giving


w=(z+ε)/(εz−1)=M_ε(z). ∎


Corollary 2.VII.C2 — Explicit two-branch forms


On ε=+1 quadrants,


cxp=(srx+1)/(srx−1),


with srx>1.


On ε=−1 quadrants,


cxp=(1−srx)/(1+srx),


with 0<srx<1.


Both formulas return a positive cxp in the same primary range as srx on the corresponding branch.


Corollary 2.VII.C3 — Reciprocal companion


For every x∈D,


crx=(εz−1)/(z+ε).


Proof.


Use crx=1/cxp and Theorem 2.VII.T1. ∎


2.VII.D — Why the map is Möbius and why it is an involution


Theorem 2.VII.T2 — Möbius involution [D]


For fixed ε and every admissible z,


M_ε(M_ε(z))=z.


Proof I — Symmetry of the FlatWave equation.


The equation


(z+w)/(zw−1)=ε


is symmetric in z and w. Solving for w gives


w=M_ε(z).


Solving the same equation for z gives


z=M_ε(w).


Therefore


M_ε(M_ε(z))=z. ∎


Proof II — Direct fractional-linear calculation.


Let


y=M_ε(z)=(z+ε)/(εz−1).


Because ε²=1,


y+ε

=[z+ε+ε(εz−1)]/(εz−1)

=2z/(εz−1),


while


εy−1

=[ε(z+ε)−(εz−1)]/(εz−1)

=2/(εz−1).


Therefore


M_ε(y)

=(y+ε)/(εy−1)

=[2z/(εz−1)]/[2/(εz−1)]

=z.


The denominators are nonzero on the admissible branch by Lemma 2.VII.L1 and the branch-preservation theorem below. ∎


Clarification 2.VII.CL1 — Projective matrix witness


The fractional-linear map M_ε is represented, up to nonzero scalar multiple, by the matrix


[ 1   ε ]

[ ε  −1 ].


Its square is 2 times the identity matrix. Explicitly,


[ 1   ε ] [ 1   ε ]

[ ε  −1 ] [ ε  −1 ]

=

[ 1+ε²    ε−ε ]

[ ε−ε      ε²+1 ]

=

[ 2  0 ]

[ 0  2 ]

=2I,


because ε²=1. Multiplication of a Möbius matrix by a nonzero scalar does not change its projective map, so the induced projective action has order two. This is a compact matrix witness of the same involution proved above and introduces no new dependency.


Theorem 2.VII.T3 — Branch preservation and strict reversal of order


M_+ maps (1,+∞) bijectively onto (1,+∞).


M_- maps (0,1) bijectively onto (0,1).


On either branch,


dM_ε/dz=−2/(εz−1)²<0.


Therefore each branch map is strictly decreasing.


Proof.


For ε=+1,


M_+(z)=(z+1)/(z−1)=1+2/(z−1)>1


for z>1.


For ε=−1,


M_-(z)=(1−z)/(1+z),


which lies strictly between 0 and 1 for 0<z<1.


The derivative follows by the quotient rule:


M_ε'(z)

=[(εz−1)−ε(z+ε)]/(εz−1)²

=−2/(εz−1)²<0.


Because M_ε is an involution, injectivity and surjectivity on each preserved interval follow automatically. ∎


2.VII.E — Möbius realization of complementary reflection


Theorem 2.VII.T4 — Complementary reflection in the z coordinate [D]


Let


z(x)=srx(x).


Then


z(π/2−x)=M_ε(z(x)),


where ε is unchanged by complementary reflection.


Proof.


Section 2.III proved


srx(π/2−x)=cxp(x).


Theorem 2.VII.T1 gives


cxp(x)=M_ε(srx(x)).


Combining the two identities gives the result. ∎


Corollary 2.VII.C4 — The Möbius involution is the coordinate image of an involutive phase symmetry


Complementary reflection satisfies


ι_d²=id


and preserves ε. In the positive half-angle coordinate z, this same involution is represented by M_ε.


Thus the third climax does not invent an unrelated algebraic transformation: it identifies the exact fractional-linear coordinate form of a symmetry already present at the primitive layer.


2.VII.F — Fixed points and the quadrant midpoint


Theorem 2.VII.T5 — Unique positive fixed point on each branch


The equation


M_ε(z)=z


has exactly one positive admissible solution:


z_*=√2+ε.


Thus


ε=+1:


z_*=1+√2,


ε=−1:


z_*=√2−1.


Proof.


Set


z=(z+ε)/(εz−1).


Then


εz²−2z−ε=0.


Multiplying by ε gives


z²−2εz−1=0.


Hence


z=ε±√2.


Exactly one root is positive on the corresponding admissible interval, namely


z=√2+ε. ∎


Corollary 2.VII.C5 — Möbius fixed point equals the half-angle midpoint value


Section 2.II proved that at the quadrant midpoint


m_k=(2k+1)π/4,


srx(m_k)=cxp(m_k)=√2+ε.


Therefore the unique positive fixed point of M_ε is exactly the point where the two same-phase primary coordinates cross.


This connects three independently established structures:


• half-angle midpoint geometry;


• complementary-reflection fixed phase;


• Möbius fixed-point algebra.


2.VII.G — FlatWave is not an independent Möbius input


The Möbius formula appears to depend on one continuous scalar z and one binary sign ε. But Section 2.II already proved that ε is encoded by the range of z.


Theorem 2.VII.T6 — Internal branch selection from the generator [D]


For every x∈D,


ε=FlatWave=sgn(z−1).


Therefore the applicable Möbius branch is determined by z itself:


z>1 selects M_+,


0<z<1 selects M_-.


Proof.


Section 2.II proved


ε=sgn(z−1).


Section 2.VI independently proved


FlatWave=ε.


Combining them gives the result. ∎


Corollary 2.VII.C6 — FlatWave is a derived branch readout, not a second generator


Once z=srx is known, FlatWave supplies no additional independent information for the reconstruction of the named system through 2.VI.


Its importance is structural rather than dimensional: it exposes which of the two Möbius branch formulas the generator already occupies.


2.VII.H — Complete reconstruction through FlatWave from z


Theorem 2.VII.T7 — One-scalar reconstruction formulas [D]


Let


z=srx>0,


z≠1,


and define


ε=sgn(z−1).


Then every named operator introduced through 2.VI is determined by z:


srx=z,


sxp=1/z,


cxp=(z+ε)/(εz−1),


crx=(εz−1)/(z+ε),


urx=(z²+1)/(z+ε),


uxp=(z²+1)/[z(εz−1)],


saw_r=(z+ε)/(z²+1),


saw_x=z(εz−1)/(z²+1),


FlatWave=ε.


Proof.


The primitive formulas follow from reciprocal pairing and Theorem 2.VII.T1.


For urx,


urx=z−crx

=z−(εz−1)/(z+ε)

=[z(z+ε)−(εz−1)]/(z+ε)

=(z²+1)/(z+ε).


For uxp,


uxp=cxp−1/z

=(z+ε)/(εz−1)−1/z

=[z(z+ε)−(εz−1)]/[z(εz−1)]

=(z²+1)/[z(εz−1)].


The Saw formulas follow by reciprocation. FlatWave=ε was proved in 2.VI, and ε itself is determined by z by Theorem 2.VII.T6. ∎


Corollary 2.VII.C7 — Rational closure on each fixed quadrant


On a fixed open quadrant Q_k, ε is constant. Therefore every named operator through FlatWave is a rational function of the single nonconstant scalar z=srx with coefficients in the constants together with the fixed branch value ε=±1.


Across all of D, the same reconstruction is piecewise rational, with the branch selected internally by the threshold z=1.


2.VII.I — Exact one-generator minimality


Definition 2.VII.D2 — Generator task for the named Book 2 system through FlatWave


On a fixed open quadrant, a nonconstant scalar g is a generator for the named system through 2.VI when every one of


srx, sxp, cxp, crx, urx, uxp, saw_r, saw_x, FlatWave


can be reconstructed from g using finitely many arithmetic operations together with branch constants already fixed on that quadrant.


The branch constant does not count as a continuous generator.


Theorem 2.VII.T8 — Sufficiency of one nonconstant generator [Q]


The scalar


z=srx


is sufficient to generate the complete named system through FlatWave on every open quadrant.


Proof.


Theorem 2.VII.T7 gives explicit reconstruction formulas for every named member. On a fixed quadrant ε is constant, and therefore every expression is rational in z. ∎


Theorem 2.VII.T9 — Necessity of a nonconstant generator [Q]


Zero nonconstant scalar generators cannot generate the named system on any open quadrant.


Proof.


Any expression built from constants alone by finitely many arithmetic operations remains constant wherever defined.


But Section 2.II proved that srx is strictly monotone, hence nonconstant, on every open quadrant. Since srx belongs to the system being generated, constants alone cannot generate the system. ∎


Theorem 2.VII.T10 — Exact one-generator theorem [Q]


Exactly one nonconstant scalar generator is necessary and sufficient for the complete named operator system through FlatWave on every open quadrant.


Proof.


Sufficiency is Theorem 2.VII.T8. Necessity is Theorem 2.VII.T9. ∎


Corollary 2.VII.C8 — Apparent two-primary structure collapses at fixed phase


The pair


z=srx,


w=cxp


does not contain two independent continuous scalar generators at the same phase. The Möbius relation determines w from z, and the branch sign required by that relation is already encoded by the range of z.


Corollary 2.VII.C9 — The derived layers add vocabulary, not continuous dimension


The UNA differences, Saw responses, and FlatWave are deterministic algebraic readouts of the same one-generator system. They expose different invariants and symmetries but do not increase the continuous generator count.


2.VII.J — One generator does not mean full phase recovery


Negative Theorem 2.VII.N1 — One-generator closure does not recover the half-turn branch


The generator z determines the complete named system through FlatWave but does not distinguish x from x+π.


Proof.


Every named operator used in Theorem 2.VII.T7 is π-periodic. Therefore the reconstructed values are identical at x and x+π. Yet those phases are distinct modulo 2π. ∎


Corollary 2.VII.C10 — Continuous generator count and discrete lift data remain distinct


To reconstruct the signed unit-circle point, one additional discrete half-turn label such as


α=sgn(sin x)


is sufficient, as proved in 2.II.


That label is not a second continuous generator.


Negative Theorem 2.VII.N2 — One-generator mathematics does not imply one physical degree of freedom


The exact algebraic reduction does not establish that physical space, spacetime, a quantum state space, a field theory, or the universe has one degree of freedom.


It establishes only the minimal generator count of the named reciprocal-trigonometric operator family for the declared reconstruction task.


2.VII.K — Third-climax theorem


Theorem 2.VII.T11 — Half-angle/FlatWave/Möbius synthesis [D]


The three central reductions of Book 2 now form one exact chain:


1. Half-angle coordinate:


z=srx


is a positive coordinate for phase modulo π on its natural sine domain.


2. Double-angle closure:


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x.


3. Binary FlatWave:


FlatWave=sgn(sin 2x)=ε∈{−1,+1}.


4. Möbius same-phase reduction:


cxp=M_ε(srx)

=

(srx+ε)/(ε·srx−1).


5. One-generator closure:


ε=sgn(srx−1),


so every named primitive, UNA, Saw, and FlatWave quantity is determined by the single positive nonconstant scalar srx.


No additional Projection-specific mathematical axiom is required at any step.


Proof.


Items 1–5 are respectively the results of 2.II, 2.V, 2.VI, and the present section. ∎


2.VII.L — Non-scope


The Möbius involution is a fractional-linear coordinate relation. It is not yet a Lorentz transformation, gauge transformation, scattering map, transfer matrix, physical duality, or dynamical evolution law.


The fixed points √2±1 are exact mathematical constants of the branch maps. Their occurrence does not establish a measured physical constant or harmonic calibration.


The fact that FlatWave selects the Möbius branch does not give FlatWave an independent ontological status, because its value is already recoverable from z.


Book 2, Section 2.VII — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: half-angle generator ranges and midpoint values from 2.II; primitive complementary reflection from 2.III; FlatWave cross-family equation from 2.VI; reciprocal primitive structure from 2.I; and standard fractional-linear algebra.


Source provenance: T0.II explicitly derives


w=(z+ε)/(εz−1),


proves the branch Möbius involution, reconstructs the primitive and derived operator layers from z, and proves exact one-generator minimality on each open quadrant. The revised section preserves those supported results while making the conceptual bridge sharper: M_ε is identified as the z-coordinate realization of complementary reflection, its fixed point is tied to the already-proved quadrant midpoint, and FlatWave is shown to select a branch that is itself already encoded by the generator range.


New mathematical notation activated: M_ε for the branch Möbius involution; I_+=(1,+∞) and I_-=(0,1) for its two admissible positive intervals.


New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

2.VIII — HARMONIC EXTRACTION: THE SMOOTH DOUBLE-ANGLE CARRIER BENEATH THE UNA SINGULARITY


2.VIII.A — Restricted reciprocal kernel


The UNA sum diverges at every common seam, yet its reciprocal has already simplified on D to an ordinary double-angle function. The present section treats that reciprocal as a derived object in its own right rather than attempting to assign values backward to the singular factors from which it was formed.


Definition 2.VIII.D1 — Reciprocal UNA-sum kernel [D]


Define on D


H_D(x)=1/[urx(x)+uxp(x)].


The subscript D records that this expression is initially defined only where the UNA operators are defined.


Theorem 2.VIII.T1 — Exact harmonic extraction on D [D]


For every x∈D,


H_D(x)=sin 2x/4.


Proof.


Section 2.V proved


urx+uxp=4/sin 2x.


Since sin 2x≠0 on D, reciprocation gives


H_D=sin 2x/4. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C1 — Sign and range of the restricted kernel [D]


For every x∈D,


sgn(H_D)=ε=FlatWave,


and


0<|H_D|≤1/4.


The maximum magnitude 1/4 occurs exactly at the quadrant midpoints m_k, where |sin 2x|=1.


Proof.


The sign and range follow immediately from H_D=sin 2x/4 and the FlatWave theorem. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C2 — Signed versus binary double-angle data [D]


On D,


FlatWave=sgn(H_D),


while


|H_D|=|sin 2x|/4=1/(urx·uxp).


Thus FlatWave retains only the sign sector of H_D, whereas H_D retains both sign and continuous magnitude.


2.VIII.B — Unique smooth extension through every primitive seam


Definition 2.VIII.D2 — Extended harmonic coordinate [R]


Define for every real x


H(x)=sin 2x/4.


Theorem 2.VIII.T2 — Smooth extension theorem [R]


H is a smooth extension of H_D from D to the full real phase line.


Proof.


H and H_D agree on D by Theorem 2.VIII.T1. The function sin 2x/4 is smooth on all of ℝ. ∎


Theorem 2.VIII.T3 — Uniqueness of the continuous extension [R]


H is the unique continuous real-valued extension of H_D from D to ℝ.


Proof.


The set D is dense in ℝ. Let G be any continuous extension of H_D. For any seam point b∈Σ, choose a sequence x_n∈D with x_n→b. Then


G(b)

=lim G(x_n)

=lim H_D(x_n)

=lim sin(2x_n)/4

=sin(2b)/4

=H(b).


The two extensions already agree on D, so they agree everywhere. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C3 — Exact seam value of the extended harmonic coordinate [B]


For every b_k∈Σ,


H(b_k)=0.


This is a value of H, the extended derived coordinate. It is not a value assigned to urx, uxp, or any primitive operator.


Negative Theorem 2.VIII.N1 — Extension does not commute with inversion through zero


The smooth value H(b_k)=0 does not imply that


urx(b_k)+uxp(b_k)


is an ordinary finite reciprocal of zero.


Proof.


The expression urx+uxp is not defined at b_k. The extension is performed only after its reciprocal has simplified on D to the smooth function sin 2x/4. A continuous extension of the reciprocal composite does not define the singular preimage factors at the excluded point. ∎


2.VIII.C — Derivative coordinate and harmonic closure


Definition 2.VIII.D3 — Derivative harmonic coordinate [R]


Define


V(x)=H'(x).


Theorem 2.VIII.T4 — Explicit derivative coordinate [R]


For every real x,


V(x)=cos 2x/2.


Proof.


Differentiate H=sin 2x/4 by the chain rule. ∎


Theorem 2.VIII.T5 — First-order harmonic system [R]


For every real x,


H'=V,


V'=−4H.


Proof.


The first equation is Definition 2.VIII.D3. Differentiating V=cos 2x/2 gives


V'=−sin 2x=−4H. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C4 — Second-order harmonic identities [R]


For every real x,


H''+4H=0,


V''+4V=0.


These are identities in the dimensionless phase coordinate x. They are not physical equations of motion.


2.VIII.D — Carrier ellipse and unit-circle normalization


Definition 2.VIII.D4 — Carrier ellipse


Define


C_car={(h,v)∈ℝ² : v²+4h²=1/4}.


Theorem 2.VIII.T6 — Carrier ellipse identity [R]


For every real x,


V(x)²+4H(x)²=1/4.


Proof.


Substitute


V=cos 2x/2,


H=sin 2x/4.


Then


V²+4H²

=cos²2x/4+sin²2x/4

=1/4. ∎


Definition 2.VIII.D5 — Normalized harmonic coordinates


Define


U=4H,


W=2V.


Corollary 2.VIII.C5 — Normalized unit circle [R]


For every real x,


U=sin 2x,


W=cos 2x,


and


U²+W²=1.


Thus the linear normalization


(h,v)↦(4h,2v)


maps C_car bijectively onto the ordinary unit circle.


Clarification 2.VIII.CL1 — Two displayed coordinates, one continuous dimension


The pair (H,V) has two displayed real components but satisfies one independent constraint. The carrier ellipse is one-dimensional. Its two-component display supplies a globally smooth Euclidean embedding; it does not create a second independent continuous generator.


This is an explicit Book 2 instance of the representation-dimension firewall proved in Book 1.


2.VIII.E — The smooth carrier map


Definition 2.VIII.D6 — Carrier map


Define


F:ℝ→C_car


by


F(x)=(H(x),V(x))

=(sin 2x/4, cos 2x/2).


Theorem 2.VIII.T7 — Smooth regularity of the carrier map [R]


F is smooth on ℝ and has no critical point.


Proof.


Differentiate:


F'(x)=(V,−4H).


If F'(x)=0, then H=0 and V=0 simultaneously. That contradicts


V²+4H²=1/4.


Therefore F' never vanishes. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C6 — Constant normalized phase-parameter speed [R]


In normalized coordinates (U,W),


U'=2W,


W'=−2U,


and


(U')²+(W')²=4.


Thus the normalized carrier is traversed at constant derivative magnitude 2 with respect to x.


Proof.


From


U=sin 2x,


W=cos 2x,


we have


U'=2W,


W'=−2U.


Hence


(U')²+(W')²

=4(W²+U²)

=4


by the normalized unit-circle identity. Therefore the derivative magnitude is exactly 2. ∎


Clarification 2.VIII.CL2 — Parameter speed is not physical velocity


The preceding statement concerns differentiation with respect to the dimensionless phase coordinate x. It does not identify x with time or the derivative magnitude with a physical speed.


2.VIII.F — Exact fibers and the half-period quotient


Theorem 2.VIII.T8 — Exact carrier fiber theorem [R]


For real x and y,


F(x)=F(y)


if and only if


x−y∈πℤ.


Proof.


Equality F(x)=F(y) is equivalent to simultaneous equality


sin 2x=sin 2y,


cos 2x=cos 2y.


Equality of both circular coordinates means


2x≡2y mod 2π,


hence


x≡y mod π.


The converse follows immediately from π-periodicity. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C7 — Complete carrier representation modulo π


The carrier ellipse C_car is a complete static representation of phase modulo π.


It is not complete for phase modulo 2π, the lifted real phase, or winding history.


Corollary 2.VIII.C8 — Relation to the one-generator theorem


On D, the scalar generator z=srx and the carrier point F(x) encode the same continuous phase modulo π, but they solve different representation tasks.


The scalar z is injective on its punctured half-period chart and cannot include the sine-zero seam as a finite ordinary value.


The pair (H,V) is globally smooth across every seam but uses two constrained displayed coordinates.


No contradiction exists: generator minimality and seam-safe global representation are different minimality tasks.


2.VIII.G — Seam classification by the derivative coordinate


Theorem 2.VIII.T9 — Exact carrier seam classifier [B]


At every seam b_k=kπ/2,


H(b_k)=0.


Moreover,


if b_k∈Σ_s, then


V(b_k)=+1/2,


while if b_k∈Σ_c, then


V(b_k)=−1/2.


Proof.


At every seam, sin 2b_k=0. At sine-zero seams b=kπ,


cos 2b=1,


so V=+1/2.


At cosine-zero seams b=π/2+kπ,


cos 2b=−1,


so V=−1/2. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C9 — Seam class survives smooth gluing


The extended carrier distinguishes the two analytic seam classes by the sign of V:


(H,V)=(0,+1/2) at sine-zero seams,


(H,V)=(0,−1/2) at cosine-zero seams.


It does not identify which translated seam number k has been reached. That information belongs to the lifted phase or a history record.


Corollary 2.VIII.C10 — Transverse crossing of H=0 in the carrier curve [B]


At every seam b_k,


F'(b_k)=(V(b_k),0),


with V(b_k)=±1/2.


Hence the smooth carrier curve crosses the line H=0 with nonzero tangent rather than developing a critical point there.


This is a statement about the derived carrier curve, not a physical seam process.


2.VIII.H — FlatWave versus the smooth carrier


Theorem 2.VIII.T10 — FlatWave is the sign projection of H [D]


For every x∈D,


FlatWave(x)=sgn(H(x)).


Proof.


H=sin 2x/4 and FlatWave=sgn(sin 2x). Multiplication by the positive constant 1/4 does not change sign. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C11 — FlatWave collapses each open half-ellipse to one bit


Every carrier point with H>0 maps to


FlatWave=+1,


and every carrier point with H<0 maps to


FlatWave=−1.


Therefore FlatWave identifies continuously many distinct carrier points in each open half-ellipse.


At the two seam points H=0, FlatWave remains undefined in the present raw domain while the carrier pair remains finite and distinguishes the seam class through V.


Corollary 2.VIII.C12 — Three levels of double-angle information


The revised calculus now contains three nested representations of the same underlying π-periodic double-angle phase:


1. full carrier:


(H,V)=(sin 2x/4, cos 2x/2),


which is complete modulo π and smooth at seams;


2. scalar harmonic coordinate:


H=sin 2x/4,


which is smooth but not injective on the complete carrier loop;


3. binary character:


FlatWave=sgn(H),


which retains only the sign half of the carrier and is undefined at H=0 on the raw domain.


This hierarchy is information-theoretic and mathematical. No physical hierarchy is implied.


2.VIII.I — Rational carrier formulas in the half-angle generator


The half-angle generator of 2.II and the harmonic carrier are exact reparameterizations of the same π-periodic phase information away from the generator’s omitted sine seam.


Theorem 2.VIII.T11 — Carrier coordinates from z=srx [D_s]


Let


z=srx>0.


Then


H=

z(z²−1)/(z²+1)²,


and


V=

(z⁴−6z²+1)/[2(z²+1)²].


Proof.


Section 2.II proved


sin 2x=4z(z²−1)/(z²+1)²,


cos 2x=(z⁴−6z²+1)/(z²+1)².


Apply H=sin 2x/4 and V=cos 2x/2. ∎


Corollary 2.VIII.C13 — Generator threshold gives the cosine-zero carrier seam


At z=1,


H=0,


V=−1/2.


This is the cosine-zero seam point, which is regular for the sine-channel generator z.


Corollary 2.VIII.C14 — Sine-zero carrier seam appears at the ends of the positive z chart


As z→0^+ or z→+∞,


H→0,


V→+1/2.


Thus the two ends of the positive half-angle coordinate chart approach the same sine-zero carrier point on the π quotient.


This is the explicit chart manifestation of why a single positive real coordinate cannot represent the complete closed carrier loop continuously and injectively without a cut or endpoint identification.


2.VIII.J — Seam-safe and seam-unsafe information


Theorem 2.VIII.T12 — Seam-safe carrier data [R]


The pair (H,V) is finite and smooth at every real phase, including every primitive seam.


At a seam its value is one of exactly two points:


(0,+1/2),


(0,−1/2).


Theorem 2.VIII.T13 — Smooth carrier does not regularize raw operators [B]


The existence of finite H and V at a seam does not supply finite values for


srx, sxp, cxp, crx, urx, uxp,


or FlatWave there.


Proof.


The primitive and UNA objects retain their own domains and one-sided boundary behavior. H is obtained only after a specific reciprocal combination simplifies to a smooth function. The mapping from singular raw data to H is not invertible at H=0, and FlatWave has opposite one-sided signs. Therefore the carrier extension cannot be used backward to define the raw seam state. ∎


Negative Theorem 2.VIII.N2 — No seam physics from carrier smoothness


Carrier smoothness does not derive passage, reflection, reset, relabeling, collapse, rebirth, termination, a finite transition duration, or any other physical process at a seam.


The carrier provides a smooth mathematical continuation of the double-angle coordinate only.


2.VIII.K — Harmonic extraction theorem


Theorem 2.VIII.T14 — Continuous carrier beneath the singular reciprocal layer [R]


The named R calculus contains a uniquely determined smooth double-angle carrier obtained from the singular UNA layer by the sequence


urx+uxp

→ reciprocal on D

→ H_D=sin 2x/4

→ unique smooth extension H on ℝ

→ derivative coordinate V=cos 2x/2

→ constrained carrier ellipse V²+4H²=1/4.


The carrier is smooth and regular across every primitive seam, represents phase exactly modulo π, and contains one continuous degree of freedom.


FlatWave is the discontinuous binary sign projection of this carrier on D:


FlatWave=sgn(H).


None of these statements introduces a physical wave, time evolution, field, or seam process.


Proof.


The chain is established by Theorems 2.VIII.T1–T13. ∎


Book 2, Section 2.VIII — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: the UNA double-angle sum and product from 2.V; FlatWave from 2.VI; the one-generator half-angle formulas from 2.II and 2.VII; Book 1 extension uniqueness and seam grammar; and ordinary differentiation and circle topology.


Source provenance: T0.IV explicitly defines H_D=1/(urx+uxp), proves H_D=sin 2x/4, extends it uniquely and smoothly to H on ℝ, defines V=H', proves the first-order harmonic system, carrier ellipse, smooth regularity, exact π-fibers, and seam classification. The revised section re-derives exactly those portions that depend only on material already admitted into revised Books 1–2. New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Certified handoff: 2.IX develops the differential and integral calculus of the named primitives themselves. In particular, it will derive the autonomous Riccati equations


srx'=−(srx²+1)/2,


cxp'=+(cxp²+1)/2


on their natural smooth components, together with reciprocal-partner equations, exact antiderivative structure where useful, and asymptotic behavior near the channel seams.


monotonicity; realization of complementary reflection in the z coordinate; unique positive fixed points √2+ε and their identification with quadrant midpoint values; internal recovery of the FlatWave branch from z; complete reconstruction of every named operator through FlatWave from z; and exact one-generator minimality.


The three planned climaxes are now complete.

H=1/(urx+uxp)=sin 2x/4,


prove its unique smooth extension through every seam, introduce its complementary derivative coordinate if justified, and contrast the continuous double-angle carrier with the discontinuous binary FlatWave character without assigning either a physical wave interpretation.


2.IX — DIFFERENTIAL, INTEGRAL, AND ASYMPTOTIC CLOSURE OF THE PRIMITIVE QUARTET


2.IX.A — Scope of the calculus layer


The preceding sections established the primitive quartet algebraically, exposed its half-angle charts, derived the UNA and FlatWave closures, reduced the same-phase system by Möbius transformation, and extracted a smooth harmonic carrier. The present section returns to the four primitive functions themselves and asks what differential and integral structure follows from their canonical definitions.


The answer is unusually compact. Each reciprocal-conjugate primitive satisfies an autonomous Riccati equation on its maximal natural channel domain. The reciprocal partner carries the opposite sign. Applying arctan linearizes every equation to constant slope ±1/2, reproducing the half-angle atlas from calculus alone.


No physical time variable or dynamical law is introduced. Derivatives are with respect to the inherited dimensionless phase coordinate x.


2.IX.B — Sine-channel differential closure


Theorem 2.IX.T1 — Envelope derivative form for the sine pair [D_s]


For every x∈D_s,


srx'=-srx|csc x|,


sxp'=+sxp|csc x|.


Proof.


On any connected component of D_s, the sign α=sgn(sin x) is constant and


srx=(α+cos x)/sin x.


Differentiation gives


srx'

=[-sin²x-(α+cos x)cos x]/sin²x

=-(1+α cos x)/sin²x.


Since


1+α cos x=α(α+cos x)


and


α/sin x=|csc x|,


we obtain


srx'=-srx|csc x|.


Because sxp=1/srx,


sxp'=-srx'/srx²

=sxp|csc x|. ∎


Theorem 2.IX.T2 — Autonomous sine Riccati pair [D_s]


For every x∈D_s,


srx'=-(1+srx²)/2,


sxp'=+(1+sxp²)/2.


Proof.


Section 2.I gives


|csc x|=(srx+sxp)/2.


Using sxp=1/srx,


srx|csc x|

=srx(srx+1/srx)/2

=(srx²+1)/2.


Substitute into Theorem 2.IX.T1. The sxp equation follows identically or by reciprocal differentiation. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C1 — Exact monotonicity on maximal sine components


On every connected component (nπ,(n+1)π) of D_s,


srx is strictly decreasing,


sxp is strictly increasing.


This strengthens the earlier quadrant-wise monotonicity: no loss of smoothness occurs when the sine pair crosses a cosine-zero point.


2.IX.C — Cosine-channel differential closure


Theorem 2.IX.T3 — Envelope derivative form for the cosine pair [D_c]


For every x∈D_c,


cxp'=+cxp|sec x|,


crx'=-crx|sec x|.


Proof.


On a connected component of D_c, β=sgn(cos x) is constant. Differentiate the signed rational form


cxp=(β+sin x)/cos x


or differentiate |sec x|+tan x directly while β is fixed. In either case,


cxp'=cxp|sec x|.


Then use crx=1/cxp to obtain


crx'=-crx|sec x|. ∎


Theorem 2.IX.T4 — Autonomous cosine Riccati pair [D_c]


For every x∈D_c,


cxp'=+(1+cxp²)/2,


crx'=-(1+crx²)/2.


Proof.


Use


|sec x|=(cxp+crx)/2


and crx=1/cxp exactly as in the sine-channel proof. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C2 — Exact monotonicity on maximal cosine components


On every connected component (−π/2+nπ,π/2+nπ) of D_c,


cxp is strictly increasing,


crx is strictly decreasing.


The cosine pair remains smooth through the sine-zero point in the middle of each such component.


2.IX.D — Arctangent linearization


The nonlinear Riccati equations are exactly linearized by the principal arctangent because every primitive is strictly positive.


Theorem 2.IX.T5 — Constant-slope arctangent coordinates [D_s,D_c]


On their natural domains,


d/dx arctan(srx)=-1/2,


d/dx arctan(sxp)=+1/2,


d/dx arctan(cxp)=+1/2,


d/dx arctan(crx)=-1/2.


Proof.


For a differentiable positive function y,


d/dx arctan y=y'/(1+y²).


Substitute the four Riccati equations. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C3 — Differential recovery of the half-angle atlas


With θ_s∈(0,π) and θ_c∈(−π/2,π/2) as defined in 2.II,


arctan(srx)=π/2−θ_s/2,


arctan(sxp)=θ_s/2,


arctan(cxp)=π/4+θ_c/2,


arctan(crx)=π/4−θ_c/2.


Equivalently,


srx=cot(θ_s/2),


sxp=tan(θ_s/2),


cxp=tan(π/4+θ_c/2),


crx=tan(π/4−θ_c/2).


Thus the half-angle atlas and the autonomous Riccati calculus are two exact descriptions of the same primitive structure.


Corollary 2.IX.C4 — Exact phase quadratures


Along any connected natural-domain component,


dx=-2 d(srx)/(1+srx²)

=+2 d(sxp)/(1+sxp²),


and


dx=+2 d(cxp)/(1+cxp²)

=-2 d(crx)/(1+crx²).


Hence a phase difference within one chart is recovered directly by an arctangent difference of any one primitive coordinate.


Clarification 2.IX.CL1 — Constant slope is not constant physical rate


The constant derivatives of the arctangent coordinates are with respect to x. No clock, elapsed time, velocity, frequency, or physical evolution parameter has been supplied.


2.IX.E — Exact primitive antiderivatives


Theorem 2.IX.T6 — Logarithmic antiderivative closure [D_s,D_c]


On each connected component of the appropriate natural domain,


∫srx dx=-ln(1+srx²)+C,


∫sxp dx=+ln(1+sxp²)+C,


∫cxp dx=+ln(1+cxp²)+C,


∫crx dx=-ln(1+crx²)+C.


Proof.


For srx,


d/dx[-ln(1+srx²)]

=-2srx·srx'/(1+srx²)

=srx


by Theorem 2.IX.T2.


For sxp,


d/dx[ln(1+sxp²)]

=2sxp·sxp'/(1+sxp²)

=sxp.


The cosine pair follows identically from Theorem 2.IX.T4. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C5 — Half-angle logarithmic forms


On the sine chart,


∫srx dx=2ln sin(θ_s/2)+C,


∫sxp dx=-2ln cos(θ_s/2)+C.


On the cosine chart,


∫cxp dx=-2ln cos(π/4+θ_c/2)+C,


∫crx dx=2ln cos(π/4−θ_c/2)+C.


Proof.


Use


1+cot²u=csc²u,


1+tan²u=sec²u.


For example,


−ln(1+srx²)

=−ln(csc²(θ_s/2))

=2ln sin(θ_s/2),


and


ln(1+sxp²)

=ln(sec²(θ_s/2))

=−2ln cos(θ_s/2).


The cosine pair follows identically with u=π/4±θ_c/2. Because the chart angles lie where the relevant sine and cosine factors are positive, no absolute-value ambiguity enters the logarithms. ∎


The logarithmic and operator forms differ only by chartwise integration constants.


2.IX.F — Local zero–pole asymptotics at the channel seams


The natural domains make the zero–pole exchange exact. Let h>0 tend to zero.


Theorem 2.IX.T7 — Sine-seam asymptotics [B]


For any sine-zero seam b∈Σ_s,


approaching from the right,


srx(b+h)=cot(h/2) ~ 2/h,


sxp(b+h)=tan(h/2) ~ h/2;


approaching from the left,


srx(b−h)=tan(h/2) ~ h/2,


sxp(b−h)=cot(h/2) ~ 2/h.


Proof.


Use π-periodicity and the maximal sine half-angle form of 2.II. The standard limits


tan(h/2)~h/2,


cot(h/2)~2/h


complete the proof. ∎


Theorem 2.IX.T8 — Cosine-seam asymptotics [B]


For any cosine-zero seam c∈Σ_c,


approaching from the left,


cxp(c−h)=cot(h/2) ~ 2/h,


crx(c−h)=tan(h/2) ~ h/2;


approaching from the right,


cxp(c+h)=tan(h/2) ~ h/2,


crx(c+h)=cot(h/2) ~ 2/h.


Proof.


Use π-periodicity and the maximal cosine half-angle form of 2.II. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C6 — Reciprocal zero–pole exchange is first-order


At its own channel seam, the diverging primitive has a simple 2/h pole while its reciprocal partner vanishes linearly as h/2.


Their product remains exactly one on either open side.


2.IX.G — Regular crossing of the opposite-channel seams


Theorem 2.IX.T9 — Sine pair at cosine-zero seams [D_s]


For every c∈Σ_c,


srx(c)=sxp(c)=1,


srx'(c)=-1,


sxp'(c)=+1.


Proof.


At a cosine-zero point, |csc c|=1 and cot c=0, so both primitive values are 1. Substitute into the sine Riccati equations. ∎


Theorem 2.IX.T10 — Cosine pair at sine-zero seams [D_c]


For every b∈Σ_s,


cxp(b)=crx(b)=1,


cxp'(b)=+1,


crx'(b)=-1.


Proof.


At a sine-zero point, |sec b|=1 and tan b=0. Apply the cosine Riccati equations. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C7 — The common seam set is composite, not function-universal


A point excluded from the common quartet domain D need not be singular for every primitive.


At Σ_s the sine pair is singular but the cosine pair crosses smoothly through value 1.


At Σ_c the cosine pair is singular but the sine pair crosses smoothly through value 1.


This is the named-operator version of Book 1’s function-relative seam rule.


2.IX.H — Improper-integral behavior


Theorem 2.IX.T11 — Logarithmic divergence on the pole side [B]


Whenever one primitive approaches its own channel seam through the side on which it behaves as 2/h, its improper integral diverges logarithmically.


On the reciprocal side, where the partner behaves as h/2, the improper integral remains finite.


Proof.


The comparison functions 2/h and h/2 have respectively logarithmically divergent and finite integrals near h=0. The asymptotic equivalences of Theorems 2.IX.T7–T8 transfer those conclusions to the primitives. The exact logarithmic antiderivatives of Theorem 2.IX.T6 give the same result. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C8 — Reciprocal pairing exchanges integrable and nonintegrable one-sided boundary behavior


At every own-channel seam, one member of the reciprocal pair is locally integrable and tends to zero, while the other has a logarithmically nonintegrable simple pole. Crossing to the neighboring component exchanges those roles.


2.IX.I — Riccati solution form and maximal components


Theorem 2.IX.T12 — Exact positive Riccati branches


Every positive solution of


y'=-(1+y²)/2


on an interval is locally of cotangent half-angle form


y(x)=cot[(x-a)/2]


for a constant a chosen so the argument lies in (0,π/2).


Every positive solution of


y'=+(1+y²)/2


is locally of tangent half-angle form


y(x)=tan[(x-a)/2]


up to the corresponding additive chart constant.


Proof.


Separate variables:


2dy/(1+y²)=∓dx.


Integration gives


2 arctan y=∓x+C.


Solving for y yields the tangent or cotangent form. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C9 — The finite-interval pole is intrinsic to the real Riccati chart


The primitive pole is not introduced by the absolute-value notation. It is already forced by the maximal positive real Riccati branch: the arctangent coordinate reaches an endpoint after a finite phase interval.


The reciprocal partner simultaneously tends to zero.


This explains the analytic seam without assigning any physical event to it.


2.IX.J — Differential closure and the one-generator theorem


Theorem 2.IX.T13 — Differential closure requires no second continuous generator [Q]


On any open quadrant, once z=srx is known as a differentiable function of x, the derivatives of every primitive operator are determined algebraically from z and ε.


Proof.


Theorem 2.VII reconstructs every primitive from z and ε, with ε fixed on the quadrant. Theorem 2.IX.T2 determines z' from z alone. Differentiating the rational reconstruction formulas therefore introduces no independent continuous function. ∎


Corollary 2.IX.C10 — Calculus does not undo one-generator minimality


Passing from algebraic values to their x-derivatives does not enlarge the continuous generator count of the named primitive system on a fixed quadrant.


Negative Theorem 2.IX.N1 — Riccati form does not imply a physical nonlinear evolution law


The equations


srx'=-(1+srx²)/2,


cxp'=+(1+cxp²)/2


are exact coordinate identities in x. They do not by themselves define temporal dynamics, dissipation, instability, force, feedback, population growth, cosmological expansion, or renormalization flow.


Negative Theorem 2.IX.N2 — Antiderivatives do not define action or accumulated physical quantity


The logarithmic primitive antiderivatives are ordinary calculus results. No action functional, entropy, work, phase accumulation, probability, or conserved quantity has been assigned to them.


Book 2, Section 2.IX — Closure ledger


Inherited mathematics used: canonical primitive definitions and reciprocal pairing from 2.I; maximal half-angle charts from 2.II; one-generator reconstruction from 2.VII; Book 1 maximal-domain and seam grammar; and ordinary differential and integral calculus.


Source provenance: T0.II explicitly proves the derivative seed


srx'=-srx|csc x|


and strict monotonicity on each open quadrant. The revised section strengthens that result using the maximal natural domains established in Book 1 and the half-angle structure established in 2.II. The autonomous Riccati equations, cosine-channel mirror equations, arctangent linearization, exact logarithmic antiderivatives, opposite-seam derivative values, and explicit one-sided zero–pole asymptotics are re-derived here from already-admitted mathematics rather than silently imported from later corpus material.


New persistent operator symbols introduced: 0.

New standard calculus notation used: arctan, ln, prime differentiation, and asymptotic equivalence ~.


New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical evolution laws: 0.


Proved in 2.IX: exact derivative-envelope forms on D_s and D_c; four autonomous Riccati equations; maximal-domain monotonicity; arctangent linearization and phase quadratures; exact logarithmic antiderivatives; first-order zero–pole seam asymptotics; smooth opposite-channel seam crossings with derivative values ±1; logarithmic improper-integral divergence on pole sides; local Riccati solution forms; and preservation of one-generator differential closure.


Certified handoff: 2.X assembles the complete named boundary atlas. It will place primitive, UNA, Saw, FlatWave, H, and V behavior side by side at sine-zero and cosine-zero seams, distinguishing divergent, vanishing, finite, discontinuous, and smoothly extendible quantities without confusing split-boundary traces with glued continuation.


2.X — COMPLETE NAMED BOUNDARY ATLAS: ZERO, POLE, JUMP, AND SMOOTH GLUING


2.X.A — Purpose and one-sided convention


Book 1 established that boundary behavior is function-relative and that split-boundary traces must not be confused with glued continuation. Book 2 can now instantiate that grammar for every named layer introduced so far.


For a seam point b, the notation


x→b^−


means approach from the quadrant immediately to the left, and


x→b^+


means approach from the quadrant immediately to the right.


Where a limit tends to zero through positive or negative values, the descriptive forms 0^+ and 0^− will be used locally to retain the one-sided sign. These are limit annotations, not new numbers.


The ordered tuples used below are:


primitive quartet:

(srx,sxp,cxp,crx),


UNA pair:

(urx,uxp),


Saw pair:

(saw_r,saw_x).


2.X.B — Sine-zero seam atlas


Let b∈Σ_s. By π-periodicity it is sufficient to analyze one sine-zero seam; every translated sine seam has the same named one-sided structure.


Theorem 2.X.T1 — Primitive sine-seam traces [B]


At every b∈Σ_s,


as x→b^−,


(srx,sxp,cxp,crx)

→

(0,+∞,1,1),


while as x→b^+,


(srx,sxp,cxp,crx)

→

(+∞,0,1,1).


Moreover, cxp and crx are actually defined at b on their natural domain D_c and satisfy


cxp(b)=crx(b)=1.


The sine pair has no ordinary real value at b because b∉D_s.


Proof.


The sine zero–pole limits are Theorem 2.IX.T7. The cosine pair is regular at every sine-zero seam by Theorem 2.IX.T10. ∎


Theorem 2.X.T2 — UNA sine-seam traces [B]


At every b∈Σ_s,


as x→b^−,


(urx,uxp)

→

(−1,−∞),


while as x→b^+,


(urx,uxp)

→

(+∞,+1).


More precisely, if h→0^+,


uxp(b−h)~−2/h,


urx(b+h)~+2/h,


while


urx(b−h)→−1,


uxp(b+h)→+1.


Proof.


Use


urx=srx−crx,


uxp=cxp−sxp


with Theorem 2.X.T1 and the first-order primitive asymptotics of 2.IX. ∎


Theorem 2.X.T3 — Saw and FlatWave sine-seam traces [B]


At every b∈Σ_s,


as x→b^−,


(saw_r,saw_x)

→

(−1,0^−),


FlatWave→−1,


while as x→b^+,


(saw_r,saw_x)

→

(0^+,+1),


FlatWave→+1.


Proof.


Reciprocate the UNA traces. Then add the Saw limits or use FlatWave=sgn(sin 2x). ∎


Theorem 2.X.T4 — Carrier sine-seam value [R]


At every b∈Σ_s,


H(b)=0,


V(b)=+1/2.


Furthermore,


H(b−h)~−h/2,


H(b+h)~+h/2


as h→0^+.


Thus H crosses zero smoothly with positive derivative while FlatWave reverses from −1 to +1.


Proof.


Use H=sin 2x/4, V=cos 2x/2, and the first-order Taylor expansion about b=kπ. ∎


2.X.C — Cosine-zero seam atlas


Let c∈Σ_c. Again, π-periodicity makes every translated cosine-zero seam equivalent at the named level.


Theorem 2.X.T5 — Primitive cosine-seam traces [B]


At every c∈Σ_c,


as x→c^−,


(srx,sxp,cxp,crx)

→

(1,1,+∞,0),


while as x→c^+,


(srx,sxp,cxp,crx)

→

(1,1,0,+∞).


Moreover, srx and sxp are actually defined at c on D_s and satisfy


srx(c)=sxp(c)=1.


The cosine pair has no ordinary real value at c because c∉D_c.


Proof.


Use Theorem 2.IX.T8 for the cosine zero–pole exchange and Theorem 2.IX.T9 for the regular sine-channel crossing. ∎


Theorem 2.X.T6 — UNA cosine-seam traces [B]


At every c∈Σ_c,


as x→c^−,


(urx,uxp)

→

(+1,+∞),


while as x→c^+,


(urx,uxp)

→

(−∞,−1).


More precisely, if h→0^+,


uxp(c−h)~+2/h,


urx(c+h)~−2/h,


while


urx(c−h)→+1,


uxp(c+h)→−1.


Proof.


Substitute the primitive asymptotics into the UNA definitions. ∎


Theorem 2.X.T7 — Saw and FlatWave cosine-seam traces [B]


At every c∈Σ_c,


as x→c^−,


(saw_r,saw_x)

→

(+1,0^+),


FlatWave→+1,


while as x→c^+,


(saw_r,saw_x)

→

(0^−,−1),


FlatWave→−1.


Proof.


Reciprocate Theorem 2.X.T6 and use FlatWave=saw_r+saw_x. ∎


Theorem 2.X.T8 — Carrier cosine-seam value [R]


At every c∈Σ_c,


H(c)=0,


V(c)=−1/2.


Furthermore,


H(c−h)~+h/2,


H(c+h)~−h/2


as h→0^+.


Thus H crosses zero smoothly with negative derivative while FlatWave reverses from +1 to −1.


Proof.


Use the carrier formulas and Taylor expansion about c=π/2+kπ. ∎


2.X.D — Unified seam formulas


Let


b_k=kπ/2.


Then even k are sine-zero seams and odd k are cosine-zero seams.


Theorem 2.X.T9 — Unified FlatWave and carrier seam law [B,R]


At every b_k,


lim_{x→b_k^-}FlatWave(x)=(-1)^{k-1},


lim_{x→b_k^+}FlatWave(x)=(-1)^k,


H(b_k)=0,


V(b_k)=(-1)^k/2.


Moreover, for signed h→0,


H(b_k+h)~[(-1)^k/2]h.


Proof.


Combine Theorems 2.X.T4 and 2.X.T8 with the alternating seam decomposition. ∎


Corollary 2.X.C1 — The sign jump is the sign of a smooth transverse zero


FlatWave reverses at every seam because it is the sign projection of H and H has a simple zero there:


FlatWave=sgn(H)


on D,


H(b_k)=0,


H'(b_k)=V(b_k)≠0.


Thus the discontinuity of FlatWave and the smoothness of H are compatible descriptions of the same zero crossing at different information levels.


2.X.E — Exact Saw endpoint exchange


Theorem 2.X.T10 — Split Saw endpoint theorem [B]


At every seam b_k, the left and right Saw limits have the form


left:

(saw_r,saw_x)

→

(ε_−,0),


right:

(saw_r,saw_x)

→

(0,ε_+),


where


ε_+=−ε_−,


ε_−=(-1)^{k-1},


ε_+=(-1)^k.


The zero component approaches zero with the same sign as the nonzero component on that side.


Proof.


For even k this is Theorem 2.X.T3. For odd k it is Theorem 2.X.T7. ∎


Corollary 2.X.C2 — Absolute Saw endpoint exchange


Taking absolute values gives


left:

(|saw_r|,|saw_x|)→(1,0),


right:

(|saw_r|,|saw_x|)→(0,1).


This is an exact split-boundary endpoint statement. It is not a probability-transfer law, a conservation process, or a physical handoff.


Corollary 2.X.C3 — UNA reciprocal endpoint theorem


The corresponding UNA limits are


left:

(urx,uxp)→(ε_−, ε_−∞),


right:

(urx,uxp)→(ε_+∞, ε_+),


where the notation ε∞ means divergence with sign ε.


Thus the finite unit UNA component and the divergent UNA component exchange labels across every seam, exactly reciprocally to the Saw endpoint exchange.


2.X.F — Why H remains smooth while FlatWave jumps


Theorem 2.X.T11 — Vanishing-magnitude factorization [D]


For every x∈D,


H=FlatWave·saw_r·saw_x.


Proof.


Section 2.VI proved


saw_r·saw_x=|sin 2x|/4=|H|


and


FlatWave=sgn(H).


Therefore


FlatWave·saw_r·saw_x

=sgn(H)|H|

=H. ∎


Corollary 2.X.C4 — FlatWave jump is annihilated by the vanishing Saw product


At every seam,


saw_r·saw_x→0


from both sides while FlatWave has opposite one-sided values ±1. Their product nevertheless tends to the same value 0 from both sides, producing the continuous H limit.


This explains the seam regularization algebraically without assigning a value to FlatWave at the seam.


2.X.G — Three exact regularity levels of the double-angle closure


Theorem 2.X.T12 — Smooth, cusp, and jump hierarchy [R,B,D]


The three closely related double-angle quantities have different seam regularity:


1. Signed reciprocal sum:


H=sin 2x/4


extends smoothly through every seam.


2. Unsigned reciprocal product:


1/(urx·uxp)=|sin 2x|/4=|H|


extends continuously through every seam with value 0, but is not differentiable there.


3. Sign quotient:


FlatWave=H/|H|=sgn(H)


on D has no continuous extension through any seam.


Proof.


The first statement is 2.VIII.


For the second, near b_k,


H(b_k+h)~V(b_k)h


with |V(b_k)|=1/2. Hence


|H(b_k+h)|~|h|/2,


whose one-sided derivatives are −1/2 and +1/2. Thus |H| has a cusp.


The third follows from the opposite one-sided FlatWave limits. ∎


Corollary 2.X.C5 — Absolute value can reduce differentiability even while improving boundedness


Passing from H to |H| removes sign but changes a smooth transverse zero into a cusp. Passing further to sgn(H) removes magnitude and turns that cusp structure into a jump.


Thus information compression and analytic regularity are not ordered by a simple rule such as fewer values means smoother representation.


2.X.H — Split boundary versus carrier gluing


Theorem 2.X.T13 — Componentwise bounded extension of Saw and FlatWave [S]


On each closed-quadrant component of the Book 1 split-boundary domain, saw_r, saw_x, and FlatWave possess continuous endpoint extensions given by Theorems 2.X.T3 and 2.X.T7.


Proof.


Inside each open quadrant the Saw responses are continuous and FlatWave is constant. The one-sided endpoint limits are finite. Because adjacent quadrant closures are kept as distinct components, incompatible left and right endpoint values are not required to coincide. ∎


Theorem 2.X.T14 — Carrier gluing and Saw split traces retain different information [S,R]


At a numerical seam, the smooth carrier assigns one glued point


(0,+1/2)


for a sine-zero seam or


(0,−1/2)


for a cosine-zero seam.


The split Saw representation assigns two distinct directional endpoint states:


(ε_−,0)


and


(0,ε_+).


Therefore the carrier seam point does not determine which of the two split Saw endpoint states was used.


Proof.


The carrier values follow from 2.VIII. The two split Saw values follow from Theorem 2.X.T10. A single carrier point is the image of two distinct split directional endpoints, so the identification is many-to-one and cannot be inverted without a boundary-side label. ∎


Corollary 2.X.C6 — Smooth gluing does not erase the legitimacy of split traces


The carrier and split-boundary representations solve different mathematical tasks.


The carrier is appropriate when one wants smooth phase tracking and seam-class identification.


The split representation is appropriate when one wants to preserve the directional endpoint limits of the bounded Saw layer.


Neither representation supplies a physical seam-crossing law.


2.X.I — Complete boundary taxonomy


Theorem 2.X.T15 — Named boundary classification through 2.IX


At the two seam classes, the named Book 2 quantities fall into five distinct analytic categories.


Category 1 — Own-channel primitive zero–pole exchange.


At Σ_s, srx and sxp exchange 0 and +∞.


At Σ_c, cxp and crx exchange +∞ and 0.


No finite own-channel seam value exists.


Category 2 — Opposite-channel regular crossing.


At Σ_s,


cxp=crx=1


with derivatives +1 and −1 respectively.


At Σ_c,


srx=sxp=1


with derivatives −1 and +1 respectively.


Category 3 — UNA mixed finite/pole traces.


On each side of every seam, one UNA component tends to a finite signed unit value while the other has a signed simple pole. The roles exchange across the seam.


Category 4 — Saw and FlatWave bounded split discontinuity.


The Saw pair has finite but unequal left and right endpoint vectors. FlatWave has opposite unit signs. These extend componentwise on the split boundary but not continuously after gluing the two directional copies into one real seam.


Category 5 — Carrier regularization.


H and V are smooth on all of ℝ. H vanishes transversely at every seam, and V retains the seam class through ±1/2.


Proof.


This is the collected content of Theorems 2.X.T1–T14 and Sections 2.VIII–2.IX. ∎


Negative Theorem 2.X.N1 — A common numerical seam does not imply a common analytic status


No theorem may state merely that an operator is singular at “the seam” without identifying which operator and which seam class are meant.


At a sine-zero seam the sine primitives are singular while the cosine primitives are regular; at a cosine-zero seam the reverse is true; H and V are regular at both.


Negative Theorem 2.X.N2 — Boundary traces do not derive boundary dynamics


The exact endpoint exchange


(|saw_r|,|saw_x|):(1,0)→(0,1)


when x is read from left to right is a statement about one-sided limits in an ordered coordinate. It does not derive physical transfer, temporal passage, probability flow, causal propagation, state reset, or conservation.


Negative Theorem 2.X.N3 — Carrier gluing does not define raw seam values


The finite carrier point at a seam cannot be substituted backward to define finite values of the own-channel primitives, UNA poles, Saw directional endpoints, or FlatWave.


The carrier regularizes the derived double-angle representation, not the singular raw factors.


2.X.J — Boundary atlas theorem


Theorem 2.X.T16 — Complete named seam architecture [B,S,R]


The reciprocal-trigonometric operator calculus now has a fully classified boundary architecture:


primitive reciprocal pairs exchange zero and pole at their own channel seams;


opposite-channel primitives pass smoothly through value 1;


UNA differences exchange finite unit and simple-pole roles;


Saw reciprocals exchange signed unit and zero endpoint roles;


FlatWave reverses its ±1 character;


the unsigned reciprocal double-angle magnitude forms a cusp;


and the signed harmonic carrier H, together with V, glues smoothly through the same numerical seam while retaining its sine-zero versus cosine-zero class.


These statements are mutually consistent because they concern different derived representations and different extension tasks.

Inherited mathematics used: Book 1 split-boundary and gluing grammar; primitive boundary traces from 1.VII and 2.IX; canonical UNA and Saw definitions from 2.IV and 2.VI; double-angle closure from 2.V; FlatWave from 2.VI; and the smooth carrier from 2.VIII.


Source provenance: T0.IV explicitly distinguishes seam-safe carrier data from seam-unsafe raw operator data, proves carrier seam classification, and distinguishes smooth carrier gluing from split-boundary endpoint data. The revised section supplements that source structure with the exact named primitive, UNA, Saw, and FlatWave one-sided traces already derivable from revised Books 1–2, and assembles them into a single boundary atlas.
