BOOK 3 — PROJECTIVE PHASE, ORIENTATION, AND LIFT STRUCTURE

Purpose

Extend the certified Book 2 calculus into projective phase geometry and topology under an explicit dependency discipline. Book 3 activates two precise pieces of vocabulary. Cophase means the sine–cosine quarter-turn relation: two lifted phases are cophase-related when their separation is Δx=±π/2 modulo 2π. Mirror means negative orientation of a frame relative to a declared positive orientation. Cophase, half-turn equivalence, mirror orientation, and double-cover lift sign are therefore distinct structures and must never be silently identified.

Book 3 uses no later historical paper as a premise. The eight-phase papers, S-series symmetry work, projective/FlatWave notes, T4 chirality analysis, and later spinor material may return only as candidate claims after the neutral mathematics has been established. The three planned theorem-level climaxes are: Cophase–FlatWave Character, Spin-Lift Condition, and Lift/Mirror Nonselection.

Front Matter — Book 3 Legend of Symbols

Book 2 symbols are repeated only when materially used. In particular, τ_{π/2} is the inherited quarter-turn translation and τ_π is the inherited half-turn translation. The cophase relation is defined by y=x±π/2 modulo 2π; no new symbol is required unless later compression warrants one. The signed half-angle projective coordinate is t=tan(x/2), reserving z=srx from Book 2. The orientation sign of a frame is introduced only after a positive reference orientation and an orientation-bearing frame space have been declared. “Mirror” denotes the negative-orientation class; it is not a synonym for reflection of a scalar coordinate, reciprocal conjugation, half-turn, or q↦−q.

Activated notation for 3.I

[x]_{2π} — the equivalence class of x modulo 2π; point of 𝕋_{2π}; inherited phase notation; dimensionless angular coordinate.

[x]_π — the equivalence class of x modulo π; point of 𝕋_π; inherited quotient notation; dimensionless angular coordinate.

κ — canonical fold κ:𝕋_{2π}→𝕋_π induced by reduction modulo π; inherited from Book 1; no physical identification.

τ_a — translation by angular displacement a on the relevant phase carrier; inherited. τ_{π/2} is the directed quarter-turn; τ_π is the half-turn.

id — identity map on the relevant carrier; standard notation.

det — determinant of a real change-of-basis matrix; standard algebra; its sign is used in 3.I to distinguish frame orientation classes.

cophase — canonical Book 3 term activated in 3.I: the symmetric sine–cosine relation Δx=±π/2 modulo 2π. It is a relation, not an equivalence class or physical state.

mirror frame — canonical Book 3 term activated in 3.I: an ordered frame in the negative-orientation class relative to a declared positive orientation. It is not a synonym for phase reflection, reciprocal inversion, half-turn, or cover-fiber sign.

R_hat — extended real line R∪{∞}, used as the affine presentation of RP1. Standard projective notation; no physical meaning.

t — signed half-angle projective coordinate activated in 3.III: t=tan(x/2), with t=∞ at x≡π mod 2π. Type: complete lifted phase coordinate modulo 2π.

Q_+, Q_- — directed cophase Möbius maps activated in 3.III: Q_+(t)=(1+t)/(1−t), Q_-(t)=(t−1)/(1+t). They represent +π/2 and −π/2 respectively.

A_+, A_- — 2×2 real matrices representing Q_+ and Q_- projectively. A_+=[[1,1],[-1,1]], A_-=[[1,-1],[1,1]].

R — scalar projective reflection R(t)=−t. It reverses the standard orientation of RP1 but is not by itself a mirror frame in any undeclared higher-dimensional frame space.

[a,b;c,d] — cross-ratio convention activated in 3.III: ((a−c)(b−d))/((a−d)(b−c)), extended by limits when one entry is ∞. An ordered quadruple is harmonic when this value is −1.

f — positive reciprocal transform activated in 3.IV: f(u)=√(1+u²)−u for real affine u. Type: strictly positive, strictly decreasing bijection R→(0,∞); not by itself a global RP1 coordinate.

u — generic signed real affine coordinate used as the input of f. Its meaning must be declared in each application; it is not a new global phase symbol.

s — positive reciprocal coordinate s=f(u). Type: positive affine-chart coordinate with exact inverse u=(1−s²)/(2s).

λ — hyperbolic linearizing coordinate λ=arsinh(u)=−ln s. Type: real additive coordinate associated with u; no physical rapidity meaning is implied.

Γ_c — directed cophase transformation group generated by τ_{π/2}; Γ_c≅C4 on the lifted 2π phase carrier. Status: derived finite symmetry group, activated in 3.V.

χ_c — cophase sign character χ_c(τ_{kπ/2})=(−1)^k from Γ_c to {−1,+1}. Type: one-dimensional real group character with kernel {id,τ_π}; not a bundle class or physical chirality.

g(t) — projective sign polynomial g(t)=t(1−t²). On the finite t chart away from marked seam values, FlatWave=sgn g(t). Status: derived projective diagnostic activated in 3.V.

RP² — real projective plane of nonzero homogeneous triples [X:Y:Z] modulo nonzero real rescaling. Standard projective geometry; no physical-space identification.

[X:Y:Z] — homogeneous coordinates on RP² activated in 3.VI. Uppercase X,Y,Z avoid collision with the inherited phase variable x.

U_X, U_Y, U_Z — standard affine charts X≠0, Y≠0, Z≠0 on RP².

P° — dense triple-overlap region { [X:Y:Z] : XYZ≠0 } on which all three cyclic pairwise ratios are simultaneously defined.

u_IJ — signed homogeneous-coordinate ratio, e.g. u_XY=Y/X, on its declared denominator domain. Pairwise ratios are projective coordinates, not new physical observables.

σ_IJ — positive reciprocal coordinate σ_IJ=f(u_IJ). Type: positive affine-chart coordinate; σ_IJ=1 iff the signed ratio u_IJ=0.

φ — inverse positive transform φ(σ)=f^{-1}(σ)=(1−σ²)/(2σ), σ>0.

ε_IJ — relative ratio sign ε_IJ=sgn(u_IJ)=sgn(1−σ_IJ²) where u_IJ≠0. In 3.VI this is not yet identified with FlatWave, a bundle class, or mirror orientation.

Φ_X, Φ_Y, Φ_Z — positive reciprocal coordinate-chart maps on U_X,U_Y,U_Z obtained by applying f coordinatewise to the standard affine coordinates. The symbol Φ is unrelated to the Book 1 seam set Σ.

γ — tautological real line bundle over RP² activated in 3.VII: fiber over [X:Y:Z] is span(X,Y,Z). Standard topology object.

e_X, e_Y, e_Z — canonical local nonzero frames of γ over U_X,U_Y,U_Z obtained by normalizing the corresponding homogeneous coordinate to 1.

ζ_JI — sign of the tautological line transition scalar g_JI relating e_J=g_JI e_I on U_I∩U_J. Type: {±1}-valued Čech transition sign.

a — first Stiefel–Whitney class a=w1(γ)∈H¹(RP²;Z2). Standard characteristic class; distinct from FlatWave and from tangent orientation until 3.VIII.

L_Z — distinguished projective line { [X:Y:0] }≅RP¹ used in 3.VII to test nontriviality of γ.

ρ — natural half-angle projective map ρ([x])=[cos(x/2):sin(x/2):0] from T_{2π} onto L_Z. Type: mathematical phase-to-projective identification used only for the explicit comparison test.

RP^n — real projective n-space for n≥1. Standard manifold/projective-space notation; no physical-space identification.

γ_n — tautological real line bundle over RP^n. For n=2, γ_2 is the bundle denoted γ in 3.VII.

a_n — first Stiefel–Whitney class a_n=w1(γ_n)∈H^1(RP^n;Z2). Nonzero for n≥1; distinct from χ_c.

T RP^n — tangent bundle of RP^n, rank n. Its orientation class is w1(T RP^n), not a_n unless the derived dimension-specific relation makes them equal.

1 — trivial real line bundle when used in stable bundle sums such as T RP^n⊕1.

w(E) — total Stiefel–Whitney class of a real vector bundle E; standard topology notation.

w2(E) — second Stiefel–Whitney class of a real vector bundle E. For an oriented bundle, w2 is the obstruction to a Spin lift.

Spin(n) — standard double cover of SO(n) used to define spin structures; no physical fermion interpretation is implied by the group name alone.

Ω_m — Clifford volume element e_1e_2···e_m used in 3.IX as an explicit lift of −I_m for even m. Its square controls the displayed stable-lift cocycle parity.

spin-structure torsor — when a spin structure exists, its equivalence classes form a torsor for H^1(M;Z2); therefore existence does not canonically select one structure.

BOOK 3 DEPENDENCY GATES

Gate A — Certified phase/operator input. Book 3 may use the Book 1 phase carriers, seams, quadrants and octants, and the certified Book 2 primitive, UNA, Saw, FlatWave, symmetry, and Möbius theorems. No Book 3 topology may be used to retroactively prove a Book 2 identity.

Gate B — Cophase before quotient interpretation. The quarter-turn relation must first be established on the lifted 2π carrier. Only afterward may its induced action on the π-periodic primitive quotient be discussed. The π half-turn is the square of a directed quarter-turn, not the definition of cophase.

Gate C — Atlas before cocycle. Projective ratios, domains, affine charts, overlaps, and transition functions must be constructed before any Čech cocycle or bundle class is asserted.

Gate D — Bundle before tangent orientation before spin. The tautological line bundle γ and its transition class precede the tangent-bundle relation; the tangent first Stiefel–Whitney class precedes orientability; orientability and w2 precede any spin-lift claim. The class w1(γ) must not be silently called the orientation class of RP^n.

Gate E — Generic projective geometry before rotation specialization. RP3 may be identified with SO(3) only after the generic projective construction is complete and the identification is explicitly declared as standard mathematics. O(3), or an equivalent orientation-bearing frame space, must be present before “mirror frame” is analyzed. The Spin(3) double cover comes afterward.

Gate F — Mathematics before physics. No cophase relation, projective sign, orientation class, spin structure, mirror label, or lift sign is to be interpreted as physical chirality, time direction, particle identity, or dynamics without an explicit later physics import or projection contract.

3.I — SCOPE, SOURCE BOUNDARY, AND CANONICAL PHASE-FRAME VOCABULARY

Purpose

Book 3 begins by fixing the meaning of its two most dangerous words before either can acquire topological or physical baggage. “Cophase” will mean the sine–cosine quarter-turn relation Δx=±π/2 on the lifted 2π phase carrier. “Mirror” will mean negative orientation of an ordered frame relative to a declared positive orientation. These definitions occupy different mathematical spaces. No theorem in this section identifies them.

The section also fixes the order of the relevant phase operations. A directed quarter-turn has order four on the lifted carrier. Its square is the π half-turn. After folding by π-periodicity, the induced quarter-turn has order two. This distinction is structural and will control the octant analysis of 3.II and the projective Möbius orbit of 3.III.

3.I.A — Admissible Inputs and Source Boundary

Inherited declaration 3.I.A1 — Phase carriers. Book 3 inherits from Book 1 the lifted phase circle

𝕋_{2π}=ℝ/(2πℤ)

and the half-period quotient

𝕋_π=ℝ/(πℤ),

together with the canonical fold κ:𝕋_{2π}→𝕋_π.

Inherited declaration 3.I.A2 — Phase translations. For a∈ℝ, τ_a denotes translation by a on the appropriate phase carrier,

τ_a([x])=[x+a].

Book 3 inherits the ordinary composition law

τ_a∘τ_b=τ_{a+b}.

In particular, τ_{π/2} is the quarter-turn and τ_π is the half-turn.

Inherited declaration 3.I.A3 — Certified operator input. Book 3 may use the certified Book 2 transformation laws, including the primitive quarter-turn and half-turn laws and the certified FlatWave identity, but only after the corresponding Book 3 object has been defined. No Book 3 projective or bundle construction is permitted to serve retroactively as a proof of a Book 2 result.

Rule 3.I.R1 — Historical noninheritance. The historical eight-phase papers, S-series symmetry language, projective notes, chirality analyses, spinor discussions, and mirror-language experiments are not premises of Book 3. They may return only as candidate claims after the neutral mathematics has been proved.

Rule 3.I.R2 — No physical semantics. A phase translation is not yet time evolution; an orientation sign is not yet particle chirality; a binary label is not yet a physical state; and a mathematical frame is not yet a laboratory frame. Any later physical identification requires an explicit import or projection contract.

3.I.B — Cophase as the Sine–Cosine Quarter-Turn Relation

Definition 3.I.D1 — Cophase. Let [x],[y]∈𝕋_{2π}. The phases [x] and [y] are cophase-related exactly when

y−x≡+π/2  (mod 2π)

or

y−x≡−π/2  (mod 2π).

Thus cophase is the symmetric adjacency relation generated by the directed quarter-turn τ_{π/2}. The word does not mean equal phase, half-turn partner, antipode, reciprocal conjugate, or spin-cover partner.

Theorem 3.I.T1 — Sine–cosine exchange under cophase. If y=x+π/2, then

sin y=cos x,

cos y=−sin x.

If y=x−π/2, then

sin y=−cos x,

cos y=sin x.

Proof. These are the standard addition formulas for sine and cosine at ±π/2. Therefore a cophase step exchanges the sine and cosine channels up to the orientation sign forced by the directed quarter-turn. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C1 — Meaning of the name. The term cophase is justified by exact sine–cosine channel exchange. It is not a claim that the two phases have the same trigonometric state.

Theorem 3.I.T2 — Two distinct cophase neighbors on the lifted carrier. Every [x]∈𝕋_{2π} has exactly two cophase neighbors,

[x+π/2]  and  [x−π/2],

and they are distinct.

Proof. If they were equal modulo 2π, then π would be an integer multiple of 2π, which is impossible. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C2 — Cophase is adjacency, not a two-point identification. On 𝕋_{2π}, cophase does not quotient the phase circle and does not identify the two cophase neighbors with one another.

Theorem 3.I.T3 — Directed cophase order on 𝕋_{2π}. The quarter-turn translation satisfies

τ_{π/2}²=τ_π,

τ_{π/2}³=τ_{3π/2}=τ_{−π/2},

τ_{π/2}⁴=id.

Moreover, four is the least positive order.

Proof. By translation composition,

τ_{π/2}^n=τ_{nπ/2}.

This equals the identity on 𝕋_{2π} exactly when nπ/2∈2πℤ. The least positive solution is n=4. The square is translation by π, and the cube is translation by 3π/2, which equals translation by −π/2 modulo 2π. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C3 — Half-turn separation. The π half-turn is the square of a directed cophase step:

τ_π=τ_{π/2}².

It is therefore generated by repeated cophase motion but is not itself the definition of cophase.

Theorem 3.I.T4 — Cophase is not an equivalence relation on the lifted carrier. The cophase relation is symmetric and irreflexive but not transitive.

Proof. Symmetry follows because +π/2 in one direction is −π/2 in the reverse direction. Irreflexivity follows because ±π/2 is not congruent to 0 modulo 2π. For nontransitivity, if y=x+π/2 and z=y+π/2, then z=x+π. The phases x and z are half-turn partners, not cophase neighbors. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C4 — Terminological lock. Expressions such as “the cophase class of x” are prohibited unless a separately defined orbit or quotient is intended. Repeated cophase steps generate an orbit; the raw cophase relation itself does not define equivalence classes.

3.I.C — Folding from the Lifted Carrier to the Primitive Quotient

Theorem 3.I.T5 — Induced cophase involution on 𝕋_π. Under the canonical fold κ:𝕋_{2π}→𝕋_π, the translations +π/2 and −π/2 induce the same map, and that induced map has order two.

Proof. In 𝕋_π,

[x+π/2]_π=[x−π/2]_π

because the two representatives differ by π. Applying the induced quarter-turn twice adds π, which is zero in the quotient. Hence its square is the identity. It has no fixed point because π/2 is not congruent to 0 modulo π. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C5 — Carrier-dependent order. The same geometric quarter-turn has different represented order on the two carriers:

order four on 𝕋_{2π},

order two on 𝕋_π.

This is not a contradiction. The quotient has erased the half-turn branch that distinguishes the first and third quarter-turn steps.

Theorem 3.I.T6 — Kernel of the phase fold. Two lifted phases [x],[y]∈𝕋_{2π} have the same image under κ exactly when

y≡x  (mod 2π)

or

y≡x+π  (mod 2π).

Thus each point of 𝕋_π has two lifted representatives separated by a half-turn.

Proof. Equality modulo π means y−x∈πℤ. Modulo 2π there are exactly two such residue classes, 0 and π. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C6 — Cophase versus half-turn degeneracy. The fold κ erases the π half-turn distinction. It does not erase the quarter-turn relation; rather, it converts the directed order-four quarter-turn into an order-two involution.

Theorem 3.I.T7 — Four-step lifted orbit and two-step folded orbit. Starting from [x]∈𝕋_{2π}, repeated directed cophase steps produce

[x]

→[x+π/2]

→[x+π]

→[x+3π/2]

→[x].

Under κ, the first and third entries coincide and the second and fourth entries coincide. The folded orbit is therefore a two-cycle.

Corollary 3.I.C7 — Dependency target for 3.II. The eight positional octants of the lifted carrier may therefore support a two-octant cophase displacement and a four-octant half-turn displacement without identifying those operations. Their quotient behavior must be derived only after this carrier theorem.

3.I.D — Orientation-Bearing Frames and the Meaning of Mirror

Definition 3.I.D2 — Oriented frame comparison. Let V be a finite-dimensional real vector space with a declared positive orientation. For any ordered basis F of V, compare F with any positively oriented reference basis. If the change-of-basis determinant is positive, F is positively oriented; if the determinant is negative, F is negatively oriented.

Definition 3.I.D3 — Mirror frame. A mirror frame is an ordered frame in the negative-orientation class relative to the declared positive orientation.

This definition is deliberately relational. “Mirror” does not mean “contains a minus sign.” It means that the frame lies in the orientation class opposite the declared positive one.

Theorem 3.I.T8 — Well-defined two-class frame orientation. The positive/negative classification of ordered frames is independent of which positively oriented reference basis is chosen.

Proof. Let E and E′ be positively oriented reference bases. The determinant of the change of basis from E to E′ is positive. For any frame F, the determinant from E′ to F is the determinant from E to F divided by the positive determinant from E to E′. Therefore both reference choices assign the same sign to F. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C8 — Exactly two orientation classes. Ordered frames split into two classes distinguished by determinant sign. Choosing which class is called positive labels the two classes but does not create them.

Corollary 3.I.C9 — Orientation-label nonselection. If the declared positive orientation is reversed, every previously positive frame becomes mirror and every previously mirror frame becomes positive. The neutral frame geometry therefore distinguishes two classes but, without additional orientation-sensitive structure, does not canonically privilege one label. This is the local frame version of the orientation-selection obstruction already isolated in Book 0.

3.I.E — Cophase–Mirror Separation

Theorem 3.I.T9 — Cophase translations preserve the standard orientation of the phase circle. The maps τ_{π/2} and τ_π are orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms of the one-dimensional phase circle.

Proof. In the lifted coordinate x, every translation x↦x+a has derivative +1. Hence it preserves the standard orientation before passing to the circle, and the induced circle map remains orientation-preserving. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C10 — Cophase is not mirror even on the phase carrier. A cophase step exchanges sine and cosine channels but does not reverse the orientation of the phase circle.

Theorem 3.I.T10 — Cophase–Mirror Separation Theorem. The cophase relation and mirror-frame status are mathematically independent under the present declarations.

Proof. Cophase is defined entirely by phase displacement ±π/2 on 𝕋_{2π}. Mirror-frame status is defined entirely by the sign of a frame change-of-basis determinant relative to a declared orientation. No map from phase points to orientation-bearing frames has been declared in 3.I. Therefore neither definition supplies the data required to evaluate the other. Moreover, Theorem 3.I.T9 shows that the cophase translations themselves preserve the orientation of the phase carrier. ∎

Corollary 3.I.C11 — Prohibited substitutions. None of the following may be called mirror solely from its notation or sign behavior:

x↦x±π/2,

x↦x+π,

x↦−x,

a reciprocal map s↦1/s,

a sign flip of a scalar observable,

or a later double-cover replacement q↦−q.

Each operation must be classified in its own mathematical space. If a particular operation is later proved to reverse a relevant frame orientation, that is a new theorem, not a terminological shortcut.

Note 3.I.N1 — Scalar reflection. The inherited reflection x↦−x reverses the standard orientation of the one-dimensional phase circle because its derivative is −1. That fact concerns the phase circle itself. It does not, without an explicit map, make the reflected phase a mirror physical frame or a preferred chirality.

Note 3.I.N2 — No temporal direction. The arrows used in the directed cophase orbit record composition order only. They do not mean past-to-future evolution. A time parameter has not been supplied.

3.I.F — Dependency Certification

The results of 3.I depend only on:

Book 1 phase carriers and quotient structure

→ standard trigonometric quarter-turn identities

→ inherited phase translations

→ elementary determinant orientation of ordered real frames.

Book 2 operator identities are available but were not required to prove the carrier and mirror-separation theorems of this section. In particular, FlatWave, the Möbius reduction, projective atlases, Stiefel–Whitney classes, spin structures, SO(3), O(3), and Spin(3) have not been used.

Section 3.I closure ledger

New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

New canonical definitions: 3 — cophase; oriented frame comparison relative to a declared positive orientation; mirror frame.

New standard-mathematics declarations: phase-translation order on quotient circles; determinant-sign orientation of ordered real frames.

New physics imports: 0.

New projection contracts: 0.

New empirical calibrations: 0.

Historical interpretive vocabulary admitted as premise: 0.

Retired identifications: cophase=π half-turn; cophase=antipodal identification; cophase=reciprocal inversion; cophase=spin-cover sign; mirror=generic minus sign; mirror=unproved scalar reflection; mirror=q↦−q by notation alone.

Certified handoff to 3.II: cophase advances ±2 octants on the eight-octant lifted carrier; the π half-turn advances 4 octants; only after that distinction is carried into the octant atlas may primitive quotient degeneracy be analyzed.

3.II — EIGHT-OCTANT COPHASE ACTION AND HALF-TURN DEGENERACY

Purpose

Section 3.I separated three operations that had previously been easy to conflate: an eighth-turn π/4, a cophase quarter-turn ±π/2, and the π half-turn. The present section carries those distinctions into the eight-octant phase atlas and then re-derives the primitive dominance cycle from the certified Book 2 calculus.

The central result is not that there are eight independent operator states. There are eight positional octants on the lifted 2π carrier, four repeated dominance labels, four primitive-value classes after half-turn identification, and an exact quarter-turn permutation acting two octants at a time. These are related structures with different kernels.

3.II.A — Open octants and the positional C8 action

Definition 3.II.D1 — Open octants. For j∈ℤ/8ℤ, define

O_j=(jπ/4,(j+1)π/4)

on the lifted phase circle 𝕋_{2π}.

Definition 3.II.D2 — Eighth-turn translation. Define

η=τ_{π/4}.

Then η advances phase by one open octant.

Theorem 3.II.T1 — Octant advancement. For every j∈ℤ/8ℤ,

η(O_j)=O_{j+1}.

Proof. Adding π/4 to every point of

(jπ/4,(j+1)π/4)

gives

((j+1)π/4,(j+2)π/4)=O_{j+1}.

Indices are read modulo eight on 𝕋_{2π}. ∎

Theorem 3.II.T2 — Positional octant group. The eighth-turn η has order eight on the lifted carrier:

η^8=id,

and no smaller positive power is the identity.

Proof. Since η^n=τ_{nπ/4}, identity on 𝕋_{2π} requires nπ/4∈2πℤ. The least positive solution is n=8. ∎

Corollary 3.II.C1 — C8 positional action. The eight open octants carry a transitive cyclic action

⟨η⟩≅C8.

This statement concerns phase position only. It does not yet describe the action on primitive-function values.

3.II.B — Cophase and half-turn on octants

Theorem 3.II.T3 — Cophase advances two octants. A directed positive cophase step satisfies

τ_{π/2}=η²

and therefore

τ_{π/2}(O_j)=O_{j+2}.

A directed negative cophase step satisfies

τ_{−π/2}=η^{-2}

and therefore

τ_{−π/2}(O_j)=O_{j-2}.

Proof. π/2=2(π/4), so the translation identities follow from the composition law. Apply Theorem 3.II.T1 twice in the positive or negative direction. ∎

Corollary 3.II.C2 — Two cophase neighbors of an octant. On the lifted eight-octant carrier, O_j has two cophase neighbors,

O_{j+2},

O_{j-2}.

They are distinct modulo eight.

Theorem 3.II.T4 — Half-turn advances four octants. The half-turn satisfies

τ_π=η⁴=(τ_{π/2})²

and

τ_π(O_j)=O_{j+4}.

Proof. Immediate from π=4(π/4)=2(π/2). ∎

Corollary 3.II.C3 — Cophase is not the half-turn in the octant atlas. A cophase displacement changes the octant index by ±2. The half-turn changes it by 4. The latter is obtained by composing two directed cophase steps of the same sign.

3.II.C — Primitive-value half-turn degeneracy

Theorem 3.II.T5 — Pointwise primitive half-turn invariance [D]. For every admissible x,

srx(x+π)=srx(x),

sxp(x+π)=sxp(x),

cxp(x+π)=cxp(x),

crx(x+π)=crx(x).

Proof. This is the certified Book 2 half-turn theorem, ultimately following from the π-periodicity of |csc x|, |sec x|, cot x, and tan x in the canonical absolute-valued combinations. ∎

Corollary 3.II.C4 — Octant half-turn degeneracy. If x∈O_j∩D and y=x+π∈O_{j+4}∩D, then the complete primitive quartet has identical values at x and y.

Thus O_j and O_{j+4} are distinct positional octants on 𝕋_{2π} but represent the same primitive state at corresponding phase offset.

Definition 3.II.D3 — Primitive octant class. Define

[O_j]_P={O_j,O_{j+4}}.

There are four such classes.

Corollary 3.II.C5 — Four primitive classes from eight positional octants. The primitive quartet factors the eight-octant positional set through the half-turn quotient

ℤ/8ℤ → ℤ/4ℤ,

j↦j mod 4.

This quotient is a consequence of primitive π-periodicity, not a declaration that the lifted phases themselves are equal.

3.II.D — Re-derivation of the unique dominant primitive

Definition 3.II.D4 — Dominant primitive. For an open octant O_j, define W(O_j) to be the primitive among

{srx,sxp,cxp,crx}

having strictly greatest numerical value throughout the interior of that octant, when such a unique primitive exists.

Theorem 3.II.T6 — First-octant dominance [O]. On

O_0=(0,π/4),

srx is the unique dominant primitive.

Proof. On the principal quadrant, Book 2 gives

srx=cot(x/2),

sxp=tan(x/2),

cxp=tan(π/4+x/2),

crx=tan(π/4−x/2).

For 0<x<π/4,

0<x/2<π/8.

Hence sxp<1 and crx<1, while srx>1 and cxp>1. It remains only to compare srx and cxp. Using cot u=tan(π/2−u),

srx=tan(π/2−x/2).

Because

π/2−x/2 > π/4+x/2

is equivalent to x<π/4, and tan is strictly increasing on the relevant interval,

srx>cxp.

Therefore srx exceeds all three other primitives throughout O_0. ∎

Theorem 3.II.T7 — Second-octant dominance [O]. On

O_1=(π/4,π/2),

cxp is the unique dominant primitive.

Proof. The same principal-quadrant formulas apply. Again sxp<1 and crx<1, while srx,cxp>1. Now x>π/4, so

π/4+x/2 > π/2−x/2.

Strict monotonicity of tan gives

cxp>srx.

Therefore cxp is uniquely dominant on O_1. ∎

3.II.E — Exact quarter-turn permutation and the full dominance cycle

Theorem 3.II.T8 — Exact primitive cophase permutation [D]. Under a positive cophase step,

srx(x+π/2)=crx(x),

sxp(x+π/2)=cxp(x),

cxp(x+π/2)=sxp(x),

crx(x+π/2)=srx(x).

Thus the induced primitive-label permutation is

P_c=(srx crx)(sxp cxp).

Proof. This is the certified Book 2 quarter-turn transformation theorem. ∎

Corollary 3.II.C6 — Negative cophase gives the same primitive permutation. Since P_c²=id, the induced primitive-label action of +π/2 and −π/2 is identical even though the two directed translations are distinct on 𝕋_{2π}.

This is the primitive-level counterpart of the order reduction proved in 3.I.

Theorem 3.II.T9 — Complete octant dominance cycle [O]. The unique dominant primitive on successive open octants is

O_0: srx,

O_1: cxp,

O_2: crx,

O_3: sxp,

O_4: srx,

O_5: cxp,

O_6: crx,

O_7: sxp.

Equivalently, the label sequence is

srx → cxp → crx → sxp → srx → cxp → crx → sxp.

Proof. The first two cases are Theorems 3.II.T6–T7. A positive cophase step carries O_0 to O_2 and O_1 to O_3. Under Theorem 3.II.T8, the value that was srx at x becomes crx at x+π/2, so crx is uniquely dominant on O_2. Likewise, the value that was cxp becomes sxp, so sxp is uniquely dominant on O_3. Theorem 3.II.T5 then repeats the same four winners on O_4–O_7. ∎

Corollary 3.II.C7 — Cophase acts by two steps in the winner cycle. Define the winner-cycle permutation

C_dom=(srx cxp crx sxp).

Then one octant advancement changes the winner label by C_dom, while a positive or negative cophase step changes it by

C_dom²=(srx crx)(cxp sxp)=P_c.

Thus the exact primitive cophase permutation agrees with two successive dominance-label advances.

Corollary 3.II.C8 — Half-turn acts trivially on winner labels. Four octant advances give

C_dom⁴=id.

Hence the half-turn O_j→O_{j+4} preserves the dominant label, in agreement with pointwise primitive π-periodicity.

3.II.F — Tie points and seam points are different boundaries

Theorem 3.II.T10 — Odd octant boundaries are dominance ties, not primitive seams. At every odd multiple

m_j=(2j+1)π/4,

adjacent dominant primitives tie. These points belong to D.

Proof. At the quadrant midpoint Book 2 proved

srx=cxp

on the first midpoint, with the corresponding quarter-turn images giving the other adjacent-winner equalities. Since sin and cos are both nonzero at odd multiples of π/4, these points lie in D. ∎

Corollary 3.II.C9 — Even and odd octant boundaries have different analytic status. Boundaries at kπ/2 are common primitive seams excluded from D. Boundaries at odd multiples of π/4 are admissible midpoint tie points. Therefore an “octant boundary” is not automatically a singular seam.

3.II.G — Eighth-turn dominance rotation is not an exact primitive symmetry

Theorem 3.II.T11 — One-octant advancement does not generally permute primitive functions exactly. In general,

srx(x+π/4)≠cxp(x).

Proof. It is enough to work on O_0. There

srx(x+π/4)=cot(x/2+π/8),

while

cxp(x)=tan(π/4+x/2).

Using cot u=tan(π/2−u), equality would require

3π/8−x/2=π/4+x/2

within the relevant injective tangent interval, hence

x=π/8.

So equality holds only at the midpoint of O_0, not identically throughout the octant. ∎

Corollary 3.II.C10 — Two discrete layers. Book 3 must distinguish:

1. exact operator symmetry, for which the cophase quarter-turn acts by P_c and the half-turn lies in the kernel;

2. dominance-label rotation, for which η advances the winner through C_dom one octant at a time.

They agree after two octant steps because

C_dom²=P_c,

but a single eighth-turn is not an exact function-substitution symmetry.

3.II.H — Quotient ledger

Theorem 3.II.T12 — Three-level octant quotient theorem. The eight-octant construction contains three related but nonidentical discrete objects:

A. Positional octants:

{O_0,…,O_7}

with faithful C8 action generated by η.

B. Primitive-value octant classes:

{[O_0]_P,[O_1]_P,[O_2]_P,[O_3]_P}

obtained by identifying j with j+4 because of primitive half-turn invariance.

C. Dominant primitive labels:

{srx,cxp,crx,sxp}

advanced by the order-four cycle C_dom.

The natural maps from A to B and from A to C have the half-turn in their kernel, but B and C are not the same object: B consists of phase-region classes, whereas C consists of function labels.

Proof. Parts A–C are Theorems 3.II.T2, 3.II.T5, and 3.II.T9. The distinction between phase regions and labels is definitional. ∎

Corollary 3.II.C11 — Eight sectors do not imply eight operator degrees of freedom. The eight lifted octants encode positional resolution. The primitive quartet remains the same π-periodic one-generator system certified in Book 2.

Negative Theorem 3.II.N1 — Dominance is not dynamics. The statements “srx dominates O_0” and “cxp dominates O_1” are numerical order statements about functions evaluated at phase positions. They do not mean growth, decay, transfer, temporal succession, causal replacement, or physical occupation.

Negative Theorem 3.II.N2 — Cophase is not dominance advancement by one octant. Cophase moves ±2 octants. The one-octant winner rotation is generated by η=τ_{π/4}, not by the cophase quarter-turn.

3.II.I — Source reconciliation and closure ledger

Historical source audit. The prior S0.I/S0.IV phase-symmetry work contained four candidate results relevant here: the eight-octant C8 positional action, the winner sequence srx→cxp→crx→sxp, the half-turn kernel on winner labels, and the warning that a single eighth-turn is not generally an exact primitive permutation. Each has now been re-derived inside the revised Book 1–3 dependency chain rather than inherited as a premise. The historical use of an eighth-turn symbol H is not carried forward because H is canonically reserved in revised Book 2 for the harmonic coordinate.

Section 3.II closure ledger

New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

New persistent notation: η=τ_{π/4}; W for the dominant-primitive map; C_dom for the order-four winner-label cycle; [O_j]_P for the half-turn primitive octant class.

New physics imports: 0.

New projection contracts: 0.

New empirical calibrations: 0.

Historical results admitted without re-derivation: 0.

Proved in 3.II: faithful C8 positional octant action; cophase displacement by ±2 octants; half-turn displacement by 4 octants; primitive half-turn degeneracy; first- and second-octant dominance; complete eight-octant dominance sequence; exact agreement between two-step dominance rotation and the cophase primitive permutation; tie-point versus seam-point distinction; failure of the eighth-turn to define a general exact primitive permutation; and the three-level positional/primitive-class/dominance-label quotient theorem.

Certified handoff to 3.III: the lifted carrier now has a proved order-four cophase orbit and a distinct half-turn kernel. Section 3.III may represent that already-established structure in the signed half-angle projective coordinate t=tan(x/2), but it may not change the carrier order or identify scalar projective reflection with mirror-frame orientation.

3.III — REAL PROJECTIVE PHASE LINE AND THE COPHASE MÖBIUS ORBIT

Purpose

Sections 3.I–3.II established the cophase quarter-turn before introducing projective coordinates. The present section now asks for a coordinate in which that already-proved order-four carrier action becomes fractional-linear.

The signed half-angle coordinate

t=tan(x/2)

provides exactly that representation. The price is one projective point at infinity; the gain is that cophase becomes a Möbius transformation, the π half-turn becomes negative reciprocal inversion, and the four cardinal phase points become the harmonic quadruple

0, 1, ∞, −1.

No new phase law is introduced. The projective formulas must reproduce the carrier order proved in 3.I.

3.III.A — Extended-real projective phase coordinate

Definition 3.III.D1 — Extended real projective line. Let

R_hat=R∪{∞}.

We use R_hat as the standard affine presentation of the real projective line RP1.

Definition 3.III.D2 — Signed half-angle coordinate. For [x]∈T_{2π}, define

t([x])=tan(x/2)

when x is not congruent to π modulo 2π, and define

t([π])=∞.

Theorem 3.III.T1 — Well-defined 2π projective coordinate. The extended coordinate t is well-defined on T_{2π} and gives a bijection

T_{2π} → R_hat ≅ RP1.

Proof. If x and y differ by 2πk, then

y/2=x/2+kπ,

so tan(y/2)=tan(x/2). Thus t is well-defined modulo 2π.

Conversely, for finite values, tan(x/2)=tan(y/2) implies

x/2−y/2∈πZ,

so x−y∈2πZ. The unique pole class occurs at x≡π mod 2π and is represented by ∞. Hence the extended coordinate is one-to-one and onto R_hat. ∎

Corollary 3.III.C1 — Why the sign must be retained. Book 2's positive generator z=srx is complete only modulo π. The signed coordinate t changes under the half-turn and therefore retains the lifted 2π information that z cannot.

3.III.B — Directed cophase Möbius maps

Definition 3.III.D3 — Positive and negative cophase Möbius maps. Define projective maps

Q_+(t)=(1+t)/(1−t),

Q_−(t)=(t−1)/(1+t),

with the usual projective conventions at poles and infinity.

Theorem 3.III.T2 — Positive cophase representation. If

t=tan(x/2),

then

tan[(x+π/2)/2]=Q_+(t).

Proof. Since

(x+π/2)/2=x/2+π/4,

the tangent addition formula gives

tan(x/2+π/4)

=[tan(x/2)+1]/[1−tan(x/2)]

=(1+t)/(1−t). ∎

Theorem 3.III.T3 — Negative cophase representation. If

t=tan(x/2),

then

tan[(x−π/2)/2]=Q_−(t).

Proof. Use

tan(x/2−π/4)

=[tan(x/2)−1]/[1+tan(x/2)]

=(t−1)/(1+t). ∎

Corollary 3.III.C2 — The two cophase directions are inverse projective maps.

Q_−=Q_+^{-1}.

Proof. Direct substitution gives

Q_−(Q_+(t))=t

and

Q_+(Q_−(t))=t

where finite; projective continuation supplies the pole cases. ∎

3.III.C — Matrix representation and exact order

Definition 3.III.D4 — Möbius matrices. Let

A_+ = [[1,1],[-1,1]],

A_- = [[1,-1],[1,1]].

Their projective actions are Q_+ and Q_- respectively.

Theorem 3.III.T4 — Matrix inverse relation. The matrices satisfy

A_+ A_-=A_- A_+=2I.

Therefore A_- represents the projective inverse of A_+.

Proof. Direct multiplication gives

[[1,1],[-1,1]][[1,-1],[1,1]]

=[[2,0],[0,2]].

A nonzero scalar multiple represents the same Möbius transformation, so 2I is projectively the identity. ∎

Theorem 3.III.T5 — Two cophase steps give the half-turn map. The second iterate satisfies

Q_+²(t)=−1/t.

Proof I — Phase derivation. Two positive cophase steps add π. Hence

tan[(x+π)/2]

=tan(x/2+π/2)

=−cot(x/2)

=−1/t.

Proof II — Matrix witness. Direct multiplication gives

A_+²=[[0,2],[-2,0]],

which acts projectively as

t↦2/(-2t)=−1/t. ∎

Corollary 3.III.C3 — Half-turn is not cophase in projective coordinates. The map

t↦−1/t

represents the square of the directed cophase transformation, exactly as 3.I required.

Theorem 3.III.T6 — Four cophase steps give projective identity. One has

Q_+⁴=id.

Moreover Q_+ has exact order four in PGL(2,R).

Proof. Squaring the matrix result,

A_+⁴=(A_+²)²=−4I,

which is projectively the identity. Q_+²(t)=−1/t is not the identity, so the projective order is exactly four. ∎

Corollary 3.III.C4 — Projective order agrees with lifted carrier order. The signed half-angle coordinate preserves the order-four cophase structure of T_{2π}; it does not collapse it to the order-two primitive quotient.

3.III.D — The marked cophase orbit

Theorem 3.III.T7 — Cardinal four-point orbit. Under repeated positive cophase action,

0 → 1 → ∞ → −1 → 0.

Proof.

Q_+(0)=1.

Q_+(1)=∞ because the denominator vanishes.

For a Möbius map (at+b)/(ct+d), the image of ∞ is a/c when c≠0. Thus

Q_+(∞)=1/(−1)=−1.

Finally

Q_+(−1)=0. ∎

Corollary 3.III.C5 — Phase meaning of the marked points. Under t=tan(x/2), the four marked values correspond to

x=0 ↔ t=0,

x=π/2 ↔ t=1,

x=π ↔ t=∞,

x=3π/2 ↔ t=−1,

modulo 2π.

Thus the projective orbit is exactly the four-step cophase orbit proved abstractly in 3.I.

Corollary 3.III.C6 — Seam and midpoint roles in the marked orbit. The values t=0 and t=∞ correspond to sine-zero phase points separated by a half-turn. The values t=±1 correspond to cosine-zero phase points separated by a half-turn. The projective coordinate itself remains complete across the cycle by using infinity as an ordinary projective point.

3.III.E — Harmonic quadruple

Definition 3.III.D5 — Cross-ratio convention. For finite distinct a,b,c,d, define

[a,b;c,d]

=((a−c)(b−d))/((a−d)(b−c)),

with values involving ∞ defined by the corresponding limit.

Definition 3.III.D6 — Harmonic ordered quadruple. An ordered quadruple is harmonic when its cross-ratio equals −1 in the stated convention.

Theorem 3.III.T8 — Harmonicity of the four marked phase points. The ordered quadruple

(0,∞;1,−1)

is harmonic:

[0,∞;1,−1]=−1.

Proof. Taking b→∞ in the declared cross-ratio gives

[0,∞;1,−1]

=(0−1)/(0−(−1))

=−1. ∎

Corollary 3.III.C7 — Projective invariance of the harmonic relation. Since cross-ratio is invariant under Möbius transformations, every projective image of this ordered quadruple remains harmonic.

Clarification 3.III.CL1 — Ordering matters. Permuting the four entries generally changes the numerical cross-ratio among its standard six equivalent values. Therefore “the four points are harmonic” means that they admit the displayed harmonic ordering; it does not mean every ordering has cross-ratio −1.

3.III.F — Scalar reflection and cophase-direction conjugacy

Definition 3.III.D7 — Scalar projective reflection. Define

R(t)=−t.

Theorem 3.III.T9 — Reflection reverses cophase direction by conjugation.

R∘Q_+∘R=Q_−.

Proof. Directly,

R(Q_+(R(t)))

=−[(1−t)/(1+t)]

=(t−1)/(1+t)

=Q_−(t). ∎

Corollary 3.III.C8 — Direction reversal without cophase redefinition. Scalar reflection interchanges the two directed cophase maps but does not change the definition of cophase as the symmetric ±π/2 adjacency relation.

Theorem 3.III.T10 — Projective orientation signs of the basic maps. The Möbius matrix A_+ has determinant +2, while a matrix for R has determinant −1.

Therefore Q_+ lies in the orientation-preserving projective component of RP1, whereas R reverses the standard orientation of RP1.

Proof. Compute

det A_+=1·1−1·(−1)=2>0.

The reflection R(t)=−t is represented by [[−1,0],[0,1]], whose determinant is −1. On the oriented real projective line, the determinant sign distinguishes the two PGL(2,R) components. ∎

Clarification 3.III.CL2 — Scalar reflection is still not automatically a mirror frame. Theorem 3.III.T10 classifies R as orientation-reversing on RP1 itself. The Book 3 term “mirror frame” refers to negative orientation in whatever orientation-bearing frame space is under discussion. No map from RP1 into a physical or higher-dimensional frame space has yet been declared. Hence R may be called projective reflection, but not yet a physical mirror transformation.

3.III.G — Relation to the primitive quotient

Theorem 3.III.T11 — Half-turn information retained by t but erased by Book 2 primitives. Under the π half-turn,

t(x+π)=−1/t(x),

which is generally different from t(x). By contrast, the primitive quartet satisfies

P(x+π)=P(x).

Therefore the signed projective coordinate separates the two lifted phases that the primitive representation identifies.

Proof. The projective transformation is Theorem 3.III.T5. Primitive half-turn invariance is certified in Book 2 and re-used in 3.II. ∎

Corollary 3.III.C9 — Recovery hierarchy. At this stage the revised theory has three distinct phase representations:

1. the lifted carrier T_{2π}, complete modulo 2π;

2. the signed projective coordinate t∈R_hat, also complete modulo 2π;

3. the positive primitive coordinate z=srx, complete only modulo π.

The loss from 1 or 2 to 3 is the half-turn branch, not the cophase relation itself.

Negative Theorem 3.III.N1 — Infinity is a chart value, not a physical singularity. The point t=∞ is the ordinary projective point corresponding to x=π. Its appearance does not imply divergent geometry, energy, dynamics, or a physical seam event.

Negative Theorem 3.III.N2 — Harmonic cross-ratio does not select chirality. The value −1 of the ordered cross-ratio is a projective invariant of four marked phase points. It does not distinguish a preferred physical orientation, mirror frame, handedness, or spin lift.

3.III.H — Wolfram verification and closure ledger

Wolfram independently verified before insertion:

Q_−∘Q_+=id,

Q_+∘Q_−=id,

Q_+²(t)=−1/t,

Q_+⁴(t)=t,

R∘Q_+∘R=Q_−,

[0,∞;1,−1]=−1,

and the matrix identities

A_+²=[[0,2],[-2,0]],

A_+⁴=−4I.

The displayed human-readable derivations, not the computer algebra, remain the proof dependencies of this manuscript.

Section 3.III closure ledger

New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

New persistent notation: R_hat, t, Q_+, Q_−, A_+, A_−, R, and the declared cross-ratio bracket.

New standard-mathematics declarations: extended-real model of RP1; fractional-linear matrix representation; cross-ratio and harmonic quadruple terminology.

New physics imports: 0.

New projection contracts: 0.

New empirical calibrations: 0.

Proved in 3.III: signed half-angle completeness modulo 2π; exact positive and negative cophase Möbius maps; inverse relation; matrix representation; Q_+²=−1/t; exact order four; marked orbit 0→1→∞→−1→0; harmonic cross-ratio; scalar-reflection conjugacy; projective orientation distinction between cophase and reflection; and separation of lifted projective phase from the π-periodic primitive quotient.

Certified handoff to 3.IV: the signed projective coordinate has now supplied the neutral real variable needed to study positive reciprocal compression. Section 3.IV may introduce s=√(1+u²)−u as a new transform of a declared signed real coordinate u, but reciprocal reversal must remain distinct from both cophase and mirror-frame orientation.

3.IV — POSITIVE RECIPROCAL TRANSFORM AND UNIT-THRESHOLD SIGN RECOVERY

Purpose

Section 3.III retained lifted phase orientation by using the signed projective coordinate t=tan(x/2). The present section studies a different task: encoding a signed real affine coordinate u by a strictly positive reciprocal coordinate.

Define

f(u)=√(1+u²)−u.

This transform does not create a new degree of freedom. It is an exact nonlinear reparameterization of one signed real coordinate. Its importance for the revised theory is that the canonical positive reciprocal primitives are all instances of the same map, while the sign discarded from the positive display is recoverable from whether the resulting coordinate lies below or above the unit threshold.

The domain distinction is essential. The transform is a bijection on an affine real chart R. It is not, by itself, a single-valued global coordinate on all of RP1.

3.IV.A — Definition, positivity, and rationalized form

Definition 3.IV.D1 — Positive reciprocal transform. For u∈R define

f(u)=√(1+u²)−u.

When convenient write

s=f(u).

Theorem 3.IV.T1 — Strict positivity. For every real u,

f(u)>0.

Proof. Since

√(1+u²)>|u|≥u,

the difference √(1+u²)−u is strictly positive. ∎

Theorem 3.IV.T2 — Rationalized reciprocal form. For every real u,

f(u)=1/[√(1+u²)+u].

Proof. Multiply by the conjugate:

[√(1+u²)−u][√(1+u²)+u]

=1+u²−u²

=1.

The second factor is positive, so division is valid. ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C1 — No cancellation singularity in the exact identity. The subtractive and rationalized forms describe the same positive function. For large positive u the rationalized form makes the small positive value explicit.

3.IV.B — Exact inverse and bijection

Theorem 3.IV.T3 — Exact inverse formula. If

s=f(u),

then

u=(1−s²)/(2s).

Conversely, for every s>0, substituting

u=(1−s²)/(2s)

into f returns s.

Proof. From

s=√(1+u²)−u

we have

s+u=√(1+u²).

Squaring gives

s²+2su+u²=1+u²,

hence

2su=1−s².

Since s>0,

u=(1−s²)/(2s).

Conversely, let s>0 and set u=(1−s²)/(2s). Then

1+u²

=[4s²+(1−s²)²]/(4s²)

=(1+s²)²/(4s²),

so

√(1+u²)=(1+s²)/(2s).

Therefore

√(1+u²)−u

=[1+s²−(1−s²)]/(2s)

=s. ∎

Theorem 3.IV.T4 — Strict monotonicity. The map f is strictly decreasing on R.

Proof. Differentiate:

f'(u)=u/√(1+u²)−1.

Because |u|<√(1+u²), one has

u/√(1+u²)<1,

so f'(u)<0 for every real u.

Equivalently, using f(u)>0,

f'(u)=−f(u)/√(1+u²)<0. ∎

Theorem 3.IV.T5 — Affine bijection theorem. The map

f:R→(0,∞)

is a bijection.

Proof. Strict monotonicity gives injectivity. The endpoint limits are

lim_{u→+∞}f(u)=0,

lim_{u→−∞}f(u)=+∞.

Since f is continuous, its image is all of (0,∞). The explicit inverse of Theorem 3.IV.T3 proves surjectivity directly as well. ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C2 — Orientation reversal of the affine coordinate. Because f is strictly decreasing, increasing u corresponds to decreasing s. This is a coordinate-order reversal on the affine line, not a mirror-frame theorem.

3.IV.C — Reciprocal reversal

Theorem 3.IV.T6 — Reciprocal reflection identity. For every real u,

f(−u)f(u)=1.

Equivalently,

f(−u)=1/f(u).

Proof.

f(−u)f(u)

=[√(1+u²)+u][√(1+u²)−u]

=1. ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C3 — Signed reflection becomes reciprocal inversion. Under

u→−u,

the positive reciprocal coordinate transforms as

s→1/s.

This is the exact algebraic origin of reciprocal conjugate pairing in the universal transform.

Negative Theorem 3.IV.N1 — Reciprocal inversion is not mirror-frame orientation. The map s→1/s follows from reflecting the signed affine coordinate u→−u. It is an algebraic coordinate involution. No orientation-bearing frame space is involved, so it is not by itself a mirror operation in the canonical Book 3 sense.

3.IV.D — Unit threshold and exact sign recovery

Theorem 3.IV.T7 — Unit-threshold theorem. Let s=f(u). Then

sgn(u)=sgn(1−s²).

More explicitly,

u>0 iff 0<s<1,

u=0 iff s=1,

u<0 iff s>1.

Proof. From the inverse formula,

u=(1−s²)/(2s).

The denominator 2s is strictly positive. Therefore u and 1−s² have exactly the same sign. ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C4 — The positive coordinate retains the lost sign relationally. Although s itself is always positive, the sign of the original affine coordinate is not destroyed: it is encoded by which side of the distinguished value 1 the positive coordinate occupies.

Corollary 3.IV.C5 — Reciprocal exchange about the threshold. The reflection u→−u exchanges

0<s<1

with

s>1

through s→1/s and fixes the threshold s=1.

3.IV.E — Hyperbolic linearization

Definition 3.IV.D2 — Hyperbolic coordinate. Define

λ=arsinh(u).

Theorem 3.IV.T8 — Exponential form. For every real u,

f(u)=e^{−arsinh(u)}=e^{−λ}.

Proof I — logarithmic definition. The standard identity

arsinh(u)=ln[u+√(1+u²)]

gives

e^{−arsinh(u)}

=1/[u+√(1+u²)].

By Theorem 3.IV.T2 this equals f(u).

Proof II — hyperbolic parameterization. Write u=sinh λ. Then

√(1+u²)=cosh λ

for real λ, so

f(u)=cosh λ−sinh λ=e^{−λ}. ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C6 — Logarithmic linearization. Since s=e^{−λ},

λ=−ln s.

Thus the positive multiplicative coordinate s is the exponential image of the signed additive coordinate λ.

Corollary 3.IV.C7 — Reflection in logarithmic form. Because arsinh is odd,

u→−u

implies

λ→−λ,

which in turn gives

s=e^{−λ}→e^{λ}=1/s.

Clarification 3.IV.CL1 — “Hyperbolic” is mathematical, not relativistic. The coordinate λ is an ordinary inverse-hyperbolic-sine parameter. No physical rapidity, velocity, Lorentz boost, or spacetime interpretation is introduced.

3.IV.F — Canonical primitive quartet as one universal transform

The universal map now reproduces the four Book 2 primitive functions directly.

Theorem 3.IV.T9 — Sine-channel universal-transform representation [D_s]. For every x∈D_s,

sxp(x)=f(cot x),

srx(x)=f(−cot x).

Proof. Since

√(1+cot²x)=|csc x|,

we obtain

f(cot x)

=|csc x|−cot x

=sxp(x),

while

f(−cot x)

=|csc x|+cot x

=srx(x). ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C8 — Sine reciprocal conjugacy from one identity.

srx·sxp=1

is exactly the specialization of

f(−u)f(u)=1

at u=cot x.

Theorem 3.IV.T10 — Cosine-channel universal-transform representation [D_c]. For every x∈D_c,

crx(x)=f(tan x),

cxp(x)=f(−tan x).

Proof. Since

√(1+tan²x)=|sec x|,

we have

f(tan x)

=|sec x|−tan x

=crx(x),

and

f(−tan x)

=|sec x|+tan x

=cxp(x). ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C9 — Cosine reciprocal conjugacy from one identity.

cxp·crx=1

is the specialization of the same universal reciprocal-reflection theorem at u=tan x.

Theorem 3.IV.T11 — Primitive logarithmic coordinates. On the natural channel domains,

arsinh(cot x)=ln srx=−ln sxp,

arsinh(tan x)=ln cxp=−ln crx.

Proof. Apply Theorem 3.IV.T8 to s=f(cot x)=sxp and to s=f(tan x)=crx. Then

arsinh(cot x)=−ln sxp.

Since srx=1/sxp,

−ln sxp=ln srx.

The cosine statement is identical. ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C10 — Reciprocal primitive pairs are exponential conjugates. Each channel pair has the form

(e^{λ},e^{−λ})

for its corresponding signed hyperbolic coordinate λ. This is a representation theorem, not a new physical exponential law.

3.IV.G — Recovery of the certified FlatWave threshold rules

Theorem 3.IV.T12 — Signed tangent/cotangent recovery [D]. On the common domain D,

sgn(cot x)=sgn(tan x)=sgn(sin 2x)=FlatWave(x).

Proof. Since

cot x=cos x/sin x,

tan x=sin x/cos x,

and neither denominator vanishes on D, both have sign

sgn(sin x cos x).

Because

sin 2x=2 sin x cos x,

the same sign is sgn(sin 2x), which is the certified Book 2 FlatWave theorem. ∎

Theorem 3.IV.T13 — Four primitive unit-threshold formulas [D]. On D,

FlatWave

=sgn(1−sxp²)

=sgn(srx²−1)

=sgn(1−crx²)

=sgn(cxp²−1).

Proof. Apply Theorem 3.IV.T7 to

sxp=f(cot x)

and

crx=f(tan x).

Since sgn(cot x)=sgn(tan x)=FlatWave,

FlatWave=sgn(1−sxp²)=sgn(1−crx²).

For reciprocal partners,

srx=1/sxp,

cxp=1/crx.

Because sxp and crx are positive,

sgn(srx²−1)=sgn(1−sxp²),

sgn(cxp²−1)=sgn(1−crx²).

Therefore all four expressions equal FlatWave. ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C11 — Unit-threshold status. The distinguished value 1 in the positive primitive charts is not an arbitrary plotting convention. It is the exact image of signed affine coordinate zero under f and therefore the threshold across which the recovered sign changes.

3.IV.H — Affine-chart limitation and projective seam warning

Theorem 3.IV.T14 — The transform is not a single-valued global coordinate on RP1. The map f is a bijection R→(0,∞), but it does not extend continuously as one single value at the projective point ∞∈RP1.

Proof. In the affine coordinate u on RP1, the positive and negative ends represent approach to the same projective point at infinity. However,

lim_{u→+∞}f(u)=0,

while

lim_{u→−∞}f(u)=+∞.

The two directional limits disagree. Therefore no single finite or extended-positive value can make f continuous at the one projective infinity point without retaining directional/chart information. ∎

Corollary 3.IV.C12 — Positive reciprocal coordinates are affine-chart coordinates. The pair of endpoint behaviors

u→+∞ ↦ s→0,

u→−∞ ↦ s→∞

must be glued projectively by a change of chart rather than treated as two distinct global projective states.

Corollary 3.IV.C13 — Primitive zero/pole exchange revisited. The zero/pole behavior of reciprocal primitive pairs is exactly the affine endpoint behavior of the universal transform under u→±∞. Book 2's seam atlas therefore has a projective-coordinate interpretation available for later use, but no physical seam law follows.

Negative Theorem 3.IV.N2 — Positive compression does not prove global regularity. Replacing a signed affine coordinate by the positive variable s removes sign from the displayed value but does not eliminate the projective chart boundary. A positive chart can still require nontrivial gluing.

3.IV.I — Source reconciliation, Wolfram audit, and closure ledger

Historical source reconciliation. The prior Flatwave paper states the exact transform

s=√(1+u²)−u,

its inverse

u=(1−s²)/(2s),

the reciprocal identity f(−u)f(u)=1, the exponential form s=e^{−arsinh u}, and unit-threshold sign recovery. Those claims are re-derived above rather than inherited. The revised section sharpens the source by explicitly limiting f to an affine real chart: its unequal limits at ±∞ prevent it from serving as a single-valued global coordinate at the unique point ∞ of RP1.

Wolfram independently verified before insertion the rationalized formula, both directions of the inverse, reciprocal identity, derivative identity, hyperbolic/exponential form, endpoint limits, and inverse derivative. The displayed proofs remain the manuscript dependencies.

New standard-mathematics declarations: real inverse hyperbolic sine and its logarithmic/exponential identities.

New physics imports: 0.

Historical results admitted without re-derivation: 0.

Certified handoff to 3.V: FlatWave sign recovery from the unit threshold has now been re-derived from a neutral signed-to-positive transform. Section 3.V may combine that result with the already-certified Book 2 phase identity FlatWave=sgn(sin 2x) to prove the cophase–FlatWave character. No bundle, tangent-orientation, mirror-frame, or spin interpretation is yet permitted.

3.V — FIRST CLIMAX: THE COPHASE–FLATWAVE CHARACTER

Purpose

Book 2 proved the binary identity

FlatWave(x)=sgn(sin 2x)

on the common reciprocal-trigonometric domain D. Sections 3.I–3.IV independently fixed the meaning of cophase as Δx=±π/2, distinguished the π half-turn, built the lifted projective phase coordinate, and re-derived the unit-threshold sign rules.

The present section is therefore the first Book 3 climax: it proves exactly how the already-certified FlatWave character transforms under the already-defined cophase action.

The conclusion is deliberately narrow and exact. A cophase quarter-turn reverses FlatWave. A π half-turn preserves it. This gives a genuine one-dimensional sign character of the cophase C4 action. No bundle class, tangent orientation, mirror-frame orientation, spin lift, or physical chirality is inferred here.

3.V.A — Domain invariance under cophase

Definition 3.V.D1 — Directed cophase group. Let

Γ_c=⟨τ_{π/2}⟩

acting on the lifted phase circle. By 3.I,

Γ_c={id,τ_{π/2},τ_π,τ_{3π/2}}

and

Γ_c≅C4.

Theorem 3.V.T1 — The common operator domain is cophase-invariant [D]. If x∈D, then

x+kπ/2∈D

for every integer k.

Proof. The excluded seam set is

Σ={nπ/2:n∈Z}.

If x+kπ/2 belonged to Σ, then

x=(n−k)π/2

would also belong to Σ, contradicting x∈D. ∎

Corollary 3.V.C1 — FlatWave can be compared along the full cophase orbit. Every element of Γ_c maps D to itself, so the Book 2 FlatWave theorem remains valid at every cophase translate of an admissible point.

3.V.B — Trigonometric cophase reversal

Theorem 3.V.T2 — Positive cophase reversal [D]. For every x∈D,

FlatWave(x+π/2)=−FlatWave(x).

Proof. By the certified Book 2 identity,

FlatWave(x+π/2)

=sgn[sin(2x+π)].

Since

sin(2x+π)=−sin 2x,

and sin 2x≠0 on D,

FlatWave(x+π/2)

=−sgn(sin 2x)

=−FlatWave(x). ∎

Theorem 3.V.T3 — Negative cophase reversal [D]. For every x∈D,

FlatWave(x−π/2)=−FlatWave(x).

Proof.

sin(2x−π)=−sin 2x.

Apply the same sign argument. ∎

Corollary 3.V.C2 — Cophase direction does not affect the binary result. Although +π/2 and −π/2 are distinct directed moves on the lifted carrier, both reverse FlatWave.

This agrees with the fact proved in 3.II that the two directed quarter-turns induce the same primitive-label permutation after passage to the π-periodic operator representation.

3.V.C — Half-turn invariance

Theorem 3.V.T4 — Half-turn FlatWave invariance [D]. For every x∈D,

FlatWave(x+π)=FlatWave(x).

Proof. Since

sin(2x+2π)=sin 2x,

one has

sgn[sin(2x+2π)]=sgn(sin 2x). ∎

Corollary 3.V.C3 — Two directed cophase reversals cancel. Because

τ_π=τ_{π/2}²,

two successive positive cophase steps multiply the FlatWave sign by

(−1)²=+1.

Thus the half-turn lies in the kernel of the binary cophase action.

Corollary 3.V.C4 — Four-step orbit. Along the directed lifted cophase orbit

x

→x+π/2

→x+π

→x+3π/2

→x+2π,

FlatWave follows

ε

→−ε

→ε

→−ε

→ε,

where

ε=FlatWave(x).

3.V.D — The cophase sign character

Definition 3.V.D2 — Cophase sign character. Define

χ_c:Γ_c→{−1,+1}

by

χ_c(τ_{kπ/2})=(−1)^k.

Theorem 3.V.T5 — χ_c is a group homomorphism. For all integers k,m,

χ_c(τ_{kπ/2}τ_{mπ/2})

=χ_c(τ_{kπ/2})χ_c(τ_{mπ/2}).

Proof. Translation composition gives

τ_{kπ/2}τ_{mπ/2}=τ_{(k+m)π/2}.

Therefore

χ_c(τ_{(k+m)π/2})

=(−1)^{k+m}

=(−1)^k(−1)^m. ∎

Theorem 3.V.T6 — FlatWave equivariance under Γ_c [D]. For every g∈Γ_c and x∈D,

FlatWave(g·x)=χ_c(g)FlatWave(x).

Proof. Every g is τ_{kπ/2} for some k mod 4. Repeated use of Theorems 3.V.T2 and 3.V.T4 gives the factor (−1)^k. ∎

Corollary 3.V.C5 — Exact kernel. The kernel of χ_c is

ker χ_c={id,τ_π}.

Thus FlatWave detects cophase parity—whether the phase has advanced by an odd or even number of directed quarter-turns—but does not distinguish the identity from the π half-turn.

Corollary 3.V.C6 — Quotient character. The sign character factors Γ_c≅C4 through the quotient

C4/{0,2}≅C2.

This is the exact algebraic sense in which the cophase action appears binary at the FlatWave level.

Clarification 3.V.CL1 — Binary does not mean complete. FlatWave does not reconstruct the four positions of the cophase orbit. It retains only the parity class of the quarter-turn step.

3.V.E — Octant form of the character

Theorem 3.V.T7 — FlatWave is constant on paired octants [O]. For j=0,…,7,

FlatWave|_{O_j}=(-1)^{floor(j/2)}.

Equivalently,

O_0,O_1: +1,

O_2,O_3: −1,

O_4,O_5: +1,

O_6,O_7: −1.

Proof. On O_j, the angle 2x lies in

(jπ/2,(j+1)π/2).

The sign of sin 2x is positive on the first two octants, negative on the next two, and repeats after four octants. The displayed formula records exactly this alternation. ∎

Corollary 3.V.C7 — Cophase-related octants have opposite FlatWave sign.

FlatWave(O_{j±2})=−FlatWave(O_j).

Corollary 3.V.C8 — Half-turn-related octants have equal FlatWave sign.

FlatWave(O_{j+4})=FlatWave(O_j).

Corollary 3.V.C9 — Dominance and FlatWave are different quotients. Using the 3.II dominance sequence,

O_0: srx dominant, FlatWave=+1,

O_1: cxp dominant, FlatWave=+1,

O_2: crx dominant, FlatWave=−1,

O_3: sxp dominant, FlatWave=−1,

with repetition after the half-turn.

Thus FlatWave groups two successive dominance sectors into one sign sector. The dominant-primitive label contains information that FlatWave alone does not retain.

3.V.F — Projective-coordinate proof

The cophase character can also be derived entirely inside the signed half-angle coordinate of 3.III.

Theorem 3.V.T8 — Rational projective form of sin 2x. Let

t=tan(x/2).

Then, wherever the finite t chart applies,

sin 2x

=4t(1−t²)/(1+t²)².

Proof. The standard half-angle formulas give

sin x=2t/(1+t²),

cos x=(1−t²)/(1+t²).

Therefore

sin 2x

=2 sin x cos x

=4t(1−t²)/(1+t²)². ∎

Corollary 3.V.C10 — Projective FlatWave formula. Away from the four marked seam values

t∈{0,1,∞,−1},

FlatWave=sgn[t(1−t²)].

The denominator in Theorem 3.V.T8 is strictly positive for finite real t.

Definition 3.V.D3 — Projective sign polynomial. Let

g(t)=t(1−t²).

Theorem 3.V.T9 — Positive cophase reverses the projective sign polynomial. For

Q_+(t)=(1+t)/(1−t),

one has

g(Q_+(t))

=[−4/(t−1)^4]g(t)

where both sides are defined.

Proof. Direct substitution gives

1−Q_+(t)^2

=[(1−t)^2−(1+t)^2]/(1−t)^2

=−4t/(1−t)^2.

Hence

g(Q_+(t))

=(1+t)/(1−t) · [−4t/(1−t)^2]

=−4t(1+t)/(1−t)^3.

Meanwhile

g(t)=t(1−t)(1+t).

Dividing yields

g(Q_+(t))/g(t)

=−4/(1−t)^4

=−4/(t−1)^4<0.

Therefore the sign reverses. ∎

Theorem 3.V.T10 — Negative cophase also reverses the projective sign polynomial. For

Q_−(t)=(t−1)/(1+t),

one has

g(Q_−(t))

=[−4/(t+1)^4]g(t).

Hence the sign again reverses.

Proof. The calculation is the reciprocal directed analogue of Theorem 3.V.T9 and expands directly to the displayed ratio. The denominator fourth power is positive away from the pole. ∎

Theorem 3.V.T11 — Half-turn preserves the projective sign polynomial. Under

T_π(t)=−1/t,

one has

g(T_π(t))

=(1/t^4)g(t).

Thus the sign is preserved.

Proof.

g(−1/t)

=(−1/t)[1−1/t²]

=(1−t²)/t³.

Since

g(t)=t(1−t²),

we obtain

g(−1/t)=g(t)/t^4.

The factor 1/t^4 is positive. ∎

Corollary 3.V.C11 — Representation independence of the character. The trigonometric proof and the projective rational proof give the same cophase sign law. The result is therefore not an artifact of choosing sin 2x notation or of choosing the t chart.

3.V.G — Unit-threshold proof from the primitive coordinates

Theorem 3.V.T12 — Cophase reverses the primitive threshold character [D]. Using the 3.IV threshold formula

FlatWave=sgn(srx²−1),

a positive cophase step gives

FlatWave(x+π/2)

=sgn[crx(x)²−1]

=−FlatWave(x).

Proof. Book 2 gives

srx(x+π/2)=crx(x).

Section 3.IV gives

FlatWave(x)=sgn[1−crx(x)²].

Therefore

sgn[crx(x)²−1]

=−FlatWave(x). ∎

Corollary 3.V.C12 — Three exact proofs of the first climax. Cophase reversal of FlatWave has now been proved by:

1. double-angle trigonometry;

2. signed projective Möbius algebra;

3. positive reciprocal primitive threshold algebra.

All three depend on previously certified mathematics and agree exactly.

3.V.H — What the climax does not prove

Negative Theorem 3.V.N1 — FlatWave is not yet a Stiefel–Whitney class. The existence of the homomorphism

χ_c:C4→{±1}

and the sign reversal under quarter-turn do not by themselves identify FlatWave with a Čech cocycle, w1 of a line bundle, or tangent orientation of a projective manifold.

Such an identification would require a separately constructed atlas, transition functions, bundle, and explicit pullback comparison.

Negative Theorem 3.V.N2 — FlatWave is not mirror orientation. Cophase translations are orientation-preserving on the phase circle by 3.I, yet they reverse FlatWave. Therefore FlatWave sign cannot simply be equated with the orientation sign of the phase circle or with the canonical Book 3 mirror-frame label.

Negative Theorem 3.V.N3 — FlatWave does not select physical chirality. The sign character records cophase parity within the present mathematical structure. No physical handedness, weak-interaction chirality, fermion state, or parity-violating law follows.

sin[2(x+π)]=sin 2x,

g(Q_−(t))/g(t)=−4/(t+1)^4,

g(−1/t)/g(t)=1/t^4.

The displayed derivations remain the formal proof dependencies.
