Certified handoff to 3.VI: the cophase–FlatWave sign character is now complete without topology. Section 3.VI may build the pairwise RP2 ratio atlas and seam taxonomy, but it may not assume that its eventual transition-sign cocycle is FlatWave. That comparison remains a later theorem test.

3.VI — PAIRWISE RECIPROCAL EXTENSION, RP² ATLAS, AND SEAM TAXONOMY

Purpose

The first Book 3 climax was completed entirely on the one-dimensional phase carrier. The present section begins the genuinely higher-rank extension.

The task is deliberately neutral. We take ordinary homogeneous coordinates on RP², form signed pairwise ratios, pass each signed ratio through the positive reciprocal transform of 3.IV, and prove that the resulting positive charts glue exactly because they are conjugates of the standard projective affine atlas.

Three distinctions are enforced throughout.

First, the cyclic three-ratio compatibility and its V4 sign pattern exist only where all three homogeneous coordinates are nonzero.

Second, the unit threshold σ=1 records the zero of a signed ratio; it is not automatically a mirror or orientation reversal.

Third, reciprocal conjugation u→−u and projective denominator exchange u→1/u are different operations and must not be conflated.

3.VI.A — Standard projective plane and affine cover

Standard declaration 3.VI.S1 — Real projective plane. RP² is the set of nonzero triples

(X,Y,Z)∈R³\{0}

modulo nonzero real rescaling,

(X,Y,Z)~(λX,λY,λZ),  λ≠0.

A projective point is written

[X:Y:Z].

Definition 3.VI.D1 — Standard affine cover. Define

U_X={ [X:Y:Z] : X≠0 },

U_Y={ [X:Y:Z] : Y≠0 },

U_Z={ [X:Y:Z] : Z≠0 }.

These three sets cover RP² because a nonzero homogeneous triple has at least one nonzero coordinate.

Definition 3.VI.D2 — Dense triple-overlap region. Define

P°=U_X∩U_Y∩U_Z

={ [X:Y:Z] : XYZ≠0 }.

This is the region on which every cyclic pairwise ratio used below is simultaneously defined.

3.VI.B — Cyclic pairwise ratios and exact compatibility

Definition 3.VI.D3 — Cyclic signed ratios [P°]. On P° define

u_XY=Y/X,

u_YZ=Z/Y,

u_ZX=X/Z.

Theorem 3.VI.T1 — Cyclic product identity [P°].

u_XY·u_YZ·u_ZX=1.

Proof.

(Y/X)(Z/Y)(X/Z)=1

because X,Y,Z are all nonzero on P°. ∎

Theorem 3.VI.T2 — Two signed ratios are complete on P°. The map

[X:Y:Z] ↦ (u_XY,u_YZ)

is a bijection

P° → (R*)²,

where R*=R\{0}.

Its inverse is

(a,b) ↦ [1:a:ab].

Proof. For a projective point in P°, rescale by X^{-1} to obtain

[1:Y/X:Z/X].

Since

Z/X=(Z/Y)(Y/X)=u_YZ u_XY,

the point is

[1:u_XY:u_XY u_YZ].

Thus the two ratios reconstruct the projective point uniquely. Conversely, for nonzero a,b, the point [1:a:ab] lies in P° and has ratios u_XY=a and u_YZ=b. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C1 — Exact continuous rank. The cyclic three-ratio system has exactly two independent continuous coordinates on P°. The third ratio is

u_ZX=1/(u_XY u_YZ).

This is a projective rank statement, not a new physical degree count.

3.VI.C — Positive reciprocal image of the cyclic ratio surface

Definition 3.VI.D4 — Inverse positive transform. Let

φ(σ)=f^{-1}(σ)=(1−σ²)/(2σ),

for σ>0.

Definition 3.VI.D5 — Cyclic positive reciprocal coordinates [P°]. Define

σ_XY=f(u_XY),

σ_YZ=f(u_YZ),

σ_ZX=f(u_ZX).

Each σ is strictly positive and, on P°, none equals 1 because none of the corresponding signed ratios vanishes.

Theorem 3.VI.T3 — Reciprocal compatibility surface [P°]. The three positive coordinates satisfy

(1−σ_XY²)(1−σ_YZ²)(1−σ_ZX²)

=8σ_XYσ_YZσ_ZX.

Proof. By Definition 3.VI.D4,

u_XY=φ(σ_XY),

u_YZ=φ(σ_YZ),

u_ZX=φ(σ_ZX).

Insert these into Theorem 3.VI.T1:

[(1−σ_XY²)/(2σ_XY)]

[(1−σ_YZ²)/(2σ_YZ)]

[(1−σ_ZX²)/(2σ_ZX)]

=1.

Multiplying by the positive denominator

8σ_XYσ_YZσ_ZX

gives the stated equation. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C2 — The high-degree surface is only a reparameterization. The reciprocal compatibility equation introduces no additional constraint beyond the ordinary projective identity u_XY u_YZ u_ZX=1.

Theorem 3.VI.T4 — Two-dimensional smooth parametrization of the reciprocal surface. On P°, choose arbitrary nonzero a,b and set

σ_XY=f(a),

σ_YZ=f(b),

σ_ZX=f(1/(ab)).

This parametrizes the reciprocal compatibility surface bijectively by (a,b)∈(R*)².

Proof. Theorem 3.VI.T2 parametrizes P° by (a,b). Applying the bijection f coordinatewise to the three cyclic ratios gives the displayed positive triple. Since f is invertible, no information is lost. ∎

3.VI.D — Relative-sign chambers and the V4 pattern

Definition 3.VI.D6 — Relative ratio signs [P°]. Define

ε_XY=sgn(u_XY),

ε_YZ=sgn(u_YZ),

ε_ZX=sgn(u_ZX).

By 3.IV,

ε_IJ=sgn(1−σ_IJ²)

for every cyclic ratio on P°.

Theorem 3.VI.T5 — Relative-sign product constraint [P°].

ε_XY ε_YZ ε_ZX=+1.

Proof. Take the sign of the positive identity

u_XY u_YZ u_ZX=1. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C3 — Four allowed sign triples. The possible triples are exactly

(+,+,+),

(+,-,-),

(-,+,-),

(-,-,+).

Under componentwise sign multiplication these four triples form a group isomorphic to the Klein four group V4.

Theorem 3.VI.T6 — The four sign triples are the four connected chambers of P°. Since

P°≅(R*)²,

its connected components are labeled by the independent signs of u_XY and u_YZ. The third sign is then fixed by Theorem 3.VI.T5.

Proof. R* has two connected components, positive and negative. Therefore (R*)² has four. The ratio bijection of Theorem 3.VI.T2 transfers this decomposition to P°. ∎

Clarification 3.VI.CL1 — Relative sign is not yet FlatWave. The symbols ε_IJ in this section mean only signs of homogeneous-coordinate ratios. Although the historical Flatwave paper called these generalized FlatWave characters, revised Book 3 does not make that identification here. Section 3.VII will test whether an explicit map justifies the comparison.

3.VI.E — Full affine coordinates and positive reciprocal charts

Definition 3.VI.D7 — Ordinary affine coordinates.

On U_X define

u=Y/X,

v=Z/X.

On U_Y define

p=X/Y,

q=Z/Y.

On U_Z define

r=X/Z,

h=Y/Z.

Each ordered pair ranges over R².

Definition 3.VI.D8 — Positive reciprocal affine charts. Define

Φ_X([X:Y:Z])=(σ_XY,σ_XZ)

=(f(Y/X),f(Z/X))

on U_X,

Φ_Y=(σ_YX,σ_YZ)

=(f(X/Y),f(Z/Y))

on U_Y,

and

Φ_Z=(σ_ZX,σ_ZY)

=(f(X/Z),f(Y/Z))

on U_Z.

Theorem 3.VI.T7 — Each reciprocal chart is a positive quadrant. Every map

Σ_I:U_I→(0,∞)²

is a smooth bijective coordinate chart.

Proof. The ordinary affine chart U_I→R² is a bijection. Section 3.IV proved that f:R→(0,∞) is a smooth strictly monotone bijection with smooth inverse φ. Applying f independently to the two affine coordinates gives a smooth bijection R²→(0,∞)². ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C4 — Three positive quadrants cover RP². The full projective plane is covered by three positive reciprocal coordinate charts, but no single one of those charts is global.

3.VI.F — Ordinary projective transition maps

Theorem 3.VI.T8 — U_X to U_Y transition. On U_X∩U_Y, where u=Y/X≠0,

(p,q)=(1/u,v/u).

Proof.

p=X/Y=1/(Y/X)=1/u,

q=Z/Y=(Z/X)/(Y/X)=v/u. ∎

Theorem 3.VI.T9 — U_Y to U_Z transition. On U_Y∩U_Z, where q=Z/Y≠0,

(r,h)=(p/q,1/q).

Proof.

r=X/Z=(X/Y)/(Z/Y)=p/q,

h=Y/Z=1/q. ∎

Theorem 3.VI.T10 — U_Z to U_X transition. On U_Z∩U_X, where r=X/Z≠0,

(u,v)=(h/r,1/r).

Proof.

u=Y/X=(Y/Z)/(X/Z)=h/r,

v=Z/X=1/r. ∎

Theorem 3.VI.T11 — Triple-overlap consistency. On P°,

T_ZX∘T_YZ∘T_XY=id.

Proof. Starting with (u,v),

(u,v)

→(1/u,v/u)

→((1/u)/(v/u),1/(v/u))

→(u,v).

Explicitly the middle pair is

(1/v,u/v),

and applying T_ZX gives

[(u/v)/(1/v),1/(1/v)]=(u,v). ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C5 — No new gluing law is required. The atlas compatibility is ordinary projective compatibility. The reciprocal representation will inherit it by coordinate conjugation.

3.VI.G — Exact reciprocal chart transitions

Theorem 3.VI.T12 — Reciprocal transition is conjugated projective transition. Let

F(a,b)=(f(a),f(b)),

F^{-1}(α,β)=(φ(α),φ(β)).

Then on every affine overlap,

S_IJ=F∘T_IJ∘F^{-1}.

In particular, on U_X∩U_Y,

σ_YX

=f(1/φ(σ_XY)),

σ_YZ

=f(φ(σ_XZ)/φ(σ_XY)).

Proof. The positive chart is defined by applying f coordinatewise to the ordinary affine chart. Changing reciprocal charts therefore means: recover the signed ordinary ratios with φ, apply the standard projective transition, then apply f again. This is exactly the displayed conjugation. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C6 — Reciprocal transition consistency. On the triple overlap,

S_ZX∘S_YZ∘S_XY=id.

Proof. Conjugate Theorem 3.VI.T11 by F. ∎

Theorem 3.VI.T13 — Denominator exchange is not reciprocal conjugation. For real u≠0,

f(1/u)≠1/f(u).

Proof. If equality held, then by 3.IV

1/f(u)=f(−u).

Since f is injective,

1/u=−u,

so u²=−1, impossible for real u. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C7 — Two different reversals. The operations

u→−u

and

u→1/u

have different meanings:

u→−u gives σ→1/σ,

whereas denominator exchange u→1/u gives σ→f(1/φ(σ)).

A minus-sign reciprocal conjugation and a projective chart change must not be identified.

3.VI.H — Unit thresholds and projective chart boundaries

Theorem 3.VI.T14 — Unit-threshold locus is a regular coordinate hyperplane. On U_X,

σ_XY=1

iff

Y=0.

Likewise σ_XZ=1 iff Z=0, and cyclic analogues hold on the other charts.

Proof. By 3.IV,

f(u)=1 iff u=0.

On U_X, u=Y/X and X≠0, so u=0 iff Y=0. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C8 — The unit threshold is regular in the chart that sees it. The value σ_XY=1 is an interior point of the positive coordinate quadrant (0,∞)². It is not a singularity of RP².

Clarification 3.VI.CL2 — But it is the boundary of a particular overlap. When Y=0, the point remains in U_X but leaves U_Y. Thus σ_XY=1 is regular in U_X while marking where the U_X∩U_Y transition ceases to exist.

Theorem 3.VI.T15 — Zero/infinity endpoint hands off to unit threshold in the denominator-swapped chart. Suppose a projective path stays with Y≠0 while X→0. Then

u=Y/X→+∞ or −∞,

so

σ_XY=f(u)→0 or +∞.

In the U_Y chart,

p=X/Y=1/u→0,

hence

σ_YX=f(p)→1.

Proof. This is the endpoint behavior of f proved in 3.IV together with p=1/u. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C9 — Reciprocal zero/pole is a chart endpoint, not a projective state divergence. The same regular projective point that appears as σ_XY→0 or ∞ when U_X fails appears at the finite unit value σ_YX=1 in the neighboring denominator chart.

Clarification 3.VI.CL3 — Simultaneous numerator and denominator zeros require a third chart. At a point such as [0:0:1], neither U_X nor U_Y is valid. U_Z supplies the regular coordinates. Therefore no single pairwise ratio controls every approach to a coordinate vertex.

3.VI.I — Scope of the V4 chamber signs

Negative Theorem 3.VI.N1 — The V4 sign triple is not defined on all of RP². The three signs ε_XY,ε_YZ,ε_ZX require XYZ≠0. When a homogeneous coordinate vanishes, at least one cyclic ratio is zero and another has a forbidden denominator, so the triple leaves its domain.

Therefore the V4 pattern labels the four chambers of P°, not four global states covering the coordinate hyperplanes.

Negative Theorem 3.VI.N2 — Crossing σ_IJ=1 is a ratio-sign reversal, not yet a mirror reversal. Crossing the unit threshold changes the sign of the signed ratio u_IJ. Mirror in the canonical Book 3 sense concerns orientation of an ordered frame. No theorem in 3.VI identifies those two notions.

3.VI.J — Local Jacobian signs without global orientation promotion

Theorem 3.VI.T16 — Jacobians of the ordinary chart transitions. With the coordinate orderings fixed in Definition 3.VI.D7,

J_XY=det ∂(p,q)/∂(u,v)=−u^{-3},

J_YZ=det ∂(r,h)/∂(p,q)=−q^{-3},

J_ZX=det ∂(u,v)/∂(r,h)=r^{-3}.

Proof. Differentiate the explicit maps of Theorems 3.VI.T8–T10 and take the 2×2 determinants. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C10 — Triple Jacobian consistency. On P°, after evaluating each determinant at the transformed coordinates,

J_XY J_YZ J_ZX=+1,

as required because the triple transition is the identity.

Corollary 3.VI.C11 — Ratio sign and chart-frame sign are not literally the same object. For example,

sgn J_XY=−sgn(u)=−ε_XY,

while the fixed sign relating other chart orderings depends on the chosen coordinate ordering. Thus a later orientation class must be constructed from the actual transition frames, not by renaming ε_IJ without proof.

Clarification 3.VI.CL4 — Local orientation comparison is allowed; global orientability is deferred. The sign of a Jacobian compares two ordered coordinate frames on an overlap. Section 3.VIII, not the present section, will determine whether those local choices can be made globally consistent on RP² or RP^n.

3.VI.K — Reconciliation with the Book 2 primitive seams

Theorem 3.VI.T17 — Sine-channel seam as affine ratio chart boundary. Section 3.IV proved

sxp=f(cot x),

srx=f(−cot x).

At a sine-zero seam, cot x=cos x/sin x approaches ±∞ from the two sides. Hence the sine reciprocal pair approaches a zero/pole pair exactly as the positive reciprocal chart approaches its 0/∞ endpoints.

Meanwhile tan x→0, so

crx=f(tan x)→1,

cxp=f(−tan x)→1.

Thus the opposite channel reaches its unit threshold.

Theorem 3.VI.T18 — Cosine-channel seam as affine ratio chart boundary. At a cosine-zero seam, tan x approaches ±∞, so the cosine reciprocal pair undergoes the corresponding zero/pole exchange, while cot x→0 and the sine pair approaches 1.

Proof. Apply the same universal-transform limits with the sine and cosine ratios exchanged. ∎

Corollary 3.VI.C12 — Book 2 seam taxonomy has a projective-coordinate explanation. An own-channel primitive divergence corresponds to failure of one signed affine ratio chart; the reciprocal partner approaches zero, and the opposite channel remains finite at the unit threshold.

Negative Theorem 3.VI.N3 — Projective regularity does not redefine the raw primitive domains. The fact that the underlying projective ratio can be represented regularly in another chart does not assign a finite value to |csc x|±cot x at sin x=0 or to |sec x|±tan x at cos x=0. Book 2's domain exclusions and one-sided boundary theorems remain unchanged.

Negative Theorem 3.VI.N4 — Projective gluing is not seam dynamics. A change of coordinate chart specifies how one mathematical state is represented across overlapping descriptions. It does not prescribe a physical trajectory, crossing law, creation/annihilation event, or temporal transition at a Book 2 seam.

3.VI.L — Cophase and mirror remain external to the generic RP² atlas

Negative Theorem 3.VI.N5 — No generic RP² cophase action has yet been defined. Cophase is the ±π/2 relation on the lifted one-dimensional phase carrier. No canonical map from all of T_{2π} into arbitrary RP² has been declared in this section. Therefore the generic pairwise atlas does not inherit a cophase operation merely because both constructions use projective ratios.

Negative Theorem 3.VI.N6 — No generic mirror classification follows from ε_IJ. A relative homogeneous-coordinate sign, a reciprocal threshold side, and a local Jacobian sign are distinct data. A mirror frame requires an orientation-bearing frame space and a declared orientation, as fixed in 3.I.

3.VI.M — Prior-paper reconciliation, Wolfram audit, and closure ledger

Historical source audit. The prior Flatwave paper supplied the candidate cyclic ratios

u_XY=Y/X,

u_YZ=Z/Y,

u_ZX=X/Z,

the product constraint, the positive reciprocal compatibility surface, the four allowed sign triples, the three-chart positive atlas, and the ordinary U_X→U_Y Jacobian. Those formulas survive re-derivation. The revised section sharpens their status in four ways:

1. the cyclic ratio triple and V4 signs are restricted explicitly to P°={XYZ≠0};

2. ε_IJ is called a relative ratio sign, not FlatWave, until the comparison test of 3.VII;

3. σ_IJ=1 is classified as a regular ratio-zero threshold rather than an automatic orientation/mirror reversal;

4. the reciprocal chart transitions are written explicitly as conjugates of the standard projective transition maps.

Wolfram independently verified before insertion the cyclic product, reciprocal compatibility equation, the three ordinary transition Jacobians, triple transition consistency, and the product-one Jacobian consistency on the triple overlap.

Section 3.VI closure ledger

New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

New standard-mathematics declarations: RP² homogeneous coordinates and the standard affine cover.

New persistent notation: [X:Y:Z], U_X,U_Y,U_Z, P°, u_IJ, σ_IJ, φ=f^{-1}, ε_IJ, and the reciprocal chart maps Φ_X,Φ_Y,Φ_Z.

New physics imports: 0.

New projection contracts: 0.

New empirical calibrations: 0.

Historical results admitted without re-derivation: 0.

Proved in 3.VI: cyclic ratio product; two-ratio completeness on P°; exact reciprocal compatibility surface; exact two-dimensional parametrization; V4 relative-sign chamber structure; full three-chart positive atlas; ordinary and reciprocal transition maps; triple-overlap consistency; distinction between denominator inversion and reciprocal conjugation; unit-threshold regularity; 0/∞-to-unit chart handoff; local Jacobian signs; reconciliation with Book 2 zero/pole seams; and strict separation from cophase, mirror, topology, and physical seam dynamics.

Certified handoff to 3.VII: the affine cover, overlaps, ratio transition functions, and relative signs now exist independently of topology. Section 3.VII may use these data to construct the tautological-line transition functions and ask whether their Z2 class pulls back to the already-certified FlatWave character. It may not assume that identification in advance.

3.VII — TRANSITION-SIGN COCYCLE, TAUTOLOGICAL LINE BUNDLE, AND THE FLATWAVE COMPARISON TEST

Purpose

Section 3.VI constructed the ordinary RP² atlas, its reciprocal reparameterization, and the pairwise ratio signs without assigning them topological meaning. The present section now introduces one standard topological object: the tautological real line bundle γ over RP².

The dependency order is strict:

projective atlas

→ local line frames

→ real transition scalars

→ transition signs

→ Z2 cocycle

→ w1(γ)

→ FlatWave comparison.

The result separates two structures that the historical literature had allowed to blur together. The projective ratio-transition signs do represent the first Stiefel–Whitney class of γ. The already-proved FlatWave cophase character does not, under the natural half-angle projective identification, equal that class or its transition cocycle.

3.VII.A — The tautological real line bundle

Standard Definition 3.VII.SD1 — Tautological line bundle. Define

γ={([X:Y:Z],v)∈RP²×R³ : v∈span(X,Y,Z)}.

The fiber γ_[X:Y:Z] is the one-dimensional real vector subspace represented by the projective point [X:Y:Z].

Definition 3.VII.D1 — Canonical local line frames. On the standard affine charts define

e_X([X:Y:Z])=(1,Y/X,Z/X),

e_Y([X:Y:Z])=(X/Y,1,Z/Y),

e_Z([X:Y:Z])=(X/Z,Y/Z,1).

Each e_I is a nowhere-zero vector spanning the tautological line over U_I and therefore gives a local trivializing frame of γ.

Theorem 3.VII.T1 — Exact local-frame transition scalars. On pairwise overlaps,

e_Y=(X/Y)e_X,

e_Z=(Y/Z)e_Y,

e_X=(Z/X)e_Z.

Equivalently,

e_Y=u_YX e_X,

e_Z=u_ZY e_Y,

e_X=u_XZ e_Z.

Proof. For example,

(X/Y)e_X

=(X/Y)(1,Y/X,Z/X)

=(X/Y,1,Z/Y)

=e_Y.

The other two identities follow cyclically. ∎

Corollary 3.VII.C1 — The tautological bundle uses the same projective ratios already constructed in 3.VI. No new Projection-specific transition law has been added.

3.VII.B — Transition signs and the Z2 cocycle

Definition 3.VII.D2 — Tautological transition sign. On U_I∩U_J define

ζ_JI=sgn(g_JI),

where e_J=g_JI e_I.

Thus, explicitly,

ζ_YX=sgn(X/Y),

ζ_ZY=sgn(Y/Z),

ζ_XZ=sgn(Z/X).

Each ζ_JI takes values in {−1,+1}.

Theorem 3.VII.T2 — Sign reciprocity. On every pairwise overlap,

ζ_JI=ζ_IJ.

Proof. The reverse transition scalar is g_IJ=1/g_JI. A nonzero real number and its reciprocal have the same sign. ∎

Theorem 3.VII.T3 — Čech cocycle identity on the triple overlap. On P°,

ζ_ZY ζ_YX=ζ_ZX.

Equivalently,

ζ_XY ζ_YZ ζ_ZX=+1.

Proof. The real transition scalars obey

g_ZY g_YX

=(Y/Z)(X/Y)

=X/Z

=g_ZX.

Taking signs gives the first identity. Multiplying by ζ_XZ=ζ_ZX gives the second. ∎

Corollary 3.VII.C2 — The V4 ratio-sign pattern of 3.VI is the sign part of the tautological transition system. On the common domain,

ζ_YX=ε_XY,

ζ_ZY=ε_YZ,

ζ_XZ=ε_ZX,

because reciprocal ratios have the same sign.

This identifies the origin of the V4 sign product without identifying it with FlatWave.

3.VII.C — Standard topological identification

Standard Import 3.VII.SI1 — First Stiefel–Whitney class of a real line bundle. For a real line bundle with nonzero real transition functions g_JI, the signs

sgn(g_JI)∈{±1}≅Z2

form a Čech 1-cocycle. Its cohomology class is the first Stiefel–Whitney class w1 of the line bundle.

Definition 3.VII.D3 — Tautological class. Define

a=w1(γ)∈H¹(RP²;Z2).

Theorem 3.VII.T4 — Ratio-sign realization of a. The transition-sign cocycle ζ constructed in 3.VII.B represents

a=w1(γ).

Proof. The frames e_X,e_Y,e_Z are local trivializations of γ, and their nonzero transition scalars are exactly the g_JI of Theorem 3.VII.T1. Standard Import 3.VII.SI1 therefore identifies the sign cocycle of those transitions with w1(γ). ∎

Clarification 3.VII.CL1 — What is standard and what is revised. The definition and characteristic-class interpretation of w1 are standard topology. The revised Projection-specific contribution is only the explicit route

positive reciprocal coordinates

→ signed projective ratios

→ tautological transition scalars

→ transition signs.

No new characteristic class has been invented.

3.VII.D — Direct nontriviality test on a projective line

Definition 3.VII.D4 — Distinguished projective line. Let

L_Z={ [X:Y:0] }⊂RP².

Then L_Z≅RP¹.

Definition 3.VII.D5 — Natural half-angle projective map. Define

ρ:T_{2π}→L_Z

by

ρ([x])=[cos(x/2):sin(x/2):0].

Theorem 3.VII.T5 — ρ is a bijective projective phase representation. The map is well-defined modulo 2π and gives the same signed half-angle coordinate

t=tan(x/2)=Y/X

on the finite X-chart.

Proof. Replacing x by x+2π multiplies both homogeneous coordinates by −1, which leaves the projective point unchanged. Conversely, equality of the projective points means the two half-angle vectors differ by a nonzero scalar; after normalization they differ by sign, corresponding exactly to x differing by 2π modulo the phase circle. The affine ratio is sin(x/2)/cos(x/2)=tan(x/2). ∎

Theorem 3.VII.T6 — Tautological sign monodromy on the phase loop. Let

v(x)=(cos(x/2),sin(x/2),0).

Then

v(x+2π)=−v(x).

Consequently the pulled-back tautological line ρ*γ over T_{2π} is nontrivial.

Proof. The first identity follows from the half-angle shift by π.

Suppose ρ*γ had a continuous nowhere-zero global section. Along the lifted interval write it as

s(x)=c(x)v(x)

with continuous c(x)≠0. Since x and x+2π represent the same base point, periodicity of the section requires

s(x+2π)=s(x).

But v(x+2π)=−v(x), so

c(x+2π)=−c(x).

A continuous real function taking nonzero values cannot change sign between the endpoints without vanishing somewhere. Contradiction. Hence the pulled-back line bundle is nontrivial. ∎

Corollary 3.VII.C3 — Nontrivial tautological class. The restriction of a=w1(γ) to L_Z is nonzero. Therefore a itself is nonzero on RP².

Corollary 3.VII.C4 — The sign cocycle contains genuinely global information. Although each local line frame is perfectly regular, no single nowhere-zero frame trivializes γ over the projective loop.

3.VII.E — Explicit pullback transition sign

Theorem 3.VII.T7 — Natural phase pullback of the tautological transition sign. On the overlap where

sin(x/2)cos(x/2)≠0,

the X-to-Y tautological transition sign pulled back by ρ is

ρ*ζ_YX

=sgn[cot(x/2)]

=sgn[tan(x/2)].

Proof. Along ρ,

X=cos(x/2),

Y=sin(x/2).

Therefore

g_YX=X/Y=cot(x/2).

A nonzero number and its reciprocal have the same sign, so this also equals sgn[tan(x/2)]. ∎

Corollary 3.VII.C5 — The tautological transition threshold on the natural phase line occurs at t=0 or t=∞, according to which affine frame fails. It is not the same four-seam pattern as FlatWave.

3.VII.F — FlatWave comparison test

Theorem 3.VII.T8 — Pointwise comparison in the signed projective coordinate. On the finite t chart away from

t∈{0,±1},

one has

FlatWave=sgn[t(1−t²)],

while the tautological X/Y transition sign is

ρ*ζ_YX=sgn(t).

Therefore

FlatWave

=(ρ*ζ_YX)·sgn(1−t²).

Proof. The FlatWave formula is Theorem 3.V.T8–C10. The transition-sign formula is Theorem 3.VII.T7. Multiply the two sign factors. ∎

Corollary 3.VII.C6 — FlatWave and the natural tautological transition sign agree only on |t|<1 and disagree on |t|>1. They are not the same pointwise sign function.

Clarification 3.VII.CL2 — Pointwise inequality alone is not yet the decisive topological test, because a change of local frame can alter a representative Čech cocycle by a coboundary. The global comparison must therefore use the type and monodromy of the two constructions.

3.VII.G — Type and monodromy obstruction to the historical identification

Theorem 3.VII.T9 — Type separation. The two binary objects constructed so far live in different mathematical categories:

χ_c∈Hom(C4,{±1})

is a group character of the directed cophase action, whereas

a=w1(γ)∈H¹(RP²;Z2)

is a cohomology class of a real line bundle over projective space.

No canonical equality between them is defined by their codomains alone.

Proof. Their domains and functorial meanings differ. χ_c assigns signs to symmetry elements. The class a assigns a cohomology obstruction to compatible local line frames. An equality would require an explicit construction relating the action group data to the bundle cohomology data. No such construction has been supplied. ∎

Theorem 3.VII.T10 — Natural comparison obstruction. Under the natural half-angle map ρ, one full 2π traversal of the phase circle has tautological lift sign −1, while the product of the four FlatWave cophase multipliers is +1.

Proof. The tautological lift satisfies

v(x+2π)=−v(x)

by Theorem 3.VII.T6.

For FlatWave, each directed cophase step contributes the character factor

χ_c(τ_{π/2})=−1.

Four such steps complete the 2π phase circuit, so their product is

(−1)^4=+1.

Therefore the natural tautological monodromy and the FlatWave cophase parity do not coincide. ∎

Corollary 3.VII.C7 — Naive FlatWave=w1(γ) identification fails. Under the most direct projective phase identification supplied by Book 3, FlatWave cophase parity is not the tautological first Stiefel–Whitney class.

Negative Theorem 3.VII.N1 — FlatWave is not promoted to a Čech cocycle. FlatWave remains the certified phase sign function and cophase character of 3.V. It is not renamed w1(γ), a, or ζ.

Theorem 3.VII.T11 — What survives from the historical claim. The projective ratio-transition sign cocycle ε/ζ does represent w1(γ). The FlatWave character χ_c does not.

Thus the correct structural chain is

ratio signs ε_IJ

= tautological transition signs ζ_JI

→ w1(γ),

while

FlatWave

→ χ_c

remains a separate binary phase structure.

Proof. The first chain is Theorems 3.VII.T1–T4. The second is the first climax, Theorems 3.V.T5–T6. Their nonidentification is Theorems 3.VII.T9–T10. ∎

3.VII.H — Orientation terminology remains deferred

Negative Theorem 3.VII.N2 — w1(γ) is not yet the tangent-orientation class of RP². The class a=w1(γ) belongs to the tautological line bundle. The tangent bundle T(RP²) is a different vector bundle. Its first Stiefel–Whitney class must be derived from the tangent-bundle relation in 3.VIII.

Negative Theorem 3.VII.N3 — Tautological line reversal is not mirror-frame orientation. A sign change in a one-dimensional bundle frame records reversal of that line frame. The Book 3 term mirror concerns negative orientation of a declared orientation-bearing frame space. No identification is made here.

Negative Theorem 3.VII.N4 — Nontrivial w1 does not select a preferred sign. The class w1 records an obstruction to globally consistent orientation of the line fibers. It does not choose which local sign should be called physically positive.

3.VII.I — Prior-paper reconciliation and closure ledger

Historical source audit. The prior Flatwave paper proposed the transition-sign product

ε_XY ε_YZ ε_ZX=1

and identified the resulting Z2 cocycle with w1(γ). Revised 3.VI–3.VII confirms that statement when ε_IJ means the signs of the actual tautological projective transition functions. That part survives.

The stronger historical tendency to describe FlatWave itself as the resulting projective orientation invariant does not survive unchanged. Revised Book 3 distinguishes the already-proved FlatWave cophase character from the tautological Čech class and proves that the natural half-angle comparison has different monodromy.

Standard provenance note. The use of transition-function obstructions and characteristic classes of real sphere/line bundles belongs to standard topology; no claim of priority is made for w1 itself.

Wolfram independently verified before insertion the half-angle lift relation

v(x+2π)=−v(x),

the pulled-back transition ratio X/Y=cot(x/2), the projective FlatWave formula, and the product +1 of four cophase character multipliers. The characteristic-class identification itself is a standard topology import, not a computer-algebra result.

Section 3.VII closure ledger

New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

New standard-mathematics imports: tautological real line bundle; Čech transition cocycle of a real line bundle; first Stiefel–Whitney class w1.

New persistent notation: γ for the tautological line bundle; e_X,e_Y,e_Z for its local frames; ζ_JI for transition signs; a=w1(γ); L_Z for the distinguished projective line; ρ for the natural half-angle projective map.

New physics imports: 0.

New projection contracts: 0.

New empirical calibrations: 0.

Historical results admitted without re-derivation: 0, except standard topology explicitly labeled as import.

Proved in 3.VII: exact tautological transition scalars; Z2 cocycle identity; equality of ratio-transition signs with the tautological sign cocycle; identification of that cocycle with w1(γ); direct nontriviality on RP¹; natural half-angle pullback; explicit pointwise comparison with FlatWave; type separation between χ_c and w1; monodromy obstruction to their naive identification; and the corrected survival statement that projective ratio signs, not FlatWave itself, realize the tautological w1 class.

Certified handoff to 3.VIII: the line-bundle class a=w1(γ) is now established and explicitly separated from FlatWave. Section 3.VIII may use the standard tangent-bundle relation for RP^n to compute w1(T RP^n). It must not substitute a for tangent orientation without the required factor and derivation.

3.VIII — TANGENT ORIENTATION OF REAL PROJECTIVE SPACE

Purpose

Section 3.VII established the nontrivial first Stiefel–Whitney class

a=w1(γ)

of the tautological real line bundle and proved that this class is not the same object as the FlatWave cophase character. The present section now asks the next dependency-ordered question:

What is the first Stiefel–Whitney class of the tangent bundle of RP^n?

Only after answering that question may Book 3 speak globally about orientability of real projective space or about positive versus mirror tangent frames.

The main theorem is standard:

w1(T RP^n)=(n+1)a_n.

Its immediate consequence is the familiar orientability criterion:

RP^n is orientable exactly when n is odd.

The revised contribution is not this topology theorem itself. It is the disciplined separation of three previously conflated binary structures:

FlatWave cophase parity χ_c,

tautological line orientation obstruction a_n=w1(γ_n),

and tangent orientation obstruction w1(T RP^n).

3.VIII.A — General projective and tautological notation

Standard Definition 3.VIII.SD1 — Real projective n-space. For n≥1,

RP^n=(R^{n+1}\{0})/R^*,

where nonzero scalar multiples are identified.

Standard Definition 3.VIII.SD2 — Tautological line bundle γ_n. The fiber of γ_n over a projective line ℓ∈RP^n is the line ℓ itself.

Define

a_n=w1(γ_n)∈H^1(RP^n;Z2).

For n=2 this notation agrees with the class a=w1(γ) established in 3.VII.

Theorem 3.VIII.T1 — Nontriviality of a_n for n≥1. The class a_n is nonzero.

Proof. Embed a coordinate projective line

j:RP^1→RP^n,

for example

[X:Y]↦[X:Y:0:...:0].

The pullback j*γ_n is the tautological line bundle γ_1 over RP^1. Section 3.VII proved directly, through half-angle monodromy, that γ_1 is nontrivial and therefore w1(γ_1)≠0.

By naturality of Stiefel–Whitney classes,

j*(a_n)=w1(j*γ_n)=w1(γ_1)≠0.

Hence a_n itself cannot vanish. ∎

3.VIII.B — Stable tangent-bundle relation

Standard Import 3.VIII.SI1 — Tangent space as a homomorphism space. At a projective line ℓ⊂R^{n+1}, the tangent space of RP^n is naturally

T_ℓ RP^n ≅ Hom(ℓ,R^{n+1}/ℓ).

Equivalently, if γ_n^⊥ denotes the orthogonal-complement bundle relative to a chosen auxiliary Euclidean inner product, then

T RP^n ≅ Hom(γ_n,γ_n^⊥).

The auxiliary inner product is used only to choose a complementary bundle; the resulting tangent bundle is intrinsic.

Theorem 3.VIII.T2 — Stable tangent relation. One has the vector-bundle isomorphism

T RP^n ⊕ 1 ≅ (n+1)γ_n.

Proof. The trivial rank-(n+1) bundle splits as

γ_n ⊕ γ_n^⊥ ≅ ε^{n+1}.

Using the standard tangent identification,

T RP^n

≅Hom(γ_n,γ_n^⊥).

Add the trivial line

1≅Hom(γ_n,γ_n),

because the endomorphism bundle of a real line bundle is canonically trivial: every fiber endomorphism is multiplication by a real scalar.

Therefore

T RP^n ⊕ 1

≅Hom(γ_n,γ_n^⊥)⊕Hom(γ_n,γ_n)

≅Hom(γ_n,γ_n^⊥⊕γ_n)

≅Hom(γ_n,ε^{n+1}).

For a real line bundle, γ_n^*≅γ_n because its transition signs square to +1. Hence

Hom(γ_n,ε^{n+1})

≅(n+1)γ_n^*

≅(n+1)γ_n.

This yields the stated stable relation. ∎

Clarification 3.VIII.CL1 — Status. The ingredients of Theorem 3.VIII.T2 are standard differential topology. No Projection-specific tangent-bundle axiom has been introduced.

3.VIII.C — Total Stiefel–Whitney class

Standard Import 3.VIII.SI2 — Whitney product law. For real vector bundles E and F,

w(E⊕F)=w(E)w(F),

and the total Stiefel–Whitney class of a trivial bundle is 1.

For a real line bundle L,

w(L)=1+w1(L).

Theorem 3.VIII.T3 — Total tangent Stiefel–Whitney class. For RP^n,

w(T RP^n)=(1+a_n)^{n+1}.

Proof. Apply the Whitney product law to Theorem 3.VIII.T2:

w(T RP^n)w(1)

=w((n+1)γ_n).

Since w(1)=1 and

w(γ_n)=1+a_n,

the right side is the product of n+1 identical line-bundle factors:

w(T RP^n)=(1+a_n)^{n+1}. ∎

Corollary 3.VIII.C1 — General degree-k formula. In every degree k≤n,

w_k(T RP^n)=C(n+1,k)a_n^k

with coefficients reduced modulo 2.

Only the degree-one consequence will be used for orientability in the present section. The degree-two consequence is deferred to the spin analysis of 3.IX.

3.VIII.D — Tangent orientation theorem

Theorem 3.VIII.T4 — First tangent Stiefel–Whitney class. For n≥1,

w1(T RP^n)=(n+1)a_n

in H^1(RP^n;Z2).

Proof. Extract the degree-one term of

(1+a_n)^{n+1}.

The coefficient of a_n is n+1. Since coefficients lie in Z2, only its parity matters. ∎

Theorem 3.VIII.T5 — Orientability criterion. For n≥1,

RP^n is orientable iff n is odd.

Proof. A connected smooth manifold is orientable exactly when the first Stiefel–Whitney class of its tangent bundle vanishes.

By Theorem 3.VIII.T4,

w1(T RP^n)=(n+1)a_n.

Theorem 3.VIII.T1 gives a_n≠0. Therefore the product vanishes precisely when n+1 is even, which is precisely when n is odd. ∎

Corollary 3.VIII.C2 — Even-dimensional projective spaces. For even n,

w1(T RP^n)=a_n≠0,

so RP^n is nonorientable.

Corollary 3.VIII.C3 — Odd-dimensional projective spaces. For odd n,

w1(T RP^n)=0,

so RP^n is orientable even though

a_n=w1(γ_n)

remains nonzero.

This gives the decisive separation between tautological-line orientation and tangent orientation.

3.VIII.E — Diagnostic dimensions

Theorem 3.VIII.T6 — RP^1 diagnostic. One has

a_1≠0,

but

w1(T RP^1)=2a_1=0.

Thus RP^1 is orientable while its tautological line bundle is nonorientable.

Corollary 3.VIII.C4 — A line-bundle orientation obstruction is not automatically a manifold orientation obstruction. This one-dimensional example already disproves any universal identification

w1(γ_n)=w1(T RP^n).

Theorem 3.VIII.T7 — RP^2 diagnostic. One has

w1(T RP^2)=3a_2=a_2≠0.

Therefore RP^2 is nonorientable.

Corollary 3.VIII.C5 — Corrected survival of the historical RP^2 claim. In dimension two, the tautological class and tangent orientation class are indeed the same cohomology class:

w1(T RP^2)=w1(γ_2).

The historical Flatwave paper's RP^2 tangent-orientation statement survives only after replacing its phrase “FlatWave class” by the revised, precise object “projective ratio-transition class a_2.” Section 3.VII proved that the cophase FlatWave character χ_c is not this class.

Theorem 3.VIII.T8 — RP^3 diagnostic. One has

w1(T RP^3)=4a_3=0.

Therefore RP^3 is orientable.

This fact is essential for the later specialization

RP^3≅SO(3),

because the rotation manifold may carry a global tangent orientation even though its tautological line bundle remains nontrivial.

3.VIII.F — Local Jacobian signs versus the global class

Section 3.VI computed, on the RP^2 overlap U_X∩U_Y,

J_XY=−u^{-3}.

Hence

sgn J_XY=−sgn(u).

Meanwhile the tautological ratio-transition sign is

ε_XY=sgn(u).

Theorem 3.VIII.T9 — RP^2 Jacobian class agrees with a_2. The fixed minus sign in

sgn J_XY=−ε_XY

reflects the chosen ordering of local affine coordinates and changes under a reversal of one chart's ordered frame. It is therefore a Čech coboundary-level convention, whereas the cohomology class represented by the tangent transition signs is

w1(T RP^2)=a_2.

Proof. The cohomology equality follows invariantly from Theorem 3.VIII.T7. Reversing the orientation convention of one local chart multiplies every transition sign incident to that chart by −1, exactly the change of cocycle representative produced by a zero-cochain. Hence a fixed chart-order sign does not alter the characteristic class. ∎

Corollary 3.VIII.C6 — Coordinate-order signs must not be mistaken for new topology. Only the cohomology class of the transition-sign system is invariant under local frame reorientations.

3.VIII.G — Global status of positive and mirror tangent frames

Definition 3.VIII.D1 — Positive tangent frame after orientation choice. Let M be a connected orientable n-manifold and choose one of its two global orientations. An ordered tangent frame is positive if it represents the chosen orientation and negative if it represents the opposite orientation.

Definition 3.VIII.D2 — Mirror tangent frame. Relative to the chosen global orientation, a negative ordered tangent frame is a mirror tangent frame in the canonical Book 3 sense.

Theorem 3.VIII.T10 — Global mirror classification on RP^n. A global positive-versus-mirror tangent-frame classification exists on RP^n exactly when n is odd.

Proof. Such a classification requires a global orientation of T RP^n. By Theorem 3.VIII.T5 this exists exactly for odd n. Once one of the two orientations is declared positive, every ordered tangent frame has a well-defined positive or negative status. ∎

Corollary 3.VIII.C7 — Even-dimensional limitation. For even n, RP^n admits local orientation choices but no globally consistent designation of one tangent-frame class as positive throughout the manifold. Consequently the term “mirror tangent frame” can be used only locally relative to a local declared orientation, not as one globally separated component of tangent frames.

Corollary 3.VIII.C8 — Odd-dimensional label nonselection. For odd n, orientability supplies two globally consistent tangent-orientation choices but does not canonically select which one is positive. Exchanging the chosen orientation swaps the positive and mirror labels everywhere.

This is exactly the setting to which the Book 0 Automorphism Obstruction may later be applied if an orientation-reversing automorphism exchanges the two choices.

3.VIII.H — Separation from cophase and FlatWave

Negative Theorem 3.VIII.N1 — Cophase parity is not tangent orientation. The cophase character

χ_c:C4→{±1}

is defined on the one-dimensional phase-symmetry group. The tangent orientation class

w1(T RP^n)

is a cohomology class of the tangent bundle of RP^n. Their mathematical types differ and no canonical equality is defined.

Negative Theorem 3.VIII.N2 — FlatWave sign reversal does not imply mirror-frame reversal. Section 3.V proved that a cophase quarter-turn reverses FlatWave, while 3.I proved that the same phase translation preserves the standard orientation of the phase circle. Therefore the mere reversal of FlatWave cannot be used as a criterion for negative tangent-frame orientation.

Negative Theorem 3.VIII.N3 — Nonorientability does not choose chirality. The fact that even-dimensional RP^n is nonorientable is a global tangent-bundle obstruction. It does not select a physical left or right orientation, a weak chiral projector, a spin state, or a mirror universe.

3.VIII.I — Dependency and prior-paper audit

The dependency chain used in this section is

3.VII tautological class a_n

→ standard tangent identification

→ stable tangent relation

→ Whitney product law

→ total Stiefel–Whitney class

→ w1(T RP^n)

→ orientability

→ global positive/mirror tangent-frame status.

No spin-lift statement has been used.

Historical source audit. The prior Flatwave paper states the standard stable relation

T RP^n ⊕ 1 ≅ (n+1)γ

and the resulting formula

w(T RP^n)=(1+a)^{n+1},

from which it correctly obtains orientability precisely for odd n. That standard topology survives. Its wording that “a=[FlatWave]” does not survive the 3.VII comparison test. In the revised theory, a_n is the tautological projective ratio-transition class, while FlatWave remains the separate cophase character χ_c. fileciteturn92file0

Wolfram was used only as a low-level parity/expansion check for representative dimensions. The stable tangent relation and characteristic-class arguments are mathematical proofs/imports, not CAS deductions.

Section 3.VIII closure ledger

New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

New standard-mathematics imports: tangent-space identification for RP^n; stable tangent relation; Whitney product law; orientability criterion w1(TM)=0.

New persistent notation: γ_n, a_n=w1(γ_n), T RP^n, and the trivial line bundle 1.

New physics imports: 0.

New projection contracts: 0.

New empirical calibrations: 0.

Historical results admitted without status declaration: 0.

Proved in 3.VIII: nontriviality of a_n; stable tangent relation; total tangent Stiefel–Whitney class; first tangent Stiefel–Whitney class; orientability iff n is odd; RP^1/RP^2/RP^3 diagnostic cases; cohomological interpretation of the RP^2 Jacobian signs; and the exact dimensions in which a global positive-versus-mirror tangent-frame classification exists.

Certified handoff to 3.IX: orientability is now settled independently of spin. Section 3.IX may extract w2(T RP^n) from the already-proved total class and apply the standard spin criterion. It must treat RP^1 separately and must distinguish existence of a spin structure from selection of one spin structure or one physical spin state.

3.IX — SECOND CLIMAX: SPIN-LIFT CONDITIONS

Purpose

Section 3.VIII established the exact tangent-orientation obstruction of RP^n:

w1(T RP^n)=(n+1)a_n,

so RP^n is orientable exactly when n is odd. Orientability is necessary for a spin structure, but it is not sufficient. The present section computes the next obstruction,

w2(T RP^n),

and then separates three questions that historical language often conflated:

1. Does a spin structure exist?

2. If it exists, how many inequivalent spin structures are available?

3. Does any structure already present in Projection Craft canonically select one of them or a physical spin/chirality state?

The answers are respectively topological existence, a torsor of choices, and no canonical selection from the present data.

3.IX.A — Standard spin criterion

Standard Import 3.IX.SI1 — Spin structure criterion. Let E→M be an oriented real rank-r vector bundle with r≥2. A spin structure on E is a lift of its oriented orthonormal frame bundle from SO(r) to Spin(r) through the double cover

Spin(r)→SO(r).

Such a lift exists if and only if

w2(E)=0.

For a smooth oriented manifold M, a spin structure on M means a spin structure on TM.

Clarification 3.IX.CL1 — Dependency. The condition w1(TM)=0 is not repeated inside the spin criterion because orientation is already assumed in the definition of the oriented frame bundle. For a general manifold the combined existence conditions are therefore

w1(TM)=0,

w2(TM)=0.

Standard Import 3.IX.SI2 — Stability under a trivial line. For an oriented real vector bundle E,

w1(E⊕1)=w1(E),

w2(E⊕1)=w2(E).

Consequently the spin obstruction is unchanged by adjoining a trivial oriented line. This permits the stable tangent relation of 3.VIII to be used in the explicit Clifford audit below.

3.IX.B — Second Stiefel–Whitney class of RP^n

Theorem 3.IX.T1 — Second tangent Stiefel–Whitney class. For n≥1,

w2(T RP^n)=C(n+1,2)a_n².

Proof. Section 3.VIII proved

w(T RP^n)=(1+a_n)^{n+1}.

The degree-two term of this binomial expansion is

C(n+1,2)a_n².

All coefficients are reduced modulo two. ∎

Corollary 3.IX.C1 — Explicit coefficient.

C(n+1,2)=n(n+1)/2.

Thus

w2(T RP^n)

=[n(n+1)/2 mod 2]a_n².

3.IX.C — Low-dimensional cohomology qualification

Standard Import 3.IX.SI3 — Mod-two cohomology ring. For real projective space,

H*(RP^n;Z2)≅Z2[a_n]/(a_n^{n+1}),

with deg a_n=1.

Theorem 3.IX.T2 — Degree-two distinction. For n=1,

H²(RP^1;Z2)=0,

so

a_1²=0

and therefore

w2(T RP^1)=0.

For n≥2,

a_n²≠0.

Proof. The cohomology ring truncates only at degree n+1. Hence a_n² survives exactly when 2≤n. ∎

Corollary 3.IX.C2 — RP^1 is a separate case. The numerical binomial coefficient C(2,2)=1 does not obstruct spin on RP^1 because its degree-two cohomology group vanishes.

3.IX.D — Spin criterion for real projective space

Theorem 3.IX.T3 — Spin existence theorem for RP^n. For n≥1,

RP^n is spin exactly in the following cases:

n=1,

or

n≡3 (mod 4).

Proof. For n=1, 3.VIII gives orientability and Theorem 3.IX.T2 gives w2=0.

Now let n≥2. By 3.VIII, orientability requires n odd. Write

n=2k+1.

Then

C(n+1,2)

=C(2k+2,2)

=(k+1)(2k+1).

The factor 2k+1 is odd, so the parity of the coefficient is the parity of k+1. Since a_n²≠0 for n≥2,

w2(T RP^n)=0

iff k+1 is even,

iff k is odd,

iff

n=2k+1≡3 (mod 4).

Thus the complete result is RP^1 together with RP^{4j+3}. ∎

Corollary 3.IX.C3 — First cases.

RP^1: orientable and spin.

RP^2: nonorientable, hence not spin.

RP^3: orientable and spin.

RP^4: nonorientable.

RP^5: orientable but not spin.

RP^7: orientable and spin.

Corollary 3.IX.C4 — Orientability is strictly weaker than spin. RP^5 supplies the first projective example in this family for which

w1(T RP^5)=0

but

w2(T RP^5)≠0.

Therefore the positive-versus-mirror tangent-frame distinction established by orientation does not itself guarantee that those frames admit a consistent spin double-cover lift.

3.IX.E — Parity form of the obstruction

Theorem 3.IX.T4 — Odd-dimensional parity pattern. For odd n≥3,

w2(T RP^n)=0

iff

n≡3 or 7 (mod 8),

which is equivalently

n≡3 (mod 4).

For

n≡1 or 5 (mod 8),

the manifold is orientable but w2 is nonzero.

Proof. This is the mod-eight refinement of Theorem 3.IX.T3. ∎

Clarification 3.IX.CL2 — The mod-eight wording adds no new obstruction. It is useful only when comparing with Clifford-volume-element periodicity. The spin-existence theorem itself is the mod-four statement.

3.IX.F — Explicit stable Clifford-lift audit

The historical Flatwave paper attempted to see the same obstruction directly in a Clifford lift. That argument can be retained only after one correction: the sign cocycle used here is the tautological ratio-transition cocycle ζ of 3.VII, not the cophase FlatWave character χ_c.

Let

m=n+1.

For the stable bundle

T RP^n⊕1≅mγ_n,

choose the direct-sum local frame induced from a local frame of γ_n. On an overlap U_i∩U_j the stable transition has the form

G_ji=ζ_ji I_m,

where ζ_ji∈{±1} is the tautological transition sign.

Theorem 3.IX.T5 — Stable transitions are oriented exactly for odd n. If n is odd, then m=n+1 is even and

det(ζ_ji I_m)=ζ_ji^m=+1.

Thus every stable transition lies in SO(m).

If n is even, a negative ζ_ji gives determinant −1 and the stable transition system is not oriented.

Proof. Immediate from determinant multiplicativity and parity of m. ∎

Definition 3.IX.D1 — Clifford volume lift. For even m, let

Ω_m=e_1e_2···e_m

in a Euclidean Clifford algebra convention. Ω_m projects to −I_m∈SO(m). Its square is

Ω_m²=(−1)^{m(m−1)/2}

in the convention e_i²=+1. In the alternative convention e_i²=−1 the exponent differs by m; because m is even, the resulting sign is identical. Hence the parity statement below is convention-independent for the present even-m application.

Theorem 3.IX.T6 — Volume-lift parity. For even m,

Ω_m²=+1

iff

m≡0 (mod 4),

and

Ω_m²=−1

iff

m≡2 (mod 4).

Proof. Let m=2r. Then

m(m−1)/2=r(2r−1),

whose parity is the parity of r. Thus the square is +1 exactly when r is even, equivalently m is divisible by four. ∎

Theorem 3.IX.T7 — Explicit Clifford cocycle obstruction. On a triple overlap, the tautological signs satisfy

ζ_ki ζ_jk ζ_ij=+1.

Hence the number of negative stable transitions around the triple overlap is even. Choose the lift

ζ=+1 → 1∈Spin(m),

ζ=−1 → Ω_m∈Spin(m).

The lifted triple product is either

1

or

Ω_m².

Therefore the displayed lift closes as a Spin(m) cocycle exactly when

Ω_m²=+1,

that is, exactly when

m≡0 (mod 4)

or

n≡3 (mod 4).

Proof. If all three signs are positive, the lift product is 1. If exactly two are negative, the lift product contains Ω_m². The cocycle condition in Spin(m) therefore succeeds for every triple overlap exactly when Ω_m²=1. Apply Theorem 3.IX.T6. ∎

Corollary 3.IX.C5 — Agreement of the two obstruction calculations. For n≥3 odd, the explicit Clifford lift closes exactly in the same dimensions in which

w2(T RP^n)=0.

Thus the Clifford sign failure is an explicit representative of the same obstruction encoded abstractly by the second Stiefel–Whitney class.

Clarification 3.IX.CL3 — Corrected historical statement. Wherever the prior Flatwave paper says that the “FlatWave cocycle” supplies this Clifford lift, revised Book 3 substitutes the projective ratio/tautological transition cocycle ζ. Section 3.VII already proved that χ_c and w1(γ_n) are different structures under the natural phase comparison.

3.IX.G — Existence does not imply uniqueness

Standard Import 3.IX.SI4 — Spin-structure torsor. If an oriented vector bundle admits a spin structure, the set of equivalence classes of spin structures is a torsor for

H¹(M;Z2).

There is no preferred element of a nontrivial torsor without additional structure.

Theorem 3.IX.T8 — Number of spin structures on spin real projective spaces. Every spin RP^n with n≥1 has exactly two spin structures.

Proof. For n≥1,

H¹(RP^n;Z2)≅Z2.

Whenever a spin structure exists, Standard Import 3.IX.SI4 makes the set of spin structures a torsor for this two-element group. Hence there are exactly two equivalence classes. ∎

Corollary 3.IX.C6 — RP^3 has two spin structures. The later identification

RP^3≅SO(3)

does not remove this topological multiplicity. A particular group-compatible or physically chosen spin structure, if required, must be selected by additional data and named explicitly.

Clarification 3.IX.CL4 — Spin structure versus pointwise double-cover sign. The statement “there are two spin structures” is global bundle data. It is not the same as saying that a point of SO(3) has two preimages q and −q in Spin(3). The former classifies inequivalent lifts of the frame bundle over the entire base manifold; the latter is the two-point fiber of one fixed double cover.

3.IX.H — No selection by cophase, FlatWave, or mirror

Negative Theorem 3.IX.N1 — Cophase does not select a spin structure. The cophase relation lives on the lifted one-dimensional phase carrier and χ_c is a character of C4. Spin-structure choices live in a torsor over H¹(RP^n;Z2). No canonical map from the former to a distinguished element of the latter has been derived.

Negative Theorem 3.IX.N2 — FlatWave does not select a spin structure. The cophase FlatWave character χ_c was separated from the tautological class in 3.VII. Even the tautological class a_n determines only the obstruction data entering w1 and w2; when the obstruction vanishes, it does not choose one of the two spin structures.

Negative Theorem 3.IX.N3 — Mirror orientation does not select a spin structure. Choosing a global orientation on an odd-dimensional RP^n determines which tangent frames are called positive and mirror. A spin structure is an additional lift of the positive oriented frame bundle. Orientation therefore precedes spin but does not uniquely determine it.

Negative Theorem 3.IX.N4 — Spin topology does not select physical chirality. A spin structure permits globally defined spinor bundles. It does not choose the Standard Model projector

P_L=(1−γ^5)/2

over

P_R=(1+γ^5)/2,

nor does it derive a parity-violating interaction. The chirality-orientation obstruction found historically in T4 therefore survives the topological completion.

3.IX.I — Prior-paper audit: Flatwave, T4, and T6

Historical Flatwave result. The prior Flatwave paper correctly stated the standard formulas

w2(T RP^n)=C(n+1,2)a²

and, for the higher-dimensional orientable cases, spin precisely when n≡3 mod4. It also gave the Clifford-volume-element witness Ω². Revised Book 3 retains these results after replacing the historical identification a=[FlatWave] by the corrected class

a_n=w1(γ_n)

represented by the projective ratio-transition signs ζ.

Historical T4 boundary. T4 imported the physical left-chiral weak projector and proved exact helicity-weight correspondences, but explicitly found that the reciprocal calculus did not choose P_L over P_R. The present spin-topology result does not remove that obstruction: admitting spinor bundles is categorically different from selecting a parity-violating interaction.

Historical T6 boundary. T6 imported Lorentzian spin geometry and used a spin connection to transport Dirac fields. Those conditional physical constructions require an available spin structure on the relevant spacetime geometry; they do not retroactively derive or canonically select the projective spin structure studied here.

C(n+1,2) mod 2

for representative dimensions, the spin pattern

n=1,3,7,11,15,...,

and the Clifford-volume-element square for even m:

m=2 mod4 → Ω_m²=−1,

m=0 mod4 → Ω_m²=+1.

These calculations are consistency checks. The manuscript proof rests on the characteristic-class and Clifford arguments displayed above.

SECOND BOOK 3 CLIMAX STATUS: PROVED.

Certified handoff to 3.X: RP^3 has now been certified as orientable and spin, but neither its global orientation nor either of its two spin structures has been physically selected. Section 3.X may specialize to the standard identification

3.X — RP³, SO(3), RODRIGUES COORDINATES, AND THE SPIN(3) DOUBLE COVER

Purpose. Section 3.IX settled the existence question for spin structures on real projective spaces and, in particular, certified RP³ as orientable and spin. The present section specializes that result to three-dimensional rotation geometry. Its task is mathematical: construct the standard double cover S³ → SO(3), identify SO(3) with RP³, introduce the Rodrigues/Gibbs affine coordinate, connect that coordinate to the certified half-angle calculus on an explicitly declared chart, and determine which information survives the projective quotient. No physical spin state, helicity, weak-interaction handedness, fermion ontology, or preferred chirality is inferred here.

3.X.A — Dependency boundary and standard mathematical import

Inherited certified antecedents are Books 0–2 and Sections 3.I–3.IX. In particular, Book 2 supplies the chart-qualified identity sxp(x)=tan(x/2), while Sections 3.V–3.IX supply projective quotient language, transition-sign discipline, orientability, and spin-lift criteria.

Standard-mathematics import 3.X.I1 — Quaternion model of Spin(3). Let H denote the real quaternion algebra with basis 1,i,j,k and multiplication i²=j²=k²=ijk=−1. For q=q0+q1 i+q2 j+q3 k define the conjugate q̄=q0−q1 i−q2 j−q3 k and norm |q|²=q q̄=q0²+q1²+q2²+q3². The unit quaternions form S³⊂H under multiplication. Standard Lie-group theory identifies this group with SU(2) and Spin(3).

Status. This identification is imported standard mathematics, not a Projection-specific axiom and not a physics import. The Projection-specific work below is the exact reconciliation of this standard structure with the already-certified half-angle, reciprocal, seam, and projective bookkeeping.

3.X.B — Quaternionic rotation action and the projective quotient

Identify R³ with the pure-imaginary quaternions Im(H). For q∈S³ define

Φ(q)(v)=q v q⁻¹=q v q̄,     v∈Im(H).

Lemma 3.X.L1 — Norm and inner-product preservation. Φ(q) preserves Im(H), Euclidean norm, and orientation; therefore Φ(q)∈SO(3).

Proof. Quaternion conjugation preserves the scalar/pure-imaginary decomposition. Multiplicativity of the norm gives |qvq⁻¹|=|v| for |q|=1. Hence Φ(q) is orthogonal. The map q↦Φ(q) is continuous and S³ is connected; because Φ(1)=I has determinant +1 and determinant cannot change sign inside O(3), every Φ(q) lies in SO(3). ∎

Theorem 3.X.T1 — Quaternionic double cover. The map

Φ:S³→SO(3)

is a surjective group homomorphism with kernel {+1,−1}. Consequently

SO(3) ≅ S³/{±1} ≅ RP³.

Proof. Homomorphism follows directly from conjugation:

Φ(q1q2)(v)=q1(q2 v q2⁻¹)q1⁻¹.

If Φ(q)=I, then q commutes with every pure-imaginary quaternion. The only quaternions commuting with i,j,k simultaneously are real scalars; unit norm leaves q=±1. Thus ker Φ={±1}. For surjectivity, any rotation through angle θ about unit axis n is represented by the unit quaternion

q(θ,n)=cos(θ/2)+n sin(θ/2),

whose conjugation action is the standard axis-angle rotation. Therefore every element of SO(3) has two preimages ±q. Quotienting S³ by antipodal identification gives RP³. ∎

Corollary 3.X.C1 — Antipodal lift pair. For every rotation R∈SO(3), the fiber Φ⁻¹(R) is exactly {q,−q}. Thus the projective point [q] determines the rotation, whereas q itself contains one additional binary lift choice.

3.X.C — Axis-angle half-angle structure

Let n∈S² and let θ be a real rotation parameter. Write

q(θ,n)=cos(θ/2)+n sin(θ/2).

Lemma 3.X.L2 — Covering periodicity.

q(θ+2π,n)=−q(θ,n),

q(θ+4π,n)=q(θ,n),

Φ(q(θ+2π,n))=Φ(q(θ,n)).

Proof. The first two identities are elementary half-angle periodicity. The third follows from Theorem 3.X.T1 because q and −q lie in the same fiber. ∎

Interpretive firewall. The 4π return of the lifted coordinate is a theorem about this double cover. By itself it is not yet a statement about a physical spinor, a fermion wavefunction, an observable phase, or a measurable sign change.

3.X.D — Rodrigues/Gibbs affine coordinate

Work on the affine chart U0⊂RP³ where the scalar quaternion coordinate q0 is nonzero. Write q=q0+qv with qv∈R³. Define the Rodrigues/Gibbs vector

r=qv/q0.

Because numerator and denominator both change sign under q↦−q,

r(−q)=r(q).

Thus r is a coordinate on the projective rotation state, not on a chosen member of its lift pair.

Theorem 3.X.T2 — Rodrigues chart and inverse lift pair. On U0, r∈R³ is an affine coordinate and the two unit-quaternion lifts of the corresponding projective point are

q±(r)=±(1+r)/sqrt(1+||r||²),

where 1+r denotes the quaternion with scalar part 1 and pure-imaginary part r.

Proof. From r=qv/q0 we have q=q0(1+r). Unit norm gives

1=|q|²=q0²(1+||r||²),

so q0=±1/sqrt(1+||r||²). Substitution gives q±(r). Conversely both signs project to the same [q], and every finite r determines one point of U0. ∎

For the axis-angle representative with cos(θ/2)≠0,

r=n tan(θ/2).

Hence

ρ:=||r||=|tan(θ/2)|.

On the declared principal chart 0<θ<π, tan(θ/2)>0, so

ρ=tan(θ/2).

Book 2 may now be used without dropping its global absolute values: on this same chart sin θ>0 and

sxp(θ)=|csc θ|−cot θ=csc θ−cot θ=(1−cos θ)/sin θ=tan(θ/2).

Therefore, and only with this chart declaration,

ρ=sxp(θ).

Corollary 3.X.C2 — Certified half-angle/Rodrigues bridge. On 0<θ<π,

||r||=sxp(θ).

This is an exact coordinate identification. It does not make sxp a new physical rotation law; it shows that one already-certified Projection coordinate coincides with the radial Rodrigues half-angle coordinate on the stated chart.

3.X.E — Quaternion multiplication and Rodrigues composition

Fix the quaternion convention ij=k, jk=i, ki=j. For vectors a,b∈R³ regarded as pure-imaginary quaternions,

ab=−a·b+a×b.

Represent two points of the Rodrigues chart projectively by the non-normalized quaternions 1+a and 1+b. Their product is

(1+a)(1+b)

=(1−a·b)+(a+b+a×b).

Theorem 3.X.T3 — Rodrigues composition law. Whenever

1−a·b ≠ 0,

the product remains in the Rodrigues affine chart and its coordinate is

a⊕b=(a+b+a×b)/(1−a·b).

Proof. The preceding product has scalar part 1−a·b and vector part a+b+a×b. Dividing vector part by scalar part is exactly the chart definition r=qv/q0. Normalization is irrelevant because r is homogeneous of degree zero. ∎

Convention note. With the conjugation action Φ(q)(v)=qvq⁻¹ and multiplication q1q2, the displayed cross-product sign follows ij=k. Reversing the multiplication/action convention reverses the corresponding cross-term ordering/sign. The convention is therefore fixed here rather than treated as invariant content.

Boundary note. If 1−a·b=0, quaternion multiplication itself remains perfectly defined. Only this affine Rodrigues chart fails because the product has zero scalar part. The resulting rotation lies on the projective chart boundary q0=0.

3.X.F — Collinear reduction and exact tangent addition

Suppose a=αn and b=βn for a common unit vector n. Then a×b=0 and a·b=αβ. Theorem 3.X.T3 reduces to

(αn)⊕(βn)=((α+β)/(1−αβ)) n,

provided 1−αβ≠0.

Theorem 3.X.T4 — Collinear half-angle reduction. If

α=tan(θ1/2),     β=tan(θ2/2),

and all quantities lie in one valid Rodrigues chart, then

(α+β)/(1−αβ)=tan((θ1+θ2)/2).

Proof. This is the ordinary tangent-addition identity applied to θ1/2 and θ2/2. Therefore quaternionic rotation composition reduces exactly to the one-dimensional projective Möbius law in the collinear case. ∎

Audit note. Wolfram algebra was used only as an independent symbolic check of the dot/cross expansion and tangent-addition reduction. The displayed derivations are the proof.

3.X.G — FlatWave seam versus Rodrigues chart boundary

The distinction between an operator seam and a projective-coordinate boundary is essential.

On the principal interval 0<θ<π,

ρ=tan(θ/2).

The internal FlatWave seam occurs where sin(2θ)=0 at θ=π/2. At that point

ρ=tan(π/4)=1.

But the Rodrigues chart condition is q0=cos(θ/2)≠0. At θ=π/2,

q0=cos(π/4)=1/sqrt(2)≠0,

so the rotation state and the Rodrigues coordinate are regular even though the Book 2 reciprocal-trigonometric domain reaches a seam.

By contrast, the Rodrigues affine chart reaches its boundary at θ=π, where

q0=cos(π/2)=0,

ρ=tan(θ/2)→∞

as θ→π from below.

Theorem 3.X.T5 — Seam nonidentity. On the declared principal chart, the internal FlatWave seam and the Rodrigues chart boundary are distinct:

FlatWave internal seam:     θ=π/2,  ρ=1,  q0≠0;

Rodrigues chart boundary:   θ=π,    ρ→∞, q0=0.

Therefore the FlatWave sign change at ρ=1 is not a singularity of SO(3), not the antipodal quotient itself, and not the Rodrigues projective chart boundary.

Corollary 3.X.C3 — Coordinate divergence is not state divergence. The divergence ρ→∞ at θ→π records failure of the chosen affine chart. The underlying point of RP³≅SO(3) remains a regular rotation state and can be represented in another projective chart.

3.X.H — Information preserved and information erased by projectivization

Lemma 3.X.L3 — Projective sign blindness. Every homogeneous projective ratio built from quaternion coordinates is invariant under q↦−q. In particular, wherever r is defined,

r(−q)=r(q),

and [−q]=[q] in RP³.

Proof. Every numerator and denominator acquires the same factor −1, which cancels. The projective quotient identifies nonzero scalar multiples by definition. ∎

Theorem 3.X.T6 — Rotation reconstruction without lift reconstruction. The projective state [q]∈RP³, equivalently the rotation Φ(q)∈SO(3), reconstructs the rotation completely but cannot distinguish the two unit-quaternion lifts q and −q.

Proof. Completeness for the rotation follows from Theorem 3.X.T1: SO(3) is the quotient S³/{±1}. Non-distinguishability follows because q and −q are precisely the two elements of every fiber and all projective coordinates agree on them. ∎

This theorem is deliberately not yet promoted to the full Book 3 lift-obstruction theorem. Section 3.XI must ask the stronger question: can any structure already admitted into the Projection calculus canonically choose one member of this lift pair? The present section establishes only the exact information loss produced by the quotient.

3.X.I — Relation to FlatWave and reciprocal projective data

Within the declared principal rotation chart, the radial coordinate ρ=sxp(θ) gives the earlier half-angle calculus an exact standard geometric host. FlatWave retains its certified status

FlatWave(θ)=sgn(sin 2θ)

on its reciprocal-trigonometric domain. In the principal interval it distinguishes the two open radial regions

0<ρ<1     corresponding to 0<θ<π/2,

ρ>1       corresponding to π/2<θ<π,

with ρ=1 as the excluded internal seam of that FlatWave chart.

This is genuine mathematical structure, but its information content is limited. FlatWave distinguishes which side of the internal unit-radius seam a projective rotation coordinate lies on. It does not distinguish q from −q, because θ and its quaternion lift sign are not recoverable from FlatWave alone and the projective coordinate itself is antipodally invariant.

Negative result 3.X.N1. The identity ρ=sxp(θ) on the principal chart does not derive a physical spin observable.

Negative result 3.X.N2. The FlatWave seam ρ=1 does not encode the SU(2) lift sign.

Negative result 3.X.N3. The 4π periodicity of q is a property of the chosen double cover and does not, without a later physical representation contract, imply fermionic behavior.

Negative result 3.X.N4. Neither quaternion conjugation nor projective antipodal identification selects a preferred physical chirality.

3.X.J — Certification, prior-paper audit, and closure ledger

Certified results of Section 3.X:

1. The standard quaternionic action gives a two-to-one covering S³→SO(3) with kernel {±1}, hence SO(3)≅RP³.

2. The Rodrigues vector r=qv/q0 is an affine coordinate on RP³ wherever q0≠0, with lift pair q±(r)=±(1+r)/sqrt(1+||r||²).

3. On the explicit principal chart 0<θ<π, ||r||=tan(θ/2)=sxp(θ).

4. Quaternion multiplication induces the exact Rodrigues composition law (a+b+a×b)/(1−a·b), with the multiplication convention fixed explicitly.

5. Collinear composition reduces exactly to the scalar half-angle tangent-addition Möbius law.

6. The internal FlatWave seam ρ=1 is distinct from the Rodrigues projective chart boundary ρ→∞.

7. Projectivization preserves the SO(3) rotation while erasing the binary distinction q versus −q.

Prior-paper audit. Earlier FlatWave/projective notes correctly anticipated the RP³/SO(3), Rodrigues, quaternion-composition, seam-separation, and lift-sign-blindness structures. None is admitted merely because it appeared there. The results above have been rederived from certified antecedents plus Standard-mathematics import 3.X.I1. Historical physical language attached to spin, mirror, or chirality remains outside the premise set.

Section 3.X extension ledger.
