Standard mathematical imports: 1 package — quaternion algebra and the standard unit-quaternion identification S³≅SU(2)≅Spin(3), including its rotation action.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Dimensional constants introduced: 0.

Physical equations of motion introduced: 0.

Quantum postulates introduced: 0.

Preferred-chirality axioms introduced: 0.

Closure status. Section 3.X is mathematically closed subject to its explicitly named standard import. It establishes the complete local algebra needed for the third Book 3 climax while preserving the distinction between a rotation state and a chosen lift.

Certified handoff to 3.XI. The quotient map now gives the obstruction problem a precise form. All RP³/SO(3) data identify q and −q, while Spin(3) retains them as distinct lifts. Section 3.XI must determine whether FlatWave, reciprocal/projective orientation data, mirror involutions, or any other already-certified structure can naturally select one lift. It must combine that analysis with the Book 0 automorphism obstruction and must keep preferred physical chirality outside the conclusion unless an explicit later physics import supplies it.

3.XI — THIRD CLIMAX: LIFT, MIRROR, AND CHIRALITY NONSELECTION

Purpose. Section 3.X established the standard double cover

p:S³≅Spin(3)→SO(3)≅RP³,

with p(q)=p(−q), and showed that all projective Rodrigues data reconstruct the rotation while erasing the binary lift sign. The present section asks whether the erased sign can nevertheless be recovered or canonically selected by any structure already certified in Projection Craft. The answer has two layers. First, the nontrivial connected double cover admits no global continuous section at all. Second, even if continuity is abandoned, a selector constructed only from deck-symmetric data cannot be canonical or natural. These are mathematical obstructions to lift selection. They are not declarations that no later asymmetric physical law could ever select chirality.

3.XI.A — Lift selector and deck involution

Definition 3.XI.D1 — Pointwise lift selector. A lift selector for the covering p:S³→SO(3) is a map

s:SO(3)→S³

such that

p∘s=id_SO(3).

For each rotation R, s(R) chooses one member of the two-element fiber p⁻¹(R)={q,−q}.

Definition 3.XI.D2 — Deck involution. Define

δ:S³→S³,     δ(q)=−q.

Then δ²=id, δ has no fixed points on S³, and

p∘δ=p.

Thus δ exchanges the two lifts lying over every rotation while leaving the base rotation unchanged.

Lemma 3.XI.L1 — Fiber decomposition. If s is any set-theoretic lift selector, then every q∈S³ belongs to exactly one of the two sets

s(SO(3)),     δ(s(SO(3))).

These sets are disjoint and their union is S³.

Proof. For q∈S³ let R=p(q). The fiber over R is exactly {s(R),−s(R)}={s(R),δ(s(R))}. Hence q belongs to one of the two sets. They cannot intersect because δ has no fixed point and s chooses exactly one element of each fiber. ∎

3.XI.B — No global continuous lift selector

Theorem 3.XI.T1 — No Global Continuous Lift Selector. There is no continuous map

s:SO(3)→S³

satisfying p∘s=id_SO(3).

Proof. Assume such a continuous section s exists. Because p is a two-sheet covering, every R∈SO(3) has a connected evenly covered neighborhood U for which

p⁻¹(U)=U_+ ⊔ U_−,

and p restricts to a homeomorphism from each sheet U_± onto U. Continuity of s and connectedness of U force s(U) to lie wholly in one of the two sheets. Consequently s(U) is open in S³. Since such neighborhoods cover SO(3), the full image s(SO(3)) is open in S³. Applying the homeomorphism δ shows that δ(s(SO(3))) is also open.

By Lemma 3.XI.L1 these are two disjoint nonempty open sets whose union is S³. This would disconnect S³. But S³ is connected. Contradiction. Therefore no global continuous section exists. ∎

Corollary 3.XI.C1 — Nontriviality of the lift cannot be removed by a smooth global sign convention. No continuous or smooth assignment of a preferred quaternion sign exists over all of SO(3).

Corollary 3.XI.C2 — Local sections remain possible. The obstruction is global, not local. On any evenly covered neighborhood one may choose either sheet continuously.

Example 3.XI.E1 — Rodrigues hemisphere branch. On the Rodrigues chart q0≠0, the restriction to the hemisphere q0>0 defines the smooth local section

s_+(r)=(1+r)/sqrt(1+||r||²).

The opposite branch is

s_−(r)=−s_+(r).

Both are legitimate local lifts. Neither extends continuously through the projective boundary q0=0 to a global section on SO(3).

Interpretive consequence. A branch condition such as q0>0 is a chart convention. It is not a globally derived preference for the plus lift.

3.XI.C — Discontinuous selectors and the distinction between existence and canonicity

The topological theorem does not say that one cannot choose one representative from each antipodal pair as a bare set. For example, one may normalize q∈S³ and declare the first nonzero coordinate in the ordered tuple (q0,q1,q2,q3) to be positive. This produces a pointwise selector, but it introduces an ordered-coordinate tie-breaking rule and jumps discontinuously across its cut sets.

Lemma 3.XI.L2 — Set-theoretic selection does not solve the geometric problem. A discontinuous selector can exist only by adding branch/cut data not contained in the quotient point [q] itself.

Proof. The quotient identifies q and −q exactly. Therefore no invariant of [q] distinguishes them. Any rule that returns one sign must use additional information: a coordinate ordering, a chart branch, an external orientation convention, a discontinuity set, or some other asymmetric datum. ∎

This gives three distinct questions that must not be conflated:

1. Can one choose a representative pointwise? Yes, after adding an arbitrary convention.

2. Can one choose it continuously over all of SO(3)? No, by Theorem 3.XI.T1.

3. Can one choose it canonically from deck-symmetric data alone? No, by the naturality obstruction below.

3.XI.D — Deck-symmetric data cannot recover the lift sign

Definition 3.XI.D3 — Deck-blind datum. A function or structured observable F on S³ is deck-blind if

F(−q)=F(q)

for all q. Equivalently, F factors through the quotient p:S³→SO(3): there exists F̄ on SO(3) with F=F̄∘p.

Theorem 3.XI.T2 — Factorization obstruction. No deck-blind datum can distinguish the two elements of a covering fiber.

Proof. If p(q)=p(−q), then for F=F̄∘p,

F(q)=F̄(p(q))=F̄(p(−q))=F(−q).

Thus the two lifts receive identical data. ∎

Corollary 3.XI.C3 — Projective ratios are lift-blind. Every homogeneous projective coordinate, including the Rodrigues vector r=qv/q0 wherever defined, is invariant under q↦−q and therefore cannot encode the lift sign.

Corollary 3.XI.C4 — Any function constructed solely from SO(3) rotation matrices is lift-blind. The two quaternions ±q give the same rotation matrix by construction.

3.XI.E — Projection operators under the deck shift

For a fixed rotation axis n, the axis-angle lift from 3.X is

q(θ,n)=cos(θ/2)+n sin(θ/2).

The deck transformation is represented by the lifted phase shift

D:θ↦θ+2π,

because

q(θ+2π,n)=−q(θ,n).

The certified Book 2 scalar operators are π-periodic. Therefore they are automatically invariant under D.

Theorem 3.XI.T3 — Certified Projection operators are blind to the quaternion lift sign. On every common domain where the named operators are defined,

srx(θ+2π)=srx(θ),

sxp(θ+2π)=sxp(θ),

cxp(θ+2π)=cxp(θ),

crx(θ+2π)=crx(θ),

urx(θ+2π)=urx(θ),

uxp(θ+2π)=uxp(θ),

FlatWave(θ+2π)=FlatWave(θ).

Proof. Every named primitive and derived Book 2 operator is π-periodic or is algebraically constructed from π-periodic operators. Since 2π is two periods, invariance follows. For FlatWave specifically,

FlatWave(θ)=sgn(sin 2θ),

and

sin(2(θ+2π))=sin(2θ+4π)=sin(2θ).

Thus all displayed values agree on the lift pair represented by θ and θ+2π. ∎

Corollary 3.XI.C5 — FlatWave is not a lift character. FlatWave cannot distinguish q from −q.

This result is stronger than a visual failure to correlate signs. It is exact: the lift-changing deck shift lies inside FlatWave’s period lattice.

3.XI.F — Cophase, half-turn, and deck shift are different operations

The historical vocabulary must now be separated at the quaternion level.

Cophase:

C_±:θ↦θ±π/2.

Half-turn in the Book 2 phase carrier:

H:θ↦θ+π.

Spin-cover deck shift:

D:θ↦θ+2π.

For the axis-angle quaternion,

q(θ+2π,n)=−q(θ,n),

but, generically,

q(θ+π,n)≠±q(θ,n),

q(θ±π/2,n)≠±q(θ,n).

Theorem 3.XI.T4 — Operation-separation theorem. Away from exceptional axis-angle coincidences, cophase, half-turn, and deck shift have different quotient effects:

(a) Cophase changes the SO(3) rotation and reverses FlatWave sign on its domain.

(b) The π half-turn changes the SO(3) rotation but preserves FlatWave.

(c) The 2π deck shift leaves the SO(3) rotation unchanged, flips q to −q, and preserves FlatWave.

Proof. The SO(3) rotation angle is changed by ±π/2 and π in the first two cases, whereas adding 2π represents the same rotation. FlatWave behavior follows from

sin(2(θ±π/2))=−sin(2θ),

sin(2(θ+π))=sin(2θ),

sin(2(θ+2π))=sin(2θ).

The corresponding half-angle identities give q(θ+2π,n)=−q(θ,n). ∎

Audit note. Wolfram independently verified these trigonometric identities and the antipodal 2π quaternion relation. The proof above is elementary and does not depend on the CAS.

Corollary 3.XI.C6 — The cophase character carried by FlatWave is not the Spin(3) fiber sign. FlatWave reverses under ±π/2 but is invariant under the operation that actually exchanges q and −q.

3.XI.G — Mirror and lift sign are different orientation structures

Recall the canonical Book 3 definition: a mirror frame is a negative-orientation frame relative to a declared positive orientation. In the three-dimensional rotation specialization this distinction is carried by O(3):

SO(3)={A∈O(3):det A=+1},

O(3)\SO(3)={A∈O(3):det A=−1}.

The covering p:Spin(3)→SO(3) lands entirely in the determinant +1 component.

Theorem 3.XI.T5 — Mirror–lift separation. The deck involution q↦−q is not a mirror transformation.

Proof. Both q and −q project to the same R∈SO(3), and det R=+1. The deck involution changes only which point of the Spin(3) fiber represents R. By contrast, a mirror frame belongs to the determinant −1 component of O(3), outside SO(3). Therefore the two operations live at different structural levels and cannot be identified. ∎

Corollary 3.XI.C7 — No sign of a quaternion coordinate is, by itself, an orientation sign of the underlying three-frame.

Corollary 3.XI.C8 — The scalar reflection θ↦−θ, reciprocal conjugation, projective antipodal identification q↦−q, and negative-orientation mirror frame are four distinct operations unless an explicit map proves a relation in a specified context.

3.XI.H — Spin structure is not a pointwise lift selector

Section 3.IX certified RP³ as spin and recorded that, under the standard classification, it admits two spin structures. This must not be confused with the two points ±q over an individual element of SO(3).

Definition-level distinction.

A spin structure on an oriented manifold is a global lift of its oriented orthonormal frame bundle through the group homomorphism Spin(n)→SO(n), subject to the standard equivariance condition. A pointwise selector s:SO(3)→Spin(3), by contrast, would be a section of the Lie-group covering itself.

Theorem 3.XI.T6 — Spin-structure existence does not supply a global quaternion sign section. The existence of a spin structure on RP³ does not contradict Theorem 3.XI.T1 and does not canonically choose q rather than −q for every rotation.

Proof. The two constructions have different bases and different objects. A spin structure is a principal Spin(3)-bundle mapping equivariantly to the oriented frame bundle of RP³. A section of p:Spin(3)→SO(3) would instead split the group covering itself. Theorem 3.XI.T1 proves that the latter global section does not exist. Therefore spin-structure existence cannot be identified with a global pointwise sign choice in the covering fiber. ∎

Corollary 3.XI.C9 — “There are two spin structures on RP³” and “each SO(3) rotation has two Spin(3) lifts” are distinct two-valued statements. Neither two-valuedness should be used as evidence that the other has been selected.

3.XI.I — Book 0 naturality obstruction applied to the lift pair

Topology has already forbidden a continuous global selector. We now ask the weaker question whether a possibly discontinuous selector could nevertheless be canonical under the symmetries of the admitted data.

The deck involution δ fixes every base point R=p(q) while exchanging the two candidate lifts with no fixed lift. This is exactly the structure required by the Book 0 automorphism obstruction.

Theorem 3.XI.T7 — No canonical deck-equivariant lift selector from symmetric data. Suppose all input data available to a proposed selector are invariant under δ:q↦−q and the selector is required to be natural under automorphisms of those data. Then no canonical choice of q over −q exists.

Proof. The input structure is unchanged by δ, while the two possible outputs are exchanged by δ and neither is fixed. Naturality would require the selected output to be invariant under δ. That would require q=−q on S³, impossible. This is Theorem 0.IV.T1 applied to the lift fiber. ∎

Corollary 3.XI.C10 — Coordinate conventions can choose; the symmetric theory cannot prefer. A branch convention, lexicographic sign rule, chosen base frame, or added orientation-sensitive datum may select representatives, but the selection then inherits the asymmetry of that added convention.

The two obstructions are logically independent in strength:

Topological obstruction: no globally continuous selector exists, even an arbitrary one.

Naturality obstruction: from deck-symmetric data, no canonical selector exists even if discontinuity is allowed.

3.XI.J — THIRD CLIMAX: lift, mirror, and chirality nonselection

Theorem 3.XI.T8 — Lift/Mirror/Chirality Nonselection Theorem. From the certified mathematical content of Books 0–2 and Sections 3.I–3.X alone, none of the following selects a preferred member of the Spin(3) lift pair or a preferred physical chirality:

(i) cophase θ↦θ±π/2;

(ii) the π half-turn;

(iii) FlatWave=sgn(sin 2θ);

(iv) the reciprocal primitive or UNA operators;

(v) projective/Rodrigues coordinates on RP³≅SO(3);

(vi) the tautological-line transition class or tangent-orientation results of Sections 3.VII–3.VIII;

(vii) the existence of a spin structure from Section 3.IX;

(viii) the quaternion fiber sign q versus −q;

(ix) the negative-orientation mirror component of O(3).

Proof. Items (iii)–(v) and the certified scalar operators are invariant under the deck shift by Theorems 3.XI.T2–T3. Items (i)–(ii) are different transformations from the deck shift by Theorem 3.XI.T4. Item (ix) is a different orientation component from the lift fiber by Theorem 3.XI.T5. Item (vii) concerns a bundle lift structure rather than a global section of the group covering by Theorem 3.XI.T6. The projective and characteristic-class data in (vi) do not furnish a distinguished fiber representative, and any proposed selection from deck-symmetric data is blocked by Theorem 3.XI.T7. Moreover, Theorem 3.XI.T1 rules out a global continuous sign branch altogether. Finally, no physical chirality law, parity-violating interaction, representation assignment, or empirical calibration has been imported into Book 3. Therefore the admitted mathematics does not select a preferred physical chirality. ∎

Scope limitation. The theorem is deliberately conditional on the present premise set. It does not claim that mathematics or physics can never select chirality. A later extension may break the symmetry by an explicit orientation-sensitive axiom, physical interaction law, boundary condition, representation choice, projection contract, or empirical input. Such an addition must be entered in the ledger under its true status rather than retroactively attributed to the present scalar/projective calculus.

3.XI.K — What the obstruction positively teaches us

The negative theorem is structurally useful. It identifies exactly where any successful chirality mechanism would have to enter.

Necessary condition 3.XI.NC1 — Deck sensitivity. Any quantity intended to distinguish q from −q must fail to factor through SO(3); it must live on the Spin(3) lift or on a still richer structure.

Necessary condition 3.XI.NC2 — Global consistency. If the mechanism is represented as a field of local lift choices over SO(3), it cannot be an everywhere continuous scalar sign section of the nontrivial double cover. Its global formulation must instead use legitimate bundle data, transition functions, or an enlarged state space.

Necessary condition 3.XI.NC3 — Orientation sensitivity for physical chirality. To select a physical handedness, the future law must distinguish opposite orientations in a physically defined representation. Mere q-sign sensitivity is not enough, because q↦−q is not mirror by Theorem 3.XI.T5.

Necessary condition 3.XI.NC4 — Empirical bridge. Even a mathematically orientation-sensitive extension does not become a law of nature until a physical import or projection contract specifies observables, units where relevant, and falsification conditions.

These conditions convert the obstruction from a dead end into a search filter. Any candidate mechanism that remains entirely on RP³/SO(3), remains π-periodic in the certified scalar calculus, or merely relabels q and −q as “left” and “right” fails before empirical testing.

3.XI.L — Certification and extension ledger

Certified results of Section 3.XI:

1. The connected double cover S³→SO(3) admits no global continuous section.

2. Smooth local lift branches exist on evenly covered charts, including the Rodrigues q0>0 hemisphere branch.

3. Bare discontinuous selectors require added branch/cut conventions and are not supplied by the quotient itself.

4. Every deck-blind datum factors through SO(3) and cannot distinguish q from −q.

5. All certified Book 2 scalar operators, including FlatWave, are invariant under the 2π deck shift that exchanges the quaternion lifts.

6. Cophase, π half-turn, and 2π deck shift are distinct operations with different quotient effects.

7. The q↦−q deck involution is not the negative-orientation mirror operation.

8. A spin structure on RP³ is not a pointwise global section Spin(3)→SO(3).

9. Book 0’s naturality obstruction independently forbids a canonical lift selection from deck-symmetric data.

10. No preferred physical chirality follows from the certified Book 3 mathematics without an added orientation-sensitive physical premise.

Section 3.XI extension ledger.

New Projection-specific mathematical axioms: 0.

New canonical definitions: lift selector, deck involution, deck-blind datum.

Standard mathematical imports beyond 3.X: 0 substantive new structures; only the local-triviality property already contained in the definition of a covering map and standard connectedness of S³ are used.

Physics imports: 0.

Projection contracts: 0.

Empirical calibrations: 0.

Physical parity-violating laws introduced: 0.

Preferred-lift axioms introduced: 0.

Preferred-chirality axioms introduced: 0.

Closure status. The third Book 3 climax is closed. The obstruction is now three-way separated: projective quotient erases the lift sign; topology forbids a continuous global recovery; naturality forbids a canonical recovery from symmetric data. Mirror orientation remains a separate structure, and physical chirality remains unselected.

Certified handoff to 3.XII. The mathematics of Book 3 is now sufficient for a final historical-vocabulary reconciliation and dependency audit. Section 3.XII should retire every use of “cophase,” “half-turn,” “antipodal,” “mirror,” “spin sign,” or “chirality” that conflates distinct operations; certify the full dependency chain from Book 1 phase carriers through the lift obstruction; and close Book 3 with an explicit ledger of what has and has not entered the revised theory.

3.XII — Historical Vocabulary Reconciliation, Dependency Audit, and Closure Ledger

Purpose. Book 3 has accumulated several structures that are individually binary, two-sheeted, sign-valued, or orientation-sensitive. That numerical resemblance is precisely where historical terminology became dangerous. The final task is therefore not to add another construction, but to freeze the meanings of the constructions already proved, audit every dependency, separate standard mathematics from Projection-specific deductions, and specify exactly what later books are permitted to inherit.

This section is constitutional. Once Book 3 is closed, a later argument may not recover an older identification merely by repeating an older name. Any identification not certified here must re-enter through an explicit map, theorem, added axiom, physics import, projection contract, or empirical calibration.

3.XII.A — Canonical vocabulary freeze

Definition 3.XII.D1 — Phase point. A phase point is an element of whichever phase carrier has been explicitly declared: the lifted carrier T₂π or the π-periodic quotient Tπ. Statements whose truth depends on the carrier must name the carrier.

Definition 3.XII.D2 — Cophase. Two lifted phase points are cophase exactly when their separation is

Δx≡±π/2  (mod 2π).

A directed cophase step is τπ/2. On T₂π it has order four; on Tπ its induced action has order two. Cophase is the sine–cosine quarter-turn relation and nothing else unless a later theorem proves an equivalent formulation.

Definition 3.XII.D3 — Half-turn. The half-turn is the phase translation

τπ:x↦x+π.

On T₂π it is the square of the directed cophase step. It is not itself cophase.

Definition 3.XII.D4 — Phase fold. The map

κ:T₂π→Tπ

forgets the lifted half-turn distinction x versus x+π. The fold is a quotient operation; it is not a mirror transformation and not a Spin(3) deck transformation.

Definition 3.XII.D5 — FlatWave sign. On its certified domain,

FlatWave(x)=sgn(sin 2x).

It is a π-periodic binary scalar character that reverses under cophase and is preserved by the π half-turn. No topological orientation class, lift sign, or physical chirality is part of this definition.

Definition 3.XII.D6 — Projective antipodal identification. In homogeneous projective coordinates, nonzero scalar multiples represent the same projective point. In the unit-sphere realization of RP³ this includes

q∼−q.

When q∈S³≅Spin(3), the map q↦−q is the deck involution of the double cover S³→SO(3). This use of “antipodal” is not interchangeable with the π half-turn of the scalar phase carrier.

Definition 3.XII.D7 — Tautological-line transition sign. The overlap signs εij arising from projective line representatives define the Z₂ transition cocycle of the tautological line bundle γ and its class w₁(γ). This is a line-bundle datum.

Definition 3.XII.D8 — Tangent orientation. Tangent orientability of RPⁿ is governed by

w₁(T RPⁿ)=(n+1)a,

where a=w₁(γ). Tangent orientation is therefore a tangent-bundle property and must not be silently replaced by the tautological-line class.

Definition 3.XII.D9 — Spin obstruction and spin structure. For an oriented manifold, the obstruction tested in Book 3 is w₂ of the tangent bundle. A spin structure is a global lift of the oriented orthonormal frame bundle through Spin(n)→SO(n). It is not a physical spin measurement and not a pointwise choice of q over −q in the group covering.

Definition 3.XII.D10 — Lift sign. In the specialization Spin(3)→SO(3), the two lifts of a rotation are q and −q. “Lift sign” refers only to this two-point covering fiber unless a richer structure is explicitly supplied.

Definition 3.XII.D11 — Mirror frame. A mirror frame is a frame in the negative-orientation class relative to a declared positive orientation. In the three-dimensional orthogonal specialization, the distinction is represented by det=−1 versus det=+1 in O(3). A generic minus sign is not a mirror theorem.

Definition 3.XII.D12 — Physical chirality. Physical chirality means an orientation-sensitive physical handedness defined by an explicit physical representation, law, observable, or projection contract. Book 3 introduces no such physical law. Mathematical two-valuedness alone does not instantiate this definition.

Rule 3.XII.R1 — Name-Requires-Map Rule. If two differently constructed structures are claimed to be the same, equivalent, dual, mirror, or physically identified, the claim must provide the map relating their underlying spaces and prove the relevant equality, covariance, pullback, or intertwining property. Shared cardinality, shared sign notation, or a common Z₂ pattern is insufficient.

Theorem 3.XII.T1 — Vocabulary Separation Theorem. Under Definitions 3.XII.D1–D12, the terms cophase, half-turn, phase fold, FlatWave sign, projective antipode, tautological-line transition sign, tangent orientation, spin structure, lift sign, mirror frame, and physical chirality denote distinct constructions unless an explicit later theorem identifies particular instances.

Proof. Each term is defined by different data and, in several cases, by different spaces: phase translation on T₂π; quotienting to Tπ; a scalar sign function; homogeneous projective equivalence; a line-bundle cocycle; tangent characteristic classes; a principal-bundle lift; a two-point covering fiber; determinant orientation of frames; and an as-yet-unimported physical handedness. Sections 3.V, 3.VII–3.XI additionally exhibit different transformation laws for several of these objects. Therefore common two-valuedness cannot supply an identification. ∎

3.XII.B — Historical vocabulary reconciliation ledger

The following classifications govern every historical use of these terms when such material is reconsidered inside the revised theory.

RETAINED, with canonical meaning:

1. Cophase is retained only as the sine–cosine quarter-turn relation Δx=±π/2.

2. Half-turn is retained only as Δx=π, the square of the directed cophase step on T₂π.

3. FlatWave is retained as the exact sign character sgn(sin 2x), cophase-reversing and half-turn-preserving.

4. Mirror is retained only as negative orientation relative to a declared positive orientation in an orientation-bearing frame space.

5. Projective half-angle coordinates, projective charts, transition maps, and the RP¹/RP²/RP³ constructions are retained as mathematics under their declared domains.

6. The Spin(3) pair q and −q is retained as the covering-fiber pair over one SO(3) rotation.

QUALIFIED — permitted only when the target structure is named:

1. “Antipodal” may describe q↦−q on a sphere/projective cover, but may not be used as a synonym for scalar half-turn without an explicit map proving that relation in the stated model.

2. “Orientation sign” must name which orientation datum is intended: coordinate orientation, determinant sign of a frame, w₁(γ), w₁(T RPⁿ), or another specified object.

3. “Spin sign” may be used informally only for the q versus −q covering-fiber label when the Spin(3) context is explicit; it does not mean physical spin state or helicity.

4. “Parity” must name the actual involution or physical parity operator being used. It is not automatically identical to phase reflection, reciprocal inversion, mirror frame reversal, or q↦−q.

5. “Left” and “right” are admissible only after the relevant orientation-bearing space or physical representation has been declared.

RETIRED as identifications within the certified Book 3 theory:

1. cophase = π half-turn;

2. cophase = antipodal identification;

3. cophase = reciprocal inversion;

4. cophase = q↦−q;

5. mirror = generic negative sign;

6. mirror = x↦−x without a proved map to the intended orientation-bearing frame space;

7. mirror = reciprocal conjugation;

8. mirror = q↦−q;

9. FlatWave = w₁(γ) without an explicit comparison map and pullback theorem;

10. FlatWave = w₁(T RPⁿ) without an explicit comparison map and pullback theorem;

11. FlatWave = Spin(3) lift sign;

12. FlatWave = physical chirality;

13. q-sign = physical chirality;

14. existence of a spin structure = pointwise selection of q rather than −q;

15. tangent orientability = existence of a spin structure;

16. octant arrows = temporal growth or decay absent an explicit time map;

17. “manifest/mirror phase” as a physical ontology absent a new explicit definition and contract;

18. the scalar reciprocal calculus by itself = derivation of a parity-violating weak-interaction law.

Rule 3.XII.R2 — Historical Noninheritance Rule. Repetition of a retired or qualified historical label in an older paper, note, diagram, spreadsheet, or conversation does not restore it as a premise. Historical material may motivate a new test, but the new claim must re-enter the revised theory by the ordinary extension protocol.

Corollary 3.XII.C1 — Vocabulary cannot smuggle physics. In particular, calling two mathematical branches “left/right,” “mirror,” “particle/antiparticle,” or “chiral” does not constitute a physical derivation.

Theorem 3.XII.T2 — Acyclic Dependency Certification. No theorem of Book 3 requires a later Book 3 theorem as a premise, and no topological or rotation-theoretic result is used retroactively to establish a Book 2 scalar identity.

Proof. The displayed dependency chain is ordered by construction. Sections 3.I–3.V use only certified Book 1–2 material and elementary phase/projective algebra. The projective atlas precedes the transition cocycle; the cocycle and tautological line bundle precede the tangent-bundle Stiefel–Whitney calculation; tangent orientability precedes w₂ and spin; the generic RPⁿ analysis precedes the RP³/SO(3) specialization; and the Spin(3) covering structure precedes the lift-selection obstruction. No reverse dependency occurs in any proof. ∎

Corollary 3.XII.C2 — Book 3 does not retro-prove FlatWave. The identity FlatWave=sgn(sin 2x) and its quarter-turn transformation law remain Book 2/early-Book-3 scalar results. Characteristic classes and Spin topology are later comparisons, not their causes.

Corollary 3.XII.C3 — The RP³ rotation specialization cannot be used as a hidden premise for the earlier generic projective sections.

3.XII.D — Comparison-map and nonidentification audit

The comparison tests introduced throughout the book now have the following final statuses.

FlatWave versus w₁(γ): NOT IDENTIFIED. A formal Z₂ resemblance exists, but no certified map has been supplied whose pullback identifies the two classes.

FlatWave versus w₁(T RPⁿ): NOT IDENTIFIED. The RP¹ diagnostic already shows that tautological-line and tangent-orientation classes cannot be casually conflated; no FlatWave-to-tangent comparison theorem has been proved.

FlatWave versus Spin(3) lift sign: PROVED DISTINCT AS CHARACTERS OF THE RELEVANT TRANSFORMATIONS. The deck shift θ↦θ+2π exchanges q and −q while leaving FlatWave invariant.

Cophase versus Spin(3) deck shift: PROVED DISTINCT. Cophase changes θ by ±π/2 and generically changes the SO(3) rotation; the deck shift changes θ by 2π and leaves the SO(3) rotation fixed.

π half-turn versus Spin(3) deck shift: PROVED DISTINCT. The first changes the base rotation generically; the second does not.

Lift sign versus mirror orientation: PROVED DISTINCT. q and −q project to the same determinant +1 rotation, whereas a mirror frame lies in the determinant −1 component of O(3).

Spin-structure existence versus pointwise lift selection: PROVED DISTINCT. The former lifts an oriented frame bundle; the latter would be a section of Spin(3)→SO(3), and no global continuous section exists.

Positive reciprocal inversion versus mirror orientation: NOT IDENTIFIED. The relation s↦1/s is an exact algebraic involution, but no theorem makes it determinant-reversing on the relevant frame space.

Projective half-angle coordinates versus physical observables: NO CONTRACT. They are mathematical coordinates unless a later projection contract assigns physical meaning.

Theorem 3.XII.T3 — Binary-Structure Noncollapse. The certified binary or two-sheeted structures of Book 3 do not collapse to one universal Z₂ variable.

Proof. Some pairs remain merely unconnected because no comparison map has been supplied; other pairs are positively distinguished by incompatible transformation laws or by belonging to different bundles/spaces. A universal identification would therefore contradict the certified separation results of Sections 3.VII and 3.XI. ∎

This theorem is one of the principal safeguards of the revised theory. A common Z₂ algebra can describe many different phenomena without making those phenomena the same object.

2. tracing that cophase action through the certified four primitive functions and eight-octant carrier;

Provenance rule. The use of standard mathematics inside this chain is not a claim that Projection Craft discovered those standard structures. The book’s claim is the internally audited route by which the certified reciprocal/phase calculus reaches them, the exact identities it carries along that route, and the nonidentifications required to prevent overstatement.

3.XII.G — Certification of the three climaxes

FIRST CLIMAX — COPHASE–FLATWAVE CHARACTER.

Certified statement:

FlatWave(x±π/2)=−FlatWave(x),

FlatWave(x+π)=FlatWave(x).

Meaning: FlatWave is an exact binary character of the certified scalar cophase action. It is not thereby a characteristic class, mirror sign, lift sign, or physical chirality.

SECOND CLIMAX — SPIN-LIFT CONDITION.

Certified statement:

w₁(T RPⁿ)=(n+1)a,

and, once orientability holds,

w₂(T RPⁿ)=C(n+1,2)a².

For n≥3 the resulting real-projective spin condition is n≡3 (mod 4), with RP¹ treated separately because H² vanishes. Meaning: Book 3 determines when the standard tangent topology admits spin structure. It does not select one spin structure and does not assign physical spin.

THIRD CLIMAX — LIFT/MIRROR/CHIRALITY NONSELECTION.

Certified statement: the connected double cover S³→SO(3) has no global continuous section; deck-symmetric data cannot canonically prefer q over −q; FlatWave is deck-blind; q↦−q is not mirror; and no preferred physical chirality follows from the admitted premise set.

Theorem 3.XII.T5 — Climax Coherence Theorem. The three climaxes are mutually compatible because they concern different structures and transformation scales.

Proof. The first climax concerns the ±π/2 action on the certified scalar phase calculus. The second concerns tangent-bundle topology of RPⁿ. The third concerns the Spin(3)→SO(3) covering, frame orientation, and nonselection. The actual lift-changing phase shift in the axis-angle parametrization is 2π, under which FlatWave is invariant. Therefore the cophase sign flip does not contradict lift-blindness, and the existence of a spin structure does not supply a forbidden global section of the group cover. ∎

Rule 3.XII.R3 — Future Physical-Use Rule. Theorem 3.XII.T6 — Inheritance Lock. A later book may extend Book 3 but may not strengthen a Book 3 theorem merely by changing vocabulary. Any stronger identification requires a new premise or a new proof and must be ledgered at the point of introduction.

Proof. This is the direct consequence of the revised-theory extension protocol together with Rules 3.XII.R1–R3. Without a new map, theorem, axiom, import, contract, or calibration, the hypotheses have not changed; therefore the conclusion cannot legitimately strengthen. ∎

3.XII.I — Final Book 3 closure ledger

BOOK 4 — GEOMETRIC RANK, COFRAMES, AND CONNECTION

PREPARATION CONSTITUTION

Constitutional status. This opening records the dependency rules under which Book 4 was constructed. The numbered sections below now carry their final stated theorem, extension, obstruction, or import statuses. The purpose of the constitution is to prevent a geometric construction from acquiring physical meaning merely because familiar spatial, frame, or curvature language appears.

Canonical Flower-role lock. The Flower construction is retained in the forward dependency spine only as a constructive witness for a continuous plane, a copied length standard, a synthetic right angle, and the resulting metric representation. It is not retained as a preferred coordinate system, tetrahedral lattice, physical substrate, or source of the independent rank-three carrier. Sections 4.VI–4.VII preserve coordinate and finite-lattice calculations as supplementary geometric diagnostics only; no downstream theorem may use them as foundational premises unless they are separately re-admitted.

Governing question. What independent geometric structure must be added to the closed one-generator phase/projective calculus of Books 0–3 in order to obtain a nondegenerate plane, a rank-three carrier, local coframes, orientation, connection, and curvature-ready geometry without importing spacetime or dynamics?

The intended answer is deliberately two-part:

1. the one-generator calculus cannot manufacture geometric rank by relabeling dependent functions, branches, sheets, or projective representatives;

2. an independently declared geometric carrier can supply the missing rank, after which Projection variables may parameterize or modulate geometric data but do not become the independent directions themselves.

Book 4 therefore takes the independently ranked field base M₃ of P4.1 as its carrier. Equal-circle/Flower, triangular, tetrahedral, sphere-packing, and vector-equilibrium constructions are optional synthetic or coordinate examples only. They may be proved and audited where useful, but none is privileged as the geometry of space.

SOURCE BOUNDARY

Live premises. Book 4 may depend directly only on the certified revised Books 0–3 and on standard mathematics explicitly declared at the point of use.

Historical T0–T3. Historical T-series results may be consulted claim-by-claim as provenance and candidate proofs. A historical conclusion is admitted only if it is already certified in the revised series or is re-established with its status visible here. Historical physical imports remain conditional; they are not inherited as Book 4 physics.

Historical T4 and later books. T4+, T5, T6, the Spacetime paper, Towards Unification, Reverse Engineering Einstein, the S essays, loose papers, notebooks, diagrams, and conversational explorations are candidate sources only. They may suggest lemmas, counterexamples, coordinate choices, or obstructions. They are not premises.

Book 4 physical status. Planned field-theory/physics imports: 0. Planned Projection contracts: 0. Planned empirical calibrations: 0. P4.1 below is a mathematical geometric extension, not a physics import: it admits only the pre-metric construction data required for the Flower operation. Physical space, physical time, Lorentzian signature, gravity, electromagnetism, quantum dynamics, action principles, and field equations remain outside the certified Book 4 layer unless the constitution is explicitly amended and the extension ledger records the change. Flower Power is retained as a source for the later mechanics layer, and because this is a consolidated reference corpus its Newtonian postulates and proofs are to be re-presented in the later physics-admission book rather than merely cited from the source; they are not imported into Book 4 geometry..

MINIMAL GEOMETRIC EXTENSION P4.1 — PRE-METRIC FLOWER SUBSTRATES

Import statement. Book 4 admits a pre-metric construction substrate independent of the one-generator Projection state. Points may be joined and straight segments continuously extended; one positive construction length r may be copied congruently; equal circles with that copied radius may be drawn and intersected; and the triangular faces produced by repeated flower operations may be continuously filled. No Euclidean inner product or Cartesian coordinate system is assumed by this admission.h

r>0..

The only metric-like datum admitted at this stage is the ability to copy the same construction segment r. When two equal circles of radius r are centered at neighboring points O and A, an intersection point B supplies a primitive three-edge congruence relationd

OA=OB=AB=r.,

This equality is initially a congruence relation between copied construction segments. It does not yet mean that an inner product, Cartesian norm, Pythagorean distance law, or numerical angle function has been supplied. Those structures must be constructed or separately admitted later..

What P4.1 imports: independent geometric construction data not generated by Books 0–3—points, straight continuation, incidence/intersection, a copyable congruence unit r, the equal-circle operation, and continuous filling of the triangular faces generated by that operation..

What P4.1 does not import: a Euclidean affine plane, an inner product, Cartesian coordinates, numerical angle measure, the Pythagorean theorem, a global distance function, the parallel postulate, global flatness, a rank-three base, a coframe, connection, curvature, time, Lorentzian signature, dynamics, physical space, units, or any coupling between Projection phase and geometry..

Theorem target enabled by P4.1. Repeated equal-circle closure is to generate a connected triangular cell complex whose continuous realization is locally planar. Six congruent primitive triangles around an interior center determine six equal native sectors. A purely synthetic bisection construction then produces a perpendicular/right angle without Cartesian axes, dot products, trigonometry, or a predeclared Euclidean inner product. The analytic metric is introduced only after that orthogonal structure has been earned.s

φ:F²→T₂π,,

Only after an independent geometric carrier and the planar right-angle/metric witness have been established may the Projection phase be placed upon that carrier as a scalar field; Projection functions may then modulate geometric coefficients but do not supply the carrier directions.

Minimality rule P4.1-M. No later Book 4 argument may attribute the rank, right angle, metric, or eventual three-dimensional extension to the Projection phase calculus. The rank-three carrier must be independently admitted or constructed from the geometric assumptions stated above; every further addition—global flatness, a bilinear metric completion, a third independent direction, connection data, or physics—must remain visible in the dependency ledger..

DEPENDENCY GATES

Gate A — Rank before dimension language. No collection of functions of one scalar may be called independent geometric directions until differential rank is proved.

Gate B — Flower before metric; metric after right angle. The equal-circle construction may use only copied-length congruence, incidence, straight continuation, intersection, and continuous triangular filling until perpendicularity has been constructed synthetically. No dot product, Cartesian norm, Pythagorean theorem, or numerical angle coordinate may be used to prove that right angle. After the perpendicular unit frame exists, a homogeneous positive-definite bilinear metric may be introduced by requiring that frame to be orthonormal. Global flatness and uniqueness of parallels remain a separate declaration and are not retroactively attributed to the local Flower construction..

Gate C — Continuous carrier before discrete lattice. The continuous affine carrier and its metric must be established before an integer lattice, sphere packing, finite neighbor shell, or discrete dynamics is discussed.

Gate D — Coframe before connection. Frame and coframe rank, domains, overlaps, and orientation must be defined before a connection is introduced.

Gate E — Connection before curvature. Variation of coordinates, frame coefficients, or metric components is not itself curvature. Curvature must be computed from a declared connection or its equivalent geometric structure.

Gate F — Orientation before handedness. A volume form or equivalent orientation structure must exist before “left,” “right,” “mirror,” or handed-frame language is used. Book 3’s mirror definition remains controlling.

Gate G — Geometry before physics. A geometric carrier, lattice, coframe, connection, torsion, or curvature does not become space, spacetime, matter, gravity, or a physical field without a later Projection contract or physics import.

Gate H — Coordinate novelty firewall. If a continuous Flower/tetrahedral description is related to ordinary Euclidean coordinates by an invertible linear change of basis, the coordinate change alone creates no new invariant geometry and no empirical novelty.

ℓ>0 — geometric contact length; no physical unit is assigned in Book 4.

4.I — FROM ANGLE TO COFRAME: THE RANK OBSTRUCTION

Purpose. Book 4 begins with a negative theorem. Books 0–3 supply many exact scalar readouts, reciprocal pairs, folds, signs, projective representatives, and finite covers, but these structures are locally generated by one continuous phase. Before any plane or spatial carrier is admitted, Book 4 must prove that representation multiplicity is not geometric rank.

4.I.1 Inherited one-generator structure. On every regular chart the canonical primitives remain

srx(x)=|csc x|+cot x,

sxp(x)=|csc x|−cot x=1/srx(x),

cxp(x)=|sec x|+tan x,

crx(x)=|sec x|−tan x=1/cxp(x),

with urx=srx−crx and uxp=cxp−sxp. Saw, FlatWave, the harmonic carrier, logarithmic coordinates, projective coordinates, and the certified Book 0–3 lifts are functions or finite enrichments of the same local continuous generator. The absolute values remain part of the global definitions; chart simplifications may be made only after the chart is declared.

Definition 4.I.1 — Differential rank. Let α¹,…,αᵐ be one-forms on a smooth region U. At p∈U,

rankₚ{α¹,…,αᵐ}=dim span{α¹ₚ,…,αᵐₚ}⊂T*ₚU.

This counts independent cotangent directions. It does not count symbols, functions, branches, sheets, displayed components, or the dimension of an ambient representation space.

Lemma 4.I.1 — Wedge criterion. A family θ¹,…,θⁿ is linearly independent at p exactly when θ¹∧⋯∧θⁿ≠0 there. In particular, two one-forms are dependent exactly when their wedge vanishes.

Theorem 4.I.1 — One-scalar coframe obstruction. Let x be one differentiable local scalar coordinate and let

θᴬ=fᴬ(x)dx,

A=1,…,m,

for arbitrary scalar coefficient functions fᴬ. Then

rank{θ¹,…,θᵐ}≤1.

For every A,B,

θᴬ∧θᴮ=fᴬ(x)fᴮ(x) dx∧dx=0,

and every n-fold wedge with n≥2 vanishes.

Proof. Every θᴬ belongs to the one-dimensional span of dx. No choice, number, or nonlinearity of the coefficients changes that span. ∎

Corollary 4.I.1 — Function count is not direction count. For any differentiable map F(x)=(F¹(x),…,Fᵐ(x)) into any finite-dimensional display space,

dF=((F¹)'(x),…,(Fᵐ)'(x))ᵀ dx

has differential rank at most one. A curve may require several ambient coordinates for an injective or globally smooth representation while remaining intrinsically one-dimensional.

Application to the primitive quartet. If one writes proposed geometric one-forms

θsrx=srx(x)dx, θsxp=sxp(x)dx, θcxp=cxp(x)dx, θcrx=crx(x)dx,

then every pairwise wedge vanishes. The exact reciprocal relations srx·sxp=1 and cxp·crx=1 make the algebraic dependence still more explicit. Four named primitives do not supply four coframe directions.

Application to UNA and Saw. Since urx, uxp, sawr=1/urx, and sawx=1/uxp are scalar functions of x on their regular domains,

d(urx)∧d(uxp)=0,

and the same holds for every pair of their differentials. Two transfer channels therefore do not constitute a two-dimensional base.

Application to FlatWave. On each regular open quadrant FlatWave=sgn(sin 2x)=±1 is locally constant, hence d(FlatWave)=0. Its sign contains discrete orientation-sensitive information; it supplies no additional local differential direction.

Application to the harmonic carrier. With H=sin(2x)/4 and V=cos(2x)/2,

V²+4H²=1/4.

The map x↦(H,V) draws a closed one-dimensional carrier in a two-dimensional ambient plane. Moreover

dH=(1/2)cos(2x)dx,

dV=−sin(2x)dx,

so dH∧dV=0. Embedding dimension is not state dimension.

Application to differential closure. If z=srx(x), then on a fixed regular quadrant z′=−(1+z²)/2. Repeated derivatives and rational differential expressions remain functions of z and hence of x. Derivative order can encode higher jets of a one-dimensional state; it does not create additional base directions.

Application to homogeneous and projective lifts. A two-component lift X=(u,v)ᵀ may linearize the Riccati dynamics, but the represented projective state [X]∈RP¹ is locally determined by one affine ratio such as z=v/u. If u=u(x) and v=v(x), then du∧dv=0. Projective completion repairs global chart structure; it does not add local dimension.

Lemma 4.I.2 — Finite labels do not increase local dimension. If D is a finite discrete set, then X×D has the same local manifold dimension as X. Consequently quadrant labels, branch signs, mirror labels, finite cover sheets, cophase copies, and central lift signs may retain real global or discrete information without creating a new tangent direction.

Corollary 4.I.2 — Cover degree is not dimension. A smooth covering map is locally a diffeomorphism. Two sheets, four sheets, or an unwrapped universal cover can change global topology and monodromy while preserving the local differential dimension of the covered state.

Theorem 4.I.2 — General local rank barrier for the certified Projection state. Let every continuously varying quantity in a proposed geometric construction factor locally through one certified phase coordinate x, even after reciprocal reparameterization, Möbius transformation, logarithmic coordinates, bounded normalization, finite covers, branch enrichment, homogeneous lifts, matrix actions, or cophase duplication. Then every one-form obtained solely by differentiating those state quantities lies in span{dx}; the continuously generated differential family has rank at most one.

This theorem does not say that higher-dimensional geometry is impossible, nor that Projection scalars cannot later modulate a metric or coframe. It says only that dependent scalar readouts cannot substitute for independent geometric directions.

4.I.2 State coordinate versus base coordinate. If a scalar state φ is later defined on an independently constructed carrier F, then for every scalar readout A(φ),

dA(φ)=A′(φ)dφ.

All such readouts still span at most the line generated by dφ. If the carrier has independent local one-forms η¹,η², or later η¹,η²,η³, their independence belongs to the carrier construction, not to the scalar field living on it.

Once an independent coframe exists, Projection functions may lawfully enter its coefficients. For example,

θᵃ=Eᵃᵢ(φ,q)dqⁱ.

If the dqⁱ are already independent, then

θ¹∧⋯∧θⁿ=det(E) dq¹∧⋯∧dqⁿ.

The phase may therefore scale, shear, rotate, or otherwise parameterize a nondegenerate coframe without being credited for the independent directions themselves.

Failure ledger. The following inferences are retired: four primitives ⇒ four dimensions; two Saw/UNA channels ⇒ a plane; (H,V)∈R² ⇒ a two-dimensional state; RP¹ completion ⇒ a new spatial line; two homogeneous components ⇒ two base coordinates; finite branch/cophase multiplicity ⇒ additional local rank; cover degree ⇒ dimension; Clifford or matrix-algebra dimension ⇒ base-manifold dimension.

First climax — the coframe rank barrier. If θᴬ=fᴬ(x)dx, then θᴬ∧θᴮ=0 for every A,B. No plane, area form, spatial volume, or nondegenerate coframe has yet appeared. The transition from Projection phase to geometry must therefore be an explicit geometric extension. Book 4 will not search for hidden spatial axes inside the scalar operators. It will ask instead for the minimum independent construction data from which a lawful carrier can be built.

Certified handoff to 4.II. The angle may parameterize coframe coefficients only after independent coframe directions exist. Section 4.II therefore defines the minimum rank conditions abstractly; Section 4.III closes the remaining state/base loopholes; and Section 4.IV constructs the first actual rank-two carrier from the pre-metric Flower substrate P4.1.

4.II — THE MINIMAL COFRAME PROBLEM

Purpose. Section 4.I proves what one scalar cannot do. Section 4.II states, without yet choosing a metric or a Flower realization, the minimum differential structure that a genuine geometric carrier must eventually exhibit.

Definition 4.II.1 — Local frame. Let M be a smooth n-dimensional carrier. A local frame on U⊂M is an ordered family of tangent vectors e₁,…,eₙ that is linearly independent at every point of U.

Definition 4.II.2 — Local coframe. A local coframe is an ordered family of one-forms θ¹,…,θⁿ satisfying θᵃ(e_b)=δᵃ_b. No metric is required to define either a frame or a coframe.

Theorem 4.II.1 — Nondegeneracy criterion. A family θ¹,…,θⁿ is a local coframe exactly when

θ¹∧⋯∧θⁿ≠0.

Equivalently, if η¹,…,ηⁿ is any already independent reference coframe and

θᵃ=Eᵃᵢηⁱ,

then

θ¹∧⋯∧θⁿ=det(E) η¹∧⋯∧ηⁿ,

so the candidate is nondegenerate exactly when det(E)≠0.

Proof. Multilinearity and antisymmetry of the wedge product give the determinant identity. A nonzero top wedge is equivalent to linear independence of the one-forms. ∎

Corollary 4.II.1 — Planar requirement. A smooth two-dimensional carrier requires two independent local one-forms. Its area-form candidate θ¹∧θ² must be nonzero. Two functions of one phase cannot satisfy this requirement merely by being numerically different.

Corollary 4.II.2 — Rank-three requirement. A smooth three-dimensional carrier requires three independent local one-forms with θ¹∧θ²∧θ³≠0. A third direction must therefore be genuinely independent of the two directions used to certify the plane.

Metric independence. The statements above concern rank only. A nondegenerate coframe does not yet specify lengths, angles, orthogonality, or curvature. A metric is additional structure. Conversely, a synthetic construction may establish incidence and perpendicularity before coframe notation is introduced; the coframe language may then certify the rank of the constructed carrier analytically. Book 4 will use the latter order for the Flower plane.

Coefficient/base separation. Suppose a carrier has an independent reference coframe ηⁱ and a Projection phase φ is later defined upon it. Then a phase-dependent candidate

θᵃ=Eᵃᵢ(φ,q)ηⁱ

may have full rank whenever det E≠0. There is no conflict with the one-scalar obstruction because the independent ηⁱ were supplied by the carrier; the scalar field supplies only coefficient data.

Negative theorem 4.II.1 — No canonical coframe from Books 0–3. The closed scalar/projective theory does not select a particular rank-two or rank-three coframe. Any such selection before a separate geometric construction would be an undeclared extension. In particular, the names srx, sxp, cxp, crx, urx, uxp, Saw, FlatWave, H, and V cannot be promoted to basis directions by notation alone.

Minimal geometric obligation. A lawful rank-two extension must provide: (i) a carrier with genuine local two-dimensional structure; (ii) two independent tangent/cotangent directions; and (iii) transition rules sufficient to compare local constructions. It need not initially provide a metric. A lawful rank-three extension must subsequently add a third independent direction and re-establish nondegeneracy.

Role of P4.1. The pre-metric Flower substrate does not declare an inner product or a ready-made Euclidean plane. It supplies the independent construction operations from which Section 4.IV must actually build a continuous two-cell carrier. Only after that construction succeeds may Book 4 certify local rank two by a nonvanishing wedge. The coframe criterion therefore acts as a test of the Flower plane, not as a premise from which the Flower plane is assumed.

Certified handoff. Section 4.III now closes the remaining loophole: no cover, branch, fold, projective lift, or multi-component representation of the old one-generator state may masquerade as the missing carrier. Once that separation is complete, Section 4.IV may construct F² from the independent P4.1 geometry and ask whether it earns a right angle and metric.

4.III — FOLDED-STATE RECONSTRUCTION AND THE STATE/BASE SEPARATION

Purpose. Before the Flower substrate is allowed to add geometry, Book 4 closes every remaining route by which the old one-generator state might be mistaken for that geometry. This section is deliberately redundant with parts of 4.I: the redundancy is a firewall against later re-identification of representation multiplicity with base-space dimension.

4.III.1 Local reconstruction of the folded state. On a regular quadrant choose the local generator z=srx(x). The reciprocal primitive sxp is 1/z, while the complementary pair cxp and crx and every certified Book 0 scalar are reconstructible from the same phase chart together with the certified discrete sector data. Hence the smooth folded state carries one local continuous parameter even when several readouts are displayed simultaneously.

Theorem 4.III.1 — Finite enrichment theorem. Let S be a one-dimensional smooth state carrier and D a finite set of branch, chart, sign, cophase, mirror, or lift labels. Then S×D is locally one-dimensional. Every point has a neighborhood contained in one discrete sheet and diffeomorphic to a neighborhood in S.

Corollary. FlatWave sign, quadrant label, projective lift sign, and other finite labels may distinguish globally meaningful states without adding a local differential direction.

Theorem 4.III.2 — Cover preservation of dimension. If π:S~→S is a smooth covering map, then π is locally a diffeomorphism. Therefore dim S~=dim S. A twofold or fourfold cover can repair periodic identification, preserve orientation data, or expose monodromy; it cannot serve as the second coordinate of a plane.

Theorem 4.III.3 — Projective-lift separation. The homogeneous two-component representation X=(u,v)ᵀ and its projective class [X]∈RP¹ separate representation dimension from state dimension. On an affine chart z=v/u, the ray is determined by one real coordinate. The central identification X~ρX removes scale rather than generating a new spatial degree of freedom. Consequently the two components of the lift are not a two-dimensional geometric base.

Cophase audit. If x_A=g_A(x) is any finite family of cophase-, complement-, reflection-, or half-turn-related phase representatives, then dx_A=g′_A(x)dx and dx_A∧dx_B=0 for every A,B. Symmetry-related copies of one phase are multiple representatives of one state relation, not independent coordinates.

Quotient audit. Passing from the lifted 2π carrier to a π-periodic primitive quotient can identify states and change global topology, but it does not manufacture missing tangent directions. Conversely, restoring a lost finite sign or sheet label restores discrete information only.

Algebra audit. A matrix, Clifford, complex, or spin representation may act on a vector space of dimension greater than one even when the represented state depends on one scalar. Three dimensions must therefore remain distinct throughout Book 4: the dimension of the base carrier, the dimension of the state manifold, and the dimension of the algebra or representation space acting on the state.

State/base separation theorem. Let B be any independent geometric carrier and φ:B→S the Projection phase field. The dimension of B is not determined by dim S. For every Book 0–3 scalar F,

d(F∘φ)=F′(φ)dφ,

so the scalar readout family spans at most the line generated by dφ even when B itself has higher rank. The base supplies the directions along which the scalar varies; the scalar does not supply the base.

Negative conclusions frozen. The following implications are invalid:

• two folded channels ⇒ two spatial axes;

• four primitive operators ⇒ four spatial axes;

• RP¹ completion ⇒ an added spatial line;

• homogeneous R² representation ⇒ a two-dimensional base;

• q and −q in a covering fiber ⇒ mirror spatial directions;

• cophase multiplicity ⇒ independent coordinates;

• finite sheet count ⇒ manifold dimension.

Positive conclusion. Books 0–3 can supply scalar coefficient fields, discrete orientation records, projective state organization, and symmetry data to a later geometry. They cannot supply the missing local rank. The geometric extension must therefore be genuinely independent of the Projection phase while remaining mathematically compatible with it.

Certified handoff to 4.IV. P4.1 is now allowed to do exactly one new kind of work: provide independent constructive geometry. Section 4.IV must not begin with E², a dot product, or Cartesian axes. It must begin with the weaker equal-circle/congruence substrate and show that repeated construction produces a continuous planar carrier, that sixfold closure supplies an angular grammar, and that a perpendicular can be constructed synthetically. Only after those results may a Euclidean metric be introduced.

4.IV — THE FLOWER PLANE: EQUAL CIRCLES, RIGHT ANGLE, AND METRIC WITNESS

This section re-presents the proofs of the historical Flower Plane books inside the revised reference corpus. The controlling source is Mathematic existentialism, “The Elements of the Flower Plane,” Books I–II. Flower Space confirms that these planar results precede any solid-geometric extension, and Flower Power confirms that the resulting geometry is not yet mechanics or physics. The proofs below are therefore included, not merely cited.

The revised dependency discipline separates the source material into three layers:

Local synthetic layer: straight lines, equal circles, copied length, congruence, intersection, continuous triangular filling, and the Flower operation.

Metric-completion layer: introduced only after the synthetic construction of a right angle.

Global Euclidean layer: uniqueness of parallels and defect-free global continuation, activated only where Book II requires it.

4.IV.A — DEFINITIONS AND LOCAL POSTULATES

Definition 4.IV.A1 — Point. A point marks a position and has no magnitude.

Definition 4.IV.A2 — Line. A line is a continuous breadthless extension through points.

Definition 4.IV.A3 — Straight line. A straight line lies evenly with the points upon itself.

Definition 4.IV.A4 — Circle. A circle is a plane figure whose boundary points are joined to one center by equal straight segments. The common segment is the radius.

Definition 4.IV.A5 — Equal circles. Equal circles have equal radii.

Definition 4.IV.A6 — Flower operation. If A and B are neighboring centers with AB=r, draw equal circles of radius r centered at A and B. Any intersection C then satisfies AC=BC=r. A native Flower triangle is a triangle whose three vertices are mutually neighboring centers produced by this operation.

Definition 4.IV.A7 — Native directions. At an interior Flower center O, the rays from O through its six nearest neighboring centers determine six native directions n₀,…,n₅ in cyclic order.

Definition 4.IV.A8 — Complete turn. One full revolution about a point is a complete turn.

Definition 4.IV.A9 — Right angle. When a straight line standing upon another makes adjacent equal angles, each adjacent angle is a right angle and the standing line is perpendicular to the other.

Local Postulate 1. A straight line may be drawn from any point to any other point.

Local Postulate 2. A finite straight line may be extended continuously in a straight line.

Local Postulate 3. A circle may be drawn with any center and any copied positive radius.

Local Postulate 4. Equal-circle Flower operations are congruent.

Local carrier declaration P4.2. The repeated triangular Flower complex is continuously filled so that each interior center has a neighborhood formed by six primitive triangular cells meeting without gap or overlap. No global parallel postulate is used in the local proofs below.

COMMON NOTIONS

1. Things equal to the same thing are equal to one another.

2. If equals are added to equals, the wholes are equal.

3. If equals are subtracted from equals, the remainders are equal.

4. Things that coincide are equal.

5. The whole is greater than the part.

6. Equal rigid rotations preserve equality of length and angle.

7. Opposite equal contributions cancel.

CONGRUENCE PRINCIPLE A — SIDE–ANGLE–SIDE

If two triangles have two corresponding sides equal and the included angles equal, then the triangles are congruent in all corresponding parts.

Proof. Place one equal side upon the other. Equality of the included angles places the second equal sides upon the same rays. Equality of their lengths then places the remaining vertices at the same point. The triangles coincide. ∎

CONGRUENCE PRINCIPLE B — SIDE–SIDE–SIDE

If two triangles have three corresponding sides equal, then the triangles are congruent in all corresponding parts, directly or by reflection.

Proof. Place one side upon its equal. The third vertex must lie at a common intersection of two circles centered at the endpoints of that side, with radii equal to the remaining two side lengths. The two possible intersections are the direct and reflected placements. In either placement corresponding angles are equal. ∎

PRELIMINARY LEMMA 4.IV.L1 — THE SIX NATIVE SECTORS ABOUT AN INTERIOR FLOWER CENTER ARE EQUAL AND TOGETHER COMPLETE ONE TURN

Let O be an interior center and let A₀,…,A₅ be its six nearest neighboring centers in cyclic order.

By the Flower operation,

OA₀=OA₁=OA₂=OA₃=OA₄=OA₅=r.

Each adjacent pair of neighboring centers is also separated by r. Therefore every triangle OAₖAₖ₊₁ is equilateral, with the index read modulo six.

The six triangles have three corresponding sides equal, so by Congruence Principle B they are congruent. Their six angles at O are therefore equal.

By the local carrier declaration, these six filled triangles surround O without gap or overlap. Their six equal central angles therefore compose one complete turn.

Hence the six native sectors are equal and together complete one turn. Rays separated by three native sectors are opposite and form a straight line. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.P0 — TO CONSTRUCT AND PROVE A RIGHT ANGLE FROM THE NATIVE FLOWER DIRECTIONS

Let O be an interior Flower center. Let OA be the native ray chosen as north, and let OB and OC be the next two native rays clockwise from OA. Let B and C be the neighboring centers upon those rays.

Then O,B,C are mutually neighboring centers, so

OB=OC=BC=r.

With centers B and C and radius r, draw equal circles. They meet at O and at a second point D. Join O to D.

Because

OB=OC,

BD=CD,

and OD is common, triangles BOD and COD have three corresponding sides equal. By Congruence Principle B,

∠BOD=∠DOC.

Thus OD bisects the native sector ∠BOC.

The angle ∠AOB is one native sector. The angle ∠BOD is one-half of a native sector. Therefore ∠AOD contains one and one-half native sectors.

Extend OA through O to the opposite native ray OE. By Lemma 4.IV.L1, the straight angle ∠AOE contains three native sectors. Hence ∠DOE also contains one and one-half native sectors.

Therefore

∠AOD=∠DOE.

These are adjacent equal angles standing upon straight line AE. By the definition of a right angle, each is a right angle and OD is perpendicular to AE.

Therefore a right angle has been constructed from the native Flower directions. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IV.P0.1 — DEGREE MEASURE

A complete turn is assigned 360° by the definition of degree measure. Four right angles complete one turn, so each right angle measures

360°/4=90°.

Six equal native sectors complete one turn, so each native sector measures

360°/6=60°.

The bisected half-sector therefore measures

60°/2=30°.

These numbers record the already-proved turn fractions; they are not premises of Proposition 4.IV.P0. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IV.P0.2 — EAST FROM NORTH

If OA is called due north, the ray OD constructed above stands one right angle clockwise from OA and may therefore be called due east. Analytically it is the equal tack between the two native rays bounding it, but the synthetic construction precedes that analytic description. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.P1 — TO CONSTRUCT AN EQUILATERAL TRIANGLE UPON A GIVEN FINITE STRAIGHT LINE

Let AB be the given finite straight line. With center A and radius AB, draw a circle. With center B and radius BA, draw another. Let the circles meet at C.

Because C lies on the first circle,

AC=AB.

Because C lies on the second,

BC=BA.

Therefore

AC=BC=AB,

so triangle ABC is equilateral. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.P2 — TO BISECT A GIVEN RECTILINEAL ANGLE

Let ∠BAC be the given angle. With center A, draw a circle meeting AB at D and AC at E. Then AD=AE.

With centers D and E and any common radius greater than half of DE, draw equal circles intersecting at F within the angle. Join A to F.

Then AD=AE, DF=EF, and AF is common. Triangles ADF and AEF therefore have three corresponding sides equal. By Congruence Principle B,

∠DAF=∠FAE.

Thus AF bisects ∠BAC. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.P3 — TO BISECT A GIVEN FINITE STRAIGHT LINE

Let AB be the given finite straight line. Construct equilateral triangle ABC upon AB by Proposition 4.IV.P1. Bisect ∠ACB by Proposition 4.IV.P2 and let the bisector meet AB at M.

Then AC=BC, CM is common, and ∠ACM=∠MCB. By Congruence Principle A, triangles ACM and BCM are congruent. Hence

AM=MB.

Thus AB is bisected at M. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.P4 — TO ERECT A PERPENDICULAR AT A GIVEN POINT UPON A STRAIGHT LINE

Let P lie on straight line AB. With center P, draw a circle meeting the line at C and D on opposite sides of P. Then PC=PD.

With centers C and D and a common radius greater than PC, draw equal circles meeting at E away from the line. Join E to P.

Then EC=ED, PC=PD, and EP is common. By Congruence Principle B, triangles EPC and EPD are congruent, so

∠CPE=∠EPD.

But CPD is a straight line. Hence the adjacent equal angles CPE and EPD are right angles. Therefore EP is perpendicular to AB at P. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.P5 — FROM A GIVEN POINT OUTSIDE A STRAIGHT LINE, TO DRAW A PERPENDICULAR TO THE LINE

Let P be outside straight line AB. With center P, draw a circle large enough to meet AB at C and D. Bisect CD at M by Proposition 4.IV.P3. Join P to M.

Because C and D lie on the same circle centered at P,

PC=PD.

Also CM=MD, and PM is common. By Congruence Principle B, triangles PMC and PMD are congruent, so

∠PMC=∠PMD.

But CMD is a straight line. Hence the adjacent equal angles are right angles, and PM is perpendicular to AB. ∎

LOCAL BOOK I CLOSURE

The local synthetic order is therefore

equal circles → equilateral triangle → sixfold closure → native-sector bisection → right angle → angle bisection → segment bisection → perpendicular constructions.

No Cartesian axes, vector components, dot products, trigonometric functions, algebraic distance formulas, or parallel postulate have been used.

4.IV.B — REVISED METRIC COMPLETION AFTER THE RIGHT ANGLE

This subsection is the revised consolidation step. The source Book I explicitly reserves analytic Flower coordinates and their metric for later; the present reference corpus now states the minimal analytic completion at the point where the synthetic construction has earned the required data.

THEOREM 4.IV.M1 — THE CONSTRUCTED UNIT AND RIGHT ANGLE FIX A LOCAL ORTHOGONAL METRIC

Let e₁ and e₂ be two constructed perpendicular directions at a point O, each normalized by the copied Flower length r. There is a unique symmetric bilinear form g on their span satisfying

g(e₁,e₁)=r²,

g(e₂,e₂)=r²,

g(e₁,e₂)=0.

In the ordered basis (e₁,e₂),

[g]=r²[[1,0],[0,1]].

This matrix is positive definite. The metric is therefore introduced after the construction of equal length and perpendicularity; it is not used to prove them. ∎

THEOREM 4.IV.M2 — NATIVE FLOWER METRIC

Let a and b be adjacent native Flower directions of copied length r. Their endpoints together with O form an equilateral Flower triangle, so

|a|²=r²,

|b|²=r²,

|b−a|²=r².

Expanding the third equality with the bilinear form established in Theorem 4.IV.M1 gives

r²=r²+r²−2g(a,b).

Hence

g(a,b)=r²/2.

Therefore the Gram matrix in the native basis (a,b) is

G₂=r²[[1,1/2],[1/2,1]],

det G₂=3r⁴/4>0.

For a displacement P=ua+vb,

|P|²=r²(u²+uv+v²).

For P₁=(u₁,v₁) and P₂=(u₂,v₂),

d²=r²[(u₂−u₁)²+(u₂−u₁)(v₂−v₁)+(v₂−v₁)²].

Thus the familiar Flower metric follows from the constructed unit, the constructed right angle, and the equilateral Flower edge relation. No cosine formula is required as a premise. ∎

4.IV.C — GLOBAL EUCLIDEAN COMPLETION AND BOOK II PROOFS

Global Postulate P4.3. The Flower Plane extends without defect, curvature, boundary, or twist; through a point not upon a given straight line there is exactly one straight line parallel to the given line.

This is the source Book II global Euclidean condition. It is activated only in the propositions that require it.

Definition — Transversal. A transversal crosses two or more straight lines at distinct points.

Definition — Alternate interior angles. Alternate interior angles lie between two crossed lines on opposite sides of their transversal.

Definition — Parallelogram. A parallelogram is a quadrilateral whose opposite sides are parallel.

Definition — Rectangle. A rectangle has four right angles.

Definition — Square. A square has four equal sides and four right angles.

CONGRUENCE PRINCIPLE C — ANGLE–SIDE–ANGLE

If two triangles have two corresponding angles equal and the included sides equal, then the triangles are congruent in all corresponding parts.

Proof. Place the equal included sides upon one another. Equality of the adjacent angles places the remaining sides on the same rays. Those rays meet at one point, so the triangles coincide. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q1 — TO CONSTRUCT AT A GIVEN POINT AN ANGLE EQUAL TO A GIVEN RECTILINEAL ANGLE

Let ∠BAC be the given angle. Let D be the target point and DE a ray from D. With center A draw a circle meeting AB at F and AC at G. With center D and the same radius draw a circle meeting DE at H. With center H and radius FG draw a circle meeting the circle centered at D at K. Join D to K.

Then AF=DH, AG=DK, and FG=HK. Hence triangles AFG and DHK are congruent by Congruence Principle B, so

∠BAC=∠HDK.

Thus an equal angle has been constructed at D. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q2 — AN EXTERIOR ANGLE OF A TRIANGLE IS GREATER THAN EITHER OPPOSITE INTERIOR ANGLE

Let ABC be a triangle and extend BC to D. Bisect AC at E by Proposition 4.IV.P3. Join B to E and extend BE to F so that BE=EF. Join F to C.

Then AE=EC, BE=EF, and vertical angles ∠AEB and ∠CEF are equal. By Congruence Principle A, triangles AEB and CEF are congruent, so

∠BAE=∠ECF.

But ∠ECF is a proper part of exterior angle ∠ACD. Hence

∠ACD>∠ECF=∠BAC.

The analogous construction on the other side gives

∠ACD>∠ABC.

Therefore an exterior angle is greater than either opposite interior angle. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q3 — IF A TRANSVERSAL MAKES ALTERNATE INTERIOR ANGLES EQUAL, THE TWO STRAIGHT LINES ARE PARALLEL

Let transversal EF meet AB and CD and suppose

∠AEF=∠EFD.

If AB and CD met on either side of EF, one of these equal angles would be an exterior angle of the resulting triangle and the other an opposite interior angle. Proposition 4.IV.Q2 says the exterior angle must be greater, contradicting equality.

Therefore AB and CD do not meet in either direction. Hence AB∥CD. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q4 — THROUGH A GIVEN POINT OUTSIDE A STRAIGHT LINE, TO DRAW A PARALLEL TO THE GIVEN LINE

Let AB be the given straight line and P an exterior point. Choose C on AB and join C to P. At P construct, by Proposition 4.IV.Q1, an angle equal to the alternate interior angle made by AB and CP. Let the new ray be PD.

The alternate interior angles made by transversal CP with AB and PD are equal. By Proposition 4.IV.Q3,

PD∥AB.

Thus a parallel has been constructed through P. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q5 — UNIQUENESS OF THE PARALLEL

Through a point outside a given straight line there is only one parallel to that line.

This is the uniqueness clause of Global Postulate P4.3. It is not proved from the local Flower construction. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q6 — IF A TRANSVERSAL CROSSES TWO PARALLEL STRAIGHT LINES, THE ALTERNATE INTERIOR ANGLES ARE EQUAL

Let AB∥CD and let EF cross them. Suppose the alternate interior angles are unequal. At one intersection construct, by Proposition 4.IV.Q1, a new line whose alternate interior angle equals the angle at the other intersection. By Proposition 4.IV.Q3 the new line is parallel to the other original line.

There would then be two distinct parallels through one point to the same line, contradicting Proposition 4.IV.Q5. Hence the alternate interior angles are equal. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IV.Q6.1 — Corresponding angles made by a transversal of parallel lines are equal, because each is vertically opposite to an alternate interior angle equal to the other corresponding angle. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IV.Q6.2 — Interior angles on the same side of a transversal of parallel lines together equal two right angles, because each such pair is supplementary on a straight line to an equal alternate interior angle. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q7 — THE THREE INTERIOR ANGLES OF A TRIANGLE TOGETHER EQUAL TWO RIGHT ANGLES

Let ABC be a triangle. Through A draw DE parallel to BC by Proposition 4.IV.Q4.

Because AB is a transversal of DE and BC,

∠DAB=∠ABC.

Because AC is a transversal,

∠CAE=∠BCA.

The adjacent angles ∠DAB, ∠BAC, and ∠CAE stand on straight line DE and together equal two right angles. Substituting the equal alternate angles gives

∠ABC+∠BAC+∠BCA=two right angles.

Therefore the angles of a triangle sum to two right angles. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q8 — OPPOSITE SIDES AND OPPOSITE ANGLES OF A PARALLELOGRAM ARE EQUAL, AND A DIAGONAL BISECTS IT INTO TWO CONGRUENT TRIANGLES

Let ABCD be a parallelogram, so AB∥CD and AD∥BC. Join A to C.

Because AC crosses AB and CD,

∠BAC=∠DCA.

Because AC crosses AD and BC,

∠BCA=∠DAC.

Also AC is common. By Congruence Principle C, triangles ABC and CDA are congruent. Hence

AB=CD,

BC=AD,

∠ABC=∠CDA,

∠BAD=∠BCD.

The diagonal AC therefore bisects the parallelogram into two congruent triangles. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q9 — TO CONSTRUCT A PARALLELOGRAM UPON TWO GIVEN ADJOINING SIDES AND THEIR INCLUDED ANGLE

Let AB and AD be the given adjoining sides. Through B draw a line parallel to AD by Proposition 4.IV.Q4. Through D draw a line parallel to AB. Let the two lines meet at C.

Then AB∥DC and AD∥BC, so ABCD is a parallelogram. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.IV.Q10 — TO CONSTRUCT A SQUARE UPON A GIVEN FINITE STRAIGHT LINE

Let AB be the given finite straight line. At A erect AD perpendicular to AB by Proposition 4.IV.P4 and choose D so that

AD=AB.

Through B draw BC parallel to AD by Proposition 4.IV.Q4. Through D draw DC parallel to AB. Let BC and DC meet at C.

Then ABCD is a parallelogram. By Proposition 4.IV.Q8,

AB=DC,

AD=BC.

But AD=AB. Therefore

AB=BC=CD=DA.

Since AD is perpendicular to AB, ∠DAB is a right angle. By the angle relations of parallel lines, the remaining three angles are equal to ∠DAB and are also right angles.

Thus ABCD has four equal sides and four right angles. Therefore ABCD is a square. ∎

STATUS OF THE CONSOLIDATED FLOWER PLANE PROOFS

The reference corpus now contains the entire dependency-bearing proof chain needed for the planar construction rather than merely pointing to the historical source. The local Flower proofs establish equilateral cells, sixfold closure, a right angle, bisections, and general perpendicular constructions without the parallel postulate. The revised metric completion is introduced only after equal length and perpendicularity have been constructed. The global Euclidean theory then enters explicitly through P4.3, from which the source Book II proofs establish parallel-angle relations, the triangle angle sum, parallelograms, and the square.

The resulting logical chain is

copied length + equal circles + continuous sixfold Flower closure

→ native planar carrier
