→ synthetic right angle/perpendicular

→ local orthogonal metric completion

→ native Flower metric

→ explicit global parallel/flatness condition

→ global Euclidean plane propositions and square.

Nothing in this section converts the geometry into physical space. Flower Power is retained as the source-level firewall for that later transition.

4.V — INDEPENDENT RANK-THREE EXTENSION AND TETRAHEDRAL WITNESS

This section preserves the solid-geometric proof chain of Flower Space, Book III, as a supplementary realization of an independently admitted rank-three Euclidean extension. The source expressly states that plane geometry alone does not prove a third dimension. Accordingly, the passage from F² to F³ is not presented as a consequence of the planar Flower alone, and no downstream theorem may use the Flower solid construction as the source of dimensional rank. The new solid-geometric premises are exposed before the proofs that use them.

Flower Power is not used as a geometric premise here. Its role remains the later boundary statement that geometry does not itself imply time, mass, motion, force, or mechanics.

4.V.A — SOLID DEFINITIONS AND EXPLICIT IMPORTS

Definition 4.V.A1 — Coplanar points. Points are coplanar when one plane contains them all.

Definition 4.V.A2 — Plane. For the solid construction, a plane is a flat two-dimensional surface extending without boundary. This is a solid-geometric primitive in the source, not a theorem derived from the one-generator Projection calculus.

Definition 4.V.A3 — Sphere. A sphere is the surface of all points at one fixed distance from one center.

Definition 4.V.A4 — Equal spheres. Equal spheres have equal radii.

Definition 4.V.A5 — Tetrahedron. A tetrahedron is a solid bounded by four triangular faces. A regular tetrahedron has six equal edges.

Definition 4.V.A6 — Octahedron. An octahedron is a solid bounded by eight triangular faces. A regular octahedron has twelve equal edges and eight equilateral triangular faces.

Definition 4.V.A7 — Face reflection. A face reflection carries each point to the point at equal perpendicular distance on the opposite side of the face plane.

Definition 4.V.A8 — Solid Flower cell. A solid Flower cell is a regular tetrahedron or regular octahedron whose edges have the common Flower length r.

SOLID POSTULATE S1 — NONCOPLANAR EXTENSION

There exist points that do not lie in one plane.

SOLID POSTULATE S2 — DETERMINATION OF A PLANE

Through any three noncollinear points there passes exactly one plane.

SOLID POSTULATE S3 — PERPENDICULAR TO A PLANE

Through any point there passes exactly one straight line perpendicular to a given plane.

SOLID POSTULATE S4 — CONSTRUCTION OF A SPHERE

A sphere may be drawn with any center and any positive copied radius.

SOLID POSTULATE S5 — EQUAL-SPHERE FLOWER INTERSECTION

Let A,B,C be the vertices of an equilateral triangle of side r. Equal spheres of radius r centered at A,B,C have two common points, one on each side of plane ABC.

SOLID POSTULATE S6 — RIGID SOLID CONGRUENCE

Solid figures that coincide under a rigid displacement or reflection are equal in all corresponding lengths, angles, faces, and enclosed regions.

Dependency note. S1–S6 are the explicit solid-geometric imports identified by Flower Space. In particular, the existence of the noncoplanar direction and of spheres is not credited to the Flower Plane or to Projection phase. Coordinates and scalar products remain unavailable until after the synthetic solid construction below.

PROPOSITION 4.V.P0 — TO CONSTRUCT A REGULAR TETRAHEDRON UPON A GIVEN EQUILATERAL TRIANGLE

Let ABC be an equilateral Flower triangle with

AB=BC=CA=r.

Draw equal spheres of radius r centered at A,B,C. By Solid Postulate S5, let D be one common intersection.

Because D lies on the sphere centered at A,

DA=r.

Because D lies on the sphere centered at B,

DB=r.

Because D lies on the sphere centered at C,

DC=r.

The three base edges already satisfy

AB=BC=CA=r.

Therefore all six edges of tetrahedron ABCD are equal. Hence ABCD is a regular tetrahedron. ∎

COROLLARY 4.V.P0.1 — EVERY FACE OF A REGULAR TETRAHEDRON IS EQUILATERAL

Each face is bounded by three of the six equal edges. Therefore every face is equilateral. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.V.P1 — THE TWO EQUAL-SPHERE INTERSECTIONS PRODUCE CONGRUENT TETRAHEDRA ON OPPOSITE SIDES OF THE BASE

Let D and E be the two common intersections of the spheres centered at A,B,C. Then

DA=DB=DC=r

and

EA=EB=EC=r.

Both tetrahedra ABCD and ABCE therefore have the same six edge lengths. By Solid Postulate S6 they are congruent, and face reflection in plane ABC carries D to E. Thus they lie on opposite sides of their common face ABC. ∎

LEMMA 4.V.L1 — POINTS EQUIDISTANT FROM THE VERTICES OF AN EQUILATERAL TRIANGLE LIE ON ONE LINE PERPENDICULAR TO ITS PLANE

Let ABC be equilateral and let G be its common planar center, obtained by intersecting two perpendicular bisectors in plane ABC.

A point equidistant from A and B lies in the perpendicular-bisector plane of AB. A point equidistant from B and C lies in the perpendicular-bisector plane of BC. These two planes meet in one straight line through G.

By Solid Postulate S3 and the source solid-congruence construction, their line of intersection is perpendicular to plane ABC. Therefore every point equidistant from A,B,C lies on the unique perpendicular through G to plane ABC. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.V.P2 — THE SEGMENT JOINING THE TWO EQUAL-SPHERE INTERSECTIONS IS PERPENDICULAR TO THE BASE AND IS BISECTED BY THE BASE PLANE

The intersections D and E are each equidistant from A,B,C. By Lemma 4.V.L1, both lie on the perpendicular through G to plane ABC. Therefore D,G,E are collinear and DE is perpendicular to plane ABC.

Reflection in plane ABC carries D to E and fixes G. Hence

DG=GE.

Therefore plane ABC perpendicularly bisects DE. ∎

RANK-THREE WITNESS AFTER INDEPENDENT NONCOPLANAR ADMISSION

The source construction has now used S1 to leave the plane and S5 to produce a noncoplanar common sphere intersection. A filled tetrahedron ABCD is a 3-cell. Its interior therefore supplies local rank three. This is the point at which the abstract coframe criterion of 4.II can be realized: on any smooth local chart of the continuous solid realization, three coordinate one-forms η¹,η²,η³ satisfy

η¹∧η²∧η³≠0.

The rank belongs to the solid carrier admitted and constructed here, not to the Projection scalar.

4.V.B — ANALYTIC DECLARATION AFTER THE SYNTHETIC TETRAHEDRON

Choose one vertex O of a regular tetrahedron OABC and let

a=OA,

b=OB,

c=OC.

Each basis segment has length r. Every pair forms an equilateral face with O, so each pair satisfies the same native Flower edge relation already metrized in 4.IV.

Therefore

a·a=b·b=c·c=r²,

a·b=a·c=b·c=r²/2.

The tetrahedral Flower Gram matrix is

G₃=r²[[1,1/2,1/2],[1/2,1,1/2],[1/2,1/2,1]].

For

R=q¹a+q²b+q³c,

the squared length is

R²=r²[(q¹)²+(q²)²+(q³)²+q¹q²+q¹q³+q²q³].

THEOREM 4.V.M1 — THE TETRAHEDRAL FLOWER METRIC IS NONDEGENERATE

The dimensionless Gram matrix has eigenvalues

2, 1/2, 1/2.

Hence it is positive definite and

det G₃=r⁶/2>0.

Its inverse is

G₃⁻¹=(1/r²)[[3/2,−1/2,−1/2],[−1/2,3/2,−1/2],[−1/2,−1/2,3/2]].

Thus the three tetrahedral directions are linearly independent and admit a reciprocal basis. Wolfram exact symbolic evaluation independently confirms the determinant, spectrum, principal minors, and inverse. The computation is a certification of the analytic structure after the synthetic solid construction; it is not a premise of Propositions 4.V.P0–P2. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.V.P3 — THE TETRAHEDRAL FLOWER METRIC VERIFIES THE REGULAR TETRAHEDRON

The primitive tetrahedron has vertices

O=0,

A=a,

B=b,

C=c.

The three edges from O have length r by declaration. For edge AB,

AB=b−a.

Therefore

AB²=(b−a)·(b−a)

=r²+r²−2(r²/2)

=r².

Hence AB=r. The same calculation gives

AC=BC=r.

Thus all six edges equal r, analytically verifying the preceding synthetic construction. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.V.P4 — THE ALTITUDE OF A REGULAR TETRAHEDRON OF EDGE r IS r√(2/3)

Let G be the center of the face ABC opposite O. In tetrahedral coordinates,

G=(a+b+c)/3.

By Lemma 4.V.L1, OG is perpendicular to plane ABC. Using the tetrahedral Flower metric,

OG²

=((a+b+c)/3)·((a+b+c)/3)

=(1/9)[3r²+6(r²/2)]

=2r²/3.

Therefore

OG=r√(2/3).

Hence the altitude of a regular tetrahedron of edge r is r√(2/3). ∎

COROLLARY 4.V.P4.1 — THE TWO COMMON SPHERE INTERSECTIONS ARE SEPARATED BY 2r√(2/3)

By Proposition 4.V.P2, the base plane bisects the segment joining the intersections. Each half is one tetrahedral altitude. Therefore

DE=2r√(2/3). ∎

COROLLARY 4.V.P4.2 — TETRAHEDRAL VOLUME

The base ABC is an equilateral triangle of side r and area √3 r²/4. Multiplying by one-third of the altitude gives

V_tet=(1/3)(√3 r²/4)(r√(2/3))

=√2 r³/12

=r³/(6√2).

This analytic corollary is consistent with |det B|=r³/√2 for any matrix B satisfying BᵀB=G₃. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.V.P5 — SIX SPECIFIED FLOWER-LATTICE POINTS FORM A REGULAR OCTAHEDRON

Consider

A=a,

B=b,

C=c,

D=a+b,

E=a+c,

F=b+c.

The three opposite pairs are

A and F,

B and E,

C and D.

Every nonopposite pair differs by one of

±a, ±b, ±c, ±(a−b), ±(a−c), ±(b−c).

The vectors ±a,±b,±c have length r. Also, for example,

|a−b|²=r²+r²−2(r²/2)=r²,

and likewise for a−c and b−c. Hence all twelve nonopposite connections have length r. The eight triangular faces bounded by these edges are equilateral.

Therefore ABCDEF is a regular octahedron of edge r. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.V.P6 — A REGULAR TETRAHEDRON AND A REGULAR OCTAHEDRON MEET FACE TO FACE

The points A,B,C form an equilateral triangle of edge r. Tetrahedron OABC has ABC as one face. The regular octahedron with vertices

A,B,C,a+b,a+c,b+c

also has ABC as one face.

The tetrahedral vertex O lies on one side of plane ABC. The remaining octahedral vertices lie on the other side in the source solid realization. Therefore the regular tetrahedron and regular octahedron meet exactly in the common face ABC. ∎

PROPOSITION 4.V.P7 — A PRIMITIVE FLOWER RHOMBOHEDRON DECOMPOSES INTO TWO REGULAR TETRAHEDRA AND ONE REGULAR OCTAHEDRON

Consider all points

R=q¹a+q²b+q³c

with

0≤q¹≤1,

0≤q²≤1,

0≤q³≤1.

These points form the primitive rhombohedron generated by a,b,c.

The subregion

q¹+q²+q³≤1

is the tetrahedron with vertices

0,a,b,c.

The subregion

q¹+q²+q³≥2

is the oppositely oriented tetrahedron with vertices

a+b+c, a+b, a+c, b+c.

The remaining region

1≤q¹+q²+q³≤2

has vertices

a,b,c,a+b,a+c,b+c,

which are the regular octahedron of Proposition 4.V.P5.

These three regions cover the rhombohedron and overlap only on their boundary faces. Therefore one primitive Flower rhombohedron decomposes into two regular tetrahedra and one regular octahedron. ∎

SUPPLEMENTARY GLOBAL SOLID COMPLETION P4.4

To pass from the local tetrahedral construction to the completed Flower Space of the source, declare that the tetrahedral coordinate continuation is continuous, flat, defect-free, boundaryless, and without topological twist, and that translations by integer combinations of a,b,c extend the primitive rhombohedral cell compatibly. This global completion is not derived from one tetrahedron.

PROPOSITION 4.V.P8 — HISTORICAL FLOWER-SPACE COMPLETION UNDER P4.4

Translate the primitive rhombohedron by every lattice vector

i a+j b+k c,

where i,j,k are integers.

Under Global Solid Completion P4.4, the translated rhombohedra fill the tetrahedral coordinate space without gaps and meet only on boundary faces.

By Proposition 4.V.P7, each rhombohedron is partitioned into two regular tetrahedra and one regular octahedron. The translated partitions agree on every shared rhombohedral face.

Therefore the translated tetrahedra and octahedra fill the completed space without gaps or interior overlaps. This tetrahedral–octahedral completion is Flower Space F³. ∎

COROLLARY 4.V.P8.1 — THE FLOWER PLANE OCCURS AS A PLANAR SECTION OF FLOWER SPACE

Set q³=0. Then

R=q¹a+q²b,

and the tetrahedral metric reduces to

R²=r²[(q¹)²+(q²)²+q¹q²],

which is precisely the triangular metric of the Flower Plane. Therefore F² is recovered as the native planar section q³=0 of F³. ∎

STATUS OF THE SUPPLEMENTARY FLOWER SPACE RE-PRESENTATION

Flower Space supplies a disciplined historical realization of an already independent rank-three extension rather than a derivation of E³ from the Flower Plane. The source itself requires noncoplanar points, planes, perpendiculars to planes, spheres, equal-sphere intersection, and rigid solid congruence as new solid-geometric imports. With those imports exposed, the supplementary proof chain is

equilateral Flower face

→ equal spheres

→ regular tetrahedron

→ reflected tetrahedron

→ perpendicular tetrahedral axis

→ tetrahedral metric

→ regular octahedron

→ rhombohedral decomposition

→ explicit global solid completion

→ tetrahedral–octahedral Flower Space F³.

The analytic Gram matrix certifies the constructed equal-edge geometry and supplies coordinates; it does not replace the synthetic construction. The global gap-free F³ statement remains conditional on P4.4, exactly as the planar global Euclidean statements remain conditional on P4.3.

This completes the supplementary re-presentation of the Flower Space solid proofs. Their value is constructive and diagnostic; the canonical downstream rank-three carrier is the independently admitted Euclidean carrier.

4.VI — SUPPLEMENTARY COORDINATE WITNESSES, RECIPROCAL BASES, AND TETRAHEDRAL CENTER LATTICE

SUPPLEMENTARY STATUS

Sections 4.IV–4.V established the planar metric witness and an independently admitted rank-three extension before introducing supplementary coordinates. The present section now proves the coordinate statements that were previously left as optional realizations. Its purpose is analytic certification and bookkeeping, not the creation of geometric rank.

Source provenance. The planar coordinate form OP=ua+vb and the tetrahedral coordinate form R=q¹a+q²b+q³c occur in The Elements of the Flower Plane and Flower Space after their respective synthetic constructions. The 12-neighbor lattice shell and its face-centered-cubic realization are retained from the prior T-series lattice analysis as supplementary discrete witnesses. They are consequences of a separately declared three-generator lattice realization, not consequences of the one-generator Projection phase and not premises of the canonical rank-three carrier.

4.VI.A — PLANAR FLOWER COORDINATES

Let a and b be adjacent native Flower basis vectors in F². By 4.IV,

g(a,a)=g(b,b)=r²,

g(a,b)=r²/2.

Thus

G₂=r²[[1,1/2],[1/2,1]],

det G₂=3r⁴/4>0.

THEOREM 4.VI.P1 — THE PLANAR FLOWER BASIS IS LINEARLY INDEPENDENT

Suppose

ua+vb=0.

Taking the squared norm gives

0=r²(u²+uv+v²).

But

u²+uv+v²=(u+v/2)²+3v²/4,

which vanishes only when u=v=0. Therefore a and b are linearly independent. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VI.P1.1 — UNIQUE PLANAR COORDINATES

Every vector in span{a,b} has a unique representation

P=ua+vb.

Once the continuous Flower Plane is identified with this rank-two affine span, every displacement has unique Flower coordinates (u,v). ∎

THEOREM 4.VI.P2 — THE PLANAR RECIPROCAL BASIS

Define reciprocal vectors a* and b* by

g(a*,a)=1,

g(a*,b)=0,

g(b*,a)=0,

g(b*,b)=1.

Since

G₂⁻¹=(1/r²)[[4/3,−2/3],[−2/3,4/3]],

the reciprocal basis is

a*=(1/r²)(4a/3−2b/3),

b*=(1/r²)(−2a/3+4b/3).

Direct substitution verifies the four defining pairings. Therefore for any planar displacement P,

u=g(a*,P),

v=g(b*,P).

Thus the Flower coordinates are recovered intrinsically from the metric and the native basis; no Cartesian embedding is required. ∎

4.VI.B — TETRAHEDRAL FLOWER COORDINATES

Let a,b,c be the three equal edge vectors from one vertex of the regular tetrahedron constructed in 4.V. Then

G₃=r²[[1,1/2,1/2],[1/2,1,1/2],[1/2,1/2,1]],

det G₃=r⁶/2>0.

THEOREM 4.VI.P3 — THE TETRAHEDRAL BASIS IS LINEARLY INDEPENDENT

Let B₃ denote any matrix whose columns are a,b,c in an orthonormal realization. Then

B₃ᵀB₃=G₃.

Taking determinants,

(det B₃)²=det G₃=r⁶/2>0.

Hence det B₃≠0, so a,b,c are linearly independent. Therefore every vector in their span has a unique representation

R=q¹a+q²b+q³c.

The rank-three coordinate system is certified only because the independent solid carrier of 4.V has already been admitted. ∎

THEOREM 4.VI.P4 — THE TETRAHEDRAL RECIPROCAL BASIS

Define a*,b*,c* by

g(a*,a)=g(b*,b)=g(c*,c)=1

and all cross-pairings zero. Since

G₃⁻¹=(1/r²)[[3/2,−1/2,−1/2],[−1/2,3/2,−1/2],[−1/2,−1/2,3/2]],

we obtain

a*=(1/r²)(3a/2−b/2−c/2),

b*=(1/r²)(−a/2+3b/2−c/2),

c*=(1/r²)(−a/2−b/2+3c/2).

Therefore, for

R=q¹a+q²b+q³c,

q¹=g(a*,R),

q²=g(b*,R),

q³=g(c*,R).

The coframe (a*,b*,c*) is the metric-dual reciprocal coframe to the tetrahedral basis. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VI.P4.1 — NONVANISHING VOLUME FORM

The oriented volume of the basis parallelepiped has magnitude

|det B₃|=√(det G₃)=r³/√2.

After an orientation is chosen, the corresponding volume form satisfies

vol(a,b,c)=±r³/√2.

Changing the sign exchanges the two orientations but leaves every metric statement unchanged. No preferred sign has been derived. ∎

4.VI.C — THE TETRAHEDRAL CENTER LATTICE

Definition 4.VI.C1 — Tetrahedral center lattice.

The discrete center lattice generated by the three Flower basis vectors is

Λ₃=ℤa+ℤb+ℤc.

A lattice vector has the form

v=n₁a+n₂b+n₃c,

with n₁,n₂,n₃∈ℤ.

THEOREM 4.VI.P5 — EXACT LATTICE NORM FORM

For v=n₁a+n₂b+n₃c,

|v|²=r²Q(n₁,n₂,n₃),

where

Q=n₁²+n₂²+n₃²+n₁n₂+n₁n₃+n₂n₃.

Proof. Expand g(v,v) using g(a,a)=g(b,b)=g(c,c)=r² and g(a,b)=g(a,c)=g(b,c)=r²/2. ∎

LEMMA 4.VI.L1 — INTEGER LOWER BOUND

The quadratic form satisfies

2Q=(n₁+n₂+n₃)²+n₁²+n₂²+n₃².

For any nonzero integer triple, the right-hand side is a positive integer. Hence Q≥1.

Proof. Expanding the right-hand side yields exactly twice the expression defining Q. Because Q is integer-valued, positivity for a nonzero triple implies Q≥1. ∎

THEOREM 4.VI.P6 — EXACTLY TWELVE NEAREST NEIGHBORS

A nonzero lattice vector has minimal length r exactly when Q=1. By Lemma 4.VI.L1,

(n₁+n₂+n₃)²+n₁²+n₂²+n₃²=2.

There are only two possibilities.

First, n₁²+n₂²+n₃²=1 and (n₁+n₂+n₃)²=1. This gives

±(1,0,0), ±(0,1,0), ±(0,0,1).

Second, n₁²+n₂²+n₃²=2 and n₁+n₂+n₃=0. This gives

±(1,−1,0), ±(1,0,−1), ±(0,1,−1).

Thus the complete nearest-neighbor displacement set is

N₁₂={±a,±b,±c,±(a−b),±(a−c),±(b−c)}.

It contains exactly twelve vectors, all of length r. No other nonzero lattice point lies at distance r. ∎

This proves the 12-neighbor shell rather than assuming it from a picture or finite sphere cluster.

4.VI.D — ORTHONORMAL REALIZATION AND FCC EQUIVALENCE

THEOREM 4.VI.P7 — AN ORTHONORMAL REALIZATION OF THE TETRAHEDRAL BASIS

In an auxiliary orthonormal coordinate system define

a=(r/√2)(1,1,0),

b=(r/√2)(1,0,1),

c=(r/√2)(0,1,1).

Then

|a|=|b|=|c|=r

and

a·b=a·c=b·c=r²/2.

Hence this realization has exactly the Gram matrix G₃ and is isometric to the abstract tetrahedral Flower basis. ∎

THEOREM 4.VI.P8 — Λ₃ IS THE FACE-CENTERED-CUBIC/D₃ LATTICE IN THIS REALIZATION

For n=(n₁,n₂,n₃)∈ℤ³,

n₁a+n₂b+n₃c

=(r/√2)(n₁+n₂,n₁+n₃,n₂+n₃).

Set

p=n₁+n₂,

q=n₁+n₃,

s=n₂+n₃.

Then p+q+s=2(n₁+n₂+n₃) is even. Hence

Λ₃⊆(r/√2){(p,q,s)∈ℤ³:p+q+s even}.

Conversely, if p,q,s are integers with even total sum, then

n₁=(p+q−s)/2,

n₂=(p+s−q)/2,

n₃=(q+s−p)/2

are integers and reconstruct the point. Therefore

Λ₃=(r/√2){(p,q,s)∈ℤ³:p+q+s even}.

This is the standard D₃ realization of the face-centered-cubic lattice, scaled so that nearest-neighbor distance is r. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VI.P8.1 — CUBOCTAHEDRAL FIRST SHELL

Under the orthonormal realization, the twelve nearest-neighbor vectors are precisely

(r/√2)(±1,±1,0)

and all coordinate permutations. Their convex hull is a regular cuboctahedron centered at the origin.

Thus the vector-equilibrium/cuboctahedral first shell is a theorem of the admitted lattice Λ₃. It is not the source of rank three. ∎

THEOREM 4.VI.P9 — POINT SYMMETRY DOES NOT SELECT A CHIRALITY

Every signed permutation of the three orthonormal coordinate axes preserves the parity condition p+q+s even and preserves Euclidean length. Hence the signed permutation group of order

2³·3!=48

acts by isometries of Λ₃ and permutes the twelve nearest neighbors.

Exactly half of these transformations have determinant +1 and half determinant −1. Therefore the lattice admits both orientation-preserving and orientation-reversing symmetries.

Consequently the metric lattice cannot canonically select one handedness over the other. An orientation may be declared, and det B₃ then records its sign, but a preferred physical chirality requires additional orientation-sensitive structure. ∎

This is the lattice form of the orientation obstruction already established earlier in the revised corpus.

4.VI.E — CONTACT-SPHERE PACKING COROLLARY

A distinction is required between two radii.

The construction spheres of Flower Space have radius r: they are the equal spheres used to locate tetrahedral vertices.

If instead one places congruent nonoverlapping packing spheres at the lattice centers of Λ₃, whose nearest-center separation is r, the largest touching packing radius is r/2.

COROLLARY 4.VI.P10 — CONTACT NUMBER AND PACKING FRACTION

Packing spheres of radius r/2 centered at Λ₃ touch exactly the twelve nearest neighbors proved in Theorem 4.VI.P6.

The primitive parallelepiped volume is

Vcell=√(det G₃)=r³/√2.

There is one lattice center per primitive cell. A sphere of radius r/2 has volume

Vsphere=(4π/3)(r/2)³=πr³/6.

Therefore the packing fraction is

η=Vsphere/Vcell

=π/(3√2).

This is the standard FCC close-packing fraction. It is a geometric corollary of the lattice and does not assert that physical space is filled with material spheres. ∎

4.VI.F — NONUNIQUENESS OF OCTAHEDRAL TETRAHEDRAL REFINEMENT

THEOREM 4.VI.P11 — THE REGULAR OCTAHEDRAL CELL DOES NOT SELECT A UNIQUE TETRAHEDRAL SUBDIVISION

A regular octahedron has three diagonals joining opposite vertex pairs. Choosing any one of these diagonals and joining it to the four remaining vertices partitions the octahedron into four congruent tetrahedra.

The three diagonal choices are permuted by octahedral lattice symmetries. No one choice is invariant under the full symmetry group.

Therefore the tetrahedral–octahedral Flower honeycomb does not by itself choose a unique tetrahedral refinement of every octahedral cell. Any all-tetrahedral finite mesh requires an additional refinement convention or boundary rule.

The four tetrahedra in such an octahedral subdivision are not regular tetrahedra: each contains the chosen long octahedral diagonal. The distinction must be retained whenever a finite “tetrahedron count” is used. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VI.P11.1 — FINITE CLUSTERS ARE NOT DEFINITIONS OF THE INFINITE LATTICE

A finite 33-center, 64-cell, or similar Flower cluster may be useful as a visualization or boundary-conditioned finite complex, but it is not the definition of lattice adjacency, rank, or global filling. Those are fixed by Λ₃, its metric, and the completion postulates of 4.V–4.VI.

Any finite cluster count must therefore state its truncation and subdivision rules explicitly. ∎

4.VI.G — COORDINATE AND LATTICE CLOSURE

The analytic dependency chain is now

F² construction

→ native basis (a,b)

→ nondegenerate G₂

→ unique planar coordinates and reciprocal coframe

→ explicit solid extension F³

→ tetrahedral basis (a,b,c)

→ nondegenerate G₃

→ unique tetrahedral coordinates and reciprocal coframe

→ integer center lattice Λ₃

→ exact 12-neighbor shell

→ FCC/D₃ realization

→ cuboctahedral first shell.

Three restrictions remain constitutional.

First, none of these coordinate facts manufactures dimension from the one-generator Projection calculus. The independent basis was supplied by the geometric extension of 4.IV–4.V.

Second, the FCC equivalence is an isometric realization of the center lattice, not an assertion that a preferred Cartesian frame exists in the underlying construction.

Third, the orientation sign of a basis is recordable but not preferential. The lattice symmetry group contains orientation reversal, so no physical handedness has yet been selected.

The next section may therefore use the 12-neighbor shell and cuboctahedral vector equilibrium as a discrete diagnostic of the constructed three-dimensional carrier, but not as an independent proof of rank or as a source of physical dynamics.

4.VII — SUPPLEMENTARY VECTOR-EQUILIBRIUM AND FINITE-LATTICE DIAGNOSTICS

PURPOSE

The preceding section proved the infinite tetrahedral center lattice Λ₃ and its exact 12-neighbor shell. The present section extracts the discrete consequences that were historically discussed under “vector equilibrium,” close packing, and finite Flower clusters.

These are diagnostics of the already-constructed rank-three geometry. They are not alternative foundations for it.

4.VII.A — THE NEAREST-NEIGHBOR GRAPH

Definition 4.VII.A1 — Flower adjacency graph.

Let Γ₃ be the graph whose vertex set is Λ₃ and whose vertices x,y are joined precisely when

|x−y|=r.

THEOREM 4.VII.P1 — Γ₃ IS 12-REGULAR

By translation invariance, the neighbors of any lattice point x are x plus the minimal vectors of Λ₃. Theorem 4.VI.P6 proved that there are exactly twelve such vectors:

N₁₂={±a,±b,±c,±(a−b),±(a−c),±(b−c)}.

Therefore every vertex of Γ₃ has degree twelve. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VII.P1.1 — BOUNDARY LOSS IN A FINITE CLUSTER IS NOT A CHANGE OF BULK COORDINATION

If K⊂Λ₃ is finite and one takes the induced adjacency graph, a boundary vertex may have fewer than twelve neighbors because some of its lattice neighbors lie outside K. This is a truncation effect. It does not alter the degree-twelve adjacency of the infinite lattice. ∎

4.VII.B — THE VECTOR-EQUILIBRIUM SHELL

THEOREM 4.VII.P2 — THE FIRST COORDINATION SHELL IS A REGULAR CUBOCTAHEDRON OF EDGE r AND CIRCUMRADIUS r

In the orthonormal realization of 4.VI,

N₁₂=(r/√2){all coordinate permutations of (±1,±1,0)}.

Every shell vertex therefore lies at distance r from the center.

Two shell vertices are adjacent in the convex hull precisely when their difference is another minimal lattice vector. For example,

|(r/√2)(1,1,0)−(r/√2)(1,0,1)|

=|(r/√2)(0,1,−1)|

=r.

Signed coordinate permutations carry this edge to every other edge of the shell. Hence all shell edges have length r.

The convex hull is therefore the regular cuboctahedron with edge length r and center-to-vertex distance r.

Thus the shell satisfies the defining metric equality traditionally called vector equilibrium:

center-to-vertex length = polyhedral edge length = r. ∎

This statement is now a theorem of Λ₃ rather than an imported visual analogy.

THEOREM 4.VII.P3 — THE FIRST MOMENT OF THE VECTOR-EQUILIBRIUM SHELL VANISHES

The shell occurs in six opposite pairs. Therefore

Σ_{v∈N₁₂}v=0.

Hence its vector centroid is the lattice center. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VII.P3.1 — NO NET DIRECTION IS SELECTED BY THE SHELL

Any construction depending only on the unweighted sum of the twelve nearest-neighbor directions returns zero. The shell therefore supplies no preferred vector direction. ∎

THEOREM 4.VII.P4 — THE SECOND MOMENT OF THE SHELL IS ISOTROPIC

Let the orthonormal coordinate realization be used only for calculation. Consider

M=Σ_{v∈N₁₂} v vᵀ.

Signed permutations of coordinates preserve N₁₂. Therefore M must be diagonal with three equal diagonal entries. Also

tr M=Σ_{v∈N₁₂}|v|²=12r².

Hence each diagonal entry is 4r² and

M=4r²I₃.

Equivalently, for the twelve unit directions u=v/r,

Σ_{u}u uᵀ=4I₃.

Thus the nearest-neighbor directions form an isotropic finite tight frame in three dimensions. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VII.P4.1 — QUADRATIC DIRECTIONAL BALANCE

For every vector w,

Σ_{u}(u·w)²=4|w|².

Therefore the shell is not only balanced at first order; its unweighted quadratic directional response is the same in every direction. ∎

This is the strongest precise sense in which the static vector-equilibrium shell is locally isotropic. It does not imply continuous rotational symmetry of the lattice itself.

4.VII.C — CELL INCIDENCE AROUND A LATTICE CENTER

COROLLARY 4.VII.P5 — THE CUBOCTAHEDRAL VERTEX FIGURE RECORDS THE LOCAL TETRAHEDRAL–OCTAHEDRAL INCIDENCE

The cuboctahedral first shell has eight triangular faces and six square faces. In the tetrahedral–octahedral honeycomb, a small link about a lattice vertex cuts a tetrahedral cell in a triangle and an octahedral cell in a square.

Accordingly, in the completed regular honeycomb each interior lattice vertex is incident to eight tetrahedral cells and six octahedral cells.

This is a local incidence statement about the completed honeycomb P4.4; it is not needed to prove rank three or the 12-neighbor shell. ∎

4.VII.D — FINITE FLOWER CLUSTERS

Definition 4.VII.D1 — Finite Flower cluster.

A finite Flower cluster is a finite subset K⊂Λ₃ together with whatever subset of lattice edges and honeycomb cells is explicitly retained.

A cluster is therefore specified by at least:

1. a vertex-selection or truncation rule;

2. an edge/admissibility rule;

3. a cell-selection rule; and

4. if octahedra are subdivided, a tetrahedral refinement rule.

THEOREM 4.VII.P6 — A FINITE CENTER COUNT DOES NOT UNIQUELY DETERMINE A CELL COMPLEX

The same finite vertex set can support different boundary edge sets and different selections of tetrahedral and octahedral cells. Moreover, by Theorem 4.VI.P11, every octahedron has three symmetry-related choices of long diagonal if an all-tetrahedral refinement is demanded.

Therefore a statement such as “33 centers” does not by itself determine a unique cell decomposition, and a statement such as “64 tetrahedra” is not canonical unless the truncation and octahedral-refinement conventions are also specified. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VII.P6.1 — STATUS OF THE HISTORICAL 33-CENTER / 64-TETRAHEDRON PICTURE

The historical finite Flower cluster may be retained as a visualization or as a deliberately chosen finite boundary model. It must not be used as the definition of F³, of lattice adjacency, or of geometric rank.

If the 64-tetrahedron count is used later, the consolidated corpus must state the exact finite region and subdivision convention that produces that count before any theorem depends on it. ∎

4.VII.E — DISCRETE LOOP CLOSURE AND THE CONNECTION OBSTRUCTION

THEOREM 4.VII.P7 — THE EMBEDDED LATTICE ALONE HAS ZERO TRANSLATIONAL HOLONOMY AROUND EVERY CLOSED GRAPH LOOP

Let

x₀→x₁→⋯→x_m=x₀

be any closed path in Γ₃. Its successive displacement vectors are

Δx_k=x_{k+1}−x_k.

Then

Σ_{k=0}^{m−1}Δx_k

=Σ_{k=0}^{m−1}(x_{k+1}−x_k)

=x_m−x₀

=0.

Therefore the static embedded lattice produces no nonzero translational closure defect by itself. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VII.P7.1 — A CONNECTION CANNOT BE IDENTIFIED WITH THE STATIC NEIGHBOR GRAPH

The existence of twelve directions, tetrahedral/octahedral cells, or closed lattice loops does not by itself produce anholonomy, curvature, torsion, or curl. Those require an additional rule describing how a frame or state is compared from point to point.

Thus a nontrivial connection must enter as new geometric structure on the already-constructed carrier, not as a relabeling of the cuboctahedral shell. ∎

4.VII.F — DISCRETE DIAGNOSTIC CLOSURE

The certified discrete chain is now

Λ₃

→ exact degree-twelve adjacency

→ regular cuboctahedral first shell

→ vector equilibrium

→ vanishing first moment

→ isotropic second moment

→ tetrahedral–octahedral local incidence.

The following do not follow from that chain alone:

• a preferred spatial axis;

• a preferred chirality;

• a unique finite 33-center cluster;

• a unique 64-tetrahedron refinement;

• a nonzero connection;

• anholonomy;

• torsion;

• curvature;

• physical force or dynamics.

This leaves the next problem sharply posed. The carrier, metric, coframe, reciprocal frame, lattice, and local isotropic shell have been constructed. What has not yet been supplied is a law for comparing frames at distinct points.

That is the subject of the next section.

4.VIII — ANHOLONOMY, CONNECTION, ANGULAR GRADIENT, AND CURL

PURPOSE

Sections 4.IV–4.VII have supplied a rank-three carrier, metric, coframe, reciprocal frame, lattice, and exact local neighbor symmetry. None of those structures alone specifies how a frame at one point is to be compared with a frame at another.

The present section introduces that comparison structure explicitly. It also separates four notions that were historically allowed to blur together: varying frame coefficients, anholonomy, torsion, and curvature.

Source provenance. The prior T-series introduced connection, torsion, and curvature only after an independent coframe had been admitted, and explicitly warned that variable metric or frame components do not automatically imply curvature. Revised Book 4 retains that structural distinction but stays entirely within three-dimensional mathematical geometry; no Lorentzian signature, physical time, gravitational field equation, or electromagnetic identification is imported here. fileciteturn21file0

4.VIII.A — VARIABLE COFRAMES AND ANHOLONOMY

Let U be a regular chart of the rank-three Euclidean carrier. Let q¹,q²,q³ be local coordinates and let

θᵃ=Eᵃ_i(q)dqⁱ,

a=1,2,3,

with det E(q)≠0 throughout U.

The dual frame e_a is defined by

θᵃ(e_b)=δᵃ_b.

Definition 4.VIII.A1 — Holonomic coframe.

A coframe is holonomic on U when there exist local coordinate functions yᵃ such that

θᵃ=dyᵃ.

Then dθᵃ=0.

Definition 4.VIII.A2 — Anholonomy coefficients.

For a general coframe define Cᵃ_bc by

dθᵃ=−(1/2)Cᵃ_bc θᵇ∧θᶜ,

with Cᵃ_bc=−Cᵃ_cb.

THEOREM 4.VIII.P1 — ANHOLONOMY COEFFICIENTS EQUAL THE DUAL-FRAME COMMUTATOR COEFFICIENTS

If

[e_b,e_c]=Cᵃ_bc e_a,

then the same Cᵃ_bc appear in

dθᵃ=−(1/2)Cᵃ_bc θᵇ∧θᶜ.

Proof. For any one-form α,

dα(X,Y)=X[α(Y)]−Y[α(X)]−α([X,Y]).

Set α=θᵃ, X=e_b, Y=e_c. Because θᵃ(e_b)=δᵃ_b is constant, the first two terms vanish. Hence

dθᵃ(e_b,e_c)=−θᵃ([e_b,e_c])=−Cᵃ_bc.

This is exactly the coefficient statement above. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VIII.P1.1 — NONCONSTANT COEFFICIENTS DO NOT NECESSARILY MEAN ANHOLONOMY

A coframe may have position-dependent coefficients Eᵃ_i(q) and still satisfy dθᵃ=0. For example, if yᵃ=yᵃ(q) is any regular nonlinear coordinate change, then

θᵃ=dyᵃ=(∂yᵃ/∂qⁱ)dqⁱ

usually has nonconstant coefficients, while

dθᵃ=d²yᵃ=0.

Therefore “the frame varies” is weaker than “the frame is anholonomic.” ∎

4.VIII.B — CONNECTION

Definition 4.VIII.B1 — Frame connection.

A frame connection on U is a matrix of one-forms

ωᵃ_b.

It prescribes infinitesimal comparison of frame components at neighboring points.

When the declared internal frame metric is δ_ab and the connection is metric-compatible, require

ω_ab=−ω_ba.

Thus a metric-compatible spatial connection is so(3)-valued.

Definition 4.VIII.B2 — Covariant exterior derivative.

For a frame-valued p-form αᵃ define

Dαᵃ=dαᵃ+ωᵃ_b∧αᵇ.

Definition 4.VIII.B3 — Torsion.

The torsion two-form is

Tᵃ=Dθᵃ=dθᵃ+ωᵃ_b∧θᵇ.

Definition 4.VIII.B4 — Curvature.

The curvature two-form is

Rᵃ_b=dωᵃ_b+ωᵃ_c∧ωᶜ_b.

These are definitions on the declared frame bundle. No physical interpretation is attached in Book 4.

THEOREM 4.VIII.P2 — ANHOLONOMY AND TORSION ARE NOT THE SAME OBJECT

For a fixed coframe θᵃ,

Tᵃ=dθᵃ+ωᵃ_b∧θᵇ.

Hence dθᵃ may be nonzero while Tᵃ vanishes if the connection term cancels the anholonomy. Conversely, choosing ω=0 makes Tᵃ=dθᵃ.

Therefore anholonomy is a property of the chosen coframe, while torsion is a property of the coframe together with a connection. ∎

THEOREM 4.VIII.P3 — TORSION AND CURVATURE OBEY THE CARTAN–BIANCHI IDENTITIES

With the definitions above,

DTᵃ=Rᵃ_b∧θᵇ

and

DRᵃ_b=0.

Proof. Apply D to Tᵃ=Dθᵃ. The square of the covariant exterior derivative acts by curvature, giving

D²θᵃ=Rᵃ_b∧θᵇ.

For curvature, expand D(dω+ω∧ω); the terms cancel by d²=0, the graded Leibniz rule, and associativity of matrix multiplication with wedge product. ∎

These identities are geometric consistency relations, not equations of motion.

4.VIII.C — PURE-GAUGE FRAME VARIATION

THEOREM 4.VIII.P4 — PURE-GAUGE CONNECTION THEOREM

Let B:U→GL(3,ℝ) be one globally defined smooth frame transformation on a regular chart, and define

ω=B⁻¹dB.

Then

R=dω+ω∧ω=0.

Proof. Differentiate B⁻¹B=I to obtain

dB⁻¹=−B⁻¹(dB)B⁻¹.

Therefore

dω=d(B⁻¹dB)

=dB⁻¹∧dB+B⁻¹d²B

=−B⁻¹dB B⁻¹∧dB

=−ω∧ω.

Hence R=0. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VIII.P4.1 — A VARYING LOCAL ROTATION DOES NOT BY ITSELF PRODUCE CURVATURE

If B(q)∈SO(3) varies smoothly with position and ω=B⁻¹dB, then ω is generally nonzero but R=0. The connection records a changing frame convention; its variation alone is not invariant curvature. ∎

NEGATIVE THEOREM 4.VIII.N1 — ONE-SCALAR FRAME ROTATION CANNOT GENERATE LOCAL CONNECTION CURVATURE BY VARIATION ALONE

Let φ:U→ℝ be one scalar field and let B(q)=B̂(φ(q)) be any smooth one-parameter family of invertible frame transformations. Then

ω=B⁻¹dB=A(φ)dφ

for A(φ)=B̂(φ)⁻¹ dB̂/dφ.

Because dφ∧dφ=0 and d²φ=0,

dω=0

up to the matrix derivative term proportional to dφ∧dφ, which also vanishes, and

ω∧ω=A(φ)A(φ)dφ∧dφ=0.

Therefore

R=0.

Equivalently, the same result follows immediately from Theorem 4.VIII.P4 because B is a single smooth frame transformation.

Thus a Projection phase, even when used to rotate an already-existing three-dimensional frame, does not create local curvature merely because the phase varies. Nonzero curvature requires connection data that are not reducible on the chart to one globally defined pure-gauge frame transformation. ∎

Caveat. A flat connection can still participate in nontrivial global topology when a global gauge B does not exist or when identifications are imposed. That is a separate global question and does not convert local scalar variation into nonzero curvature.

4.VIII.D — EXPLICIT FLAT ANHOLONOMIC EXAMPLE

THEOREM 4.VIII.P5 — NONZERO ANHOLONOMY CAN OCCUR IN FLAT EUCLIDEAN GEOMETRY

On ordinary Euclidean three-space away from the cylindrical axis, take the orthonormal coframe

θ¹=dr,

θ²=r dφ,

θ³=dz.

Then

dθ¹=0,

dθ³=0,

but

dθ²=dr∧dφ=(1/r)θ¹∧θ²≠0.

Thus the coframe is anholonomic.

Choose the metric-compatible connection

ω¹_2=−dφ,

ω²_1=dφ,

with all other components zero. Then

T¹=T²=T³=0.

Also

dω¹_2=−d²φ=0

locally, and the quadratic connection terms vanish, so

Rᵃ_b=0.

Hence anholonomy can be nonzero while both torsion and curvature vanish. ∎

This example forbids the inference

varying/rotating coframe ⇒ curvature.

4.VIII.E — ANGULAR GRADIENT AND THE HISTORICAL TERM “ANGULAR SHEAR”

Definition 4.VIII.E1 — Angular-gradient one-form.

For an SO(3)-valued orientation field B(q), define

Ω=B⁻¹dB∈Ω¹(U,so(3)).

Using the standard axial-vector isomorphism so(3)≅ℝ³ after orientation is declared, one may equivalently represent Ω as an ℝ³-valued one-form.

This object measures the spatial gradient of local frame orientation.

Terminology rule. The historical phrase “angular shear” may be used in the consolidated corpus only when it refers explicitly to this angular-gradient object or to another separately defined tensor. It must not be used as a synonym for curl, torsion, or curvature.

THEOREM 4.VIII.P6 — ANGULAR GRADIENT IS NOT CURVATURE

If Ω=B⁻¹dB for one smooth orientation field B, then by Theorem 4.VIII.P4

dΩ+Ω∧Ω=0.

Thus Ω may be nonzero even though its connection curvature vanishes identically. ∎

4.VIII.F — ORDINARY VECTOR CURL

Assume the positive-definite spatial metric and an orientation already declared for the Euclidean carrier. Let ♭ lower a vector index and let ⋆ be the resulting Hodge star.

Definition 4.VIII.F1 — Vector curl.

For a vector field v,

(curl v)♭=⋆d(v♭).

THEOREM 4.VIII.P7 — VECTOR CURL IS NOT FRAME-CONNECTION CURVATURE

Vector curl is, after applying ♯, an ordinary vector field. Connection curvature Rᵃ_b is an End(T)-valued two-form, or in a metric-compatible frame an so(3)-valued two-form.

The two objects have different tensor types and different transformation laws. Therefore

curl v ≠ Rᵃ_b

as a structural identity.

They can enter the same later model only through an explicitly declared relation or contraction. ∎

COROLLARY 4.VIII.P7.1 — A COMMON LOOP DOES NOT IDENTIFY DIFFERENT CURVATURE NOTIONS

Circulation of a vector field around a loop, frame holonomy around the same loop, and discrete edge closure of the lattice can all be evaluated on geometrically related loops. Their sharing a loop does not make them the same object. ∎

4.VIII.G — LOCAL HOLONOMY

THEOREM 4.VIII.P8 — CURVATURE MEASURES LEADING-ORDER FAILURE OF FRAME TRANSPORT TO CLOSE

For a sufficiently small oriented surface Σ with boundary ∂Σ, parallel transport generated by ω around ∂Σ differs from the identity at leading order by the curvature flux through Σ.

Symbolically,

Hol(∂Σ)=I+∫_Σ R+O(area^{3/2})

in a regular local trivialization, with the precise higher-order form depending on path ordering.

Thus curvature is the infinitesimal obstruction to path-independent frame transport. ∎

This is the appropriate geometric meaning of “failed frame closure.” It is distinct from the telescoping positional closure proved for the static lattice in 4.VII.

4.VIII.H — DISTINCTION LEDGER

The following implications are invalid without additional hypotheses:

nonconstant Eᵃ_i ⇒ dθᵃ≠0;

dθᵃ≠0 ⇒ Tᵃ≠0;

Tᵃ≠0 ⇒ Rᵃ_b≠0;

ωᵃ_b≠0 ⇒ Rᵃ_b≠0;

B(q) varying ⇒ Rᵃ_b≠0;

curl v≠0 ⇒ frame curvature≠0;

one scalar phase varying ⇒ geometric curvature.

The exact hierarchy is instead

frame coefficients E(q)

→ coframe θ

→ anholonomy dθ

plus an independently specified connection ω,

from which

T=dθ+ω∧θ,

R=dω+ω∧ω.

4.VIII.I — THIRD CLIMAX

The rank obstruction was resolved only by adding independent carrier directions. The connection obstruction is resolved in the same disciplined manner: changing coefficients, rotating a local frame, or feeding the Projection phase into an SO(3) rotation does not manufacture curvature.

A smooth frame rotation B(q) gives a pure-gauge connection with R=0. A one-scalar rotation B(φ(q)) is an especially strong flat case. Therefore any later nonzero curvature claim must identify the additional nonintegrable connection structure from which that curvature follows.

Book 4 has now separated:

carrier rank,

metric,

coframe,

anholonomy,

connection,

torsion,

curvature,

and vector curl.

The next section audits the remaining metric freedom and states exactly what is determined once a metric is declared, without importing Lorentzian spacetime or physics.

4.IX — METRIC FREEDOM, FRAME REDUNDANCY, AND WHAT MUST BE DECLARED

PURPOSE

The independent rank-three extension supplies the required manifold, while the Flower construction supplies the planar copied-length, perpendicular, and metric witness. Section 4.V then adds the separately admitted noncoplanar data required for rank three. The native planar and tetrahedral Gram matrices record how that metric is obtained, but they are not retained as preferred physical coordinates or as a lattice ontology. Once the Euclidean structure is established, an ordinary orthonormal Cartesian coframe may be adopted without loss of content. Sections 4.VI–4.VIII then distinguish coordinates, coframes, connections, and curvature.

The present section answers the remaining metric question:

Which changes merely re-express the same metric, which changes genuinely alter the metric, and which metric deformations can lawfully depend on Projection scalars without pretending that those scalars created the underlying geometric rank?

Historical boundary. The prior T-series correctly states that a variable component matrix may arise either from curved geometry or from a nonlinear chart on flat geometry; curvature must be tested invariantly rather than inferred from coefficient variation. Revised Book 4 retains that rule while stripping away the historical Lorentzian and physical interpretations.

4.IX.A — THE FLOWER REFERENCE METRIC

Definition 4.IX.A1 — Reference Flower metric.

Let h_F denote the positive-definite metric constructed in 4.IV–4.V from the copied construction length r and the synthetic right-angle standard.

In the planar native basis (a,b),

[h_F]_2=r²[[1,1/2],[1/2,1]].

In the tetrahedral native basis (a,b,c),

[h_F]_3=r²[[1,1/2,1/2],[1/2,1,1/2],[1/2,1/2,1]].

The scale r is part of the construction datum. If one chooses units with r=1, the remaining matrices are dimensionless Gram forms.

THEOREM 4.IX.P1 — THE REFERENCE METRIC IS POSITIVE DEFINITE

The leading principal minors of the tetrahedral Gram matrix are

r²,

3r⁴/4,

r⁶/2,

all positive for r>0. Hence h_F is positive definite. Its planar restriction is likewise positive definite. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IX.P1.1 — ORIENTATION IS NOT NEEDED TO DEFINE h_F

The metric depends on symmetric pairings of vectors and is unchanged when an ordered basis is replaced by one of opposite orientation. Therefore metric structure precedes any choice of volume orientation. ∎

4.IX.B — GENERAL COFRAME METRICS

Let θ=(θ¹,θ²,θ³)ᵀ be any nondegenerate coframe on a regular region U⊂F³.

Definition 4.IX.B1 — Internal metric form.

Let H=(H_ab) be a symmetric positive-definite 3×3 matrix field. Define

h=H_ab θᵃ⊗θᵇ.

In local coordinates qⁱ with

θᵃ=Eᵃ_i dqⁱ,

the coordinate matrix of h is

[h]_{ij}=(EᵀHE)_{ij}.

THEOREM 4.IX.P2 — NONDEGENERACY CRITERION

If H is positive definite and det E≠0, then h is positive definite and nondegenerate.

Proof. For any nonzero coordinate vector v,

vᵀ(EᵀHE)v=(Ev)ᵀH(Ev).

Since E is invertible, Ev≠0; since H is positive definite, the right-hand side is positive. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IX.P2.1 — METRIC RANK IS SUPPLIED BY THE COFRAME, NOT BY THE NUMBER OF SCALAR COEFFICIENTS

Changing the entries of H or E can alter lengths and angles only because the independent one-form directions θ¹,θ²,θ³ already exist. Scalar coefficient multiplicity does not substitute for coframe rank. ∎

4.IX.C — FRAME-CHANGE REDUNDANCY

THEOREM 4.IX.P3 — INTERNAL FRAME REDUNDANCY

Let A:U→GL(3,ℝ) be any invertible frame transformation and define

θ'=Aθ.

If simultaneously

H'=A^{-T}HA^{-1},

then

h'=H'_{ab} θ'^a⊗θ'^b=h.

Proof. In matrix notation,

h'=θ'^T H' θ'

=θ^T A^T(A^{-T}HA^{-1})Aθ

=θ^T Hθ

=h. ∎

Thus the pair (θ,H) is not unique. Different coframe/internal-metric pairs can represent the same geometric metric.

COROLLARY 4.IX.P3.1 — ORTHONORMAL-FRAME GAUGE

If H=I is held fixed, the frame transformations preserving h satisfy

A^T A=I.

Hence the metric-preserving frame group is O(3). Once an orientation is separately chosen, the orientation-preserving subgroup is SO(3).

A general nonorthogonal A changes the metric if H is held fixed, but is merely a change of internal basis if H is transformed simultaneously by A^{-T}HA^{-1}. ∎

4.IX.D — COORDINATE CHANGE IS NOT METRIC CHANGE

THEOREM 4.IX.P4 — COORDINATE-COMPONENT VARIATION IS REPRESENTATIONAL

Let y^α=y^α(q) be a regular coordinate transformation. The same metric h has transformed components

h'_{αβ}(y)

=(∂qⁱ/∂y^α)(∂qʲ/∂y^β)h_{ij}(q(y)).

The component functions may become nonconstant even when h is the same flat metric.

Therefore

nonconstant h_{ij} ⇏ changed geometry,

and

nonconstant h_{ij} ⇏ nonzero curvature. ∎

Example. Euclidean three-space has constant Cartesian components, but in spherical coordinates its metric reads

ds²=dr²+r²dϑ²+r²sin²ϑ dφ².

The coefficients vary with position although the Riemann curvature remains zero.

4.IX.E — GENUINE METRIC DEFORMATION

Definition 4.IX.E1 — Metric deformation relative to h_F.

A second metric h on the same carrier is a genuine deformation of h_F when h≠h_F as tensor fields after all coordinate and internal-frame redundancies have been accounted for.

THEOREM 4.IX.P5 — INVARIANT TEST FOR MERE RE-EXPRESSION

A coordinate transformation or an internal frame transformation performed according to Theorem 4.IX.P3 does not change any scalar metric invariant evaluated at the corresponding geometric point.

Consequently, if two purported metric configurations differ in invariant quantities such as sectional curvature, scalar curvature, geodesic distances between fixed geometric points, or eigenvalues of a geometrically defined endomorphism, they are not merely different coordinate descriptions of one fixed metric. ∎

Caveat. Equality of a small finite list of invariants need not prove global isometry. The theorem is one-way: a demonstrated invariant difference certifies genuine geometric difference.

4.IX.F — LEVI–CIVITA CONNECTION AFTER THE METRIC IS DECLARED

THEOREM 4.IX.P6 — LEVI–CIVITA EXISTENCE AND UNIQUENESS

For every smooth positive-definite metric h on the Euclidean carrier there exists a unique affine connection ∇ satisfying

1. metric compatibility:

∇h=0,

and

2. zero torsion:

T=0.

This is the Levi–Civita connection of h.

Proof sketch. In any coordinate chart, metric compatibility together with symmetry of the lower connection indices uniquely gives the Christoffel symbols

Γ^k_{ij}

=(1/2)h^{kℓ}(∂_i h_{jℓ}+∂_j h_{iℓ}−∂_ℓ h_{ij}).

These coefficients transform as an affine connection and therefore define a connection globally across compatible charts. Substitution verifies ∇h=0 and Γ^k_{ij}=Γ^k_{ji}, hence zero torsion. If two connections satisfy both properties, their difference tensor is simultaneously constrained by metric compatibility and torsion symmetry to vanish. Therefore the connection is unique. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IX.P6.1 — CONNECTION IS DERIVED FROM h ONLY AFTER THE LEVI–CIVITA CONDITIONS ARE CHOSEN

Section 4.VIII allowed a general connection ω. Once h is declared and one additionally requires metric compatibility and zero torsion, ω is no longer arbitrary: it becomes the Levi–Civita connection expressed in the chosen frame.

Thus “connection” and “Levi–Civita connection” must not be used interchangeably before these conditions are stated. ∎

4.IX.G — METRIC CHANGE IS NOT CURVATURE

NEGATIVE THEOREM 4.IX.N1 — A DIFFERENT METRIC NEED NOT BE CURVED

Let A be any constant invertible matrix on a flat coordinate region and set

h=A^T A

in the coordinate coframe dq. This metric differs in components from I unless A is orthogonal, but its components are constant. Its Levi–Civita Christoffel symbols vanish in these coordinates, hence its Riemann curvature vanishes.

Therefore

h≠h_F ⇏ Riem(h)≠0. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IX.N1.1 — ANISOTROPY IS NOT CURVATURE

A constant positive-definite anisotropic metric can be flat. Curvature measures failure of local metric geometry to fit together as one flat geometry, not merely unequal scale factors along different directions. ∎

4.IX.H — PROJECTION-SCALAR METRIC ANSÄTZE

The Projection calculus may now interact with geometry only through an explicit bridge. The relevant distinction is between an internal one-generator state variable and a scalar field defined over the independent geometric carrier.

Definition 4.IX.H1 — Spatial Projection field declaration.

A spatial Projection field is an explicitly declared map

φ:F³→I,

where I is a regular phase interval or chart of the Projection state.

This declaration is new base-dependent data. It is not supplied by Books 0–3 merely because those books contain a scalar phase variable.

Definition 4.IX.H2 — Scalar metric modulation ansatz.

Let K_ab(φ) be a smooth positive-definite matrix-valued function. Relative to an existing coframe θᵃ define

h_φ=K_ab(φ(q)) θᵃ⊗θᵇ.

A particularly simple case is conformal modulation,

h_φ=Ω(φ(q))² h_F,

with Ω>0.

THEOREM 4.IX.P7 — SCALAR MODULATION DOES NOT CREATE GEOMETRIC RANK

If θ¹,θ²,θ³ are already a nondegenerate coframe and K(φ) is positive definite, then h_φ is rank three. Its rank follows from the coframe and positive definiteness of K.

If instead one attempts to build a metric only from one-form factors proportional to dφ, then the resulting bilinear form has rank at most one.

Therefore a Projection scalar may modulate an existing rank-three metric but cannot generate rank three from dφ alone. ∎

THEOREM 4.IX.P8 — SCALAR MODULATION CAN REPRESENT GENUINE METRIC DEFORMATION

Suppose the base metric h_F is held fixed as the reference geometry and Ω(φ(q)) is nonconstant. Then

h_φ=Ω²h_F

is not merely an internal orthogonal frame rotation of h_F, because orthogonal frame rotations preserve h_F exactly.

Whether h_φ is isometric to h_F through some nontrivial coordinate transformation is a separate invariant question. In general it need not be.

Thus scalar dependence in metric coefficients can encode genuine geometry once the scalar has been declared as a field on F³. ∎

THEOREM 4.IX.P9 — CURVATURE OF A CONFORMALLY MODULATED FLOWER METRIC

Let

h'=e^{2σ}h

on a three-dimensional Riemannian carrier. Their scalar curvatures satisfy

R[h']

=e^{-2σ}

(R[h]−4Δ_hσ−2|∇σ|_h²).

In particular, if h=h_F is flat on the region considered,

R[h']

=e^{-2σ}(−4Δσ−2|∇σ|²).

Therefore a nonconstant scalar modulation can produce nonzero curvature, although it need not do so for every σ.

If σ=σ(φ(q)), this curvature arises from the declared spatial field φ and the declared metric ansatz. It is not a theorem of the one-generator Projection calculus by itself. ∎

This result complements 4.VIII.N1 rather than contradicting it. A pure frame rotation B(φ) leaves the metric unchanged and has pure-gauge connection curvature zero. A conformal metric modulation changes the metric itself; its Levi–Civita connection need not be pure gauge relative to the original metric.

4.IX.I — NECESSITY BARRIER FOR PROJECTION COUPLINGS

NEGATIVE THEOREM 4.IX.N2 — A PROJECTION-DEPENDENT METRIC FUNCTION IS AN ANSATZ UNTIL A NECESSITY THEOREM IS PROVED

Let A(x) be any scalar readout from Books 0–3. Expressions such as

h=A(φ)²h_F,

h=A(φ)^{-2}θ¹⊗θ¹+A(φ)²(θ²⊗θ²+θ³⊗θ³),

or any other positive-definite coefficient assignment define mathematically admissible metric candidates wherever their coefficients are regular and positive.

But the Projection identities alone do not select one such assignment from the infinitely many possible positive-definite functions of φ.

Therefore each such relation has status

metric ansatz,

unless an independent theorem proves uniqueness or necessity from previously admitted structure. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IX.N2.1 — RECIPROCITY IS NOT YET A METRIC LAW

The reciprocal identities among srx, sxp, cxp, and crx may motivate reciprocal scale factors, but motivation does not establish that physical or geometric lengths must transform by those factors. Any such assignment remains a later projection contract or geometric ansatz until derived. ∎

4.IX.J — SIGNATURE BOUNDARY

THEOREM 4.IX.P10 — POSITIVE-DEFINITE SPATIAL METRICS DO NOT CONTAIN A TIMELIKE DIRECTION

Every metric constructed in this section has positive signature (+++). An invertible real coordinate change or frame change preserves the inertia of a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form.

Therefore no real change of basis can convert the spatial Flower metric into Lorentzian signature (−+++), nor can it manufacture an independent temporal covector. ∎

COROLLARY 4.IX.P10.1 — SPACETIME REQUIRES A NEW EXTENSION

A Lorentzian spacetime construction requires at least

1. an additional independent covector direction or equivalent rank-four carrier structure, and

2. a separately declared indefinite metric signature.

Neither follows from the three-dimensional Euclidean carrier or from scalar modulation of its positive-definite metric.

Physical time and Lorentzian geometry remain deferred. ∎

4.IX.K — METRIC LEDGER

Certified in Book 4:

• the reference positive-definite Flower metric h_F;

• planar and tetrahedral Gram matrices;

• general positive-definite coframe metrics h=H_ab θᵃ⊗θᵇ;

• frame/internal-metric redundancy;

• coordinate transformation law;

• distinction between component variation and invariant metric change;

• Levi–Civita existence and uniqueness after h is declared;

• flat anisotropic metrics as a counterexample to “metric change implies curvature”;

• lawful scalar modulation of an already-existing metric after a field φ:F³→I is declared;

• the possibility, but not necessity, of curvature from nonconstant conformal modulation;

• preservation of rank and signature boundaries.

Not certified as Projection consequences:

• any unique function K_ab(φ);

• any physical length response to Projection phase;

• any field equation for φ;

• any dynamical equation for h;

• any preferred conformal factor;

• any Lorentzian signature;

• physical time;

• gravity.

The distinction may be summarized as

coframe + positive internal form

→ metric,

metric + torsion-free metric compatibility

→ Levi–Civita connection,

Levi–Civita connection

→ curvature test,

but

Projection scalar alone

⇏ coframe,

Projection scalar alone

⇏ unique metric,

Projection scalar alone

⇏ curvature law.

The next section returns to the Flower foundation itself and audits exactly which geometric assumptions precede the metric construction, which Euclidean statements have actually been reconstructed, and which global Euclidean properties remain declarations.

4.X — FLOWER FOUNDATIONS, CARTESIAN NONPRIMACY, AND THE EUCLIDEAN POSTULATE AUDIT

PURPOSE

Sections 4.IV–4.IX have used the Flower construction as the route by which a planar carrier, perpendicular, metric, solid carrier, lattice, coframe, and metric geometry enter the revised theory. The logical direction must now be frozen so that later books cannot silently reverse it.

The Flower construction is not allowed to assume a Euclidean inner product and then claim to have derived that same inner product from circles. Nor is it to be demoted to a decorative lattice placed inside an already accepted Cartesian plane.

The correct claim is narrower and stronger:

A minimal synthetic construction substrate with copyable segment congruence supports the equal-circle Flower operation. Together with an explicit triangle-congruence principle and a continuous sixfold cell completion, that operation constructs an equilateral local grammar and a perpendicular/right angle without Cartesian coordinates or an inner product. Once a rank-two affine/vector realization is admitted, the copied unit and constructed perpendicular determine a positive-definite local metric standard. Global Euclidean completion requires further stated assumptions or proofs.

Source provenance. This section re-certifies the dependency structure of The Elements of the Flower Plane contained in Mathematic existentialism. That source explicitly states that its Book I right-angle and perpendicular constructions use only points, straight lines, circles, equality, congruence, intersection, and construction, while global flatness and uniqueness of parallels remain foundational declarations rather than results of those propositions.

4.X.A — PRE-METRIC SYNTHETIC SUBSTRATE

Declaration P4.1 — Construction substrate.

Before numerical metric geometry, admit only the following synthetic data on the planar construction domain:

1. points;

2. straight-line incidence and continuous straight extension;

3. intersection of admissible lines and construction circles;

4. an equivalence relation ≅ on finite segments, interpreted as construction congruence;

5. a compass-transfer operation capable of copying a segment-congruence class from one center to another;

6. equality/common-notion rules required for synthetic comparison; and

7. a continuous triangular filling rule for the local Flower complex.

No dot product, Cartesian coordinate system, numerical distance function, trigonometric angle measure, Pythagorean identity, global parallel postulate, or Euclidean inner product is included in P4.1.

Definition 4.X.A1 — Construction circle.

Given a center O and a segment class [AB], a construction circle C(O;[AB]) is the locus constructible by holding the compass opening congruent to AB about O.

Writing “radius r” is permitted later as a numerical normalization. At the pre-metric stage the primitive datum is the congruence class [AB], not a real-valued distance function.

THEOREM 4.X.P1 — NONCIRCULAR EQUAL-CIRCLE FORMULATION

The statement

OX≅AB

for points X on C(O;[AB]) uses only the admitted segment-congruence relation. It does not require a prior bilinear metric.

Therefore equal-circle construction can precede numerical metric geometry provided segment congruence and compass transfer are admitted synthetically rather than defined by an already-existing Euclidean distance. ∎

NEGATIVE THEOREM 4.X.N1 — METRIC-DEFINED CIRCLES CANNOT NONCIRCULARLY DERIVE THE SAME METRIC

If instead “equal circle” is defined at the outset by

d(O,X)=r

for a previously declared Euclidean distance d, then any later argument claiming to derive d solely from those equal circles is circular: the target metric relation was already used in the construction premise.

Thus the revised foundation must keep construction congruence logically prior to numerical metric distance. ∎

4.X.B — THE CONGRUENCE DEBT

The historical Flower source uses triangle congruence to prove equality of the six native sectors and to bisect a native sector. The critical right-angle proof uses side–side–side congruence.

Declaration P4.1-C — SSS congruence principle.

If two triangles have three corresponding sides pairwise congruent, then their corresponding angles are equal, up to direct or reflected placement.

This is admitted as a synthetic congruence principle of the construction substrate.

THEOREM 4.X.P2 — SSS IS A REAL FOUNDATION INPUT, NOT A FLOWER-DERIVED THEOREM

The Flower right-angle proof requires the inference

OB≅OC,

BD≅CD,

OD≅OD

⇒

∠BOD=∠DOC.

That inference is exactly P4.1-C. Therefore the right-angle theorem is conditional on SSS congruence and must not be presented as deriving all rigid congruence from equal circles alone. ∎

Interpretive consequence. This explicit debt is preferable to accepting Euclidean metric geometry wholesale. The imported structure has been reduced from a complete Euclidean plane to a local synthetic congruence principle plus incidence/construction operations.

4.X.C — LOCAL FLOWER GRAMMAR BEFORE METRIC

THEOREM 4.X.P3 — EQUILATERAL FLOWER TRIANGLE

Let A and B be neighboring centers. Draw construction circles centered at A and B with common copied segment class [AB], and let C be an intersection.

Then

AC≅AB,

BC≅BA,

so

AB≅BC≅CA.

Thus ABC is equilateral in the synthetic congruence sense. ∎

Declaration P4.2-L — Sixfold local completion.

At every ordinary interior Flower center O, six congruent primitive Flower triangles meet cyclically without gap or overlap, and rays separated by three consecutive native sectors form one straight line.

THEOREM 4.X.P4 — SIX EQUAL NATIVE SECTORS

The six primitive triangles around O are mutually congruent by P4.1-C. Their angles at O are therefore equal. By P4.2-L they exactly complete one turn.

Hence the complete turn is partitioned into six equal native sectors. ∎

THEOREM 4.X.P5 — SYNTHETIC RIGHT-ANGLE CONSTRUCTION

Let OA be a native ray. Let OB and OC be the next two native rays clockwise, with B and C neighboring centers. Then

OB≅OC≅BC.

Draw equal construction circles centered at B and C with the common Flower opening. Besides O, let their second intersection be D. Join OD.

Because

OB≅OC,

BD≅CD,

OD≅OD,

triangles BOD and COD are congruent by P4.1-C. Therefore

∠BOD=∠DOC,

so OD bisects the native sector BOC.

The angle AOB is one native sector and BOD is one-half native sector. Thus AOD comprises one and one-half native sectors.

Extend OA through O to its opposite ray OE. The straight angle AOE comprises three native sectors by sixfold closure. Therefore DOE also comprises one and one-half native sectors.

Hence

∠AOD=∠DOE.

They are adjacent equal angles on the straight line AE. By the synthetic definition of perpendicularity, OD⊥AE and both adjacent angles are right angles. ∎

COROLLARY 4.X.P5.1 — NUMERICAL ANGLE NORMALIZATION IS DOWNSTREAM

If a complete turn is subsequently normalized to 360°, then

native sector = 60°,

half native sector = 30°,

right angle = 90°.

The numerical degree convention is not required for Theorem 4.X.P5. ∎

COROLLARY 4.X.P5.2 — PARALLEL THEORY IS NOT USED TO CONSTRUCT THE RIGHT ANGLE

The proof of Theorem 4.X.P5 invokes no parallel-line axiom. Therefore the perpendicular is available before the global Euclidean parallel theory. ∎

4.X.D — THE EXACT POINT WHERE METRIC ENTERS

The synthetic construction has now supplied:

• a continuous local rank-two carrier;

• a copied segment unit;

• equal native sectors;

• opposite/straight directions; and

• a constructed perpendicular.

It has not yet supplied a real-valued bilinear metric.

Declaration P4.2-M — Affine/vector realization.

On each ordinary continuous Flower chart, admit a real two-dimensional translation space carrying the constructed directions as rays and respecting the synthetic line/incidence structure.

Let e₁ and e₂ be two copied-unit vectors along the constructed perpendicular rays.

THEOREM 4.X.P6 — MINIMAL ORTHOGONAL METRIC COMPLETION

There exists a unique symmetric bilinear form g on the two-dimensional translation space satisfying

g(e₁,e₁)=r²,

g(e₂,e₂)=r²,

g(e₁,e₂)=0,

where r>0 is the numerical representative assigned to the copied construction unit.

In the basis (e₁,e₂),

[g]=r²I₂.

This form is positive definite.

Proof. Bilinearity and symmetry determine a bilinear form uniquely from its values on basis pairs. For v=xe₁+ye₂,

g(v,v)=r²(x²+y²)>0

for every nonzero v. ∎

Interpretation. The Flower construction does not conjure a metric from nothing. It constructs the unit and orthogonality data that permit a minimal Euclidean metric to be declared without privileging Cartesian axes beforehand.

COROLLARY 4.X.P6.1 — LOCAL PYTHAGOREAN IDENTITY AFTER METRIZATION

If u and v are perpendicular displacement vectors, then

g(u+v,u+v)=g(u,u)+g(v,v).

Thus the analytic Pythagorean relation follows immediately from the admitted bilinear metric. A separate synthetic Euclidean proof of the classical triangle proposition is not being claimed here. ∎

4.X.E — FLOWER COORDINATES AND CARTESIAN COORDINATES

Let a and b be adjacent native unit Flower directions after metric normalization. Then

g(a,a)=g(b,b)=r²,

g(a,b)=r²/2.

Choose the orthonormal construction frame so that

a=e₁,

b=(1/2)e₁+(√3/2)e₂.

THEOREM 4.X.P7 — FLOWER–ORTHOGONAL COORDINATE EQUIVALENCE

For a displacement

P=ua+vb,

its orthogonal-frame coordinates are

X=u+v/2,

Y=(√3/2)v.

The transformation matrix

[[1,1/2],[0,√3/2]]

has determinant √3/2≠0 and is therefore invertible.

Moreover,

X²+Y²=u²+uv+v².

Hence the native Flower metric and the orthogonal Cartesian metric are the same local Euclidean metric written in two different bases. ∎

COROLLARY 4.X.P7.1 — CARTESIAN AXES ARE DOWNSTREAM, NOT PRIMITIVE

One may introduce Cartesian coordinates after a perpendicular frame has been constructed, but they are not required to obtain the right angle itself.

Thus Cartesian nonprimacy is a statement about dependency order, not a claim that Cartesian and Flower coordinates describe different local Euclidean geometries once the metric has been completed. ∎

4.X.F — LOCAL CONSTRUCTION VERSUS GLOBAL EUCLIDEAN COMPLETION

The historical Flower source correctly separates its local synthetic Book I from the later parallel theory. Revised Book 4 adopts the same separation.

Declaration P4.3 — Global Flower completion.

When a global Euclidean Flower Plane is required, additionally assume or independently prove the following global conditions on the continuous completion:

1. extension without boundary on the intended domain;

2. absence of branch points or topological twist that would obstruct a single planar continuation;

3. defect-free metric gluing of neighboring Flower charts;

4. global flatness, equivalently zero intrinsic metric curvature on the ordinary domain; and

5. the Euclidean parallel property: through a point outside a straight line, exactly one parallel to that line exists.

These are not consequences of Theorems 4.X.P3–P5 alone.

THEOREM 4.X.P8 — SQUARES ARE DOWNSTREAM OF THE RIGHT ANGLE AND GLOBAL PARALLEL STRUCTURE

Given a segment AB, erect perpendiculars at A and B using the local Flower construction. After the unique-parallel property is admitted, choose equal lengths AD≅BC along the two perpendiculars and join C to D. The resulting quadrilateral has four right angles; if all four sides are copied equal, it is a square.

Therefore the square is a later construction. It is not the primitive object from which the Flower right angle is obtained. ∎

4.X.G — WHAT THE FLOWER DOES AND DOES NOT RECONSTRUCT

THEOREM 4.X.P9 — RECONSTRUCTION LEDGER

From P4.1, P4.1-C, and P4.2-L, before numerical metric or parallel theory, Book 4 obtains:

• equilateral construction triangles in the congruence sense;

• sixfold native-sector equality;

• straight/opposite native rays;

• native-sector bisection;

• a right angle;

• perpendicular lines;

• and the local synthetic constructions dependent on those operations.

After P4.2-M and numerical normalization of the copied unit, Book 4 obtains:

• a positive-definite local bilinear metric;

• the 60° Flower Gram form;

• orthogonal coordinates;

• local Euclidean length and angle formulas;

• and the analytic Pythagorean identity.

Only after P4.3 does the corpus claim the full global Euclidean planar continuation used for parallel lines, global rectangles/squares, unrestricted affine translation, and later global Euclidean constructions. ∎

4.X.H — NO-EUCLID-WHOLESALE THEOREM

THEOREM 4.X.P10 — THE REVISED FLOWER FOUNDATION IS STRICTLY WEAKER THAN IMPORTING E² WITH ITS INNER PRODUCT AT THE START

The starting data P4.1 do not contain a real-valued distance between arbitrary point pairs, a dot product, Cartesian coordinates, numerical angles, the parallel postulate, the Pythagorean theorem, or global flatness.

Those objects appear only after later construction, metric completion, or global declaration.

Therefore Revised Book 4 does not accept Euclidean metric geometry wholesale before the Flower construction.

At the same time, P4.1 and P4.1-C are genuine inherited synthetic assumptions. The Flower does not derive incidence, segment congruence, compass transfer, or SSS from nothing.

The correct foundational claim is therefore neither

“Euclid was assumed first,”

nor

“all of Euclid was proved from a picture.”

It is

minimal synthetic congruence substrate

→ Flower local construction

→ perpendicular/right angle

→ metric completion

→ optional global Euclidean completion. ∎

4.X.I — NO-NOVELTY-FROM-COORDINATE-CHANGE RULE

THEOREM 4.X.P11 — FOUNDATION PATH AND FINAL COORDINATE EQUIVALENCE ARE COMPATIBLE

A geometry may be reached by a non-Cartesian foundational construction and nevertheless become isometric, after completion, to ordinary Euclidean geometry.

The historical path by which the metric was constructed is not itself a new metric invariant.

Therefore once h_F has been completed and Flower coordinates are related to orthogonal coordinates by an invertible isometry, any prediction depending only on continuous Euclidean metric invariants is unchanged by choosing one coordinate system rather than the other. ∎

COROLLARY 4.X.P11.1 — WHERE NOVELTY COULD STILL ENTER

Any later mathematical or physical novelty must come from additional invariant structure, for example:

• a discrete finite-spacing lattice retained as physical rather than coordinatized away;

• defects or nontrivial gluing;

• a non-Euclidean metric deformation;

• a nontrivial connection or curvature;

• boundary conditions;

• dynamics;

• field content;

• or a Projection-specific coupling law.

It cannot come from the continuous Flower coordinate basis alone. ∎

4.X.J — FOUNDATIONAL FAILURE LEDGER

The following inferences are prohibited:

metric-defined equal circles ⇒ derivation of that same metric;

sixfold Flower picture ⇒ SSS congruence;

local sixfold closure ⇒ global flatness;

right angle ⇒ unique parallels;

Flower coordinates ⇒ new continuous geometry by coordinate naming alone;

orthogonal coordinate equivalence ⇒ Flower construction was foundationally unnecessary;

constructed plane ⇒ physical space;

constructed right angle ⇒ physical law.

The correct distinction is between foundational economy and invariant novelty. The Flower construction can reduce which geometric notions must be primitive while still recovering ordinary Euclidean geometry after its explicit completion.

4.X.K — SECTION CLOSURE

The dependency order is now frozen:

incidence + segment congruence + compass transfer + SSS

→ equal-circle Flower operation

→ equilateral triangular cells

→ sixfold local closure

→ synthetic right angle/perpendicular

→ rank-two affine/vector realization

→ copied-unit orthogonal metric

→ Flower and orthogonal coordinate equivalence

→ separately admitted/proved global Euclidean completion.

This is the foundation to be inherited by all later geometric books.

The next section asks a different question. Once a spatial metric and connection exist, can the carrier support genuine intrinsic curvature without importing spacetime, gravity, or dynamics? The answer is mathematical and must be established before any physical interpretation is permitted.

4.XI — CURVATURE WITHOUT ASSUMING SPACETIME

PURPOSE

Curvature is a geometric property of a connection or metric on a carrier. It does not require a time coordinate, Lorentzian signature, gravitation, an action principle, or a physical interpretation.

This section proves that the three-dimensional Euclidean carrier can host both flat and genuinely curved Riemannian geometries, re-establishes the one-generator flatness obstruction for scalar-generated connections, and separates smooth curvature from discrete lattice-defect curvature.

Nothing in this section identifies curvature with gravity.

4.XI.A — RIEMANNIAN CURVATURE NEEDS NO TIME DIRECTION

Let (F³,h) be a three-dimensional Euclidean carrier equipped with any smooth positive-definite metric h. Let ∇ be its Levi–Civita connection.

Definition 4.XI.A1 — Riemann curvature operator.

For vector fields X,Y,Z define

R(X,Y)Z

=∇_X∇_Y Z−∇_Y∇_X Z−∇_[X,Y] Z.

Equivalently, in an orthonormal coframe with connection one-forms ωᵃ_b,

Rᵃ_b=dωᵃ_b+ωᵃ_c∧ωᶜ_b.

THEOREM 4.XI.P1 — CURVATURE IS WELL-DEFINED ON THE SPATIAL FLOWER CARRIER

The definitions above require only a differentiable rank-three carrier and a declared connection, or a metric from which the Levi–Civita connection is derived. No temporal coordinate or indefinite signature appears.

Therefore intrinsic spatial curvature is mathematically available before spacetime is introduced. ∎

COROLLARY 4.XI.P1.1 — “CURVED” DOES NOT MEAN “GRAVITATIONAL”

A nonzero Riemann tensor establishes non-Euclidean intrinsic geometry. It does not specify why the metric has that geometry, whether it evolves, what sources it, or whether it represents physical space. Those are separate later contracts. ∎

4.XI.B — FLAT GEOMETRY CAN LOOK NONTRIVIAL

Sections 4.VIII and 4.IX already supplied two exact counterexamples to coefficient-based curvature claims:

1. the cylindrical orthonormal coframe

θ¹=dr,

θ²=r dφ,

θ³=dz

has dθ²≠0 while its Levi–Civita curvature vanishes; and

2. ordinary Euclidean space in spherical coordinates has position-dependent metric components

ds²=dr²+r²dϑ²+r²sin²ϑ dφ²

while its Riemann tensor is zero.

NEGATIVE THEOREM 4.XI.N1 — NONTRIVIAL COMPONENTS ARE NOT A CURVATURE CERTIFICATE

None of the following is sufficient by itself to prove intrinsic curvature:

• position-dependent coordinate components;

• a rotating local frame;

• nonzero connection coefficients in one gauge;

• nonzero coframe anholonomy;

• a non-Cartesian basis;

• a nonconstant conformal factor written without evaluating invariants.

A curvature claim must ultimately exhibit nonzero curvature tensor/two-form data or an equivalent invariant. ∎

4.XI.C — A GENUINELY CURVED THREE-DIMENSIONAL FLOWER METRIC

Consider an ordinary Flower chart coordinatized by (x,y,z) on the region z>0. Let L>0 be a declared geometric scale and define

h_H=(L²/z²)(dx²+dy²+dz²).

This is a positive-definite Riemannian metric on a rank-three region. It contains no time coordinate.

Define the orthonormal coframe

θ¹=(L/z)dx,

θ²=(L/z)dy,

θ³=(L/z)dz.

Then

dθ¹=(1/L)θ¹∧θ³,

dθ²=(1/L)θ²∧θ³,

dθ³=0.

THEOREM 4.XI.P2 — LEVI–CIVITA CONNECTION OF THE HYPERBOLIC FLOWER METRIC

The torsion-free metric-compatible connection is

ω¹_3=−(1/L)θ¹,

ω²_3=−(1/L)θ²,

ω¹_2=0,

with antisymmetry

ω³_1=+(1/L)θ¹,

ω³_2=+(1/L)θ².

Proof. Substitution into

dθᵃ+ωᵃ_b∧θᵇ=0

cancels dθ¹ and dθ² and leaves dθ³=0. The displayed connection is antisymmetric, hence metric-compatible. By Levi–Civita uniqueness it is the required connection. ∎

THEOREM 4.XI.P3 — THE METRIC h_H HAS NONZERO CONSTANT NEGATIVE CURVATURE

Its curvature two-forms are

R¹_2=−(1/L²)θ¹∧θ²,

R¹_3=−(1/L²)θ¹∧θ³,

R²_3=−(1/L²)θ²∧θ³,

with antisymmetric partners.

Proof. For example,

R¹_2

=dω¹_2+ω¹_3∧ω³_2

=0+[−(1/L)θ¹]∧[(1/L)θ²]

=−(1/L²)θ¹∧θ².

Also,

R¹_3

=d[−(1/L)θ¹]

=−(1/L²)θ¹∧θ³,

and similarly for R²_3. ∎

COROLLARY 4.XI.P3.1 — CONSTANT SECTIONAL AND SCALAR CURVATURE

Every orthonormal two-plane has sectional curvature

K=−1/L².

In three dimensions the scalar curvature is therefore

R_scalar=6K=−6/L².

Hence h_H is intrinsically curved. No change of coordinates can transform its curvature tensor to zero on an open region. ∎

This supplies the required positive example: the same rank-three carrier that supports the flat Flower reference metric also supports a genuinely curved spatial metric once that metric is independently declared.

4.XI.D — CONNECTION CURVATURE FROM ONE GENERATOR

The exact one-generator obstruction can now be stated independently of frame-rotation language.

THEOREM 4.XI.P4 — ONE-GENERATOR MATRIX CONNECTION FLATNESS

Let φ be one smooth scalar field on a regular chart and let

ω=A(φ)dφ

for any smooth matrix-valued function A of that one scalar. Then

R=dω+ω∧ω=0.

Proof. First,

dω

=d[A(φ)]∧dφ+A(φ)d²φ

=A'(φ)dφ∧dφ

=0.

Second,

ω∧ω

=A(φ)A(φ)dφ∧dφ

=0.

Therefore R=0. ∎

COROLLARY 4.XI.P4.1 — ONE SCALAR CANNOT SUPPLY TWO INDEPENDENT CONNECTION DIRECTIONS

A connection built only from dφ has differential support in one covector direction. Curvature is a two-form and requires antisymmetric two-direction content. The wedge of the single available differential with itself vanishes.

Thus no choice of nonlinear matrix coefficients A(φ) defeats the obstruction. ∎

COROLLARY 4.XI.P4.2 — LOCAL PURE-GAUGE REPRESENTATION

On a simply connected regular interval of φ-values, the matrix ordinary differential equation

dB/dφ=B A(φ)

has a local invertible solution B. Then

ω=B⁻¹dB

up to the chosen left/right convention for the frame action.

Thus the one-generator connection is locally gauge-removable. ∎

Caveat. Flat local curvature does not exclude nontrivial global holonomy produced by topology, identifications, singular exclusions, or transition functions. Such global structure must be specified independently.

4.XI.E — WHY SCALAR METRIC MODULATION ESCAPES THE CONNECTION OBSTRUCTION

Section 4.IX showed that a declared scalar field φ:F³→I may modulate an already-existing metric, for example

h_φ=e^{2σ(φ(q))}h_F.

This does not contradict Theorem 4.XI.P4.

THEOREM 4.XI.P5 — METRIC MODULATION AND ONE-GENERATOR CONNECTIONS ARE DIFFERENT CONSTRUCTIONS

A connection ansatz

ω=A(φ)dφ

restricts the connection one-form itself to the single differential direction dφ and is therefore flat by Theorem 4.XI.P4.

A metric ansatz

h_φ=e^{2σ(φ)}h_F

instead changes the bilinear form on all three pre-existing coframe directions. Its Levi–Civita connection contains derivatives of the metric contracted with the independent spatial basis one-forms. It need not have the restricted form A(φ)dφ in one fixed matrix generator.
